In my experience, wounded parts want us to talk to them directly. They love being seen, heard, and
understood. We aim to help them get an embodied felt sense of the Resources. We want them to
actually feel seen, heard, and valued by the Resources. The more Resources are able to meet the
emotional needs and attachment needs of wounded parts, the deeper the healing gets. Wounded parts
come to understand that the Resources are there to lean on for support 24-7. This kind of loving,
nurturing dialogue promotes neural integration and reduces dependence on the therapist.
I believe this approach is the best, with one exception. Sometimes wounded parts start out extremely
mistrusting of the Resources and will not accept any nurturing from them or from the client’s most
adult self. When that happens, we may need to temporarily adopt the therapist as nurturer approach.
We provide lots of loving, attuned empathy, validation, and support. As soon as these parts have
healed enough to trust the Resources, the Resources become their primary caregivers, and we just
facilitate that nurturing relationship.

Reluctance to Talk to Wounded Parts
Many therapists new to ego state therapy are reluctant to talk directly to wounded parts. They consider
it risky because (1) clients will think it’s too weird, and/or (2) that it will feel weird to do so.
 Clients will think it’s weird: To handle this, we explain ego state therapy in advance before we ever
talk to parts. We explain about ego states in general, how parts get stuck in the past, and how
talking directly to parts to meet their needs can help them heal. We encourage clients to ask lots
of questions, so they can give advance consent. (See Chapter 4 for guidance on explaining ego state
therapy to clients.)
 Therapist thinks it’s weird: This is a novel approach for many clinicians. The best way to get past
this block is to try it enough times that it no longer seems risky or weird. If you’re skittish about
trying this with clients, it might help to pair up with a colleague who is just as interested in learning
this. Practice the interventions with each other, with the mutual understanding that it will feel odd at
first. Make sure it’s someone who will support you as you stumble through the scripts the first few
times. You may be surprised how easy it is to bring wounded parts forward to talk to and delighted
to see how well they respond to healing interventions. This personal discovery can alleviate the
reluctance to talk to parts. It may also help to do your own personal work with an experienced DNMS
therapist. It can be a great experience personally and professionally.

Chapter 2: Ego State Theory and Therapy Overview

19

System-Wide Announcement
Sometimes many wounded parts share a similar mistrust, concern, or fear that stops the process from
smoothly moving forward. A system-wide announcement is a reassuring message conveyed to many
wounded parts at once, tailored to their specific concern. This sample script demonstrates how we
might reassure parts who are concerned that the therapy aims to eliminate them.
I’d like all parts of you to listen now. Each one of you is valuable to me. I don’t want to harm any
of you or eliminate any of you. I want to help each of you heal so you can grow to meet your full
potential. You get to decide what that is. If you’ve been stuck in the past, I’d like to help you get
unstuck. I’m interested in any concerns or fears you have. We will not do any processing as long
as any of you object. I’m willing to patiently answer all your questions. How does that sound?
A message like this could have an instant calming effect, but even when the effects are not instant, we’re
planting a seed for future growth and healing.

Overview of DNMS Stabilization
Before we start stabilizing wounded parts, we must make advance preparations. After we explain ego
state therapy and the client agrees to try it, we apply these three interventions.
1. Mobilize a Resource Team. We help clients mobilize a team of vetted inner Resources that can bring
attuned love, compassion, validation, and empathy to wounded parts. The DNMS provides two
different Resource mobilization protocols. One is long and thorough; one is short and quick. We
select the one that’s the best match for the client’s needs. (Chapters 6 and 7 describe these protocols
in detail.)
2. Establish a Special Safe Place. We help clients establish an imagined Special Safe Place where
wounded parts can rest and play while waiting for their time to heal. It’s a fun place like a beach
resort scout camp, mountain retreat, water park, etc. It’s populated with Resources, so the wounded
parts can get loving support there whenever they need it. (Chapter 8 describes this in detail.)
3. Set an Effective Therapy Goal. Clients begin therapy with certain goals in mind—usually to reduce
or eliminate unwanted behaviors, beliefs, emotions, or urges. When wounded parts feel threatened
by these goals, they block our attempts to meet them. We work with wounded parts to set therapy
goals they will gladly pursue. (Chapter 9 describes this in detail.)
We then stabilize wounded parts with five key DNMS needs-meeting interventions. These alone are
not likely to get wounded parts completely unstuck, but they will provide enough trait change to
stabilize them well. We use these interventions every time a wounded part gets triggered and needs our
help. Here’s a brief overview.
1. Nurturing Interventions. We invite forward wounded parts and get them in dialogue with the
Resources. We invite them to tell us their story. We listen, validate, and empathize. We ensure they
feel seen, heard, valued, understood, and loved unconditionally by the Resources. We help them
to feel safe being authentic and vulnerable with the Resources. (Chapter 10 describes this in detail.)
2. Provide Needed Information. Wounded parts often misperceive the world because of missing
information and misunderstandings. This can include ideas about the therapy process (“I’ll do it
wrong”), the therapy outcome (“Healing is dangerous”), the nature of the Resources (“They won’t
like me”), their personal responsibility (“The abuse was my fault”), or what they deserve (“I don’t
deserve love”). Whenever wounded parts reveal harmful beliefs like these, we provide the important
information they’re missing. (Chapter 11 describes this in detail.)

20

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

3. Manage Enmeshment. Some wounded parts fear that the healing work threatens important enmeshed
childhood relationship. This sense of threat can block or stall processing. The DNMS has two
interventions laser-focused on addressing enmeshment, so processing can flow. (Chapter 12 describes
this in detail.)
4. Orient Wounded Parts to the Present. Many wounded child parts are so stuck in the past that
they believe they’re still in a child’s body, living in their childhood home. We partner with the
Resources to help them self-discover the power and safety that comes with being in an adult body
now—no longer dependent on parents. (Chapter 13 describes this in detail.)
5. Explain about the Harmless Recording. When a triggering event presses “play” on an old recording
and a wounding message from the past is broadcast internally, wounded parts feel like the old
event is actually happening now. We partner with the Resources to help them understand they’re
just reacting to a harmless recording. They’re not reliving the event; they’re just reliving their
reaction to it. This news comes as a great relief. (Chapter 14 describes this in detail.)
6. Wrapping Up and Settling In. When it appears wounded parts are finally stabilized, we invite them
to settle into their Special Safe Place with the Resources. We invite the client to talk about any
changes in reactivity. (Chapte15 describes this in detail.)
Collectively, all these interventions bring wounded parts out of trance and into the safety of the
present. Generally, we start with connecting parts to the Resources, orienting them to the present time,
and then explaining about the harmless recording. We bring into this conversation needed information
to clear up misunderstandings, fill in missing information, or manage enmeshment. But this sequence
of steps is a guideline, not a rigid protocol. From moment to moment, we must select from this list of
interventions. If we stay attuned to the wounded parts and follow them wherever they tell us they need
to go, we’ll provide the most helpful support

Alternating Bilateral Stimulation (ABS) for Stabilizing Wounded Parts
Alternating Bilateral Stimulation (ABS) is a form of mild brain stimulation that can help isolated neural
networks holding trauma memories more easily connect to adaptive information—when applied with
an appropriate protocol like EMDR.18 ABS can be applied in three ways: back-and-forth eye movements,
alternating bilateral audio stimulation, and alternating bilateral tactile stimulation.
 Eye Movements: Clients move their eyes rapidly back and forth—often aided with a device with
moving or blinking lights. This appears to be the most emotionally intense of all three options. It’s
effective but complicated by the reality that, when clients feel emotionally overwhelmed in the
middle of processing, they’re inclined to close their eyes and cry.
 Audio: Clients listen through headphones to alternating tones throughout the processing—either
beeps or special music with an alternating-tone feature.
 Tactile: Tactile stimulation can be applied three different ways. (1) Clients can tap anywhere on their
body bilaterally, like with the butterfly hug.19 (2) The therapist can alternate tapping on the client’s
hands or knees. (3) A client can hold pulsers connected to a device called a TheraTapperTM. Pulsers
vibrate off and on in an alternating fashion at various speeds and intensities. This is my preferred
method because it’s completely passive for the client and hands-free for the therapist. (For more
information, go to theratapper.com.)
During EMDR Phase 4, ABS is applied to desensitize trauma, but when we do DNMS interventions in
EMDR Phase 2, ABS is applied to strengthen positives. First it’s used to strengthen a team of Resources.
Then it’s used to strengthen a Special Safe Place. When wounded parts are feeling seen, heard, valued,

Chapter 2: Ego State Theory and Therapy Overview

21

and understood by the Resources, ABS is applied to strengthen those loving interactions. We use it in
Phase 2 to make big smiles bigger.
EMDR trainers often refer to ABS as Dual Attention Stimulus (DAS) or Bilateral Stimulation (BLS). I prefer
to call it Alternating Bilateral Stimulation because it feels more precise.

Chapter 2 Summary
This chapter provides an overview of ego state theory. Healthy parts of self arise from continuous,
repeated, positive, affirming relationships with people who are loving and attuned—especially in
childhood. Wounded parts of self arise from significant distressing experiences that go
unprocessed—especially in childhood. Parts of self within the self-system can interact with each
other in a variety of ways, from cooperative to combative. This chapter describes the mechanics
of how wounding experiences are stored in the brain and how reminders of those painful
experiences can trigger wounded parts. It illustrates how we can communicate with wounded
parts directly or indirectly—in particular how we can facilitate a supportive dialogue between
wounded parts and loving, supportive internal Resources. An overview of the DNMS stabilization
interventions that are detailed in the book, is provided at the end. The concepts presented in this
chapter should provide an essential foundation for therapists eager to integrate aspects of DNMS
ego state therapy into an EMDR practice.

Chapter 3
Treating Adults Wounded in Childhood

Good case conceptualization is necessary when treating adult clients wounded in childhood. In my
experience, this begins with understanding the intersection between attachment wounding and trauma
wounding. In this chapter I’ll describe several cases with different wounding profiles and offer
treatment suggestions for each. But I start with a brief overview of attachment theory, so the definitions
of secure and insecure attachment are understood when I use them later in this chapter. (See Appendix
E for a more detailed discussion of attachment styles.)

Attachment Styles
A child will develop a secure attachment to caregivers who meet her emotional needs well.1 (See the
Developmental Needs sidebar.) When caregivers consistently fail to meet emotional needs, two kinds
of insecure/anxious attachments can develop: avoidant and ambivalent. An avoidant attachment
develops when caregivers are overly rejecting or distancing. An ambivalent attachment develops when
caregivers are overly intrusive or enmeshing.2 A child can be securely attached to one caregiver who is
loving and attuned while being insecurely attached to another who is not. Independent of attachment
style, a young child may also develop a disorganized attachment in reaction to a particular stressful
caregiver behavior. This can happen, for example, when a child frequently witnesses a caregiver slip
into dissociated states,3 when a caregiver has wildly inconsistent or chaotic strategies for caring for a
child, or when a caregiver’s attempt to calm an upset child actually generates more distress.4
Early childhood relationships influence our attachment styles in adulthood. Generally speaking, if we
grow up feeling securely attached to parents, we’ll have a secure attachment style in adulthood. If we
grow up feeling avoidantly attached to parents, we’ll have a dismissive attachment style in adulthood.
If we grow up feeling ambivalently attached to parents, we’ll have a preoccupied attachment style in
adulthood. Different child ego states can have different attachment styles, so in adulthood, we may
manifest a secure attachment with people we trust, a dismissive attachment with people we fear, and
a preoccupied attachment with people we yearn for.
Even if we start out with an insecure status, we can develop an earned-secure attachment status in
adulthood. This can happen when loving, empathic friends, family, or therapists help us make sense of
our childhood wounds. We’ve reached earned-secure status when we can understand, organize, and
discuss our painful childhood attachment relationships in a clear and coherent way.

Childhood Wounding from Unmet Developmental Needs
Childhood wounding can come from unmet emotional needs (attachment wounding) and unmet
physical safety needs (trauma wounding). How well a client’s emotional needs and physical safety
needs were met in childhood can be rated on two separate continuums. Let’s look at four points on
each continuum, from often met well to never met well.

Continuum of How Well Emotional Needs Were Met
1. Often Met Well: The child’s emotional needs were consistently met well. Loving, attuned caregivers
soothed emotional stress well. The child felt seen, heard, and valued, and trusted caregivers to
solve basic problems. Emotional breaks with caregivers were consistently and lovingly repaired.
The child felt very securely attached to caregivers.
2. Sometimes Met Well: The child’s emotional needs were inconsistently met well. Caregivers could
usually soothe emotional stress. The child felt seen, heard, and valued most of the time. The child
usually sought caregivers for help solving problems, although sometimes the help was disappointing.

Chapter 3: Treating Adults Wounded in Childhood

25

Some emotional breaks with caregivers were lovingly repaired, but others were not. The child felt
somewhat securely attached to at least one caregiver.
3. Seldom Met Well: The child’s emotional needs were seldom met well. Caregivers’ inability to attune
evoked emotional stress. The child seldom felt seen, heard, or valued and seldom trusted caregivers
to help in managing painful emotions or solving problems. Emotional breaks with caregivers were
seldom repaired. The child felt insecurely attached to caregivers.
4. Rarely Met Well: The child’s emotional needs were rarely met. Caregivers evoked significant
emotional stress with verbal abuse, double binds, mixed messages, and/or manipulative mind
games. The child never asked caregivers for help managing emotions or solving problems, because
doing so would make matters worse. Emotional breaks with caregivers were never repaired. The
child felt significant insecure and/or disorganized attachment to caregivers.

Continuum of How Well Physical Safety Needs Were Met.
1. Often Met Well: The childhood environment was extremely safe. Very effective protective measures5
were in place to mitigate potential dangers and resolve safety issues. The child felt very protected
and empowered.
2. Sometimes Met Well: The childhood environment was mostly safe. Moderately effective protective
measures were in place, so the child felt somewhat protected and empowered.
3. Seldom Met Well: The childhood environment was mostly unsafe. The protective measures in place
were seldom effective, so the child felt mostly unprotected and powerless.
4. Rarely Met Well: The childhood environment was very unsafe. Dangers could be life threatening.
No effective protective measures were in place, so the child felt completely unprotected and powerless.

Types of Childhood Wounding Graphed on a Grid
These two continuums can be graphed on a grid. Grid columns 1–4 represent how well emotional
needs were met. Grid rows 1–4 represent how well physical safety needs were met. The higher the
number, the more needs were not met, and the more wounding was endured.

It helps to place our clients’ childhood experiences on this grid, because each quadrant of the grid
requires a different treatment approach. Let’s take a closer look.

26

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Attachment Wounding on the Grid
The right half of the graph (columns 3 and 4) is where many attachment-wounded clients
live. Attachment wounding happens in childhood only in relationships with primary caregivers
like parents. It comes from unmet emotional needs. Attachment wounds can be inflicted
by a wide range of parental behaviors. For example, it can happen when well-meaning
parents are chronically misattuned, when distracted parents are emotionally neglectful, when needy
parents are enmeshing, or when hostile, angry parents are verbally abusive. It’s about parental behavior
that leads a child to feel chronic loneliness, insecurity, and emptiness. The severity of attachment wounding
can range from mild to full-blown existential crisis on par with the most grievous trauma wounding.
Attachment wounding can happen whether physical safety needs were met well or not.

Trauma Wounding on the Grid
The top half of the graph (rows 3 and 4) is where
our chronically fearful clients live. There are three
main sources of this fear: (1) non-relational
trauma wounding from a tragic event like an
accident or natural disaster, (2) relational trauma wounding
inflicted by a nonprimary caregiver, and (3) relational trauma
wounding inflicted by a primary caregiver. (See Types of
Childhood Trauma Wounding sidebar.) These distinctions
matter when conceptualizing the case.
In my view, all childhood traumas can be considered an
unmet need for physical safety. That’s because children are
vulnerable and dependent on grown-ups. They need
grown-ups to protect them—to reduce the risk of
traumatic events happening. If something traumatic does
happen, they need grown-ups to provide comfort and
support to restore an internal sense of safety and to take
action to restore a safe routine, so their external world is
safe again. That dependence makes it about unmet needs.
(See the Developmental Needs sidebar on page 24.)
Much of the responsibility for meeting safety needs falls to
parents, but it’s a responsibility shared with authority figures
like police, community leaders, politicians, and government
officials. Laws, law enforcement practices, regulations, public
policy, and funding of public safety services can protect
children or endanger them. Public officials can inflict
relational trauma wounds when they fail to meet the safety
needs of the children they’re entrusted to protect. For
example, when politicians switched Flint, Michigan’s drinking
water supply from Detroit’s system to the Flint River, the
city’s tap water became contaminated with toxic lead—
putting everyone at risk but especially the children. That
makes it a relational trauma.

Little or No Childhood Wounding
Adults with little or no childhood wounding may seek therapy for help in dealing with
extreme adulthood stress like a job loss, caring for a disabled child, the death of a spouse,
or for a recent traumatic event. Here’s a story about a client raised by wonderful supportive
parents, who came to therapy for help with a single, unresolved childhood trauma.
Julie was an only child of two college professors. Her father taught math, and her mother taught
child development. During Julie’s first ten years, her mother stayed home to raise her. Both parents
were very loving and attuned. They delighted in her and comforted her upsets. Whenever she
misbehaved, they used loving correction to inspire good behavior and to convey she was cherished.
They gave her age-appropriate freedoms while setting good limits. Julie had no siblings, so her
parents created opportunities for her to socialize with peers. They taught her how to make and
keep friends. When she was interested in dinosaurs, they got her books about them. When she was
interested in the piano, they arranged for lessons. They encouraged her to read about lots of
different subjects during weekly library visits. Dinnertime talk was always about interesting topics.
She felt encouraged to express her own opinions. She felt safe going to her parents with any kind
of problem. Julie trusted them to help her grow to meet her full potential both emotionally and
intellectually. One day, at age thirteen, she was eating lunch with her friends in the school cafeteria.
A student came in with a gun and started shooting. Julie was not physically wounded, but she
witnessed others being shot and a teacher being killed. Her parents responded to her emotional
needs very well. They got her into therapy right away for her nightmares and panic attacks. The
cognitive-behavioral therapy helped, and within a year, the trauma had faded into the background,
and she was functioning well again, but at age twenty-five, a horrible high school shooting in the
news brought back her panic and nightmares, making it difficult for her to function.
The AIP model would posit that Julie’s school shooting memory got dysfunctionally stored in the brain
with all the emotions, beliefs, and sensations that were present at the time. EMDR would aim to
desensitize this memory by linking it to adaptive information. For Julie, that could be a full-body
awareness that “It’s over, I’m safe now.” Because Julie was foundationally stable before the school
shooting, EMDR should easily restore her pre-trauma stability.

Nonprimary-Caregiver Relational Trauma Wounding
Some of our clients have all the benefits of loving, attuned caregivers in early childhood
before a tragic trauma takes it all away. This story illustrates how a relational trauma,
inflicted by government officials, can unleash devastation on the life of a happy child.
Jameel was a happy child living with his parents in Syria. As the youngest of five siblings in a large,
close-knit, nurturing family with many aunts, uncles, cousins, and grandparents, he felt very loved,
protected, and safe. Most of his relatives lived nearby. Many of them worked at the family’s grocery
store and restaurant. His parents read to him, played with him, and delighted in him. When he was
upset, they comforted him. He was securely attached to them. He was especially smart, so by age
fourteen, his father was already planning to teach him the business. This pleased Jameel. Then the
war started. Early in the conflict, his town got bombed. The store and restaurant took a direct hit,
killing most of his family including his parents. His oldest brother, his wife, and their two children
survived. They took Jameel and fled to a refugee camp, where they lived for six years. They arrived
in Michigan when Jameel was twenty to begin a new life as immigrants. He lives with his brother’s
family where he feels welcomed and loved. They support each other through the loss and grief and
the cultural adjustments of a new country. He’s challenged with symptoms of PTSD and disturbed
sleep. Nightmares of the bombing, fleeing, and refugee-camp horrors awaken him often.

28

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

The AIP model would posit that Jameel’s experiences of surviving the bombing, losing his family,
fleeing Syria, and living as a refugee are all traumas that got dysfunctionally stored in the brain. EMDR
would aim to desensitize these memories by linking them to adaptive information. For Jameel, adaptive
information could be an awareness that the grief connected to his many losses can process through
and the reality that he’s now far away from the war and the present moment is safe. Because Jameel
was foundationally stable before the war, and lives in a safe and supportive home now, EMDR might
easily treat his PTSD and help him restore his pre-trauma stability.
But Jameel will also have wounded ego states that formed from witnessing violence, losing most of his
family, and experiencing the perils of life as a war refugee. Jameel would probably benefit greatly from
mobilizing a Resource team to help stabilize his most traumatized wounded parts with needs-meeting
interventions. Once his Resources have helped his wounded parts grieve their losses, and once they
feel safe in the present moment, EMDR should be highly effective at processing his trauma memories.

Nonrelational Trauma Wounding
Some of our clients enjoyed a secure attachment to loving, attuned caregivers in early
childhood before a traumatic event shattered the calm. This story illustrates how a
nonrelational trauma from a hurricane can destabilize a happy child.
Roshawna grew up in a small Gulf Coast town. Her father was a dentist, and her mother was a
happy homemaker. She was very close to her older sister, Lawanda. Her parents enjoyed raising
the girls. Meeting their needs was a high priority. The family was close knit. They spent a lot of
together time, much of it on the beach on weekends. They would take two trips a year as a family,
always somewhere different so the girls could get a sense of the big, wide world. Roshawna felt
securely attached to her parents and enjoyed being in the family. The summer she turned ten, a
Category 5 hurricane hit their town. It was devastating. They were upstairs in the home while the
downstairs flooded. Huddled together, they all worried the house would blow apart and they’d be
killed. The next morning, they were rescued by boat. Her father’s dental office had been completely
destroyed. Homes in her neighborhood were badly damaged or destroyed. It took two years for the
community to rebuild enough to get back to normal. During most of that time, the family lived
with relatives in another state. This took her away from her friends and familiar routine. Roshawna
began having frequent nightmares and panic attacks. Whenever she went to school, she felt fearful
away from her mother and sister. Her father spent a lot of time commuting back home to check
on the progress of repairing the house and dental office. He also volunteered his time to help
rebuild the town. She feared losing him while he was away. A school counselor taught her to
manage the panic attacks, but general anxiety about losing her family persisted. After two years,
her father reopened his dental practice, and they moved back into their state, back to their fully
remodeled home. She lost contact with many of her friends who did not return. Now, Roshawna is
eighteen. A part of her wants to attend college away from home, to find her independence, but
another part wants to stay close to home, fearing that if she leaves, something bad will happen
and she’ll never see her family again.
The AIP model would posit that the trauma of living through the hurricane and the post-hurricane
chaos got dysfunctionally stored in the brain. EMDR would aim to desensitize these memories by linking
them to adaptive information. For Roshawna, that could be the knowledge that the hurricane is over,
and she survived. It could include an awareness that her grief can process through and that she can
stay connected to her family even if she moves away from home. Because Roshawna was foundationally
stable before the hurricane, EMDR might easily treat her PTSD and help her restore her pre-trauma
stability.

Chapter 3: Treating Adults Wounded in Childhood

29

But the hurricane and post-hurricane chaos likely formed several fearful ego states. Roshawna would
probably benefit greatly from mobilizing a Resource team to help stabilize anxious child parts who are
stuck in the hurricane. Bringing them into present time should have a profound calming effect. Her
wounded parts could learn how to lean on the Resources for reassurance and comfort between
sessions, especially when she’s away from her family. Once her wounded parts feel safe in the present,
EMDR should be highly effective at processing the hurricane trauma memories.

Attachment Wounding without Trauma Wounding
Some of our most wounded clients were raised by kind parents, in safe homes, in safe
neighborhoods, getting all their physical and intellectual needs met well. Here’s a story
about how well-meaning but misattuned, detached parents can unwittingly inflict
substantial wounding.
Abby grew up as an only child in an upper-middle-class home in a safe area. Both parents were
tax lawyers, married to their work. They loved each other and worked through their disagreements
respectfully. They did not cuss, smoke, drink, or do drugs. They had friends, took trips, and loved
life. They believed as long as they met all of Abby’s physical and intellectual needs, she would grow
up to be a happy, healthy adult. They gave her lots of books and toys but no quality time. When
Abby was two, she worried her parents didn’t like her. To please them, she drew a large picture of
them on the living room wall in crayon. She was excited for them to see it and praise her for it.
Instead, they were just annoyed. They didn’t ask her about it, discuss it with her, or punish her.
They just told her to go away and then painted over it. She felt confused, rejected, embarrassed,
misunderstood, and ashamed. Throughout Abby’s childhood, her parents were cordial but cold.
They never hit her and rarely raised a voice. Once she was old enough, they would send her away
for two months of summer camp so they could travel the world. They gave her lots of books, toys,
clothes, and gifts, but they never gave her what she wanted most—a loving, attuned, heart-toheart connection. She eventually adapted to their emotional distance with an avoidant attachment
style and by retreating into books to manage her chronic loneliness. Now, at age thirty, she’s an
educated, successful professional, but she feels profound emptiness all the time. She’s socially
awkward and expects people won’t like her. Intense feelings of defeat, insecurity, rejection, shame,
and confusion overwhelm her when social interactions get a little bit stressful.
The AIP model would posit that her parents’ misattuned reaction to her drawing on the wall was a
traumatic event that got dysfunctionally stored in the brain. It would be considered a small t trauma.
EMDR would be applied to process it—perhaps with some needs-meeting interweaves.
In my experience, EMDR would not help Abby at all. That’s because her wounding did not come from
bad things her parents did; it came from important good things they didn’t do. For example, when
Abby drew on the wall, she needed her parents to (1) respond with loving attunement, (2) show delight,
(3) validate her wish to please them, (4) reassure her that they loved her, (5) provide loving correction
about drawing on walls, and (6) keep an open channel of loving communication far into the future.
Their inability to do this conveyed nonverbal messages like “You’re not important to us” and “Don’t
expect us to understand you.” Their chronic misattunement formed lots of wounded child parts over
the years. Abby will need help mobilizing a Resource team that can meet the emotional needs of her
child parts. Once they form a heart-to-heart, loving connection with the Resources, they’ll finally feel
seen, heard, valued, and loved. That would be the starting point for establishing an earned-secure
attachment and foundational stability. Additional DNMS needs-meeting interventions would be
necessary to complete the healing and get wounded child parts totally unstuck.

30

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Nonprimary-Caregiver Trauma + Attachment Wounding
Sometimes attachment wounding is stressful but tolerable. When a trauma occurs on top
of that stress, it can overwhelm the system. The story of Rodney illustrates how the context
of unmet emotional needs from parents at home can exacerbate a relational trauma inflicted
away from home.
Rodney grew up in a family of eight in a middle-class home in a very small town. His physical
needs were met well enough. His father was a respected minster in a local fundamentalist church.
His mother was a homemaker. His father had very strict and rigid ideas about child-rearing. His
mother could occasionally be comforting, but most of the time, she was depressed and
overwhelmed by the demands of childcare. Rodney did not see much warmth and affection in the
home—not between his parents or between his parents and the six children. His parents never hit
them, but they did use shame to motivate “appropriate” behavior. They were always concerned
about how their children’s behavior at church and school reflected on them. This was a frequent
topic at the dinner table. Conformity was rewarded; individuality was discouraged. He believed
they loved him, because they said so, but he didn’t feel loved, seen, heard, or understood. He didn’t
feel it was safe to be authentic. He had an avoidant attachment to his father and an ambivalent
attachment to his mother. When Rodney was twelve, he began having romantic feelings for his
best friend, Denzel. He kept his feelings hidden, believing they were wrong and that he was bad
for having them—because that’s what his father preached. One day, he went to Denzel’s house to
play video games. Denzel’s uncle was visiting. He showed them a gay porn video and talked them
into trying out what they’d watched. When it was all over, Rodney had been sodomized against
his will by the uncle in front of his friend. He felt traumatized and humiliated. He knew that if his
parents ever found out, he’d be blamed, shamed, and rejected. He became withdrawn, depressed,
and suicidal. His parents made frequent unkind comments about his surly moods. They never asked
him why he was moody. At age fourteen, he wrote a suicide note and made a plan to kill himself.
His mother found the note and showed it to his father. They were furious, because if anyone found
out, people would say they were abusing him. Rodney managed to pull himself together until he
graduated from high school. He joined the army right away. Once away from home, he began to
drink heavily. At age twenty-two, he was arrested for disorderly conduct and public intoxication.
He was sent to a therapist for help.
The AIP model would posit that the sexual assault got dysfunctionally stored in the brain. EMDR would
process this trauma memory by linking it to adaptive information—like an awareness that the assault
is over now, that it wasn’t his fault, and that he has the power now to protect himself from sexual abuse.
Rodney’s sexual assault was certainly a trauma—a terrible thing for a child to experience. So, while
starting EMDR early in treatment may seem like a good idea, it probably isn’t. That’s because the worst
part of the assault was the context—the messages conveyed by his parents that led him to believe an
assault like that was his fault and a same-sex attraction made him a bad person. On top of that, they
conveyed messages that made it impossible for him to ask for help. Rodney has many wounded child
parts from years of unmet emotional needs. He’ll need help mobilizing a team of loving, internal
Resources who can meet the emotional needs of child parts wounded by the rigid, shaming, rejecting,
judgmental parental messages. Once his child parts feel a heart-to-heart, loving connection with the
Resources they’ll finally feel seen, heard, and loved. They’ll finally feel safe being authentic. That will
lead to an earned-secure attachment and foundational stability. Once wounded parts know they have
a safe place to be vulnerable, surrounded by Resources, EMDR should effectively process the assault.

Chapter 3: Treating Adults Wounded in Childhood

31

Primary-Caregiver Trauma + Attachment Wounding
Some of our most challenging, dissociative clients had childhood caregivers who inflicted
attachment wounds and trauma wounds with physical or sexual abuse or dangerous
neglect. The story of Becky illustrates such a case.
DeeDee was a young, single mom with an alcohol problem. When DeeDee was eight, she witnessed
her dad shoot and kill her mom. After that, she was raised by her grandparents, Grammy and
Poppy. DeeDee’s four children—a boy and three girls—were from four different fathers. Becky was
the oldest. During Becky’s first ten years, she lived with her mom and siblings in a ramshackle
mobile home behind Grammy and Poppy’s dilapidated rural farmhouse. Grammy would check in
to do laundry and leave food, because DeeDee was often sleeping off a binge. In that way Grammy
was kind, but she could also be judgmental and verbally abusive. Mostly she enabled DeeDee’s
reckless drinking. When Poppy came over, he’d play games with them and tell funny stories. He’d
also molest the girls while the older brother watched. When her older brother got moody, he’d
punch and bite his sisters. He’d also molest them. Becky told her mother and Grammy about being
molested. They told her to get used to it. Her mother’s many boyfriends were gang members with
criminal records. Their visits to the mobile home were often punctuated with drunkenness, violence,
and/or sexual abuse. Becky endeavored to keep her mom in a good mood, so she’d be less likely
to drink. When DeeDee was nourished, rested, and sober, she could be very warm and kind to
Becky, but most of the time, she failed to meet Becky’s emotional needs. Sometimes when she tried
to support Becky, her lack of attunement made the situation worse. Because of this, Becky
developed an ambivalent/disorganized attachment to her mother. When Grammy died on Becky’s
tenth birthday, DeeDee completely checked out. The little care Becky was getting abruptly stopped.
Her teacher noticed the crisis and called Child Protective Services. Becky was eventually placed
with a nice foster care couple who were determined to meet her emotional needs. Becky’s
expectation of danger and betrayal made it hard for her to trust. Her teen years were marked with
tattoos, bad grades, boyfriends, arrests, and the consumption of too much alcohol. After sobering
up in rehab, her foster parents helped her become a hairdresser. At twenty-one, she had a son out
of wedlock. Her determination to be a good mother helped her work hard and stay sober. Now at
age forty, Becky is stable compared to childhood, but she’s depressed and anxious much of the
time. Coffee, cigarettes, and AA help her cope. She’s aware of many young parts of self—fearful
parts, mistrusting parts, angry parts, and feisty teen parts—all yearning for safety and love. She
hopes therapy will stop the intrusive memories of childhood violence that she wrestles with every
day.
The AIP model would posit that each of Becky’s hurtful, wounding experiences growing up with her
mother was a trauma that got dysfunctionally stored in a memory network. As soon as she could
demonstrate sufficient emotion management skills, EMDR Phase 4 would reprocess these memories.
This treatment approach would be based on several assumptions about Becky. They include: (1) her core
foundational instability was not a barrier to successful EMDR, (2) trauma processing will have a
stabilizing effect, (3) all her adverse childhood experiences are traumas, (4) adding needs-meeting
interweaves during Phase 4 will process the attachment component of the trauma, and (5) successful
trauma processing will lead to greater harmony within the self-system of parts. There’s a lot to unpack
here, so let’s take a closer look.

Faulty Assumptions about Doing EMDR with Clients Like Becky
1. Core foundational instability is not a barrier to successful EMDR: Foundational stability occurs on
a continuum from very unstable to very stable. Becky is very unstable, because during her early years,
she had no one to support her emotionally or to help her feel safe. She got the double whammy

32

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

of attachment wounds and trauma wounds daily for ten years. Starting trauma processing as soon
as possible is not likely to be helpful, even if she were able to tolerate the emotional intensity. It
might even make things worse. If her wounded parts are in chaos at the start of therapy, they first
need to be stabilized and calmed. They need their emotional needs met much more than they
need trauma work. Doing EMDR with Becky might seem like a good idea, because sometimes it
helps with childhood wounding like this. When it helps, there’s a reason why. To understand this,
let’s compare Becky with another client, Mary, who had similar chaotic, violent, abusive events
happening at home. Mary spent half of her childhood hanging out with her best friend, Julie, at
her house. Julie’s mom was loving, comforting, validating, and supportive of Mary. Her loving bond
with Julie’s mom helped Mary feel seen, heard, and valued over many years. It helped her construct
important neural architecture for managing emotions. Despite terrible violence and abuse at home,
Mary will have moderate foundational stability. That means processing Mary’s traumatic homelife
with EMDR might work well. The take-home message is that the childhood history details matter!
2. Trauma processing will have a stabilizing effect: When a foundationally stable client has been
destabilized by an adult-onset trauma like a life-threatening accident, EMDR can quickly and easily
restore pre-trauma stability. When a client like Becky has never felt stable, there is no pre-trauma
stability to restore. In my experience, these clients need lots of needs-meeting interventions to
build that foundational stability first. Trauma desensitization doesn’t do that.
3. All adverse childhood experiences are traumas: Becky experienced two different types of childhood
adversity: unmet emotional needs and unmet safety needs. Of these two, the unmet emotional
needs have done the most harm. Because Becky’s childhood threats to safety were not soothed by
DeeDee, Grammy, or Poppy, the neural architecture for managing emotions did not develop. Her
foster parents helped develop a little of it, but not as much as she needed to grow up to enjoy an
anxiety-free adulthood. In my experience, intense needs-meeting interventions can further develop
this neural architecture, which can have a substantial stabilizing effect.
4. Just add needs-meeting interweaves: Phase 4 EMDR aims to lower the disturbance level of trauma
memories. Cognitive interweaves can provide important assistance in the process. If a client has
moderate foundational instability from a few attachment wounds, adding some needs-meeting
cognitive interweaves might take the client all the way to adaptive resolution. In my experience,
clients like Becky, with lots of child parts stuck in the past, need much more. Becky’s wounded parts
will need lots of intense needs-meeting from a competent Resource team. It’s not enough for needsmeeting to be a garnish. It must be the main course to truly stabilize these very wounded child parts.
5. Trauma processing will lead to self-system harmony: Over the course of Becky’s childhood, many
wounded parts came into being—some more dissociated than others. Some parts formed to hold
painful emotions, some to hold details of frightening experiences, some to search for love and
approval some to mistrust and distance, and others to distract from or numb the pain. An intervention
with one child part in the self-system can ripple to other parts—sometimes creating more friction
and chaos. Ego state therapy interventions are ideally suited for calming that kind of friction as
quickly as it arises. In my experience, prioritizing trauma processing over needs-meeting with
clients like Becky is likely to lead to more internal chaos, not less.

How to Treat Clients Like Becky
Becky’s emotional needs and physical safety needs were not met during her first ten years. This had a
profound negative effect on the development of her neuroanatomy, resulting in significant foundational
instability. Our first treatment priority should be raising her level of foundational stability. She’ll have
many wounded child parts stuck in the past and stuck in trance, that need to come into the safety of
the present moment. The work begins with mobilizing a Resource team that will engage with child

Chapter 3: Treating Adults Wounded in Childhood

33

parts, to meet their emotional needs and reassure them they’re safe now. As wounded parts feel seen,
heard, and valued by the Resources, exactly as they are, with all their fears and maladaptive coping
strategies, they’ll begin to heal. They’ll become more stable with each corrective emotional experience.
As more and more child parts stabilize, eventually Becky should be able to tolerate EMDR to process
through her physical and sexual traumas.

Stabilization Interventions before EMDR
I recommend that when we work with attachment-wounded clients, we start by stabilizing wounded
child parts no matter what their trauma history, even if they appear ready for EMDR. This is why:
1. Some attachment-wounded clients appear ready but really aren’t. We can learn a lot about their
level of instability when we watch how child parts respond to the Resource team. Parts who start
out fearful and mistrusting of the Resources tend to be more dissociated than parts who feel
comfortable with them right away. So, watching how child parts interact with the Resources can
be diagnostic. (See Observational Clues to Dissociative Disorders section on page 263.) Whether
it takes one session, one month, or one year to stabilize attachment-wounded child parts, we can
feel confident that when we move on to EMDR, the client is ready.
2. Stabilizing wounded parts well, in advance, ensures the client will stay in her window of tolerance
during emotionally taxing trauma processing.
3. This needs-meeting process is aligned with a core tenet of the AIP model—the idea that we need
to link what’s stuck in the past to what’s healthy in the present. We help child parts link to adaptive
information—loving messages from the Resources like “You matter,” “You’re important,” and
“You’re lovable.” The more we help build that loving relationship, the stronger that AIP link gets.
That helps ensure that adaptive information will be readily available to heal the trauma wounds
when we move to Phase 4.
4. If we fail to stabilize wounded child parts well enough in Phase 2, we may get only partial resolution
in Phase 4. That means either failing to get a SUD of 0, a VOC of 7, a clear body scan, or a completed
future template. Or, it could mean if these are achieved, failing to see the expected improvement
in the client’s quality of life. A partial resolution may simply mean EMDR healed as much as it can,
and the remaining attachment wounds require a different kind of intervention. Of course, we can
always follow a partial EMDR resolution with DNMS needs-meeting interventions, but if we clear
attachment wounds with needs meeting up front in Phase 2, we can be confident trauma processing
will go well.

Stabilization Interventions after EMDR
Sometimes EMDR will come before stabilizing wounded parts. Here are three ways that can happen.
 Inadvertently: We might become aware of attachment wounds only after we’ve started EMDR,
when wounded parts emerge in the middle of trauma processing. At this point, we can stop EMDR
and start stabilization.
 Deliberately: An attachment-wounded client may come to us having intrusive images of a recent
trauma like a school shooting or bombing. It may not be possible to do any ego state therapy until
at least some of the intrusive trauma memories have been cleared out of the way. After sufficient
desensitization, we can pivot to stabilization. This approach will be contraindicated for some clients,
so we must use our best judgment when considering do this.

34

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

 Cyclically: We may do some stabilization, then EMDR, followed by additional stabilization, followed
again by EMDR. We can cycle back and forth as needed between these two healing modes until the
client’s goals are met.

Chapter 3 Summary
This chapter offers additional clarity into the differences between trauma and attachment wounds.
Childhood wounding is framed in terms of unmet needs. Trauma wounding in childhood is
about unmet physical safety needs while attachment wounding is about unmet emotional needs.
Non-relational trauma wounding, like an accidental injury, is usually easier to treat, than
relational trauma inflicted by a person, especially when the hurtful person was a primary
caregiver, like a parent. When we prepare clients for EMDR we must consider the context of the
trauma in light of how well attachment needs were met. I offer a variety of sample case scenarios
to illustrate how we might conceptualize cases with varying combinations of childhood trauma
and attachment wounds.

Chapter 4
Getting a History

Most clients seek therapy with hopes of replacing unwanted behaviors, beliefs, emotions, or urges with
positive, beneficial ones. This journey to healing starts when we listen to a client tell her story. I
advocate taking a history with a particular focus on a client’s childhood relationships, because those
relationships have had a big impact on the client’s foundational stability—for better or worse. The
better we are able to assess foundational stability, the better our treatment plans will be. In this chapter
I describe how I take a history.
For a checklist and an attachment questionnaire to help guide you through the interview process, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

Getting Acquainted
I give potential clients a free half-hour interview, before beginning therapy. This gives me a chance to
determine if a person’s therapy needs are a match for my skills. I ask about their motivation to begin
therapy, their desired outcome, and past therapy experiences. If current triggers seem rooted in
parental attachment wounds, I briefly talk about my needs-meeting approach. If current triggers seem
rooted in traumas, I briefly describe EMDR. If we determine working together is mutually agreeable,
we schedule a first session.

squares and females by circles. I write the
person’s name beside and the age inside each
circle and square. An X goes inside the symbol
for deceased family members. I also make short
notes about family members beside their
names. (Genogram-drawing instructions can be
found with a simple internet search.)

Getting a History
EMDR therapists are taught to get a history with two aims in mind: (1) determine the client’s suitability
for EMDR, and (2) identify trauma memories for EMDR processing. This works well for foundationally
stable clients seeking EMDR for adult-onset traumas. For everyone else, I take a different kind of history.
When I take a history, I aim to (1) assess the nature and severity of attachment wounds, (2) identify
issues connected to child parts who will need to be stabilized, and (3) identify trauma memories for
EMDR processing. My interview could take a single session or several sessions, depending on how
much information the client has to share or how easily her story flows.
Once I have a genogram, I ask about relationships with primary caregivers first. I then ask about relationships
with siblings, grandparents, aunts, uncles, friends, teachers, etc. I also inquire about community, media,
or cultural influences. Once I have a felt sense of what it was like to be the client growing up in that
family, in that community, I pivot to important adulthood relationships like spouses, children, pets,
friends, or past therapists. I ask about current sources of stress and loving support. I conclude by asking
about any other significant events over the lifespan—traumas in particular. This kind of interview helps
me get to know clients very well by revealing both trauma and attachment wounds.
I aim to get an overview of the client’s childhood experience—the big picture. When the time comes
to process with wounded child parts or to process through trauma, the details of wounding events will
come out, right on time—exactly when I need them.

Relationships with Primary Caregivers
Most of my interview is focused on relationships with primary caregivers, because that’s where attachment
wounds form. A primary caregiver would be anyone who had sole responsibility for a client’s wellbeing for a period of time in childhood. That will usually be the biological mother and father but could
also include stepparents, adoptive parents, or foster parents.
Once I have a genogram, I invite the client to select the parent/caregiver she wants to talk about first. If,
for example, she wants to talk about her mother first, I’ll say, “Tell me about your childhood relationship
with your mother.” I’ll let the client share whatever comes to mind, prompting with follow-up questions.
When the client has finished talking about her mother, I’ll guide her through my Quality of Attachment
Interview Questionnaire. It’s a list of twenty-two discussion questions about different aspects of the
caregiver-child relationship. The frequency of each item is rated from very rarely to very often. Here are
the interview questions about the client’s childhood relationship with her mother.

Did you fear your mother?

The questionnaire document provides a complete description of the information we’re aiming to get
for each question. It also includes definitions of terms clients might not know, like loving correction, repair
breaks, and double binds. If a client does not understand a question, I provide clarifying information.
I encourage clients to answer each interview question with specific examples. These elaborations can
reveal valuable information about the nature of the caregiver-child relationship. They can also reveal
misunderstandings that require clarification. For example, when asked question 8, a client asserted his
mother was often warm and affectionate, recalling as evidence that she would cook dinner and do
laundry. When pressed to describe why that felt like warmth and affection, he said, “It proves she cared.”
This response suggested he might not understand what warmth and affection are. To clarify, I’d say
something like, “Picture a mother reading to a child who is sitting in her lap. She’s smiling and happy.
Her voice is soft and gentle. Her arms are encircling the child who feels warm and safe with her. This is
warmth and affection. Did you have any experiences like that with your mother?” The client admitted
he had few experiences like that with her. Clarifications like this ensure an accurate final score.
I base frequency ratings solely on a client’s childhood experiences with a caregiver, because that’s when
attachment wounds form. If a client elaborates on adulthood experiences, I’ll make a note of it, but I
won’t consider it when selecting ratings. An adulthood relationship could be radically different from
the childhood experience. For example, a father who was rejecting when the client was young may be
supportive of her now, but her wounded parts are still stuck in the early rejection experience.
Each time an interview question reveals a wounding aspect of the relationship, I provide an empathic
validation. For example, if a client reveals her mother gave her harsh, shaming discipline instead of
loving correction, I’d say something like, “Children need to know they’re loved and cherished even
when they misbehave or make a mistake. Loving corrections do that. I’m so sorry your mother’s idea of
correction was harsh, shaming punishment. That must have been very hard on you.” The questionnaire
provides a sample empathic validation for each of the twenty-two items. These validations provide an
important measure of healing while keeping clients in their window of tolerance.
With the client’s help I estimate the frequency, on a 0-4 scale from very rarely to very often, of the
relationship experience for each question. I record this on the questionnaire. The more attachment
wounding was experienced, the more points are earned. When I have the frequency ratings for all
twenty-two items for a caregiver, I add up the points to get a final score. A final score of 0-22 means
there was most likely a secure attachment to the caregiver. A score of 23-44 means there was most
likely mild to moderate attachment wounding from this caregiver. A score of 45-66 means there was
most likely moderate to severe attachment wounding from this caregiver. And a score of 67-88 means
most likely severe attachment wounding from this caregiver, and possible disorganized attachment if
the ratings on questions 14-16 are high.
After a client has fully described her relationship with the first caregiver, I’ll get a full description of the
second. If a client had more than two primary caregivers (like stepparents or foster parents), I’ll get

38

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

descriptions of them too. The questionnaire has forms for mother, father, and other caregivers. Each
caregiver gets a separate score. Scores could reveal a secure attachment to one childhood caregiver
and an insecure attachment to the other.

Other Significant Childhood Relationships
Next, I ask about other influential childhood relationships, both positive and negative. I start with other
close family members, focusing mostly on those who were consequential. This could include siblings,
grandparents, aunts, uncles, or cousins who were especially supportive or abusive. I’ll also ask about
any significant relationships with friends, teachers, ministers, etc.
I inquire about parental reactions to or knowledge of these other significant relationships. For example,
did the parents know that siblings were being abusive and intervene in a helpful way? If not, what did
they do instead? Were the parents happy about the close emotional connection to Aunt Dot? If not,
how did they react?

Significant Cultural Messages from Community or Media
Some clients grew up hearing very important cultural messages from the community (local schools,
churches, synagogues, mosques, etc.) or from national media. Such messages can be positive and
affirming or negative and shaming. Common wounding messages are pejorative judgments about
race, ethnicity, skin color, weight, gender, sexual orientation, and gender identity. To inquire about
these wounding messages, I’ll ask a question like “Did you get any messages from your community or
the media that made you feel inferior, unimportant, ashamed, or uncomfortable?” If “yes,” I find out
what the messages were. Then I ask about parental reactions to those messages. Did the parents notice
and intervene? Were the parents conveying the same or a similar message? Did the parents’ reaction
make it better or worse?

Full Contextual Validation
Next, I summarize the client’s childhood history with a full contextual validation. I like to add insightful
comments, with lots of validation and empathy. Here’s a brief example.
It sounds like your parents didn’t like each other. Your dad spent most of his time at work or reading,
while your mother spent most of her time cooking, cleaning, and sewing. When they were at home
together, they avoided each other, and they avoided you. They did not know how to be present for you,
support you, and enjoy you—so you grew up feeling lonely and insecure. And because they didn’t
spend time socializing you, you had trouble making and keeping friends, which just exacerbated
your sense of loneliness. When your soccer coach, Mr. Jones, paid special attention to you, it felt
really good—like you finally important to someone. But then he began to molest you. You had
nowhere to go to talk about it, no one to help you understand what it meant. You felt weak and
powerless. I’m so sorry your parents did not know how to show up for you, to support, protect, and
cherish you and to help you deal with problems like Mr. Jones. You deserved better than that.
It's not just repeating back the story that was told, it’s telling the client’s story in a way that ensures
she feels seen, heard, and valued. I aim to convey that I don’t just understand what happened, I also
understand how it made her feel. This helps the client feel felt.

Significant Adulthood Events and Relationships
I’ll invite clients to walk me through what’s happened in adulthood—things like schooling, jobs,
marriages, children born, traumas, etc. I’ll inquire about consequential adulthood relationships from
the past and the present. I’ll ask if any current relationships are especially loving and supportive or

Chapter 4: Getting a History

39

highly stressful. I’ll also ask about who they love, support, or care for now including children, pets,
plants, patients, students, etc.

Other Significant Lifetime Experiences
I always wrap up my history-taking interview with a question like “Are there any other significant events
in your life—positive or negative—that you haven’t mentioned yet, that it would help me to know
about?” Clients might mention a positive event, like a scholarship or peak spiritual experience, or a
negative event, like an accident, illness, loss, or trauma.

Taking a History with Empathy and Attunement
In my experience, clients want us to react to their stories with authentic human emotions—genuine
shock, anger, or sadness—while we stay calm and centered. Our authentic emotions help clients feel
seen, heard, and understood in a way that’s not achieved with emotional neutrality. As we stay calm,
centered, fully present, and emotionally connected throughout the interview, wounded parts learn that
we can be trusted to help. This can make the history-taking experience extremely therapeutic.
Each wounding experience a client reports is an opportunity for us to provide an attuned, empathic
validation. We’re not just collecting information; we’re building rapport and trust. Thoughtful, insightful
questions and comments can convey that we don’t just understand the chronology; we also understand
the emotional impact. Here are some examples of empathic validations.
 It sounds like your dad was always angry at you. It makes perfect sense you wouldn’t go to him for
help with your problems. That left you to solve them on your own. It must have been hard for you to
face such big problems at such a young age. I’m so sorry.
 It sounds like your mother depended on you to manage her anxiety. That’s a huge burden for a small
child to carry. That wasn’t fair. I’m so sorry that happened.
 I’m so sorry your mother couldn’t repair breaks with you. It’s so important to handle breaks. If they
don’t get handled, they just pile up, right?!
 Wow! It sounds like they gave you lots of mixed messages about what you should and shouldn’t do.
That must have been very confusing for you.
 I’m so sorry to hear you grew up afraid of everyone around you. That sounds awful!

Listening for Positives
Our clients’ positive life experiences and relationships are just as important as the wounding ones, so
we must listen for them and ask about them. Whenever clients talk about moments of personal
competency and strength, we invite them to elaborate. These experiences can provide valuable
information about the skills and abilities of the client’s most adult self—information we can use later
to help wounded parts heal. For example, a client’s adult self may know how relationship breaks get
repaired but dissociated wounded parts may not. Her personal experience of repairing a break may be
a compelling story we can draw on later to help educate wounded parts.
Positive relationships can inform us of our clients’ inner Resources. For example, if a client had a loving
bond with her Aunt Dot, she may have an Aunt Dot Resource. If a client had good experiences raising
animals, she may have an Animal-Lover Self. If a client drew comfort watching Mr. Rogers on TV, she
may have a Mr. Rogers Resource. Knowing about these relationships gives us the possibility of using
these Resources “on the fly” when a part gets triggered while telling her story. We can get the triggered
part talking to Aunt Dot, Animal-Lover, or Mr. Rogers even before we have officially mobilized these
Resources. (As described in Chapter 7.)

40

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Managing Intense Emotions
Talking about a painful past can be stressful for clients. We must stay attuned to their emotions and
proceed carefully so the experience of giving a history is not overwhelming. If we suspect up front that
a client will likely get triggered while giving her history, we should consider mobilizing Resources first.
That way, if wounded parts do get triggered, the Resource team will be ready to calm and reassure
them so clients can get quickly centered again.
If wounded parts happen to get triggered before we mobilize Resources, there are steps we can take
to get the client’s most adult self back in executive control. One or more of the following may work.
 Invite the client to look around the room to help her get back into the present.
 For just a moment, change the subject to something unrelated, like the weather or a recent headline.
 Ask the client to tell you about a recent success at work or school.
 Tell a joke or ask the client to tell you a joke.
 Ask questions only the adult self can answer like “What’s COVID-19?” or ”What’s Bluetooth?”
If these fail to bring the adult self front and center, we can talk directly to the triggered part to bring
her back to present time. (See Chapter 13.) If a client has already told us about a positive, meaningful
relationship, we may be able to mobilize that as a Resource “on the fly.” We simply invite the Resource
we expect the client probably has (e.g., Aunt Dot, Animal-Lover Self, or Mr. Rogers) to talk to the
triggered part, to offer loving reassurance. Here’s a sample dialogue with a client, Grace, who was talking
about her Uncle John molesting her when she was five years old. This triggered a five-year-old part into a
vivid flashback. Grace grew up feeling loved by her Aunt Dot. She currently lives in Montana.

Assessing Foundational Stability
Developing a good treatment plan starts with accurately assessing a client’s level of foundational stability.
The two most important factors to consider are a client’s trauma/attachment history and current level
of reactivity. I’ll explore both of these here.

Trauma/Attachment History
Generally speaking, clients are more likely to be foundationally stable if (1) they had few, if any, attachment
wounds, (2) the wounding occurred later in childhood or in adulthood, (3) the wounding was brief,
and/or (4) the wounding was followed with loving comfort and support. It helps if the client had an
especially resilient temperament.

42

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Clients are more likely to be foundationally unstable if (1) they have a history of moderate to severe
attachment wounding, (2) their wounding experiences occurred in early childhood, (3) the wounding
persisted over many years, (4) caregivers were unable to meet emotional needs, (5) caregivers were
the source of the wounding, (6) caregivers were unprotective, and/or (7) no one provided comforting,
loving support. This is compounded if the client had an especially sensitive temperament.
The history we take should inform us about all these variables. The “wounding” I refer to here is any
significantly distressing experience. This could include an overt trauma like incest from a sadistic parent,
but it could also include subtle wounding chronic misattunement from an enmeshing parent.
Clients who have had a lot of healing therapy may be more foundationally stable than their history would
suggest. For these clients, the second factor, reactivity, may be more important in assessing current
levels of stability.

Current Level of Reactivity
Reactivity refers to how much wounded parts emotionally overreact to triggering experiences. Because
reactivity is relative, there are three variables to watch: (1) how easily parts get triggered, (2) how
intensely they get triggered, and (3) how long it takes them to recover. I’ll elaborate on each one.
 How easily are parts triggered? Triggering stressors can range from mild to intense. Running out
of toothpaste is usually less stressful than running out of money. Being easily triggered by mild
stressors points to foundational instability. We take notice when we see clients are easily and
frequently triggered in session and between sessions, especially by seemingly innocuous things.
Likewise, we take notice when they’re emotionally resilient to stress.
 How intensely do parts get triggered? Emotional overreactions can range from mild to intense.
Foundationally unstable clients are more likely to have very intense overreactions like a sudden
surge of paralyzing fear or homicidal anger. We take notice when clients have intense overreactions.
Likewise, we take notice when emotional overreactions are mild.
 How long does it take parts to recover? Emotional overreactions peak and then fade out as the
body comes back to homeostasis. Foundationally unstable clients have a harder time returning to
normal after an overreaction. What a foundationally stable person might work through in an hour,
an unstable person might hang on to for days. We take notice when a client’s overreaction is slow
to process through. Likewise, we notice when emotional overreactions quickly resolve.

Foundational Instability Changes over Time
As attachment wounds heal over time, signs of increased stability begin to appear. We see it when
clients become triggered less easily and less often, when the intensity of overreactions is lower, and
when they recover more quickly.

Sample Client-Therapist Dialogue: Getting a History
The following is a typical client-therapist dialogue for getting a history.
Initial Thirty-Minute Interview
In the initial phone interview, Carla said she’s forty-two years old, in a good marriage, and raising a
fourteen-year-old daughter. She’s struggled with depression and anxiety off and on her whole life but
had been doing okay until recently. A month ago, right after the family returned from a vacation, she
read in the newspaper that while they were gone, their next-door neighbor was arrested for shooting
and killing his wife and child. Ever since, she’s been flooded with nightmares and flashbacks of the

Chapter 4: Getting a History

43

violence she experienced and witnessed in her childhood. She said her father was a police officer and
gun collector. When something made him angry, he had a red-hot temper. She’d been dealing with
the stress the way she’d always did, by baking lots of cookies, cakes, and pies, and eating lots of
fattening, sugary comfort foods. Before the vacation, she’d been thinking about going back to work
part time, but now she’s too anxious to do anything that courageous. Her husband would gladly
provide the emotional support she needs but doesn’t know how. She’s hoping therapy will stop the
flashbacks and the emotional eating. She said she’d had some Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT)
about twenty years ago. She liked her therapist, and the CBT seemed to help, but about a year after
she stopped therapy, the symptoms came back. She said she feels her best emotionally when she’s
focused on mothering her daughter, Nina.

He wouldn’t think so, but he probably was. I didn’t think he drank when he was on patrol.
It was too dangerous, but he liked to drink when he was home or out with his buddies.
The more he drank, the meaner he got.
I imagine it was very stressful to live with him.

Next question. Did your father provide loving correction?
What do you mean?
When a child is disobedient or makes a mistake, some parents will punish the child—
maybe with a spanking, slap to the wrist, or withholding a treat. Often the child feels
shamed. Parents who provide loving correction will patiently love, validate, and correct the
child while conveying she has a sense of value. It becomes a helpful learning experience.
How would your father treat you if he thought you made a mistake?
He’d get really angry and yell. He might grab my arm and twist it. His face would get
red. I’d be afraid he’d lose control of this anger.
Did he ever hit you?
Yes, sometimes, but worse than that, he could become so enraged I worried he’d lose it
completely and kill someone—like me or Mama.
How did you protect yourself?
I got good at staying invisible to him. I figured if he didn’t see me, he couldn’t hurt me.
Mama would protect me. She wouldn’t talk to him about me unless absolutely necessary.
He was so self-absorbed I don’t think he noticed. For years, he worked the night shift.
As I was getting out of school, he was leaving for work, so that worked out. When he
did have a night off, he would often hang out with Papaw next door.
I’m so sorry. It must have been very scary. You deserved a father who could be patient
and kind when you made a mistake—a father who was safe to be around no matter
what you did.
Yeah, well, that wasn’t Daddy.
Would you say he provided loving correction rarely or very rarely?
I’d say never. (Circled 4 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Was your father warm and affectionate with you?
Not usually. I remember once, when I was about three or four, I got really sick. I had a
bad fever. While Mama was taking a nap, he heard me crying for her. He sat with me
awhile. He tried to cheer me up with hand puppets. He read a book to me. It was really
sweet. Soon as Mama woke up, he disappeared. He wasn’t one to hug me or kiss me.
He never told me he loved me. Mostly I just felt like a nuisance to him.
I’m so sorry. I’m sure you were a delightful child, not a nuisance. You needed a father
who could appreciate who you were and give you the love and affection you deserved.
Thanks.

Would you say he was warm and affectionate with you rarely or very rarely?

Next question. Was your father able to repair breaks with you?
What does that mean?
Break repair means bringing a win-win resolution to a conflict or friction. For example,
a parent apologizing after getting angry with a child and saying something unkind, or
after a child lashes out in anger at a parent, the parent lovingly engages to reconcile.
Break repair calms painful emotions and hurt feelings. It builds rapport. The relationship
becomes stronger.
Oh, he never did that. If he got mad at me, he’d just shame and blame me. He’d want
me to feel bad about whatever I’d done—forever. I loved my bike. One day, I was out
riding on the street, and a big dog ran out in front of me. I was startled and lost control
of the bike. I crashed into a parked car and put a small dent in it. He was furious. He
took the bike away and said he could no longer trust me to ride it without destroying
someone’s property.
Yikes. That’s horrible.
Whenever stuff like that happened, Mama would try to smooth it over. She’d say things
like “He didn’t really mean it. He really loves you,” but I never believed her.
I can see why. You deserved to get those breaks repaired. If I’d been your parent when
you crashed your bike, I’d have taught you how to be more careful on it.
That would have been nice.
Would you say he was able to repair breaks with you rarely or very rarely?
Very rarely. (Circled 4 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Did you feel understood and validated by your father?
I don’t think it ever occurred to him to understand or validate me. The only thing he
registered was how things affected him.
That’s unfortunate. Children need validation from their parents. It helps them learn to
understand and trust themselves. It’s pretty tragic that he couldn’t do that for you. A big
loss.
Yeah, thanks.
Would you say you felt understood and validated by him rarely or very rarely?
Very rarely. (Circled 4 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Did you feel appropriately protected by your father?
Yes and no. He was a policeman, so I was certain if anyone tried to mess with me or
Mama, he’d protect us. Once we heard a prowler outside. Daddy went outside with a
pistol. The prowler heard him coming and took off. I’m sure he was prepared to shoot
to kill, especially if he’d caught a black or Hispanic man in the yard, but he was a danger
to us. He didn’t protect us from his rage, especially if he’d been drinking too much.
I’m so sorry. There’s nothing worse than growing up with a danger inside your home—
especially when you’re powerless to protect yourself.
Yeah. It was pretty bad.
Would you say you felt appropriately protected by him sometimes, rarely, or very rarely?
Rarely. (Circled 3 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Would you go to your father for help with a problem?
It would depend on the problem and his mood. Once one of the shelves in my closet
fell off the wall. When he was in a good mood, I showed it to him. After he shamed me
for having too much stuff in my closet and putting too much weight on the shelf, he got
out his power tools and reattached it. I suppose if I liked guns and had a gun-related
problem, I could have gone to him for help, because he liked talking about guns, but
