Next question. Did you feel your father put you in double binds?
What do you mean?
A double bind is a lose-lose proposition. Like when a parent gives a child two choices
but criticizes or punishes her for either choice or gives her something she wants if she
does something really bad that she doesn’t want to do.
Yeah. He would sit on the couch drinking beer watching football on TV, getting more
and more drunk. He’d tell me to get him another beer from the fridge. If I got him
another beer, he’d get more drunk and more dangerous, especially if his favorite team
lost, but if I didn’t get the beer, he’d scream at me and call me a little slut.
Yikes! That’s a great example of a double bind. Did he put you in binds like that often?
Yeah. Especially with Mama. He was always screaming at her, calling her names. If I tried
to defend her or comfort her, he’d say I was just like her. That would make her cry. It was
just awful.
Double binds can be paralyzing for children. I’m so sorry that happened to you. You
didn’t deserve to be treated that way.
Thanks.
Would you say he put you in double binds sometimes, often, or very often?
Often. (Circled 3 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Did you feel your father violated your boundaries?
What do you mean?
Like listening in on your phone conversations, watching you in the shower, touching you
in ways that were inappropriate. Anything like that.
Not so much when I was younger, but when I got into middle school, he started glaring
at me, like I was a piece of female meat to be ogled. He never touched me, but he would
make inappropriate comments like “so you’re finally getting boobs” or “wow, you look
great in those jeans.” I worried he might one day decide to cross the line.
That must have been scary for you.
It was. Papaw would have poker parties next door. When I was about twelve, Daddy
offered to pay me to come over and serve snacks. I was supposed to refill drinks or bake
a pizza and serve it. Stuff like that. Daddy’s friends would leer at me and make creepy
comments. Some of them would brush up against me, wink at me, or pat my butt. I’d
cringe, but Daddy would just smile, like he approved. They didn’t really need me to serve
snacks. I think Daddy just wanted to show off his pretty prepubescent teen.
Yuck!
I told Mama about it. She said I didn’t have to do that again. She’d arrange for me to do
a sleepover at Bridget’s house on his poker night.
I’m glad your mother was looking out for you. Would you say he violated your boundaries
sometimes, often, or very often?
I’ll go with often. (Circled 3 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Did you feel enmeshed with or smothered by your father?
What do you mean?
For example, did he rely on you to meet his emotional needs, try to live vicariously through
you, or treat you like he wanted you to stay a dependent child? Anything like that.
No. Mostly he ignored me, and I went out my way to stay off his radar.
Would you say you felt enmeshed with or smothered by him very rarely, rarely, sometimes,
often, or very often?
I’ll go with rarely. (Circled 1 on the interview questionnaire)

Do you mean the opposite of emotionally nurtured?

Next question. Did your mother provide loving correction?
I learned to be a pretty obedient child. My brother was a troublemaker even when he
was little. I saw him get yelled at and spanked a lot—mostly by Daddy. After watching
him catch hell, I figured out what I had to do avoid punishment. Mama wasn’t so big on
hitting or spanking, but she was good with guilt and shame. If I misbehaved, she’d make
me feel horrible about myself. Does that sound like loving correction?
Was that the kind of correction you needed most?
Not really.
If you’d had a skillful mother and you’d done something that required a correction, she
would have started by telling you how much she loved and treasured you and then
asked what you were thinking just before you misbehaved. She would have tried to
figure out if it was an expression of an unmet need. If so, she would have made sure you
got that need met. If it was because of something you didn’t know but needed to know,
she would have filled in that missing information. When the conversation was over,
you’d be apologizing for what you’d done. Then she’d give you a big hug.
That’s what I try to do with my daughter, Nina. I don’t always get it right, but I try.
Would you say your mother provided loving correction very rarely, rarely, sometimes,
often, or very often?
I’d say rarely. (Circled 3 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Was your mother warm and affectionate with you?
She was when I was little. She’d hug me and tell me I was her precious. She’d say, “I love

After the miscarriage, Daddy became more even more abusive. She became more timid,
scared, and depressed. We had fewer of those warm moments together, and the ones
we had were in the kitchen, baking and eating. When I became a teen, I spent as much
time as I could with my friends, which made her angry with me all the time.
I’m so sorry her mood took a downturn. I imagine you missed those close, intimate
moments with her.
I did.
Would you say she was warm and affectionate with you very rarely, rarely, sometimes,
often, or very often?
Sometimes. (Circled 2 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Was your mother able to repair breaks with you?
Not really. When I was younger, we didn’t really have many breaks. As long as I stayed
focused on keeping her in a good place, we did okay. When I got older and started to pull
away from her, we had lots of breaks. We could not repair them at the time. We eventually
talked through a lot of it after Daddy died. Our relationship is pretty good now, but I’ve
just had to accept that there are some things about me she’ll never accept or understand.
I’m so sorry. I wish you she could have supported you in being an authentic, empowered
teen.

Next question. Did you feel understood and validated by your mother?
She understood Daddy scared me. She understood I liked to read, so she helped me get

Did she ever invalidate you? Like if you said, “I’m cold,” would she say “No, you’re not,”
or if you said “I’m hungry,” would she say “You can’t possibly be hungry”?
It would depend on her mood. If she was very depressed, she’d probably just ignore

my friends, my choice of music, how I decorated my room. She didn’t understand why I

I’m so sorry. It does sound awful. Would you say you felt understood and validated by

Rarely. (Circled 3 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Did you feel appropriately protected by your mother?
Not protected enough.
Tell me about that.
We lived with a violent man, and as my brother got older, he became violent too.
Because of her religious convictions, she’d never walk away, but she did a few good
things to protect me. She kept me out of my Papaw’s dreadful poker parties, and she
knew better than to tell Daddy about anything I did that she thought would upset him.
She’d motion to me to keep quiet when she thought he was getting punchy.
I’m glad she could do those things for you. I wish she’d had the power to give you a safe
place to grow up.
That would have been nice.
Would you say you felt appropriately protected by her very rarely, rarely, sometimes,
often, or very often?
Sometimes. (Circled 2 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Would you go to your mother for help with a problem?
It would depend on the problem and her mood. If I had a problem with a hem, a ripped
pocket, a stain, a recipe, or something like that, I knew she’d help me.
What if you’d had trouble with a teacher at school? Would you have gone to her for
help with a problem like that?
Probably not. She was very uncomfortable with confrontation, so she wouldn’t have
gone to the school on my behalf. She would only offer to pray about it with me.
When she offered to pray with you, did it help?
It helped her feel better, but it never helped me.
I’m so sorry. Kids like to know their parents can help them solve problems. I’m sorry she
couldn’t do that for you. Would you say you could go to her for help with a problem
sometimes, rarely, or very rarely?
Rarely. (Circled 3 on the interview questionnaire)
Next question. Would you go to your mother for emotional support?
No. She needed MY emotional support. She was not equipped to give it.
Did you ever seek her out for emotional support?
Once when I was four, I accidently broke Daddy’s glasses. He was livid. He screamed at
me and Mama. When he started to spank me, she froze. It wasn’t a little pat on the butt.

Mostly I got the consistent message that she needed me nearby to help her manage her
emotions. When I became a teen and refused to do that for her, I got the consistent message
that I was bad.

Yes.

Next question. Did you feel your mother violated your boundaries?
Absolutely. When I was in high school, she’d search my room. She’d read my diary. She’d
ask me personal questions about by friends. She’d ask intrusive questions about my sexual
experiences with boys. She’d leave magazine articles about the dangers of premarital

Yikes! That’s not what a girl in high school needs to learn healthy independence.

Next question. Did you feel enmeshed with or smothered by your mother?
Definitely.
You’ve already described a lot of enmeshing experiences with her. Are there others you
want to talk about?
When I was about nine or ten, she started confiding in me. She thought Daddy was
having an affair, so she’d tell me about stuff like finding a woman’s earring in his pocket
or looks he’d give woman when they were out with friends. Then someone told her he
thought he’d saw Daddy coming out a motel with a woman. She became obsessed with
this, and I was supposed to listen to all of it. When I got into middle school, I told her I
was too busy with friends and homework to listen anymore. I told her to talk to her own
friends about it or get a therapist. She looked really hurt. She was able to stop the long,
epic rants, but she felt compelled to give me periodic status reports every time she found
evidence or heard a rumor he was cheating. I refused to listen for more than five minutes.
I didn’t know what I was supposed to do with the information. There was no way she was
going to divorce him, and there was no way I’d confront him about it. Honestly, if he
was having an affair, that was a few more hours a week that he was not at home with
us. That was fine with me.
I’m so sorry she did that. That must have been hard on you.
When she first started confiding in me, I felt a mixture of privilege and horror. I liked that
she was treating me like an adult, like a peer. I like that she trusted me enough to share
her personal worries, but I hated what she was saying. None of it surprised me, but she
was obsessed about it. She never seemed to gain any insights or figure out how to learn
from it and grow. She just wallowed endlessly in her grief about it. It got really old.
I completely understand your frustration. Kids want to know their parents feel empowered
to solve problems. It sounds like your mother’s sense of disempowerment became a big
burden for you. I’m so sorry.
Thanks.
Would you say you felt enmeshed with or smothered by her very rarely, rarely, sometimes,
often, or very often?
Very often. (Circled 4 on the interview questionnaire)

Okay. Let’s shift gears. Tell me about your relationship with your brother, Billy Bob.
It was not good. He was spoiled rotten. Mama never made him do any chores. When he
was little, she bent over backwards to give him whatever he wanted. All he had to do
was demand something loudly enough. He loved Star Wars, so he’d whine until she’d
buy him the action figure he wanted. He eventually had a huge collection. We didn’t
have a lot of money for extra stuff like that, so it was stressful for her to make those
purchases. Sometimes she ran out of money for laundry soap or toilet paper.
Oh my!
My Aunt Sue would send me dolls, and he’d attack them with his action figures. Once
he decapitated all my dolls. If he saw something I was really fond of, like a book or a toy,
he’d do something to damage it. Once he covered my teddy bear with mud and left it
on my pillow. The older he got, the more he acted like Daddy. He’d threaten me and
belittle Mama and me. Sometimes he’d hit me.
How did your parents react to that?
Mama would try to discipline him when he hurt me or my things, but she wasn’t effective.
He’d just keep doing the same mean stuff. She’d plead with Daddy to punish him, but

When Billy Bob was a teenager, he was very handsome and charming. He had a lot of

spot in the woods where they’d drink. They could have gotten arrested for underage
drinking but never did. I think he also sold drugs. He seemed to always have money.
When he was seventeen, he bought a car. There was a rumor he’d caught Daddy with a
“very young” girlfriend, and he threatened to tell if he got arrested for anything. He had
explosive anger and knew how to shoot guns. By the time he was eighteen, even Daddy
was a little scared of him. Papaw died when Billy Bob was eighteen. He dropped out of
high school and moved into to Papaw’s house next door. He seemed to have something
illegal going on there. I never asked.
Yikes. How’d he turn out?
Not good. He’s been in prison for the last twenty years for attempted murder. He’d
picked up a hitchhiker, expecting to rob him. But the man was broke. He offered Billy
Bob sexual favors in exchange for money. He was extremely homophobic. He drove the
car to a deserted roadside rest stop and beat the man nearly to death and then drove
away. He only survived because a truck driver found him in time.
Wow. It must have been hard to grow up in a home with a dangerous father and brother.
It was. I learned to avoid my brother like I avoided Daddy. When he was a teenager, he
spent a lot of time away from home, hanging out with friends, selling pot, seducing girls,
and I don’t know what. That made it easier to avoid him.
That was lucky for you.
Mostly. But when I was twelve, and he was fifteen, Mama had to go to Nashville. Nana
was hospitalized for an appendicitis. Mama was gone about a week. Because she was
gone, Billy Bob brought his friends over to the house. Some of them wanted to “play”
with me. When I realized they were aiming to sexually abuse me, I snuck out the back
door. I ran over to Bridget’s house. I told her parents what had happened. They let me
stay there until Mama came home.
So it sounds like you were on your own to protect yourself from Billy Bob. Your mother
could see there was a problem but couldn’t get his behavior under control, and your
father just didn’t care. Does that sound right?
Yeah. Pretty much.
I’m so sorry. That sounds horrible. I’m sure glad Bridget’s parents gave you a safe place
to land.
Me too.

Did any other relatives significantly influence you in childhood, in positive or negative
ways? You had your Grandfather Papaw next door, your Nana, and Aunt Sue in Nashville.
Anyone else?
No one else.
Tell me about your relationship with your Grandfather Papaw?
Like Daddy, he believed women and girls were objects. We’re only here to serve men.
When I was old enough to do household chores, he hired me to vacuum his house and
wash dishes. He had an archive of gun magazines and girlie magazines. I didn’t have
many conversations with him. When I tried to talk to him, I got mostly one-word answers.
I didn’t like him. When I was middle-school-age he’d sometimes stare at me, like he was
imagining having sex with me. He gave me the creeps.
What was your mother’s reaction to him?
She was always polite and deferential to him. She embraced the local cultural norm that
women should be subservient to men. So, his misogyny didn’t seem to bother her, or if
it did, she didn’t show it.
No one deserves to be objectified. I’m sorry your mother gave you the impression it was
okay.
Yeah. Me too.
Tell me about your relationship with your Nana.
I loved to visit Nana. I wish she’d lived next door! She was wonderful. Very nurturing and
kind. Nana has an old historic Victorian house that had been in the family for decades. I
always felt safe there, like I could let my guard down. Mama and I would go every
summer for two weeks to see Nana and Aunt Sue without Daddy. At first, Billy Bob would
come with us, but as he got older, he protested, so Daddy told him he didn’t have to go,
so we’d have girl time in Nashville. My best childhood memories were in Nashville.
Awesome. Were Nana and Aunt Sue religious, like your mother?
Nana was. She’d been a minister’s wife, so this was important to her. She read her Bible
every day. Went to church every Sunday and Bible study every Tuesday. Mama’s religion
was always about right and wrong, good and bad, obedience and disobedience—I think
she got that from her daddy. Nana’s was about loving and accepting everyone—about
kindness and forgiveness. She volunteered at a homeless soup kitchen twice a week.
Once she took me with her. I remember seeing a lot of black women with kids. Nana
knew them all by name. She’d welcome them and give them hugs. They all loved her.
She said God loved everyone no matter what their skin color, and we should too. That’s
when I knew Nana would love and accept me no matter what.
She sounds like a very special woman.
She is, and at eighty-eight, she’s still just as wonderful. She’s very frail now. May not last
much longer. I’ll miss her when she’s gone.
I can see why. How about Aunt Sue?
She’s pretty awesome too. She’s not especially religious, but she’s just as warm and
loving. She’s now a retired elementary school principal. A feminist. When I was two, my
grandfather and Nana were in a car accident. He was killed. She was injured and took a
long time to recover. Aunt Sue moved in with her to help her out. They got along so
well, she stayed. She never married—unusual for a Southern woman. She had no
children but said being able to love and nurture children at work was perfect for her. I
really liked being around her. She’d tell me I was wonderful and beautiful and to never
let a man control me. She’d read me stories about strong, powerful women. I told her
about how Daddy treated Mama and how scary it was. She taught me ways to avoid him
and stay off his radar. I followed her advice. She’d say, “If you’re going to swim with

Definitely. She was secretly gay. Her best friend, Savannah, had been her girlfriend for

Me too.
It sounds like your mother supported your great relationship with Nana and Aunt Sue.
How about your father? How did he react?
He noticed I felt more alive and confident after we’d come back from Tennessee. He’d

really good at distracting him and staying out of his way.
I’m so sorry he didn’t want you to feel confident.
Yep. It’s just what I came to expect.
Did anyone else significantly influence you in childhood, in positive or negative ways?
For example, teachers, friends, neighbors, or ministers?
Yes. My friend, Bridget, and her parents, and my 8th grade English teacher, Miss Washington.
Tell me about your relationship with Bridget and her parents.
Bridget was my best friend from about first grade to middle school. She lived three
houses down. I spent as much time at her house as possible. Her parents were amazing.
They were everything I wished my parents could be. I knew they loved me. Her mama
could see when I was upset about something and say just the right things to comfort
and reassure me. They never said so, but I think they knew my daddy could get physically
violent. They gave me a standing invitation to come over anytime I needed a safe place
to hang out. I don’t think I would have survived my childhood without them.
Wow. That sounds like a lifeline for you. What happened to the friendship?
They moved away when I was 14. I was pretty devastated. But by the time we got to
middle school, we were already starting to drift apart. It was only a matter of time before
we were hanging out in different cliques, but I really hated to lose touch with her parents.
How did your parents react to your close relationship with Bridget and her parents?
Mama liked that I had a friend, but I think she was worried that I liked Bridget’s mom
more than her. She’d always let out a deep sigh when I asked to go to Bridget’s, like she
didn’t want me to leave her, but if Daddy was home, she’d always let me go.
Thanks for pointing that out. Tell me about your relationship with Miss Washington.
She was amazing. She was the only black teacher at the school. I expected she’d be timid
around all the racism, but she wasn’t. She was the most popular teacher, because she
was strong and nurturing. She’d look you in the eye and see your soul. She saw the
goodness in everyone—even the mean kids. She wanted to make a big impression, and
she did. There was required reading she had to assign the eighth graders like Animal
Farm, Huck Finn, and Anne Frank, but she had her own personal recommended reading
list for the girls. They were books about feminism, racism, and personal empowerment.
About five of us girls formed an after-school book club to talk about what we were reading.
Whenever possible, Miss Washington would join us. We all wanted to be just like her.

Wow! That’s amazing. I’m so glad you had such a good role model to guide you.
She was beautiful, just like Nana and Aunt Sue, but I got to see her almost every day
while I was in the 8th grade. She made a big impression, and it’s stayed with me ever since.
How did your parents react to all that?
I never shared anything with Daddy. I tried to talk to Mama about it, but she scolded
me. Told me not to fill my head with nonsense—especially the feminism stuff. By then, I
was pulling away from her. She was already started losing her credibility with me. By the
time I was eighteen, it was all gone. Her opinion didn’t matter.
I’m so sorry. It must have been hard to have no one at home to talk to about so many
new ideas. That’s a big loss.
Yeah. Thanks for understanding. It was very hard.
Did you get messages from your community or the media that made you feel inferior,
ashamed, unimportant, or uncomfortable?
The traditional, Southern fundamentalists wanted me to believe I was inferior to men. They
said God wanted me to get married, have babies, and serve my man in every way he
requested. I saw how that worked out for my mother and her friends and was not
interested, so yeah, I would say that message made me very uncomfortable.
When your parents finally figured out you were not going to follow that path, how did
they react?
Daddy was contemptuous as always. He favorite name for me in high school was “bitch”
followed by “slut” and “whore.” Mama was just bewildered. She was afraid God would
punish her for failing to raise a girl who conformed to these norms.
Yikes! That must have been really hard on you.
It was, but I wasn’t going to stay and listen to their drivel forever. I was prepared to leave
as soon as possible.
So I’d like to summarize my understanding of what your childhood experience was like.
Throughout your childhood, your father was angry, hateful, and sometimes violent. He
enjoyed controlling his family with intimidation. That was very scary for you, especially
because he was a policeman. It felt like there was no local authority to go to for
protection. He raised your brother to be just like him, so as you got older, you had two
aggressive males in the house to deal with. They both victimized you and your mother.
Your best strategy for handling them was to avoid them. You spent as much time as
possible away from them. When you were younger, you’d often visit your friend Bridget,
three doors down. You felt safe at her home, and felt loved and supported by her
parents. When you were little, you had a good relationship with your mother. You’d
enjoy spending time together baking and sewing. You had a nice bond. When you were
seven, she had a miscarriage that put her in a deep depression. You felt responsible for
comforting her and encouraging her to be as functional as possible. This was a big
burden for you, but you genuinely wanted to help her. When she began to suspect your
father was having an affair, she made you her confidant. She leaned on you for support
in ways that were painful and burdensome. At age thirteen, you felt empowered by your
English teacher to say “enough.” You set a boundary with your mother, forbidding her
to obsess about your father with you, but that started a period of deepening friction
with her. Throughout your teen years, she was constantly intrusive and critical in ways
that seemed calculated to interfere with your path to independence. That was a rough
childhood. I’m so sorry it didn’t go smoothly. (Full contextual validation)
Thanks. I appreciate being understood. These things are hard to talk about. Thanks for
helping me tell the story.

Chapter 4 Summary
This chapter describes a way of taking a history that specifically helps us understand a client’s
degree of attachment wounding, since that will help us assess foundational stability. It involves a
thorough exploration of childhood relationships. Part of the questioning comes from my Quality
of Attachment Interview Questionnaire, which helps us estimate the degree of attachment
wounding inflicted by primary caregivers. While this history-taking process does invite clients
to tell us about traumatic events, the focus is primarily on relationships. We often learn about
traumatic events as we learn about wounding relationships. This chapter also illuminates steps
we can take to make this interview validating and healing, while minimizing the risk of overwhelm.
Many EMDR therapists might find this an unusual departure from a history interview focused
more on adverse, traumatic events.

Chapter 5
Introducing DNMS Ego State Therapy to Clients

When we take a history focused on childhood relationships, we learn a lot about a client’s attachment
wounds. Clients who reveal attachment wounds will have lots of wounded child parts, which we will
need to stabilize with ego state therapy. Most clients are unaware of their child parts and are unfamiliar
with ego state therapy. They’ll need us to explain it. This chapter outlines how we can introduce parts
of self, and the DNMS stabilizing interventions, to clients who need ego state therapy.
For a checklist that will guide you through the bullet points of information we need to convey, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

The Main DNMS Ego State Therapy Concepts
I list here the main concepts I believe we should share with clients. To make this explanation vivid, we
should weave into the discussion examples from the client’s personal story. That helps make it uniquely
relevant to each client. A sample script at the end of the chapter illustrates how we can do that.
 Trauma wounding comes from an overwhelming, physical threat or harm. The trauma can be inflicte
by a person we know or don’t know, maliciously or unintentionally. It could come from an accident,
sports injury, or natural disaster. It could be something we experience directly or something we
witness happening to someone else.
 Attachment wounding comes from unmet attachment needs in childhood. This happens when parents
fail to meet our emotional needs, like when they fail to attune to us, comfort us, and delight in us.
We need them to understand, validate, and love us. If we don’t get enough of these nurturing
experiences, we grow up feeling lost, empty, and chronically insecure.
 DNMS therapy treats attachment wounds from unmet emotional needs in childhood. EMDR treats
trauma wounds. When childhood trauma wounds are connected to attachment wounds, it usually
works best to treat the attachment wounds first.
 Significant trauma and attachment wounding in childhood can evoke painful emotions, uncomfortable
body sensations, and negative beliefs—often with a sense of powerlessness. These reactions can
get frozen in time, forming wounded child parts who are stuck in the hurtful experiences—stuck in
a trance.

68

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

 When this kind of wounding happens, the brain makes a recording
of it, like filming a home movie. It’s the brain’s way of memorializing
the experience. The recording may not be a perfect representation
of the wounding event, but it is an essential representation of what
was perceived.
 The recording gets stored in a two-step process. At first, it gets stored
in a mental in-box. That gives the brain time to make sense of the
experience, organize it, and heal it. When this process is complete
and fully resolved, the recording moves to the archives. While the
recording is in the in-box, reminders of the wounding event can
evoke intensely painful emotions. Once it’s archived, it’s no longer
distressing to recall.

I’m
bad.
You’re
bad.

 Sometimes the brain needs help organizing and processing a
wounding experience. In childhood, that help must come from a
loving person who supports the child in making sense of it. The
wound can heal when the child’s physical and emotional needs are
met—when she feels safe and valued. If this healing help comes
soon after the event, the recording can be archived right away.
 If that help does not come right away, the recording will stay in the
in-box, often for many years. As long as it’s in the in-box, a reminder
of the wounding experience can press play on the recording.
 When a recording plays back, a wounded child part will see it and reexperience the painful emotions,
sensations, and negative beliefs. We call this getting triggered. The intensity of this reaction can
range from mild to severe. When it’s severe, it can feel as if the event is still happening—as if a
physical or emotional threat is imminent.
 The DNMS remedies this by working with wounded child parts. This involves talking directly to
child parts, which can seem weird at first. Once it’s experienced as helpful and effective, it will feel
natural. These parts want to be seen, heard, and understood. They want direct, welcoming, validating
contact. They’ve waited a long time for that.
 The DNMS is a multistep process. First, we mobilize a team of loving, attuned, internal Resources. They’ll
be drawn from personal experiences as a competent caregiver, experiences of being cared for by
others, or peak spiritual experiences.
 Next, we establish a Special Safe Place where wounded parts can hang out with the Resources. It’s
a fun place for them to rest and play while they’re waiting for their time to heal.
 Next, we talk about the therapy goal. We want to make sure we only pursue therapy goals that all
wounded parts are happy with. If a therapy goal upsets wounded parts, we find a new one that they
will all like.
 Next, we invite wounded child parts forward to meet the Resources—usually parts who have been
recently triggered. We encourage them to tell their story. The Resources will give them lots of
loving, validating, empathic attention, meet their emotional needs, and answer all their questions.
 Because wounded child parts feel as if an old wounding experience is actually happening now, we
orient them to present time. We help them self-discover that it’s all over, and they’re safe now. We
show them they’re in an adult body, so they can see they have power now they didn’t have in childhood.

Chapter 5: Introducing Ego State Therapy

69

 We teach child parts that when a wounding experience happens, the brain makes a recording of it
like filming a home movie. We explain that the old recording can play back, making it feel like the
wounding is happening now, but they’re not reliving the old experience, just their reaction to it. We
help them see the recording is actually harmless. Once they understand this, it gives them a huge
sense of relief.
 Collectively these interventions help wounded parts heal by helping them feel safe and valued. It
brings them out of trance. Afterward, they should be able to reflect on the triggering event without
overreacting.
 If these interventions are not enough to archive the recording, additional DNMS interventions or
EMDR trauma processing can finish the job.
 ABS is used periodically throughout these steps to strengthen positive experiences, like a connection to
Resources and a loving connection between the Resources and wounded parts. (If clients do not
already know what ABS is, we explain it here.)
 We invite clients to react to this information and to tell us if they want to try it.

Why wouldn’t we do that first?
Because it can be overwhelming for child parts who aren’t ready. That overwhelm can
make it hard to work through the trauma. Your father was not just violent and threatening;
he was also belittling, rejecting, and humiliating. Attachment wounds like that can
actually be more destabilizing for children than physical harm, because they draw
erroneous conclusions about their value and worth. The kind of parental rejection and
betrayal you experienced can be very destabilizing. Stabilizing wounded parts, getting
them feeling loved and safe in the care of the Resources, can be extremely healing.
When you’re ready, we do EMDR to process the trauma, it will go quickly and smoothly,
without being overwhelming.
I like that. Sounds like you want to cover all the bases.
Exactly. One of the tools we use for EMDR and the DNMS is alternating bilateral
stimulation. A little background. Francine Shapiro developed EMDR when she discovered
that she could desensitize a trauma memory by guiding clients to think about a memory
while moving their eyes rapidly back and forth. Later, others discovered they could get
the same effects with alternating bilateral tones, delivered through headphones, or
alternating bilateral tactile stimulation, like alternating taps to the knees. All three
methods seem to help rapidly process trauma memories by linking them to positive
adaptive information that’s already in the brain. It also seems to help strength positive
feelings and beliefs. In the DNMS, alternating bilateral stimulation is used to strengthen
positives like a connection to Resources and a loving connection between the Resources
and wounded parts. There are three ways to deliver it—rapid eye movements,
alternating tones, or alternating taps. When it’s delivered with tones or taps, you have
the option to close your eyes. I’ve found most prefer to process with their eyes closed..
(This is a good time to demonstrate different ways to apply ABS.) So now that you’ve
heard all about the DNMS, are you ready to try it?
Let’s go for it.

Chapter 5 Summary
This chapter outlines steps for explaining the main DNMS ego state therapy concepts to clients,
including guidance about how these concepts fit into EMDR treatment. It illustrates how we can
explain these ego state therapy concepts in the context of the client’s personal history. When
clients understand these concepts, they’re able to give us informed consent about whether or
not to proceed.

Chapter 6
Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

The idea of resourcing to prepare clients for EMDR was introduced by Andrew Leeds in the late 1990s.1
His Resource Development and Installation (RDI) protocol aims to help clients access specific desirable
qualities deemed helpful in assisting with the challenges of trauma processing. These might include
positive affects (like courage or confidence) and/or adaptive responses (like patience or persistence).
He proposed three types of resources: mastery, relational, and symbolic. Mastery resources are drawn
from personal experiences of embodying a desirable quality, relational resources are real or fictional
positive role models who embody a desirable quality, and symbolic resources represent a desirable
quality symbolically (like the strength of a mountain). Once a resource is accessed, the positive state of
mind it elicits is installed with ABS along with associated positive images, sounds, emotions, and
sensations. This type of resource installation has been shown to help stabilize clients with complex PTSD.2
Over the years, many EMDR trainers have taught clinicians to integrate resourcing into their trauma
work.3 While their different approaches vary in the details, most of them, like RDI, aim to resource the
client’s adult self with a positive state of mind so that it may assist in trauma processing. This can be
very helpful for some clients, but when clients have lots of wounded child parts stuck in the past because
of unmet developmental needs, a different kind of resourcing may be more helpful.
Children require loving, attuned caregivers to meet their needs. That caring relationship is their resource.
Now, in therapy, a loving relationship can be a resource that helps wounded child parts heal. Some ego
state therapies4 and inner child therapies5 encourage child parts to draw comfort from a nurturing
figure like an adult part, a wise one, or an ideal parent. Some EMDR trainers have incorporated this
general concept into therapeutic interactions with child parts.6 This chapter will teach a DNMS protocol
for mobilizing a loving, supportive Resource team that can meet the emotional needs of wounded parts.
For a worksheet that will guide you through these intervention steps while working with clients, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

Protocol Overview
I introduced my DNMS Resource Development Protocol
to the EMDR community in 2002.7 This protocol helps
clients mobilize a team of Resources. I use the term
Resource mobilization, because the process aims to
mobilize skills and strengths the client already has. The
Healing Circle team consists of three individual
Resource ego states already within the client, including
a Nurturing Adult Self (NAS), a Protective Adult Self
(PAS), and a Spiritual Core Self (SCS).

74

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

DNMS resourcing differs from other approaches to EMDR resourcing in several ways. (1) It provides
structured, guided meditations for mobilizing each Resource. (2) Each Resource on the team is a loving
part of self drawn from the clients’ own positive experiences as a competent caregiver. And (3) the
process fully vets each Resource, so when they’re called upon to meet the emotional needs of wounded
child parts, we know they can do it well.
Not only are Healing Circle Resources available to help stabilize wounded parts in session, they’re also
available between sessions to comfort and support child parts whenever they get triggered. This Resource
team can be essential in helping clients learn to self-soothe.
Connecting clients to a Healing Circle can have some unexpectedly wonderful results. Some clients
report an immediate boost in overall confidence and a reduction in symptoms. For example, Lisa began
therapy with me after in-patient treatment for substance abuse. She was quite depressed and cried
constantly. The only bright spot in her life was a loving connection to her two-year-old niece. That
heart-to-heart connection made it easy for her to connect to her Nurturing and Protective Adult Self.
Once she’d mobilized her Healing Circle, her crying jags stopped. She could see her potential to change
and grow and began feeling hopeful.

Nurturing and Protective Adult Self Resources
The NAS and PAS Resources are considered a person’s most mature, caregiver self, expressed in a
nurturing role and a protective role. The NAS is a state of mind that can competently nurture a loved one
with qualities like empathy and compassion. The PAS is a state of mind that can competently protect
a loved one with qualities like strength and courage.
These are not imaginary, archetypal, or fantasy Resources.
They are not merely positive mood states. Instead, they
are real parts of self drawn from a client’s confirmed
ability to lovingly attune to and meet the needs of
another living being like a person, pet, or plant. These
caregiver abilities are embodied in a real relationship
with a past or present loved one. This could include a
someone inside the family, like a niece or grandson, or
outside the family, like a student or patient. It could even
include a relationship with a pet, houseplants, or garden.
Clients who have had no such experiences to draw on
may be able to anticipate a time as a future caregiver
like becoming a parent, aunt, uncle, teacher, doctor,
nurse, pet owner, or gardener—a time when they’ll be
able to effortlessly and competently nurture and protect
another living being.

PAS

NAS

Protective
Adult Self

Nurturing
Adult Self

The NAS and PAS are not superhuman. They have needs, they are imperfect, they are vulnerable, and
they can experience the full range of emotions, just as you’d expect of competent parents, but they
can meet their own needs, manage their vulnerabilities, and regulate their own emotions. They can be
discerning, solve problems, take action, and be appropriately protective. They can also learn and grow
from their mistakes and continue to mature over time. These skills (and others) make them especially
good caregivers for helping wounded parts heal.
The NAS and PAS are mobilized using interactive guided meditations based on a list of twenty-four
competent caregiver skills and traits that clients already possess. (See the Nurturing and Protective
Adult Self Skills & Traits list on page 77). This list was constructed over time from clinical trial and error

Chapter 6: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

75

and with feedback from clients. While it may or may not be a perfect list, it does appear to cover the
most important areas.

Discussing Personal Caregiving Experiences
Before we guide a client in the NAS and PAS meditations, we look for positive, caregiving experiences
to serve as reference points to anchor the meditations. This helps make the process more believable,
so the Resources feel real. We invite clients to discuss personal experiences of enjoying being a nurturer
and protector—times when competent caregiver skills were naturally, effortlessly, and appropriately
applied, at the same time, while in a mature, adult state of mind. The best caregiving experiences are
positive examples of caring for someone vulnerable or needy. (See Caregiving Relationships to Anchor
Meditations sidebar on page 76.) These could be relationships the client is currently in or was in in the
past. It could be about a single event or a series of events. Here are some examples from a current
relationship.
1.

A single event as a nurturer: Alice recalled babysitting her six-year-old nephew, Nathan, for a long
weekend. When he got moody, she calmed him with her soothing presence. If something traumatic
happened to him, she’d help him talk it through. At bedtime, they snuggled together as she read
him a story. Alice felt intense joy and affection for the boy, and he felt nurtured by her. This could
anchor the NAS meditation.

2.

A single event as a protector: One afternoon, Alice took Nathan to a park to fly kites. An unleashed
dog charged the boy. Alice intervened by spraying the dog with pepper spray, picked up the child,
and ran. He screamed in pain, reacting to a small amount of overspray. She took him directly to
Emergency Care, where he was seen by a doctor. As he recovered, he felt protected by her. This
could anchor the PAS meditation.

3.

A single event as a nurturer and protector: When Nathan was charged by the dog, Alice intervened
with pepper spray and got him immediate medical attention. She also calmed him with her soft,
soothing voice. Later, she got him talking about and drawing pictures of what happened. She gave
him a safe space to discharge his emotions. This helped him feel nurtured and safe. This could
anchor the NAS and PAS meditations.

4.

A series of events in a nurturer role: For many years, Alice has actively supported her sister in raising
Nathan. He spent one weekend a month with her. She delighted in him, soothed him, played with
him, read books to him, and cuddled him. Their bond of love and trust grew stronger over time.
This could anchor the NAS meditation.

5.

A series of events in a protector role: For years, Alice has been vigilant to ensure Nathan’s safety.
She got him needed medical care when he broke his arm, got a bee sting, and had allergic reactions
to peanuts. When he was old enough to use an EpiPen himself, she showed him how. He now
trusts her to protect him. This could anchor the PAS meditation.

6.

A series of events in a nurturer and protector role: Over the years, Nathan had many moments in
peril—a dog attack, a broken arm, a bee sting, allergic reactions, and so forth. Alice always got him
needed medical care. She also gave him warm, loving soothing and comfort to help him manage
his distress. He trusted her to nurture and protect him. This could anchor the NAS and PAS
meditations.

Ideally, we look for adulthood caregiver experiences, but some young clients haven’t yet had any such
experiences. For them, a caregiving experience from childhood might work well enough. For example,
the young client may have treasured childhood or adolescent memories of babysitting, gardening, or

76

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

caring for a cherished pet. So long as an early experience is not associated with any unmet developmental
needs, it might work well enough to anchor an NAS or PAS meditation.
When clients struggle to recall or articulate their caregiving experiences, we help them out. (See the
Problems Discussing Personal Caregiving Experiences section on page 86.) With a little support from
us, most clients can describe competent caregiving experiences. If they cannot, we help them mobilize
Provisional Resources instead. (See Chapter 7.)

Showing Client the List of Twenty-Four Skills and Traits
Once a client is able to describe experiences of competence in a nurturer and protector a role, we show
her the list of twenty-four skills and traits from page 77. We invite her to tell us if there are any skills or
traits listed that she thinks she does not have when she cares (or cared) for her loved one. We’re looking
for hesitations to endorse items on the list. Most hesitations are linked to concerns or misunderstandings
that can be easily cleared. (See the Common Concerns about Skills and Traits section on page 87.)
With a little support from us, most clients can readily recognize ownership of all twenty-four items in
the context of their anchoring caregiving experience.
Clients are often surprised to see how many adult skills and strengths they actually possess. They often
say things like ”Wow! I really can do these things. I really can be nurturing, patient, and protective.”
This new awareness is very ego strengthening. Once a client can endorse all twenty-four skills from a
personal experience of nurturing and protecting a living being, we have the anchors needed for these
two meditations.

Reading the NAS and PAS Meditations
We mobilize the NAS and PAS by reading two two-phased guided interactive meditations. (See the full
NAS mediation on page 82 and full PAS meditation on page 83.) During the first phase we read the
skills, giving the client a chance to acknowledge each one with a “yes” or a nod. During the second
phase, we read the list of skills again while ABS strengthens the Resource. If body disturbances or
doubts arise at any point before, during, or after a mediation, there’s a concern we must identify and
clear. (Ways to do that are discussed in the Discomfort Before, During, or After a Meditation section
on page 89.)

Caregiving Relationships to Anchor Meditations
Strong Anchors
 Experiences as a loving, attuned parent,
grandparent, aunt, uncle, doctor, nurse,
teacher, clergy, therapist, etc.
 Caring for a sick, disabled, or elderly person
who is dependent on others for care.
 Having a loving bond with another living
thing, like a child, teen, animal, or plant.
 Rescuing a stranger from danger.

Weak Anchors
 Caring for an elderly parent who was
wounding to the client in childhood.
 Supporting a self-sufficient adult friend
through a relationship crisis.
 Caring for younger siblings in
childhood while in a parentified role.
 Any caregiving experiences that felt
joyless or burdensome.

24 Nurturing & Protective Adult Self Skills & Traits
Empathic

Able to recognize someone’s distress and sense it from their perspective

Compassionate

Able to feel concern and sympathy for someone’s distress with an urge to relieve it.

Understanding

Able to fully comprehend and empathize with someone’s story of distress.

Accepting

Able to welcome and value someone as they are, with their strengths and weaknesses.

Patient

Able to stay calm, pleasant, and tolerant with someone who is distressed.

Nurturing

Able to be loving, patient, and kind while meeting someone’s physical/emotional needs.

Warm

Able to be welcoming, friendly, generous, affectionate, cordial, polite, and empathic.

Open

Able to listen to someone without prejudice. Approachable and friendly.

Able to attune

Able to read someone’s nonverbal cues and respond appropriately with empathy.

Good at
listening

Able to hear someone’s verbal or nonverbal communication, comprehend it, and
follow up with a thoughtful, attuned response.

Good with
boundaries

Able to be nurturing and protective of self or others by setting and maintaining
appropriate rules and limits in a kind, respectful way.

Reliable

Able to be reliable and dependable. Able to keep promises and commitments.

Trustworthy

Able to be honest and dependable. Worthy of someone’s confidence.

Confident

Able to feel self-assured in one's actions and decisions.

Respectful

Able to convey to someone their intrinsic value by being kind and courteous.

Appropriately
responsible

Able to make and fulfill appropriate commitments and obligations in the best
interest of self and others.

Problem solver

Able to acknowledge a problem, understand it well, and find a solution.

Action taker

Able to take steps to solve a current problem or prevent a future problem.

Decision maker

Able to select a best option after thoughtful consideration of many.

Logical

Able to be rational and reasonable.

Strong

Able to be resilient in the face of adversity and stay grounded when distressed or confused.

Courageous

Able to stay present and lean in to deal with or withstand danger or adversity.

Protective

Able to take actions that prevent harm, reduce harm, or stop harm.

Grounded

Able to be “in the body,” in present time, with a sense of peace and stability.
