Spiritual Core Self Resource
The Spiritual Core Self (SCS) could be considered the core of one’s being. Some people call it the soul.
Some believe it’s a part of self that existed before the body arrived and will exist after the body dies.
That would make it unwoundable. An SCS is an essential part of self that does not change over time.
It is loving, glowing energy—balanced and wise. In my view, all of us have an SCS whether we’re aware
of it or not. It’s our breath of life. As a Resource, it can bring a quality of transcendent love, calm, and
reassurance to wounded parts. It embodies these ten qualities.
 A sense of interconnectedness to all beings
 A sense of completeness and wholeness
 A sense of safety and invulnerability
 No ego, no struggles
 No desires or aversions
 Unconditional, effortless happiness
 Unconditional, effortless acceptance
 Unconditional, effortless loving kindness, compassion
 Timeless, cosmic wisdom and understanding
 Present moment is precious and full
We mobilize the SCS by reading a guided meditation. I have not found a single meditation that’s
perfect for all clients, so I offer four alternative meditations. They’re listed below in the Reading an SCS
Meditation section.

Discussing Peak Spiritual Experiences
Before we guide a client in an SCS meditation, we look for a transcendent peak spiritual experience to
serve as a reference point to anchor the meditation. We show clients the ten qualities listed above and
then invite them to discuss any times they’ve experienced them. This may be a moment during meditation,
Tai Chi, yoga, or prayer, a peak spiritual experience, a blissful near-death experience, or a communion
with nature. Such moments may not be especially long, but they’re often quite memorable and profound.
If the client offers an experience that seems connected to issues of survival—like staying safe, making
others happy, earning more money—we keep looking for a transcendent experience.
Transcendent experiences can happen in many different contexts. For example, a Catholic might recall
feeling a profound connection to divine love while praying the rosary. A Buddhist might recall feeling
especially connected to all beings during a meditation retreat. An atheist might recall feeling a deep
connection to the universe on a wilderness campout. Most, but not all clients, will have a peak spiritual
experience to share.
Some clients are dismissive of spirituality or get triggered by the idea of an SCS. We can read a meditation
without the word spiritual and simply connect them to a Core Self instead. This might be more
comfortable for an atheist or a victim of religious abuse. Getting clients in touch with the state of mind
that holds the ten SCS qualities can be very helpful, no matter what they want to call it.

Reading an SCS Meditation
We mobilize the SCS by reading one of the following four guided mediations. Three of them do not
contain the word spiritual. The more we understand a client’s personal views about spirituality, the
better equipped we’ll be to select the best meditation. If one meditation falls short, another may work.
We can modify these meditations or create a whole new one if any of these miss the mark.

Chapter 6: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

79

We invite clients who have named a transcendent experience to think about it during the meditation
to help anchor it. If a client cannot recall any peak spiritual experiences, we just read a meditation
without that anchor. We speak softly and slowly. Meditations are read with ABS off.
Here are the four meditations.
Regular Spiritual Core Self Meditation: The meditation I use most frequently.
(Think about transcendent experience .) Close your eyes, if that’s comfortable, and take a deep
breath. Now get in touch with the center of your being… that place within you that is quiet…
peaceful… and still. In this place… it’s possible you’ll connect to your spiritual core. Your spiritual
core has been with you from the beginning… it’s the essence of who you are… your core of
goodness. Pure… resilient… and whole. Your body knows exactly how to connect you. Notice as
you connect to this part of you, and when you’re ready, tell me what you notice.
Core Self Meditation: A good choice for those with an aversion to the word spiritual.
(Think about transcendent experience .) Close your eyes, if that’s comfortable, and take a deep
breath. Now get in touch with the center of your being… that place within you that is quiet…
peaceful… and still. In this place… it’s possible you’ll connect to your core self. Your core self has
been with you from the beginning… it’s the essence of who you are… your core of goodness. Pure…
resilient… and whole. Your body knows exactly how to connect you. Notice as you connect to this
part of you, and when you’re ready, tell me what you notice.
Breath Meditation: A way to connect to an SCS through the body.
There’s a place in your mind that is quiet, peaceful, and calm. It's always been there, even though
you may never have noticed it. You'll get there by simply watching your breath. First, deliberately
inhale, noticing it takes muscle tension to do this. You do a little work to get air into your lungs.
Now release the air, and notice you can just let go. Just let yourself float down, while the air leaves
your body. Notice how good it feels just to let go. Now take another breath, and again notice that
muscle tension is needed. Then let go and just float down. Notice that right at the bottom of your
breath, right at the point where you stop exhaling, there is a still moment, a moment where nothing
is happening, where all is quiet, calm, peaceful. Okay, now just breathe again, and watch for that
point at the bottom of your breath. Now, breathe again, and this time when you get to the bottom
of your breath, just sit there a moment or two, still and quiet, not breathing. Notice what that feels
like, what your mind is like: empty, quiet, and calm. Now as you keep breathing, just stay connected
to this calm and quiet part of you, now that you've found it… and when you’re ready, tell me what
you notice.
List Meditation: Based on the list of ten SCS qualities.
(Think about transcendent experience .) Close your eyes, if that’s comfortable, and take a deep
breath. Now get in touch with the center of your being… that place within you that is quiet…
peaceful… and still. In this place… it’s possible you’ll connect to a sense of interconnectedness to
all beings… a sense of completeness and wholeness… of safety and invulnerability. A place where
all things and events are equally special… a place of no desires or aversions… no ego or struggles.
A place of unconditional effortless happiness, acceptance. A place of loving kindness and
compassion. Notice as you connect to this part of you, and when you’re ready, tell me what you
notice.
If the body suddenly feels tense, uncomfortable, or sleepy at any point during or after the SCS meditation,
there’s a concern to identify and clear. (Read about that in the Discomfort Before, During, or After a
Meditation section on page 89.)

80

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Ten Steps to Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources
Step 1: Provide an Overview
In Chapter 5, I described how we might introduce ego state therapy to clients. When it’s time for the
client to mobilize Healing Circle Resources, we explain it in detail with something like this:
When we talked about how ego state therapy can help heal wounded child parts, I’d mentioned
that we start the process by mobilizing a team of Resource parts of self. We call it mobilizing
Resources because they’re already a part of you. We’re just going to find them. Once you’ve
connected to them, they’ll be available to help you manage painful emotions between sessions
and help heal your wounded parts in session. First, an overview.
I’ll guide you to mobilize three Resources—a Nurturing Adult Self, a Protective Adult Self, and a
Spiritual Core Self. Your Nurturing Adult Self is a state of mind that can competently nurture a
loved one. Your Protective Adult Self is a state of mind that can competently protect a loved one.
This is your most mature, adult, caregiver self, expressed in these two roles. These parts of you are
not superhuman. They have needs. They’re imperfect and vulnerable and can experience the full
range of emotions, but they can meet their own needs, manage their vulnerabilities, and regulate
their own emotions. They can learn and grow from their mistakes and continue to mature over
time. These skills, and others, make them competent caregivers.
Now let’s talk about the Spiritual Core Self. This is the core of your being. Some people call it the
soul. It’s a part of you that’s not woundable. It may have existed before the body arrived and may
exist after the body dies. It’s a part of self you may have contacted during meditation, prayer, or
yoga, a peak spiritual experience, a transcendent near-death experience, or a connection you made
with nature. It’s a state of mind associated with a sense of interconnectedness to all beings, a sense
of completeness and wholeness, a sense of safety and invulnerability, no ego, no struggles, no desires
or aversions, unconditional, effortless acceptance, loving kindness and compassion, timeless, cosmic
wisdom and understanding. It’s not just a feel-good state of mind—it’s a transcendent state of mind.
I’ll be guiding you in three meditations to mobilize your Nurturing Adult, Protective Adult, and
Spiritual Core Self. Each meditation will be anchored with a personal experience, like an experience
of being a competent nurturer or protector, or having a peak spiritual experience. After each
meditation, we’ll use alternating bilateral stimulation to strengthen the Resource. Then we’ll bring
all three Resources together and strengthen them as a team. Later, I’ll guide your Resource team
to help your wounded child parts heal. Any questions?
Most clients will be intrigued by this approach, but some will have an aversive reaction. Most aversions
can be remedied quickly with helpful information. (See the Common Resourcing Concerns section on
page 96 for suggestions on how to address the most common concerns.) When aversions are not easily
cleared, we should help the client mobilize Provisional Resources instead. (See Chapter 7.)
Sometimes ritual abuse survivors are triggered by the word circle in Healing Circle Resources. Using
this word is not necessary. Notice in my description above that the word circle is not used. We can be
flexible with our terminology and language so all clients get the benefits of a loving Resource team.

Step 2: Get an Anchor for the NAS and PAS Meditations
We invite the client to discuss her favorite competent caregiving experiences so we can find one or
two to use as anchors for the NAS and PAS meditations. We say something like:
First let’s look for experiences we can use to anchor your Nurturing Adult Self and Protective Adult
Self meditations. This would be times you enjoyed being a nurturer and protector, when your

Chapter 6: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

81

competent caregiver skills were naturally, effortlessly, and appropriately applied, at the same time,
while in your most mature, adult state of mind. Can you think of a time you were an especially
good nurturer?
We discuss this until the client can name a positive, caregiving experience that can anchor the NAS
meditation. Then we ask about experiences as a protector.
Can you think of a time you were an especially good protector?
We discuss this until the client can name an experience of being competently protective, that can
anchor the PAS meditation. When clients struggle to recall or articulate experiences being nurturing
and protective, we offer helpful guidance and support. (See Problems Discussing Personal Caregiving
Experiences section on page 86.)
Once a client has described competent caregiving experiences, we give her the list of twenty-four skills
and traits (page 77) and ask the following:
Look at this list of twenty-four caregiver skills and traits. Are there any skills on the list you think you
do not (or did not) have when you nurture and protect (or nurtured and protected) loved one ?
If doubts about individual skills or traits are revealed, we help address each one. (See the Common
Concerns about Skills and Traits section on page 87.) Once the client can endorse all twenty-four skills
from a personal experience of nurturing and protecting a living being, we have the anchors needed
for the NAS and PAS meditations.

Step 3: Get an Anchor for the SCS Meditation
We invite the client to discuss peak spiritual experiences that align with a list of ten transcendent
qualities (page 78). We might say something like this:
Let’s look for an experience we can use to anchor your Spiritual Core Self meditation. Have you
ever had a peak spiritual experience—maybe during meditation, prayer, yoga, or a time out in
nature—a time you experienced all the qualities on this list? (We give client the list.)
Most clients can name some kind of peak spiritual experience. We encourage a full description. We’re
not just looking for a feel-good experience like enjoying time on the beach but a spiritually meaningful,
transcendent experience, like a sense of connection to all beings. Once a client describes such an
experience, it can serve as an anchor when we read an SCS meditation. If a client has had no such
experience, we read the mediation without the benefit of an anchor.

Step 4: Alert the Client to Possible Processing Blocks
One important aspect of vetting the Resources is giving space for fears and concerns to arise before,
during, and after each meditation. The body can signal a concern with a discomfort like a headache,
stomachache, butterflies, dissociation, distracting thoughts, numbness, or sleepiness. Since we don’t
want to miss this, we prepare clients in advance to tell us about such discomforts. We might say
something like this:
Sometimes while mobilizing Resources, concerns or fears about the processing will arise. Unconscious
fears can be expressed by the sudden arrival of a body disturbance such as unpleasant sensations,
dissociation, distracting thoughts, numbness, or sleepiness. That may or may not happen to you, but
if it does, please tell me about it even if it doesn’t seem important. You’re welcome to have concerns
or fears—just let me know if they come up so we can address them right away. Okay?
If a client reports a discomfort arising just before, during, or after a meditation, we lean in to find out
what it’s about. (See the Discomfort Before, During, or After a Meditation section on page 89.) It could

82

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

be something simple like a doubt or a question that’s remedied quickly with helpful information. It
could be a wounded part who’s triggered and in need of lots of loving care. Either way, we provide
what’s needed and then return to mobilizing the Resource team. Fully handling fears and concerns is
an important part of the Resource vetting process.

Step 5: Select a Resource to Mobilize
We invite the client to select the Resource which will be the easiest to mobilize. That’s because there
could be a complication mobilizing the second or third Resource. If one is already mobilized, we can use
it to help clear those blocks. We ask a simple question.
Which Resource would be the easiest to mobilize first, the Nurturing Adult Self, Protective Adult
Self, or Spiritual Core Self?
When we have an answer, we mobilize that Resource.

Step 6: Mobilize the First Resource: Nurturing Adult Self
A client could select any one of the three Resources to go first, but for the purposes of demonstrating
how the process flows and providing the meditation scripts, I’m starting with the NAS meditation.
We mobilize the NAS with a two-phase guided interactive meditation. We read the meditation with a
soft voice and an open, loving heart. We start by saying:
We’re going to mobilize your Nurturing Adult Self in two phases. I’ll guide you through the meditation
once. If your response is positive, I’ll guide you through it again, using alternating bilateral
stimulation to let the positive feeling strengthen all the way. But first, when you think about beginning
the meditation to mobilize your Nurturing Adult Self, what do you notice in your body?
If the body feels uncomfortable at this point, or any other point in the processing, we find and address
all concerns. (See Discomfort Before, During, or After a Meditation section on page 89.) When the
body feels clear, we start phase 1 with:
Think about
experience of being a competent nurturer ___ as I name each skill on the list. Nod
or say “yes” with each skill you know you possess. Stop me if I name a skill you believe you don’t
have, if you have any questions, or if you feel any discomfort that suggests a fear or concern is
arising. Close your eyes if that’s comfortable, and take a deep breath. Now get in touch with the
following skills and traits, which you already have, including your ability to be empathic…
compassionate… understanding… accepting… patient… nurturing… warm… open… able to attune…
good at listening… good with boundaries… reliable… trustworthy… confident… respectful…
appropriately responsible… problem solver… action taker… decision maker… logical… strong…
courageous… protective… and grounded. Now bring all these skills together into a single sense of
self, your Nurturing Adult Self, and when you’re ready, tell me what you notice.
If the client does not give a clear indication of how the body feels, we ask ”How does that feel in the
body?” If the body feels good, we start the second phase with ABS on. It goes like this:
Now, I’m going to apply alternating bilateral stimulation as I read the list again. Again, think about
. experience of being a competent nurturer ___ as I name each skill on the list. You don’t have to
nod or say “yes” this time. Just listen to each item and let each strengthen. Stop me if I name a
skill you believe you don’t have, if you have any questions, or if you feel any discomfort that
suggests a fear or concern is arising. Now get in touch with the following skills and traits, which
you already have, including your ability to be empathic… compassionate… understanding…
accepting… patient… nurturing… warm… open… able to attune… good at listening… good with

Chapter 6: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

83

boundaries… reliable… trustworthy… confident… respectful… appropriately responsible… problem
solver… action taker… decision maker… logical… strong… courageous… protective… and grounded.
Now bring all these skills together into a single sense of self, your Nurturing Adult Self, and tell me
when it’s strengthened all the way.
Once it’s strengthened all the way, we turn off ABS and gather the following information.
What do you notice? How does that feel in the body?
Do you get a mental picture of your Nurturing Adult Self?
Clients typically report a sense of calm and confidence. The mental picture (or felt sense) of the NAS
should be of the client’s ordinary adult self, nurturing her cherished loved one. If it doesn’t, we’ll have
to follow up. (Ways to follow up are discussed in the Problematic Mental Picture of the Nurturing or
Protective Adult Self section on page 92.)

Step 7: Select the Next Resource to Mobilize
Once the first Resource has been successfully mobilized, we invite the client to identify the easiest Resource
to mobilize, of the remaining two. We ask something like:
Which Resource would be easiest to mobilize next, the Protective Adult Self or Spiritual Core Self?
When we have an answer, we mobilize that Resource.

Step 8: Mobilize the Second Resource: Protective Adult Self
A client could select any one of the remaining two Resources to mobilize next, but for the purposes of
demonstrating how the process flows and providing the meditation scripts, I’m describing the PAS
meditation second.
We mobilize the PAS the same way we mobilized the NAS. The only difference is we reverse the order
of the skill list, but both lists end with the word grounded. We read each meditation with a soft voice
and an open, loving heart. We start by saying:
We’re going to mobilize your Protective Adult Self the same way we mobilized your Nurturing
Adult Self. But first, when you think about beginning the meditation to mobilize your Protective
Adult Self, what do you notice in your body?
If the body feels uncomfortable at this point, or any other point in the processing, we find and address
all concerns. (See Discomfort Before, During, or After a Meditation section on page 89.) But if the
body feels clear, we start phase 1 with:
Think about
experience of being a competent protector ___ as I name each skill on the list. Nod
or say “yes” with each skill you know you possess. Stop me if I name a skill you believe you don’t
have, if you have any questions, or if you feel any discomfort that suggests a fear or concern is
arising. Close your eyes if that’s comfortable, and take a deep breath. Now get in touch with the
following skills and traits, which you already have, including your ability to be protective…
courageous… strong… logical… decision maker… action taker… problem solver… appropriately
responsible… respectful… confident… trustworthy… reliable… good with boundaries… good at
listening… able to attune… warm… open… nurturing… patient… accepting… understanding…
compassionate… empathic… and grounded (or centered). Now bring all these skills together into
a single sense of self, your Protective Adult Self, and when you’re ready, tell me what you notice.
If the client does not give a clear indication of how the body feels, we ask ”How does that feel in the
body?” If the body feels good, we start the second phase with ABS on. It goes like this:

84

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Now I’m going to apply alternating bilateral stimulation as I read the list again. Again, think about
. experience of being a competent protector___ as I name each skill on the list. You don’t have to
nod or say “yes” this time. Just listen to each item and let each strengthen. Stop me if I name a
skill you believe you don’t have, if you have any questions, or if you feel any discomfort that
suggests a fear or concern is arising. Now get in touch with the following skills and traits, which
you already have, including your ability to be protective… courageous… strong… logical… decision
maker… action taker… problem solver… appropriately responsible… respectful… confident…
trustworthy… reliable… good with boundaries… good at listening… able to attune… warm… open…
nurturing… patient… accepting… under-standing… compassionate… empathic… and grounded (or
centered). Now bring all these skills together into a single sense of self, your Protective Adult Self,
and let it get as strong as it wants to. Tell me when it’s strengthened all the way.
Once it’s strengthened all the way, we turn off ABS and gather the following information.
What do you notice? How does that feel in the body?
Do you get a mental picture of your Protective Adult Self?
Clients typically report a sense of calm and confidence. The mental picture (or felt sense) of the PAS
should appear as the client’s ordinary adult self, protecting her cherished loved one. If it doesn’t, we’ll
have to follow up. (Ways to follow up are discussed in the Problematic Mental Picture of the Nurturing
or Protective Adult Self section on page 92.)
Once the second Resource has been successfully mobilized, we mobilize the third Resource.

Step 9: Mobilize the Third Resource: Spiritual Core Self
Any one of the three Resources could be left to mobilize, but for the purposes of demonstrating the
process flow and providing each script, I’m describing the SCS meditation last. I’m demonstrating this
step with the Regular Spiritual Core Self meditation, but any of the four SCS meditations (from page
79) could be used here. We start by asking:
When you think about beginning the meditation to connect to your (Spiritual) Core Self, what do
you notice in your body?
If the body is clear, we read a meditation with a soft voice and an open, loving heart without ABS.
Think about transcendent experience . Close your eyes (if that’s comfortable), and take a deep
breath. Now get in touch with the center of your being… that place within you that is quiet…
peaceful… and still. In this place… it’s possible you’ll connect to your spiritual core. Your spiritual
core has been with you from the beginning… it’s the essence of who you are… your core of
goodness. Pure… resilient… and whole. Your body knows exactly how to connect you. Notice as
you connect to this part of you, and when you’re ready, tell me what you notice.
If the client does not give a clear indication of how the body feels, we ask ”How does that feel in the
body?” If the body feels good, we turn on ABS and say:
Notice that good feeling. Let it get as strong as it wants to. Tell me when it’s strengthened all the
way. (If helpful, we reread the mediation again while it’s strengthening.)
Once it’s strengthened all the way, we turn off ABS and gather the following information.
What do you notice? How does that feel in the body?
Do you get a mental picture of your Spiritual Core Self?
Clients typically report a sense of calm and confidence. The mental picture (or felt sense) of the SCS
should appear as some sort of light or energy such as a ball or column of light, a luminous body, a ray

Chapter 6: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

85

of sunlight, or a wave of loving energy. This description is a sure sign of a connection to a Spiritual Core
Self. When clients report a different type of mental picture, we’ll have to follow up. (Ways to follow-up
are discussed in the Problematic Mental Picture of the Spiritual Core Self section on page 92.)

Step 10: Bring Together the Healing Circle Resource Team
Once a client has connected to each individual Resource, we bring them together to form a team. We
ask something like:
What happens when you bring all the Resources together? How does it feel in the body?
If it feels good, we ask:
Does it feel like they can work together as a team?
If they can work as a team, we turn on ABS to strengthen the team.
Take as long as you need to let that good feeling strengthen. Tell me when it’s strengthened all
the way.
Once it’s strengthened all the way, we turn ABS off and say something like:
Notice you now have a team of Resources to provide comfort, reassurance, and soothing to help
wounded child parts heal.
If bringing the Resource team together suddenly brings up tension, discomfort, or sleepiness, there’s
a concern to identify and clear. (For suggestions about how to find and address concerns go to the
Discomfort Bringing the Resources Together as a Team section on page 94.)

Pairing the Resource Team with a Scent, Object, or Music
When wounded parts get triggered, clients can fall into a very dark and fearful mood. Connecting
wounded parts to loving Resources can lift the dark mood. Paradoxically, it can be harder to access the
Resources when wounded parts are overwhelmed. To remedy this, we can prepare clients in advance
to connect to Resources quickly and easily when they’re triggered. We do this by pairing the Resources
with something like a scent, object, or sound. Using that something when wounded parts are triggered
can help bring the Resources forward.
▪ Pair with a Scent: The Resources can be paired with anything scented like a cologne, perfume,
aftershave, hand lotion, lip balm, essential oil, or spice. (I’m especially fond of the smell of ground
cloves.) Ideally the scent would come from something that’s easy to carry around, so it can be accessed
quickly when needed. A scent may be the most powerful option because the olfactory nerves travel
directly to the frontal lobe where the Resource team resides.
▪ Pair with an Object: The Resources can be paired with an object like a rock, coin, or charm that
fits in a pocket. It could be an item of jewelry like a bracelet or ring. It could be a textile like a
sweater, scarf, or pillow. It could be a picture like a drawing, collage, or photo. Ideally, it’s something
clients can access easily when triggered. Pictures can be stored and retrieved on a smartphone.
▪ Pair with a Sound: The Resources can be paired with a recorded sound. This could be something
simple like a meow, doorbell, or chime. It could be an inspirational piece of music like “Bridge Over
Troubled Water” by Simon and Garfunkel, “Let It Be” by The Beatles, “Don’t Stop Thinking about
Tomorrow” by Fleetwood Mac, “How Could Anyone” by Shaina Noll, or “I Am Light” by India Arie.
The recorded sound or music and can be stored and retrieved on a smartphone.

86

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

We do this pairing when the client is feeling a positive connection to the Resources—ideally right after
we’ve mobilized the team. We invite the client to notice that good feeling at the same time they smell
a scent, look at or hold an object, or listen to a sound. Resources can be paired with more than one
thing, like a scent and music. We apply ABS to strengthen the pairing as much as possible.
Once they’ve been paired, we can test it when the client is distressed to see if the scent, object, or
sound brings the Resources front and center again. If it doesn’t work, we can use ABS to strengthen
the pairing even more or try a different scent, object, or sound.

Resourcing Complications and Processing Blocks
Some clients can easily mobilize Healing Circle Resources within one to three sessions, but others will
find it challenging. It will be easier for those with (1) an experience of feeling cherished in childhood
by at least one loving, attuned attachment figure, and/or (2) an experience of being a loving, attuned
attachment figure for at least one cherished loved one in adulthood. Clients with one or both of these
experiences will have the fewest processing complications. Any fears or concerns that arise can usually
be overcome quickly by clearing misunderstandings or providing missing information.
For clients without one or more of these caregiving experiences, simply providing missing information
may not be enough, especially those with many highly defended wounded parts. These parts will need
to be stabilized to fully handle their concerns, but stabilizing wounded parts requires a Resource team.
We can help clients mobilize Provisional Resources with a faster, easier protocol. They are “provisional”
in the sense that they’ll be a Resource Team provisionally until Healing Circle Resources can be mobilized
later. Steps for mobilizing Provisional Resources are described in Chapter 7. Steps for stabilizing wounded
parts are described in Chapters 10–14. Once enough wounded parts have been stabilized with the help
of Provisional Resources, the client will be ready to mobilize Healing Circle Resources.

Problems Discussing Personal Caregiving Experiences
Several problems can arise when we invite clients to discuss meaningful experiences of nurturing and
protecting loved ones. For example, clients might recall childhood caregiving experiences, they might
overfocus on caregiving incompetencies, they might overlook competent caregiving experiences, they
might be too afraid to talk about competent caregiving experiences, or they might have an aversion
to caregiving. Young clients may have no caregiving experiences to recall. I offer remedies for each of
these below. If these remedies fail to help, the client may not be ready to mobilize Healing Circle
Resources and may need to mobilize Provisional Resources first.
▪ Client Recalls Childhood Caregiving Experiences: Chrissy recalled that at age twelve, she often
cared for her younger brothers while their mother was passed out drunk. Memories of childhood
caregiving like this are not good anchors for connecting to adult Resources for three reasons: (1)
they’re associated with many unmet developmental needs, (2) children resent being trapped in a
stressful caregiver role, and (3), a child who sees inadequate caregiving modeled by parents may
fail to care for neglected siblings well. When clients offer up childhood stories like this as anchors
for an NAS or PAS meditation, we must redirect them to their enjoyable experiences of competent
adulthood caregiving.
▪ Client Overfocuses on Caregiving Incompetencies: A discussion of caregiving abilities reminds
some clients of their caregiving failures. A client who sometimes gets angry, frustrated, or impatient
with a loved one might label this undesirable behavior as proof she has no NAS or PAS to mobilize.
In these regrettable caregiving moments, a wounded part is triggered. To prove this, we can ask,
“When you behaved that way, were you in your most adult self?” Most will answer “no.” Wounded
parts can be disrespectful, but a true NAS or PAS cannot. For the purposes of mobilizing an NAS

Chapter 6: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

87

and PAS, we can disregard all moments of unskillful caregiving coming from wounded parts. A
client should focus only on experiences of competently caring for a loved one while in her most
adult self. If this is not enough, it may help to focus on recently cultivated caregiving skills (e.g., only
as a grandparent), very selective caregiving skills (e.g., only as a gardener), or a single positive
caregiving experience (e.g., rescuing an injured stray dog).
▪ Client Overlooks Competent Caregiving Experiences: Clients who are not currently in a parenting
role may have difficulty recognizing or recalling caregiver experiences. We can remind them of
experiences they may be overlooking. For example, caregiving can happen in many contexts besides
parenting, like babysitting, caring for a pet, tending a garden, teaching school, mentoring, being a
camp counselor, or working as a healthcare professional. Clients who are not currently in such a
relationship can be reminded of caregiving relationships from their past.
▪ Client Is Afraid to Talk about Competent Caregiving Experiences: Clients may be reluctant to
talk about their caregiving skills and strengths when wounded parts believe that saying positive
things about self is bragging, which should be punished. We can clear this misunderstanding by
explaining that recognition and acknowledgement of healthy skills and traits is not the same as
self-absorbed, egotistical bragging, calculated to put others down. Wounded parts who are not
reassured by this may need to be stabilized first with help from Provisional Resources.
▪ Client Has an Aversion to Caregiving: Clients who have lived their whole life without caregiving
responsibilities or actively avoiding caregiving responsibilities, may have no such experiences to
discuss. We can encourage them to experiment with new caregiving experiences by adopting a pet,
babysitting, or becoming a tutor, mentor, or Big Brother/Sister. If wounded parts are very fearful of
this experiment, they may need to be stabilized first with help from Provisional Resources. Once the
client has had caregiving experiences she feels good about, she should be able to mobilize an NAS
and PAS.
▪ Young Client Has Had No Caregiving Experiences: Some young clients have not yet had personal
caregiving experience meaningful enough to anchor an NAS or PAS meditation, but they may be
able to imagine themselves caregiving. We can ask something like “Can you picture yourself caring
for a sick puppy? How does that feel?” They may be able to imagine themselves as a future
caregiver. We can ask something like “Picture yourself in the future, holding your first baby in your
arms. How does that feel?” If imagining this feels good in the body, it may be sufficient to serve as
an adequate anchor. If not, we can encourage clients to seek out a real-life caregiving experience
like adopting a pet, babysitting, or becoming a tutor, mentor, or Big Brother/Sister. Once they’ve
had enough caregiving successes, they will have an anchor for the meditations.

Common Concerns about Skills and Traits
Clients frequently have questions and concerns about the list of skills and traits. The most common
concerns are about the definition of a skill, failures embodying a skill, doubts about a skill, or how to
endorse a skill that was not applied during the anchoring experience. I’ll explore each of these.
▪ Definition of a Skill: Even though the list of skills includes definitions, some clients will overlay
their own private definition of a skill with a negative connotation. For example, a client who was
burdened as a child with the job of nurturing an emotionally needy parent might equate “being
nurturing” with “being burdened.” A client who grew up with hypervigilant parents might equate
“protective” with “overprotective.” A client who grew up with an intrusive mother might equate
“open” with “exposed.” When a client has an aversive reaction to a skill, we ask for their definition.
“What does _______ mean to you?” Once we understand what they think it means, we can empathize
with their concern and then clarify what the word is supposed to mean. Any word of concern can

88

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

be prefaced during the meditation with the word “appropriately.” For example, it may be easier for
clients to endorse being appropriately nurturing, appropriately protective, or appropriately open.
▪ Failures Embodying a Skill: Clearly, we cannot embody all these skills all the time, but if a skill has
been applied once, it can be applied again in the future. The caregiving experience clients name to
anchor a mediation draws a box around moments of competently embodying these skills. We want
clients to match the list specifically to that adult self caregiving experience. If their attention wanders
off, we gently bring it back. These two sample scripts show how we can do this.
Example 1
Therapist:

Jason, take a look at this list of twenty-four caregiver skills and traits. Are
there any skills on the list you think you do not have when you nurture and
protect your dogs?
I’m not good with boundaries.
Tell me about that.
I have the hardest time setting boundaries with my autistic son. I’m not very
effective with him, and I get very frustrated.
I understand. I expect this is a challenge for all parents of autistic children.
There are no simple answers for that. But bring your attention back to caring
for your dogs. Can you set and maintain good boundaries with your dogs?
Yes. I can do that with my dogs.
Then clearly you have this skill. If you can apply it with your dogs, you can
apply it again in the future.
You’re right. I can also set boundaries with employees at my restaurant.

89

▪ Doubts about a Skill: Some clients doubt their ability to embody a skill. For example, a client isn’t
sure she’s good with boundaries because she lets her toddler grandson snack as much as he wants.
To remedy this, we ask questions calibrated to get her to see she can set boundaries with him. “If
your grandson asked for ice cream just before dinner, would you give it to him?” “Would you let
him play in the street?” “Would you let him hurt your dog?” If the client still doubts she has this
skill, she may need to select a different anchoring experience.
▪ Skill Missing from the Anchoring Experience: A caregiving experience that’s very broad, like
nurturing and protecting nursing home patients, is likely to touch on all skills and traits in the list,
but a narrow experience may miss some. For example, consider the client who tutors grade school
children in an after-school program. We ask, “Are there any skills on the list you think you do not
have when you tutor the children?” She says courageous because the children are not in eminent
danger and courage is not required. To address this, we ask a hypothetical question like “If the
school were to catch fire, would you have the courage to get the children to safety?” If she can
endorse this hypothetical, she has the skill. Alternatively, we can ask about any experiences she’s
had embodying that skill. For example, we can ask, “Can you think of any time in your life you have
been courageous?” If the client can name a good example, even if it’s not related to her anchoring
experience, she can endorse the skill.

Discomfort Before, During, or After a Meditation
If the body suddenly feels tense, uncomfortable, or sleepy before, during, or after a meditation, we must
investigate. Symptoms like this could come from something benign like anticipatory anxiety or sleep
deprivation, but they’re often a sign of unexpressed fears or concerns.
Our first challenge is to determine why a symptom is present. There are several ways to do that. Here
are some helpful questions. If we don’t get a meaningful answer from one, we ask another.
 If the

sleepiness

could talk, what would it say?

 Is there a wounded part connected to this
 Why does this

headache

sleepiness

?

need to be here right now? What problem does it solve?

 If you were to successfully mobilize your
NAS / PAS / SCS today, what would you lose, or what
bad thing would happen? Would there be a downside to that?
 If your childhood parents were sitting here right now, commenting on your interest in mobilizing
a
NAS / PAS / SCS today, what would they say to you?
Sometimes it helps to name concerns we think might be triggered. For example, we could ask “If you
were to successfully mobilize a Nurturing Adult Self, would that be the same as being disloyal to your
mother? Abandoning your mother? Discounting her positive qualities?” Very often just listing several
wrong items helps a client to articulate her concern. (See Chapter 18 for more suggestions about how
to elicit hidden concerns.)
Once concerns are revealed, we address them. They’re usually connected to missing information or
simple misunderstandings that are easily remedied. (For scripted remedies, see the Common Resourcing
Concerns section on page 96.) Concerns can be held by the adult self or wounded parts. When they’re
held by wounded parts, we talk to them directly. We listen carefully to their worries. Once we understand,
we provide empathic validations with the information and reassurance they need.
If a wounded part is very stuck in the past, a simple informational remedy may not be enough. She may
also need to be fully stabilized with the interventions in Chapters 10–14. This additional work requires
at least one Resource. If one or two Healing Circle Resources have already been mobilized, they can be
engaged for this. Otherwise, we can use Provisional Resources.

90

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Once the informational remedy or stabilization remedy has been applied, we verify all concerns have
been fully addressed.
▪ Verifying an adult-self concern is addressed: If the concern was held only by the adult self, we ask
the client “When you think about mobilizing your NAS / PAS / SCS now, what do you notice in
your body?” If the body is clear, we continue with the protocol from wherever we left off. If not, we
look for additional concerns to address.
▪ Verifying a wounded part’s concern is addressed: If the concern was held by wounded parts, we ask
them, “Are you comfortable with the idea of mobilizing a NAS / PAS / SCS now?” If the discomfort
persists, we look for additional concerns to address. When they can answer “yes,” we ask the adult
self, “When you think about mobilizing your NAS / PAS / SCS now, what do you notice in your
body?” If the body is clear, we continue with the protocol from wherever we left off. If not, we look
for additional concerns to address.
If a meditation discomfort seems intractable, we explore these three possibilities. (1) The discomfort is
connected to a here-and-now issue, like sleep deprivation or morning sickness, not a blocking concern.
(2) The experience selected to anchor the meditation could be inadequate. If so, we find an alternative
anchor. And (3) the client may not yet be stable enough to develop Healing Circle Resources, in which
case we help her mobilize Provisional Resources. We can return to this protocol later, after many
wounded parts have been stabilized.

Problematic Mental Picture of the Nurturing or Protective Adult Self
We want clients’ mental picture of their NAS and PAS to correspond directly to their ordinary experience
of being a competent caregiver, because it’s more believable to wounded parts. Sometimes clients
surprise us with a different mental picture. For example, we’d want Alice (from page 75) to see her NAS
as herself caring for her nephew, Nathan. Instead she might picture her NAS as a mama bear or a mother
earth goddess. Or she might picture her NAS as her ordinary self with some extraordinary features—
like a superhero cape or angel wings. Because these extra features are not part of real life, an NAS pictured
this way may be less believable. Wounded parts will be healed by a loving connection to an NAS and
PAS with ordinary caregiving skills. Supernatural powers are not required and could be a distraction.
To address this, we simply say “I’d like you to hold a picture of your NAS / PAS as not ordinary self .
side by side with a picture of you as you ordinarily care for cherished loved one . Which image feels
more real, believable, and comfortable to wounded parts?” For example, “Alice, I’d like you to hold the
picture of your Nurturing Adult Self as a Mama bear side by side with a picture of you as you ordinarily
care for Nathan. Which image feels more real, believable, and comfortable to wounded parts?” Most
of the time, clients realize the ordinary-self picture feels best.
If the ordinary-self mental picture does not feel best, we must investigate to find out why. We start by
checking on the choice of anchoring experience. “The experience you selected to anchor the NAS /
PAS meditation was
. Does that still seem like a good choice?” If not, we look for a better
one and then redo the meditation with the new anchor.
If the anchor is good, there may be a concern to identify and clear. We can ask, “If you were to picture
your NAS / PAS as yourself, as you ordinarily care for cherished loved one , is there something
you lose or something bad thing that would happen? Would there be a downside to that?” Once
concerns are revealed, we address them. They’re usually connected to missing information or simple
misunderstandings that are easily remedied. (For scripted remedies, see the Common Resourcing Concerns
section on page 96.)
Concerns can be held by the adult self or wounded parts. When they’re held by wounded parts, we
talk to them directly. We listen carefully to their worries. Once we understand, we provide empathic
validations with the information and reassurance that they need. If a wounded part is very stuck in the
past, a simple informational remedy may not be enough. She may also need to be fully stabilized with
the interventions in Chapters 10–14. This additional work requires at least one Resource. If one or two
Healing Circle Resources have already been mobilized, they can be engaged for this. Otherwise, we
can use Provisional Resources.
Once the informational remedy or stabilization remedy has been applied, we verify the adult self and/or
wounded part is okay with the NAS or PAS looking like ordinary self.
 Verifying adult-self concern is addressed: If the concern was held only by the adult self, we ask the
client “How does the NAS / PAS appear now?” If it appears as ordinary self, we continue with
the protocol from wherever we left off. If not, we look for additional concerns to address.

Chapter 6: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

93

 Verifying a wounded part’s concern is addressed: If the concern was held by wounded parts, we
ask them, ““Would you be okay picturing your NAS / PAS as yourself as you ordinarily care for
cherished loved one ?” If not, we look for additional concerns to address. But if it’s okay, we ask the
adult self “How does the NAS / PAS appear now?” If it appears as ordinary self, we continue with
the protocol from wherever we left off. If not, we look for additional concerns to address.
Sometimes clients report no mental picture or a fuzzy mental picture of the NAS or PAS. I explore this in
detail in the Difficulty Holding a Clear Mental Picture of a Resource or the Team section on page 94.

Problematic Mental Picture of the Spiritual Core Self
Clients usually picture their SCS as some sort of light or energy such as a ball or column of light, a
luminous body, a ray of sunlight, or a wave of loving energy. This description is a sure sign of a connection
to a (Spiritual) Core Self. Clients who report a different type of mental picture may need a nudge in the
right direction. We say “As you picture yourself
doing yoga / meditating / praying , erase everything
from the picture but your (Spiritual) Core Self. Tell me what’s left.” For example, “As you picture yourself
communing with nature in the forest, erase everything from the picture but your Spiritual Core Self.
Tell me what’s left.” Clients will usually say what’s left is a radiant light or energy—their SCS.
Some clients who disclose a peak spiritual experience within a religious faith may be inclined to picture
their SCS as a higher power like God, Allah, or Jesus. An SCS is not a higher power, rather it’s a part of
self that resonates with love from a higher power. To make this distinction, I might say to the client
something like, “Put your hand on the part of your body that feels God’s love the most.” When the
client covers her heart, we say, “Notice the part of self holding that love in your heart. What does that
part of you look like?” It will probably look like radiant light or energy.
If a client has an aversion to experiencing her SCS as light or energy, we must investigate to find out
why. We start by checking on the choice of anchoring experience. “The experience you selected to
anchor the SCS meditation was
. Does that still seem like a good choice?” If not, we look for
a better one and then redo the meditation with the new anchor.
If the anchor is good, there may be a concern to identify and clear. To find that concern, we can ask,
“If you were to picture your (Spiritual) Core Self as light or energy, is there something you’d lose or
something bad thing that would happen? Would there be a downside to that?” Once concerns are
revealed we address them. They’re usually connected to missing information or misunderstandings
that are easily remedied. (For scripted remedies, see the Common Resourcing Concerns section on
page 96.)
Concerns can be held by the adult self or wounded parts. When they’re held by wounded parts, we
talk to them directly. We listen carefully to their worries. Once we understand, we provide empathic
validations with the information and reassurance that they need. If a wounded part is very stuck in the
past, a simple informational remedy may not be enough. She may also need to be fully stabilized with
the interventions in Chapters 10–14. This additional work requires at least one Resource. If one or two
Healing Circle Resources have already been mobilized, they can be engaged for this. Otherwise, we
can use Provisional Resources.
Once the informational remedy or stabilization remedy has been applied, we verify the adult self and/or
wounded part is okay with the SCS looking like ordinary self.
▪ Verifying the adult-self concern is addressed: If the concern was held only by the adult self, we ask
the client “How does the SCS appear now?” If it appears as light or energy, we continue with the
protocol from wherever we left off. If not, we look for additional concerns to address.

94

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

▪ Verifying a wounded part’s concern is addressed: If the concern was held by wounded parts, we
ask them, “Would you be okay picturing your SCS as light or energy now?” If not, we look for
additional concerns to address. But if it’s okay, we ask the adult self “How does the SCS appear
now?” If it appears as ordinary self, we continue with the protocol from wherever we left off. If not,
we look for additional concerns to address.
Sometimes clients report no mental picture or a fuzzy mental picture of the SCS. I explore this in detail
in the Difficulty Holding a Clear Mental Picture of a Resource or the Team section below.

Difficulty Holding a Clear Mental Picture of a Resource or the Team
At the conclusion of each Resource meditation, we invite clients to describe their mental picture. Most
clients easily get a clear mental picture of each Resource or of all three together as a team, but some
will report no mental picture or a fuzzy mental picture. We ask questions to find out why. These three
questions are most likely to help us find the reason. If one does not give us the answer, we ask another.
▪ Are you generally able to visualize things well?
If “no,” the client may be one of those people who are simply unable to visualize things well. To
verify this, we might ask a client if she can picture herself cutting open a lemon. Can she see the
bright yellow rind, the knife slicing through, and the juice ooze out? Those who are unable to
visualize in general will have trouble visualizing Resources (and wounded parts). Fortunately, these
clients are usually able to process well by relying on a felt sense of the Resources (and wounded
parts). As long as a client can get a felt sense of the Resources and that felt sense feels good in the
body, no further intervention is necessary.
▪ It is possible this Resource is not actually robust?
If “yes,” the visualization problem is signaling a problem with the Resource. We ask the client, “The
experience you selected to anchor the NAS / PAS / SCS meditation was
. Does that still
seem like a good choice?” If not, we look for a more helpful anchor and do the mediation again.
This is analogous to a TV with a fuzzy picture because it’s broken. To get a clear picture, we replace
the TV. Because of a weak anchor, the Resource was “broken.” So, we replace the Resource.
▪ Is a wounded part preventing this Resource from being visible (or clearly visible)?
If the client answers “yes,” a wounded part is concerned about something. We start a dialogue with
the part. Then say, “I expect you’re preventing this Resource from being visible (or clearly visible)
for a very good reason. Tell me about that.” If the part cannot easily give us the reason, it may help
to reread the meditation slowly, inviting her to stop us the moment we say the word or phrase that
causes distress. Once we understand the concern, we can address it. (See the Common Resourcing
Concerns section on page 96 for a list of common concerns and remedies.) Once all concerns have
been cleared, the robust Resource should come into focus. This is analogous to a perfect TV with a
picture obscured by a drape of cheesecloth. To get a clear picture, we remove the cloth. The perfect
Resource can come into focus after wounded part concerns are fully handled.

Discomfort Bringing the Resources Together as a Team
Sometimes discomfort or sleepiness arises when a client brings the Resources together to form a team
This can happen when the adult self or wounded parts have misunderstandings or faulty assumptions
about what bringing together a Resource team will mean. If we suspect a wounded part is worried, we
can ask, “Is a wounded part stopping them from coming together?” If so, we ask to speak to the part and
start a welcoming, loving, validating dialogue. We invite the part to tell us what she’s worried about For
example, she may be worried the Resources will assert themselves in ways that upset childhood parents,
resulting in shame or punishment, or that the Resources will assert their power indiscriminately without
concern for consequences, leading to terrifying rejections. Most concerns can be addressed by clearing

Chapter 6: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

95

misunderstandings, filling in missing information, and orienting the part to present time (see Chapter 13).
We can engage the Resources to help us do this. Once we’ve addressed all the part’s concerns, the
Resources should be able to come together as a team. Here’s a sample client-therapist dialogue:

Common Resourcing Concerns
Fears and concerns that block resourcing can originate with either a client’s adult self or wounded parts.
They can be about the process of connecting to Resources or about the outcome of healing. Because
addressing a simple concern may be all that’s needed to clear a resourcing block, we start there. Below
I list concerns that often come up when mobilizing Healing Circle Resources. (Additional guidance and
scripts for clearing misunderstandings can be found in Chapter 11, in the Scripts That Fill In Missing
Information section, on page 170, and in the Scripts That Clear Up Misunderstandings section, on

Chapter 6: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

97

page 172.) If clearing a misunderstanding is not enough to calm a concern held by wounded parts,
we’ll need to apply the stabilization interventions with help from one or two Healing Circle Resources
or Provisional Resources.
ADULT SELF: “I had to meet my own needs in childhood. It sounds like you’re proposing I meet my
own needs again. I don’t want to do that. It’s time for someone else to meet my needs.”
I’d like to tell you about two parts of the brain—the amygdala and prefrontal cortex. The amygdala
gets activated when danger is perceived, even if the danger is not real. The prefrontal cortex—
where maturity and intellect reside—can assess danger and calm the amygdala, but only if they’re
connected. When a child gets her emotional needs met by parents—especially when they soothe
her upsets—a neural pathway forms to connect the amygdala and prefrontal cortex. As this
connection gets bigger and stronger over time, this child matures into an adult who is able to
assess danger and manage painful emotions. If emotional needs are not met well enough in
childhood, this neural architecture does not develop well. A weak connection between the amygdala
and prefrontal cortex means difficultly assessing danger and managing painful emotions. Mobilizing
a Resource team will activate your prefrontal cortex. When your Resources comfort, soothe, and
reassure wounded child parts, it will strengthen a weak connection between the amygdala and
prefrontal cortex. Over time, this neural architecture will change to function more like it would
have, if your needs had been met well from the beginning. How does that sound?
ADULT SELF: “I can’t connect to a Spiritual Core Self, because I don’t believe in God.”
It’s not necessary to believe in God. If you’ve ever experienced these ten qualities while doing
meditation or yoga or while communing with nature, you’ve been in touch with your Spiritual
Core. If you object to the word “spiritual,” just think of it as mobilizing your Core Self.
ADULT SELF: “If I mobilize Resources, I’ll have to act like a mature grown-up all the time. I don’t think
I can do that.”
If you mobilize Resources, you will not have to act like a mature grown-up all the time. Having
Resources is like having a dishwasher. You can let dirty dishes pile up or use your dishwasher. It’s
your choice. Once you’ve mobilized Resources, you can access them for between-session help and
support or not. It’s your choice.
ADULT SELF assumes a skill must be applicable all the time, with everyone, to be endorsable.
A skill does not need to be applied all the time for you to endorse it. If your most adult self has
applied it in the past, even for short periods of time, you can apply it again in the future.
ADULT SELF: “I wasn’t very good at raising my kids. I don’t think I have a Nurturing Adult Self.”
If you’ve ever been appropriately nurturing of your children while in your most adult self, even for
short periods, you have the potential to be nurturing again in the future. For the purposes of
mobilizing a Nurturing Adult Self Resource, it does not matter that you were not good at nurturing
all the time. All that matters is that you can recall times that you were, and those times could be
with someone other than your children.
ADULT SELF: “There’s no point in developing Resources, because they won’t be real.”
The Resources are about real skills held in your real neural networks. Is your love and affection for
. cherished loved one real? If YES: That’s proof that the Resources would be real. If NO: Okay,
then let’s mobilize a Provisional Resource Team.
ADULT SELF: “Fictional Resources would be better at caregiving than I am.”
Would cherished loved one say you’re a competent caregiver when you’re in your most mature
adult self? If YES: If your Resources are who you are as that competent caregiver, wounded parts

98

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

would probably find them more real and more believable than fictional Resources. If NO: Okay,
then let’s mobilize a Provisional Resource Team.
ADULT SELF: “If the Resources help me heal, my abusive parents are off the hook.”
Your parents’ abusive behavior is not excusable. In a perfect world, they’d be held accountable for
their behavior, but that may not happen. Fortunately, your child parts can heal whether your
parents pay for their abusive behavior or not.
ADULT SELF: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll have to end my relationship with ________.”
If you heal, you will not have to end your relationship with _________. You may stay connected, you
may disconnect a bit, or you may disconnect completely. This is your choice to make. You’ll be able
to evaluate each option on a moment-to-moment basis and change your mind at any time. As the
Resources help your wounded parts heal, you’ll gain wisdom that will help you make the best choice.
CHILD PART: “I had to meet my own needs in childhood. It sounds like you want me to meet my own
needs again. I don’t want to do that. It’s time for someone else to meet my needs.”
So, little one, I completely agree. You should not be expected to meet your own needs. The Resources
are healthy, mature, grown-up parts of self. It will be their job to meet your needs so you can relax
and go play.
CHILD PART: “If I admit to having adult skills, I’m bragging. Bragging is bad and will be punished.”
So, little one, bragging is bad only if it involves putting others down to boost one’s ego. There’s
nothing wrong with acknowledging one’s own skills and strengths to express healthy self-confidence,
self-esteem, or self-assurance.
CHILD PART: “It’s my job to protect younger parts. The Resources will make me irrelevant.”
Hi, little one. I understand you’ve made it your mission to protect younger parts. I’m so grateful to
you for doing that. It’s a big job, and you deserve many thanks for your service. If we put together
a nurturing and protective Resource Team, you will not lose your relevance. In fact, if you like, the
Resources will help you heal and grow to meet your full potential so you can be relevant in bigger
and better ways. You will always be important and valuable.
CHILD PART: “The Resources will eventually betray or abandon me.”
So, little one, would your most adult self betray or abandon cherished loved one ? If NO: Then
there’s no way the Resources would betray or abandon you or any other wounded parts. If YES:
Okay, then let’s mobilize a Provisional Resource Team instead.
CHILD PART: “The Resources will smother or engulf me.”
So, little one, would your most adult self smother or engulf cherished loved one ? If NO: Then
there’s no way the Resources would do that to you or any other wounded parts. If YES: Okay, then
let’s mobilize a Provisional Resource Team instead.
CHILD PART: “The Resources will expect me to meet their needs.”
So, little one, would your most adult self expect cherished loved one to meet her needs? If NO:
Then there’s no way the Resources would expect that of you or any other wounded parts. If YES:
Okay, then let’s mobilize a Provisional Resource Team instead.
CHILD PART: “The Resources will impose their agenda on me.”
So, little one, would your most adult self impose an agenda on cherished loved one ? If NO:
Then there’s no way the Resources would do that to you or any other wounded parts. If YES: Okay,
then let’s mobilize a Provisional Resource Team instead.

Chapter 6: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources

99

CHILD PART: “The Resources will expect me to be perfect all the time.”
So, little one, would your most adult self expect cherished loved one to be perfect all the time?
If NO: Then there’s no way the Resources would expect that of you or any other wounded parts. If
YES: Okay, then let’s mobilize a Provisional Resource Team instead.
CHILD PART: “The Resources will be authority figures, so they cannot be trusted.”
So, little one, does cherished loved one believe your most adult self can be trusted? If YES: Then
the Resources would be trustworthy with you and other wounded parts. If NO: Okay, then let’s
mobilize a Provisional Resource Team instead.
CHILD PART: “I’m a troublemaker. The Resources will want to get rid of me.”
So, little one, you’re a treasure. You matter. The Resources will not want to get rid of you. They’ll
want to love you, support you, meet your needs, and help you heal so you can grow to meet your
full potential.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I don’t know who I’ll be.”
So, little one, are you afraid of losing a familiar sense of identity? If YES: I completely understand.
Fortunately, you’ll never be without a sense of identity. While you maintain your old familiar
. anxious / depressed / self-loathing, etc. identity, the Resources would help create a new one
based on feeling safe / self-love, etc. . Anything of value from your old identity will be saved,
and anything you no longer need will fade away.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll be expected to meet everyone else’s needs.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, your most adult self will be better able to set and
maintain clear and appropriate boundaries. That means being able to choose to meet others’ needs
when it’s reasonable and appropriate and choosing not to when it’s burdensome and inappropriate.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll have to be responsible for everything.”
So, little one, the burden of responsibly for navigating life’s challenges falls to your most adult self,
not you. As you and other wounded parts heal, the adult self will be better equipped to face those
challenges and make good choices.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll start setting boundaries and people will reject me.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, your most adult self will be able to set boundaries
more effectively. It’s true that some people might not like the boundaries and might be rejecting
because of it, but healing can also enable the most adult self to distance herself from people who
violate boundaries and surround herself with kind people who respect good boundaries.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll let my guard down, which is dangerous.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, your most adult self will be able to see danger
more clearly and handle it more effectively. She’ll be better able to put her guard up when it’s
necessary and take it down when it’s not.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll be weaker and more vulnerable.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, your most adult self will become stronger and
more resilient. She’ll be better able to set boundaries, confront adversity, solve problems, and deal
with difficult people and challenging situations. Healing will not make you more vulnerable; it will
make you safer.

100

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll have to assert myself all the time.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, your most adult self will become better able
to assert herself in appropriate ways. She’ll be better able to discern when it’s helpful to be assertive
and when it’s not. Healing will not lock her into being assertive all the time.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll have to end my relationship with _________.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, your most adult self will become better able
to evaluate her relationship with _________. She may stay connected, she may disconnect a bit, or
she may disconnect completely. This is her choice to make. She’ll be able to evaluate each option
on a moment-to-moment basis and change her mind at any time. The wisdom she gains as you
and other wounded parts heal will help her make the best choice.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll have to come out of hiding.”
So, little one, sometimes hiding is a good strategy. As you and other wounded parts heal, your most
adult self will become better able to determine when it’s safe to be seen and when it’s best to hide.
CHILD PART: “If I come out of hiding, people will see how inadequate I am and reject me.”
So, little one, you feel inadequate because you’re stuck in the past—in painful experiences of being
judged, belittled, and rejected. As you and other wounded parts heal, your most adult self will feel
more confident and secure in spite of personal shortcomings. While unkind people may continue
to be judgmental and rejecting, this will feel less personal. The most adult self will be better able
to keep rejecting people at a distance and surround herself with those who are kind and accepting.

We aim to put together a Resource team one Resource at a time. Each robust Resource we mobilize
can be called upon to help us work through complications in mobilizing other Resources, until we have
the whole Healing Circle team. Here’s an example of using the NAS to help a wounded part overcome
fears about the PAS revealed at the end of a meditation.

I feel afraid.

Seven.

Does she understand why the idea of protection feels scary to you?
Yeah, I think so.
Very nice. It might help to know there are two kinds of protection. One kind is
prevention—like buckling a child in a car seat, helping her brush her teeth, or
holding her hand while crossing the street.
That’s protection?
Yep. The other of kind is taking action when someone’s in danger—like pulling a
drowning child out of water, tending to a child’s bleeding injury, or rushing a child
to the hospital. When there’s a threat from a dangerous person, protective action
could involve hiding, running, or fighting. Sometimes fighting can be confrontational
and violent, but that is just one very small aspect of protection. But don’t take my
word for it, ask the Nurturing Adult Self. What does she say?
She says you’re right.
Most of the time, good prevention is all that’s needed for good protection. For
example, if your father had patched the hole in the fence to keep the raccoon out,
he would not have had to shoot it. If a girl had been given floaties to wear in the
water, she would not have drowned. If a boy had held his father’s hand crossing
the street, he would not have been hit by a car. What’s it like to think about all this?
I get it.
So, seven-year-old, ask the Nurturing Adult Self if this Protective Adult Self can offer
you protection that’s loving and wise. Tell me what she says.
She says “yes.”
What’s it like to hear that?
I like it.
Good. Now bring all these skills together into a single sense of self, your Protective
Adult Self. When you’re ready, tell me what you notice.
It feels good.

Sample Client-Therapist Dialogue: Mobilizing Healing Circle Resources
The following is a typical client-therapist Resource Development Protocol dialogue. This is a continuation
of ego state therapy with Carla.
Step 1: Provide an Overview
Therapist:

Client:
Therapist:
Client:
Therapist:

So, Carla, last session we talked about how ego state therapy can help heal wounded
child parts. I mentioned that we start the process by mobilizing a team of Resource parts
of self. We call it mobilizing Resources because they’re already a part of you. We’re just
going to find them. Once you’re connected to them, they’ll be available to help you
manage painful emotions between sessions and help heal your wounded parts in session.
Sounds good.
Are you ready to go there today?
Yes.
Great. First an overview. We’re going to mobilize three Resources—a Nurturing Adult
Self, a Protective Adult Self, and a Spiritual Core Self. Your Nurturing Adult Self is a state
of mind that can competently nurture a loved one. A Protective Adult Self is a state of
mind that can competently protect a loved one. This is your most mature adult caregiver
self, expressed in these two roles. These parts of you are not superhuman. They have
needs. They’re imperfect and vulnerable and can experience the full range of emotions,
but they can meet their own needs, manage their vulnerabilities, and regulate their own

102

Client:
Therapist:

Client:
Therapist:

Client:

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

emotions. They can learn and grow from their mistakes and continue to mature over
time. These skills, and others, make them competent caregivers. Would you say you’ve
had some experiences as a competent caregiver?
Yes. Definitely.
I thought so. Now let’s talk about Spiritual Core Self. This is the core of your being. Some
people call it the soul. It’s a part of you that’s not woundable. It may have existed before
the body arrived and may exist after the body dies. It’s a part of self you may have
experienced during meditation, prayer, or yoga, a peak spiritual experience, a
transcendent near-death experience, or a moment out in nature. It’s a state of mind
associated with a sense of interconnectedness to all beings, a sense of completeness
and wholeness, a sense of safety and invulnerability, no ego, no struggles, no desires or
aversions, unconditional, effortless acceptance, loving kindness and compassion,
timeless, cosmic wisdom and understanding. It’s not just a feel-good state of mind—it’s
a transcendent state of mind.
Oh yeah, like how I felt at the end of a yoga retreat I did about twenty years ago.
Good to know. So I’ll be guiding you in three meditations to mobilize your Nurturing
Adult Self, Protective Adult Self, and Spiritual Core. Each one will be anchored with a
personal experience, like an experience of being a competent nurturer or protector, or
having a peak spiritual experience. After each meditation, we’ll use alternating bilateral
stimulation to strengthen the Resource. Then we’ll bring all three Resources together and
strengthen them as a team. Later, I’ll guide your Resource team to help your wounded
child parts heal. Any questions?
No. Sounds good.

Step 2: Get an Anchor for the NAS and PAS Meditations
Therapist:

Client:

Therapist:
Client:

Let’s start by looking for experiences we can use to anchor your Nurturing Adult Self and
Protective Adult Self meditations—times you enjoyed being a nurturer and protector,
when your competent caregiver skills were naturally, effortlessly, and appropriately
applied, at the same time, while in your most mature adult state of mind. Can you think
of a time you were an especially good nurturer?
Yes. When my daughter, Nina, was about five years old, she got into some poison ivy on
a nature walk. She had an allergic reaction. It was pretty bad. She had a rash that was
very itchy. She cried a lot, and it was hard to keep her calm. I did my best to treat her
medically. I knew what she needed, but she kept fighting me. She didn’t like the baths
or the lotion. To change her attitude, I told her she was very lucky because she’d
encountered a magic plant that could impart a divine blessing to special children. It
worked by giving a child a rash. If the rash became itchy it, it was a sign the blessing was
on the way, but they would be bestowed only if the child treated the rash with oatmeal
baths, lotion, and medicine. That would ensure the child would grow up to be smart,
strong, and beautiful. She immediately became more compliant with the treatment. Later,
when the itchiness started to subside, I explained that meant the blessing was nestling
in. I told her the treatment had to continue until the rash was completely gone for the
blessing to become permanent. I brought her crayons and paper and asked her to draw
for me what she wanted her blessing to look like. Once she got hooked by the story, her
anxiety dropped. She went from seeing the rash as an ominous curse to a welcome
blessing. It was healed within a week. Of course, I also read to her and sang to her. I tried
every trick I could think of to keep her calm and comfortable until the rash was gone.
Wow, that’s a great story. You knew just how to meet her needs.
Yes, I think so.

She’s lucky to have you for a mother!

Those are great examples of being protective.

That sounds pretty normal. No parent is going to know what to do in every situation.

book about it, or ask someone for guidance?
Always.
So, are you confident that when you’re confused about what Nina needs, your most
adult can get answers?
Yes, I am. Hmmm. I guess I am confident.
I agree. You also mentioned patience was an issue.
Yeah. Sometimes I get really impatient with her and raise my voice, especially when we

Probably a wounded part.

Yes, I was.

Pairing the Resources with Something
Therapist:

Client:
Therapist:

When you get stressed, connecting to your Resources can have a calming effect, but
that might be difficult to do if you’re overwhelmed. There’s something we can do today
that will make it easier for you to connect to the Resources even if you’re overwhelmed.
Okay. How does that work?
We can pair your Resource Team with a scent, object, or sound. Later, when you smell
the scent, hold or look at the object, or hear the sound, it will bring the Resources
forward. It could be any scent—a perfume, lip balm, even a spice. It could be an object
like a ring, stone, or picture. It could be a sound recorded on your phone like a cat meow,
bell, or song.
Could I do more than one?
Yes, of course! Using a scent may be the most effective because the olfactory nerves
travel directly to the frontal lobe where the Resource Team resides. Is there a scent that
you’re really fond of?
I keep a small bottle of thieves oil in my purse. It’s good at boosting immunity, so I use
it instead of a hand sanitizer.
Do you have some with you now?
Yes. (Retrieves it from her purse)

Yes.
