I will try it.

Chapter 6 Summary
This chapter describes the three DNMS Healing Circle Resources—a Nurturing Adult Self (NAS),
Protective Adult Self (PAS), and Spiritual Core Self (SCS). The NAS and PAS are based on the
client’s personal experiences of being a competent nurturer and protector of a cherished loved
one. Multiple steps to mobilizing each Resource ensures they are fully vetted. The SCS is a part
of self that’s transcendent love. Most clients can describe an experience of embodying this state
of mind, which helps in the vetting process. Once each Resource has been mobilized, we bring
them together to form a team. Because they’re fully vetted, they’re well prepared to meet the
emotional needs of wounded child parts. A substantial portion of this chapter is devoted to
complications that can arise when mobilizing these Resources, and remedies for handling each
complication. This Resource team can play an essential role in stabilizing wounded parts in
preparation for EMDR. Many therapists also use them during EMDR to support them in
delivering cognitive interweaves.

Chapter 7
Mobilizing Provisional Resources

Loving, compassionate inner Resources play an essential role in stabilizing wounded parts. The protocol
described in Chapter 6 helps clients mobilize fully vetted Healing Circle Resources, usually in about
one to three sessions. But clients who are very unstable may take much longer. The longer it takes a
client to mobilize Resources, the longer we delay stabilizing wounded parts. Fortunately, we can usually
help clients mobilize a Provisional Resource team with a faster, easier protocol with a simpler vetting
process. These Resources are considered provisional in the sense that they will help out provisionally
until the client is ready to mobilize a Healing Circle Resource team.
For a worksheet that will guide you through these intervention steps while working with clients, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

Mobilizing Provisional Resources
While Healing Circle Resources are always parts of self, Provisional Resources can be self, other, fictional,
or mystical. They can be drawn from very real, personal experiences or pure imagination. Because
they’re not all equivalent in effectiveness, I’ve given each type of Provisional Resource a one-, two-, or
three-star rating. A three-star rating is the best. Two- and three-star Resources will feel the most real
and believable to wounded parts. That makes them more effective. We want Provisional Resources
who are able to nurture wounded parts with attuned, loving empathy, compassion, and wisdom.
We’ll guide clients to mobilize their best possible team of two or three Resources with as many twoand three-star Resources as possible. We only mobilize one-star Resources when a client cannot
mobilize enough two- and three-star Resources.
We vet each possible Resource with a one or two simple questions. This vetting is not as thorough as
the Healing Circle Resource vetting, but it’s usually good enough for mobilizing Resources able to
stabilize wounded parts.

Caregiver-Self Resource 
A caregiver-self Resource is based on a personal experience of loving and enjoying caring for another
living being who is relatively dependent or needy—like a child, pet, patient, student, elderly person,
plant, or garden—either past or present. The intimacy and duration of such experiences can vary
widely. A client could draw on a long-term parenting experience, like raising a child into adulthood, or
a short-term professional experience, like caring for a cancer patient. Clients who have no such past or
present caregiving experiences may be able to envision themselves in a future caregiving relationship.

114

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

For example, a young woman who wants to become a mother one day may be able to envision herself
forming a loving and protective bond with her future child. A medical student may be able to envision
himself in compassionate, empathic relationships with future patients. Any caregiving experiences that
feel (or felt) joyless or burdensome should be excluded from this process.
We start by looking for one, two, or three caregiver-self Resources. Some clients will not have even
one, but it’s always where we start. We ask, “Who in your life have you been an especially supportive
caregiver for, either past or present?” This begins a discussion. Sometimes clients give a clear, distinct
answer, but often we need to ask many follow-up questions to get a clear understanding.
Once a client has described a competent caregiving experience, we vet the possible Resource with two
questions. For example, Crystal says she’d been a compassionate pediatric nurse for 30 years. We’d ask
her, “Would your patients recommend you as a competent caregiver?” If “yes,” we also ask, “If you’d
been raised by someone who was as good to you as you are to your patients, would that have been a
good thing?” If we get a “yes” to both, we ask, “How does the idea of a pediatric nurse Resource feel
in your body?” If it feels good, we have a three-star, caregiver-self Resource. If the name of this
Resource is not obvious, we ask, “What name would you like to give this Resource?” If the client isn’t
sure, we make suggestions. Crystal might choose to name it Miss Crystal, because that’s what her
patients called her.
Next, we look for another caregiver-self Resource. We ask, “Who else in your life have you been an
especially supportive caregiver for, either past or present?” Crystal says she’s had many cats over the
years. Fluffy, the cat she has now, is her favorite. So, we ask “Would your cats recommend you as a
competent caregiver?” If “yes,” we ask, “If you’d been raised by someone who was as good to you as
you are to your cats, would that have been a good thing?” If we get a “yes” to both, we ask, “How does
the idea of a cat-lover self Resource feel in your body?” If it feels good, we have a Cat-Lover Self
Resource. Crystal might choose the name this Resource Fluffy’s Mama.

Spiritual-Self Resource 
A spiritual-self Resource is based on a personal transcendent, peak spiritual experience—the kind one
might have doing yoga, practicing Tai Chi, praying, during a meditation, having a blissful near-death
experience, or while communing with nature. It can bring a quality of transcendent love, calm, and
reassurance to wounded parts.
Not all clients can readily mobilize a spiritual self, but we invite them to look for it. We ask, “Have you
ever had a transcendent peak spiritual experience that connected you to a spiritual part of self?” This
begins a discussion. We may need to ask many follow-up questions to get a clear picture. If a client
can describe an experience of accessing a spiritual part of self, we vet this as a possible Resource. For
example, Mary says she connects to a peaceful inner core when she meditates by the ocean. To vet this
Resource, we ask, “Does this peaceful spiritual inner core you experience by the ocean embody loving
energy that could be helpful to wounded parts?” If we get a “yes,” we ask, “How does the idea of a peaceful
spiritual inner core Resource feel in your body?” If it feels good, we have a spiritual-self Resource. If the
Resource name is not obvious, we make suggestions to help the client select the best name.

Real-Other Resource (Personal) 
There are several types of real-other Resources. This one is someone the client personally knows or
knew who is or was especially supportive—like a grandparent, aunt, uncle, neighbor, minister, teacher,
or healthcare professional. This Resource will be more compelling if this person was helpful to the
client at a time when she was relatively young, powerless, or needy—for example, when the client was
a child or especially ill.

Chapter 7: Mobilizing Provisional Resources

115

Not all clients have had a relationship like this, but we invite them to look. We ask, “Who in your life
has been an especially supportive caregiver for you, either past or present?” This begins a discussion.
Some clients have had many such relationships; some have had none. We ask follow-up questions to
help narrow down the most supportive, conflict-free relationships. If a client can describe such a
supportive relationship, we vet this possible Resource. For example, Paco recalls as a boy spending
many delightful hours fishing with his grandfather, Tito. His grandfather would tell him stories, teach
him about nature, and emotionally connect to him. They had a great love for each other. To vet this
Resource, we ask, “If you’d been raised by someone who was as good to you as your Tito was to you,
would that have been a good thing?” If we get a “yes,” we ask, “How does the idea of a Tito Resource
feel in your body?” If it feels good, we have a real-other Resource.

Real-Other Resource (Observed) 
This real-other Resource is someone the client personally observed being a good caregiver but who
did not directly care for the client—like a friend’s loving mom, a teacher, or school counselor. Good
caregiving that was observed frequently and up close will be more impactful. For example, a nine-yearold child spent every day after school at a friend’s house observing her friend’s mother being loving,
patient, and kind. Even though she was too shy to personally interact with the mother, she could
imagine receiving that kind of loving care from her.
To identify an observed caregiver, we ask, “Who have you personally observed being an especially
supportive caregiver for someone, either past or present?” This begins a discussion. We may need to
ask many follow-up questions to get a clear picture. If a client has observed such a supportive
relationship, we vet this possible Resource. For example, Elvin recalled a second-grade classmate losing
her mother to cancer. Their teacher, Miss Jones, had a loving and compassionate reaction to the griefstricken girl. He deeply appreciated Miss Jones’ ability to support his classmate in grief throughout the
school year. To vet this Resource, we ask, “If you’d been raised by someone who was as good to you
as Miss Jones was to your grief-stricken classmate, would that have been a good thing?” If we get a
“yes,” we ask, “How does the idea of a Miss Jones Resource feel in your body?” If it feels good, we have
a real-other Resource.

Real-Other Resource (Public) 
This real-other Resource is an inspirational public figure or celebrity who has touched a client in a very
helpful way. For example, Dr. Martin Luther King, Jr.’s stand against racism sent a message of hope and
courage to young black children in racist communities. Even if a client did not personally know Dr.
King, she could have felt supported by his speeches, writings, or actions. Clients might find inspiration
from people like Nelson Mandela, Oprah Winfrey, Maya Angelou, Mr. Rogers, or Mother Theresa.
To identify such a celebrity, we ask, “What inspirational public figure or celebrity have you felt, or could
feel, personally encouraged and supported by from afar?” This begins a discussion. If a client can
describe such a celebrity, we vet this possible Resource. For example, Linda grew up feeling emotional
validation and support from Mr. Rogers on TV. To vet this as a Resource, we ask, “If you’d been raised
by someone who was as good to you as Mr. Rogers was to the kids in his TV audience, would that have
been a good thing?” If we get a “yes,” we ask, “How does the idea of a Mr. Rogers Resource feel in your
body?” If it feels good, we have a real-other Resource.

Fictional-Other Resource 
A fictional-other Resource is based on a fictional character your client has felt supported by or could
feel supported by. For example, the Brady Bunch TV show portrayed a blended family where two
reasonable parents successfully met the physical and emotional needs of their six children. Lonely or

116

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

abused children watching this might vicariously benefit from the positive life lessons the Brady parents
taught their kids. The more exposure a client had to a positive fictional character, the more likely it can
serve as a helpful Resource. Some possible fictional Resources include Superman, June Cleaver, Molly
Weasley, Captain Jean-Luc Picard, Mary Poppins, Yoda, Santa Claus, or Atticus Finch.
To identify such a character, we ask, “What inspirational fictional character have you felt, or could feel,
nurtured and supported by?” This begins a discussion. If a client can describe such a fictional character,
we vet this possible Resource. For example, Ben grew up watching lots of Star Trek and felt inspired by
the courage and leadership of Captain Jean-Luc Picard. To vet this as a Resource we ask, “If you’d been
raised by someone who was as good to you as Captain Picard was to his crew, would that have been
a good thing?” If we get a “yes,” we ask, “How does the idea of a Captain Picard Resource feel in your
body?” If it feels good, we have a fictional-other Resource.

Mystical-Other Resource 
A mystical-other Resource is based on a spiritual, mystical, or archetypal being the client has felt or
could feel supported by like a guardian angel, spirit guide, shaman, Indian warrior, God, Jesus, or
Buddha. For example, a religious client who feels a personal relationship with a supportive, protective
higher power may be able to experience that higher power as a Resource.
To identify such a mystical being, we ask, “What spiritual, mystical, or archetypal being have you felt
(or could feel) nurtured and supported by?” This begins a discussion. If a client can describe such a
being, we vet this possible Resource. For example, Missy felt protected in childhood by a guardian
angel. To vet this as a Resource, we ask, “If you’d been raised by someone who embodied all the
admirable qualities of your guardian angel, would that have been a good thing?” If we get a “yes,” we
ask, “How does the idea of a guardian angel Resource feel in your body?” If it feels good, we have a
mystical-other Resource.

Three-Star Rating Scale Caveat
My ratings may not be exactly right for every client. For example, a deeply religious client may feel God
as more of a two- or three-star Resource. To a client who experienced Mr. Rogers as a significant positive
parental figure, Mr. Rogers may be a two-star Resource. What’s important is helping clients mobilize
supportive, loving, attuned Resources who are able to meet the emotional needs of wounded parts.

Establishing a Provisional Resource Team
We aim to help clients mobilize their best possible team of two or three Provisional Resources. Ideally
that would be a team of 3 three-star Resources—like a Therapist Self, a Dog-Lover Self, and a Buddha
Self. When that’s not possible we aim for the highest-ranking Resources the client can mobilize—like
a Teacher-Self, Aunt Louise, and a Guardian Angel, or Uncle Hugh, Mr. Rogers, and Atticus Finch.
When the client has identified two or three Resources, we ask, “What happens when you bring all the
Resources together? How does it feel in the body?” If it feels good, we ask, “Does it feel like they can
work together as a team?” If “yes,” we turn on ABS and say, “Notice that good feeling. Take as long as
you need to let that good feeling strengthen. Tell me when it’s strengthened all the way.” Once the
team is fully strengthened, we say, “You now have a team of Resources to help your wounded parts
heal.” Then we pair the Resource team with a scent, object, or music as described on page 85.
Sometimes bringing the Resource team together doesn’t feel good. If it brings up tension, discomfort,
or sleepiness, that’s usually a sign there’s a concern to identify and clear. Read about how we find and
address it in the Discomfort Bringing the Resources Together as a Team section on page 122.

Caveat About Naming Provisional Resources
Clients usually know right away what to call a Resource. When it’s not obvious, we can help them select
a name by offering several possible options. When a caregiver-self Resource is based on parenting a
child, some names are better than others. For example, a client with a five-year-old daughter named
Madison could name her caregiver-self Resource “Mom Self” or “Madison’s Mom.” Naming this
Resource “Madison’s Mom” is better, because that name goes directly to the client’s current
experiences of competently nurturing Madison. The name “Mom Self” might confuse wounded parts
if it takes them back to their unkind mother rather than Madison’s nurturing mother.

Iffy Resources
Occasionally a client will insist on mobilizing a Resource that sounds iffy, like a pet, a sibling, or an
outlaw. The best way to handle this is to say, “Check inside. Would your wounded parts find Butch Cassidy
to be a comforting Resource to connect to?” If the wounded parts say “no,” we keep looking for a more
suitable Resource. If the wounded parts say “yes,” we put the iffy Resource on the team for now. Later,
as we work with the wounded parts, we watch how well this Resource meets emotional needs. If it’s
helpful to the wounded parts, we keep it on the team. If it fails to help, we remove and replace it.

Formal and Informal Ways to Mobilize Provisional Resources
Sometimes in the process of getting a history, we learn about relationships and experiences that inform us
about possible Resources. For example, my client Niki would light up when she talked about her horses.
They were the love of her life. She was good at nurturing them and fiercely protective. I made a mental
note that she’d have a Horse-Lover Resource. My client Terrance described his close connection to his
Uncle Don in childhood. He teared up as he described how is Uncle Don lovingly supported him in
recovering through some painful losses. I made a mental note that he’d have an Uncle Don Resource.
Information like this can help us mobilize Provisional Resources, both formally and informally.
▪ Formally: To mobilize Provisional Resources we start a discussion with questions like, “Who in your
life have you been an especially supportive caregiver for, either past or present?” and “Who in your
life has been an especially supportive caregiver for you, either past or present?” If a client has already
told us about nurturing relationships, we can bring them into the discussion. For example, I’d remind
Niki of her loving connection to horses and Terrance about his close connection to Uncle Don.
▪ Informally: Sometimes in the process of getting a history, a wounded part gets triggered. That part
urgently needs a Resource to provide loving reassurance. If we already know about a potential
Resource, we can help the triggered part connect to it. For example, while Niki was talking about
her violent Dad, a child part got triggered. I said say, “Does your Horse-Lover Self understand why
talking about Dad scares you? Can she keep you safe now?” While Terrance was talking about an
overwhelming loss, a child part got triggered. I said, “Does Uncle Don understand why you’re so
sad? Can he comfort you now, while you feel sad as long as you need to?” This informal Resource
mobilization can have an immediate calming effect. Later, when the client’s Resource team can be
mobilized in a more structured way, with all the necessary vetting questions.

How Provisional Resources Compare to Healing Circle Resources
While all Healing Circle Resources are robust, Provisional Resources may or may not be as strong. The
more one- and two-star Resources on the team, the less robust it will be. Provisional Resources are
vetted but not as thoroughly as Healing Circle Resources. Mobilizing Provisional Resources can lay the
groundwork for mobilizing Healing Circle Resources later. A caregiver-self Provisional Resource is

Chapter 7: Mobilizing Provisional Resources

119

comparable to a Nurturing and/or Protective Adult Self. A spiritual-self Provisional Resource is essentially
the same as the Spiritual Core Self. When we guide a client to mobilize Healing Circle Resource later,
these Provisional Resources can serve as anchors for Healing Circle Resource meditations.

Resourcing Complications and Processing Blocks
Most clients will be able to mobilize a team of two to three Provisional Resources fairly easily. Some
may only be able to mobilize one. We encourage clients to do the best they can. If they can mobilize
only one, we work with what’s available. As clients heal more and more old wounds, they’ll become
better able to mobilize more Resources and/or stronger Resources. There tend to be fewer processing
complications for Provisional Resources compared to Healing Circle Resources. Below I reiterate the
most common concerns we’re likely to encounter, as they apply to Provisional Resources.

Problems Mobilizing a Caregiver-Self Resource
Several problems can arise when we invite clients to recall experiences of being a supportive caregiver.
For example, clients might recall childhood caregiving experiences, they might overfocus on caregiving
incompetencies, they might overlook competent caregiving experiences, or they might be afraid to
acknowledge competent caregiving experiences. And sometimes child parts fear a caregiver-self
Resource will be unkind, like caregivers from the past. I offer remedies to each of these below.
▪ Client Recalls Childhood Caregiving Experiences: When invited to recall a competent caregiving
experience, clients may recall something from childhood. For example, a client might recall the burden
of mothering neglected siblings, or having to emotionally support a suicidal parent. These are not
helpful in mobilizing a caregiver-self Resource for three reasons. (1) Memories like these are
associated with many unmet developmental needs. (2) Children resent being trapped in a stressful
caregiver role. And (3), a child who sees her parents model inadequate caregiving may fail to care
for neglected siblings well. When clients offer up childhood stories like this, we must redirect them to
their enjoyable experiences of competent adulthood caregiving. If they have no such experiences,
we should look for another type of Provisional Resource.
▪ Client Overfocuses on Caregiving Incompetencies: Sometimes talking about caregiving competencies
reminds clients of their caregiving failures. Clients who have gotten angry, frustrated, impatient, or
disrespectful with a loved one might label this undesirable behavior as proof of caregiving
incompetence. But these regrettable behaviors come from wounded parts who are triggered. To prove
this we ask, “When you behave that way, are you in your most adult self?” Most will answer “no.”
Wounded parts can be disrespectful, but a true Resource part cannot. We invite clients to focus
only on experiences of competently caring for a loved one while in their most adult self. If this is
not enough, it may help to focus on recently cultivated caregiving skills (e.g., only as a grandparent),
very selective caregiving skills (e.g., only as a gardener), or a single positive caregiving experience
(e.g., rescuing an injured stray dog). Clients who cannot set aside their personal caregiving
incompetencies should skip looking for a caregiver-self Resource and look for another type of
Provisional Resource.
▪ Client Overlooks Competent Caregiving Experiences: Clients who are not currently in a parenting
role may have difficulty recognizing or recalling caregiver experiences. We can remind them of
experiences they may be overlooking. For example, caregiving can happen in many contexts besides
parenting, like babysitting, caring for a pet, tending a garden, teaching school, mentoring, being a
camp counselor, or working as a healthcare professional. We can always remind clients of past
caregiving relationships. Clients with no such caregiver relationships—past or present—should skip
looking for a caregiver-self Resource and look for another type of Provisional Resource.

120

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

▪ Client Is Afraid to Acknowledge Competent Caregiving Experiences: When looking for a caregiverself Resource, a client may be reluctant to acknowledge competent caregiving experiences. This is
more likely to happen when wounded parts believe that saying positive things about self is
bragging, which should be punished. We can clear this misunderstanding by explaining that
recognition and acknowledgement of healthy skills and traits is not the same as self-absorbed,
egotistical bragging, that’s calculated to put others down. If wounded parts are not reassured by
this, we skip looking for a caregiver-self Resource and look for another type of Provisional Resource.
▪ Parts Are Afraid Resources Will Be Unkind: Wounded parts who grew up with caregivers who were
abusive, enmeshing, neglectful, rejecting, or misattuned may expect a Resource to behave the same
way. We ask something like “So, little one, would your most adult self ever treat your horses the way
your parents treated you?” or “Would Uncle Don ever treat you the way your parents treated you?”
If the answer is “no,” we offer that as proof the Resources would never be unkind. If the answer is
“yes,” we look for another type of Provisional Resource.

Common Concerns about Resourcing and Healing
Concerns about resourcing and healing can originate with a client’s adult self or wounded parts. Below
I list concerns that often come up when mobilizing Provisional Resources. Scripted remedies for each
are provided. (Additional guidance and scripts for clearing misunderstandings can be found in Chapter
11, in the Scripts That Fill In Missing Information section, on page 170, and in the Scripts That Clear
Up Misunderstandings section, on page 172.)
ADULT SELF: “I had to meet my own needs in childhood. It sounds like you’re proposing I meet my
own needs again. I don’t want to do that. It’s time for someone else to meet my needs.”
I’d like to tell you about two parts of the brain—the amygdala and prefrontal cortex. The amygdala
gets activated when danger is perceived, even if the danger is not real. The prefrontal cortex—
where maturity and intellect reside—can assess danger and calm the amygdala, but only if they’re
connected. When a child gets her emotional needs met by parents—especially when they soothe
her upsets—a neural pathway forms to connect the amygdala and prefrontal cortex. As this
connection gets bigger and stronger over time, this child matures into an adult who is able to
assess danger and manage painful emotions. If emotional needs are not met well enough in
childhood, this neural architecture does not develop well. A weak connection between the amygdala
and prefrontal cortex means difficultly assessing danger and managing painful emotions. Mobilizing
a Resource team will activate your prefrontal cortex. When your Resources comfort, soothe, and
reassure wounded child parts, it will strengthen a weak connection between the amygdala and
prefrontal cortex. Over time, this neural architecture will change to function more like it would
have, if your needs had been met well from the beginning. How does that sound?
ADULT SELF: “If I mobilize Resources, I’ll have to act like a mature grown-up all the time. I don’t
think I can do that.”
If you mobilize Resources, you will not have to act like a mature grown-up all the time. Having
Resources is like having a dishwasher. You can let dirty dishes pile up or use your dishwasher. It’s
your choice. Once you’ve mobilized Resources, you can access them for between-session help and
support or not. Your choice.
ADULT SELF: “I wasn’t very good at raising my kids. I don’t have a competent caregiver self.”
If you’ve ever been appropriately nurturing and protective of your children while in your most adult
self, even for short periods, then you have the potential to do that again in the future. So for the
purposes of mobilizing a caregiver-self Resource, it does not matter that you were not a good

Chapter 7: Mobilizing Provisional Resources

121

caregiver all the time. All that matters is that you can recall times that you were, and those times
could be with someone other than your children.
ADULT SELF: “There’s no point in developing Resources, because they won’t be real.”
The Resources are about real skills held in your real neural networks. Is the love and affection you
feel for cherished loved one real? OR Was the profound peace and love you felt during peak
spiritual experience real? OR Was the love and affection you felt from supportive caregiver real?
If YES: That’s proof this Resource would be real.
ADULT SELF: “If the Resources help me heal, my abusive parents are off the hook.”
Your parents’ abusive behavior is not excusable. In a perfect world, they’d be held accountable for
their behavior, but that may not happen. Fortunately, your child parts can heal whether your
parents pay for their abusive behavior or not.
ADULT SELF: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll have to end my relationship with ________.”
If you heal, you will not have to end your relationship with _________. You may stay connected, you
may disconnect a bit, or you may disconnect completely. This is your choice to make. You’ll be able
to evaluate each option on a moment-to-moment basis and change your mind at any time. As the
Resources help your wounded parts heal, you’ll gain wisdom that will help you make the best choice.
CHILD PART: “I had to meet my own needs in childhood. It sounds like you want me to meet my own
needs again. I don’t want to do that. It’s time for someone else to meet my needs.”
So, little one, I completely agree. You should not be expected to meet your own needs. The Resources
are healthy, mature, grown-up parts of self. It will be their job to meet your needs so you can relax
and go play.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I don’t know who I’ll be.”
So, little one, are you afraid of losing a familiar sense of identity? If YES: I completely understand.
Fortunately, you’ll never be without a sense of identity. While you maintain your old familiar
identity of
anxiety / depression / self-loathing, etc. , the Resources would help create a new
one based on
safety/self-love, etc. . Anything of value from your old identity will be saved, and
anything you no longer need will fade away.
CHILD PART: “I create problems. The Resources will want to get rid of me.”
So, little one, you’re a treasure. You matter. The Resources will not want to get rid of you. They’ll
want to love you, support you, and meet your needs so you can grow to meet your full potential.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll constantly have to meet everyone else’s needs.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, the most adult self will be better able to set and
maintain clear and appropriate boundaries. That means being able to choose to meet others’ needs
when it’s reasonable and appropriate, and choosing not to when it’s burdensome and inappropriate.
CHILD PART: “It’s my job to protect younger parts. The Resources will make me irrelevant.”
Hi, little one. I understand you’ve made it your mission to protect younger parts. I’m so grateful to
you for doing that. It’s a big job, and you deserve many thanks for your service. If we put together
a Resource team, you will not lose your relevance. In fact, if you like, the Resources will help you
grow to meet your full potential so you can be relevant in bigger and better ways. You will always
be important and valuable.

122

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll have to be responsible for everything.”
So, little one, the burden of responsibly navigating life’s challenges falls to the most adult self, not
you. As you and other wounded parts heal, the adult self will be better equipped to face those
challenges and make good choices.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll start setting boundaries and people will reject me.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, the most adult self will be able to set boundaries
more effectively. It’s true that some people might not like the boundaries and might be rejecting
because of it, but healing can also enable the most adult self to distance herself from people who
violate boundaries and surround herself with kind people who respect good boundaries.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll let my guard down and be endangered.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, the most adult self will be able to see danger
more clearly and handle it more effectively. She’ll be better able to put her guard up when it’s
necessary and take it down when it’s not.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll be weaker and more vulnerable.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, the most adult self will become stronger and
more resilient. She’ll be better able to set boundaries, confront adversity, solve problems, and deal
with difficult people and challenging situations. Healing will not make you more vulnerable; it will
make you safer.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll have to assert myself all the time.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, the most adult self will become better able to
assert herself in appropriate ways. She’ll be better able to discern when it’s helpful to be assertive
and when it’s not. Healing will not lock her into being assertive all the time.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll have to end my relationship with _________.”
So, little one, as you and other wounded parts heal, the most adult self will become better able to
evaluate the relationship with _________. She may stay connected, she may disconnect a bit, or she
may disconnect completely. This is her choice to make. She’ll be able to evaluate each option on a
moment-to-moment basis and change her mind at any time. The wisdom she gains as you and
other wounded parts heal will help her make the best choice.
CHILD PART: “If the Resources help me heal, I’ll have to come out of hiding.”
So, little one, sometimes hiding is a good strategy. As you and other wounded parts heal, the most
adult self will become better able to determine when it’s safe to be seen and when it’s best to hide.
CHILD PART: “If I come out of hiding, people will see how inadequate I am and reject me.”
So, little one, you feel inadequate because you’re stuck in the past—in painful experiences of being
judged, belittled, and rejected. As you and other wounded parts heal, the most adult self will feel
more confident and secure in spite of personal shortcomings. While unkind people may continue
to be judgmental and rejecting, this will feel less personal. The most adult self will be better able
to keep rejecting people at a distance and surround herself with those who are kind and accepting.

Discomfort Bringing the Resources Together as a Team
Sometimes discomfort or sleepiness arises when a client brings the Resources together to form a team.
This can happen when the adult self or wounded parts have misunderstandings or faulty assumptions
about what bringing together a Resource team will mean. If we suspect a wounded part is worried, we
can ask, “Is a wounded part stopping them from coming together?” If so, we ask to speak to the part
and start a welcoming, loving, validating dialogue. We invite the part to tell what she’s worried about.

Chapter 7: Mobilizing Provisional Resources

123

For example, she may be worried the Resources will assert themselves in ways that upset childhood
parents, resulting in shame or punishment, or the Resources will assert their power indiscriminately
without concern for consequences, leading to terrifying rejections. A part may perceive the dangers of
the past are still present now. Most concerns can be addressed by clearing misunderstandings, filling
in missing information, and orienting the part to the present time (see Chapter 13). We can recruit the
Resources to help us do this. Once we’ve addressed all concerns, the Resources should easily come
together as a team.

Definitely.

thought about ending my life by jumping into the river. I sat there for
about an hour, just feeling intense despair, searching for a reason to

down in front of me. Maybe a sparrow. He looked at me without fear
as if to say, “I see your goodness.” I expected he’d fly away, but he
stayed. When I put my finger out for him, he hopped on and looked
right at me. It’s like he really saw me. It moved something inside me. I didn’t stop being
sad, but I also felt peaceful and grounded. That was new. It felt like the bird was telling
me I was more than just my depression—I was also light and energy. I’m not sure how
long he was there—maybe ten minutes, maybe a half hour—but when he finally flew
away, I was in a completely different space. I felt a warm glow inside. I felt connected to
the nature around me. Thoughts of jumping off the cliff were gone. When I got home,
there was a package waiting for me on my bed. My grandmother had mailed me a
birthday present. It was a throw pillow with a picture of a bird. From then on, looking at
the pillow helped me to connect to that peaceful space inside. I’d found a part of me
that could transcend the horror of my miserable life.
Wow. That’s an amazing story. I’m so glad you had that experience. Do you still have the
pillow?
Yes, and it still calms me.
Does the peaceful space inside that you experience when you look at the pillow embody
loving energy that could be helpful to wounded parts?
Yes. Definitely.
How does the idea of this peaceful space as a Resource feel in your body?

Chapter 7 Summary
This chapter details the steps for vetting and mobilizing a team of two or three internal
Provisional Resources and bringing them together to form a team. Seven different Provisional
Resources are described in detail. Each one is given a one-, two-, or three-star rating. The threestar Resources are the best. They’re based on personal experiences of competent caregiving,
while lower rated Resources are not. While this process is usually much faster and easier for
clients than mobilizing Healing Circle Resources, Provisional Resources may not be as powerful.
They’re “provisional” in the sense that they’ll be a supportive Resource team until Healing Circle
Resources can be mobilized later. A substantial portion of this chapter provides remedies for
processing complications. A Provisional Resource team can play an essential role in stabilizing
wounded parts, so clients can be well prepared for EMDR.

Chapter 8
Establishing a Special Safe Place

The idea of a safe place to help manage painful emotions has been around a long time. It’s one of
several emotion management strategies recommended for EMDR Phase 2 preparation. Shapiro
advocated the eight-step “Safe/Calm Place” exercise.1 Clients are invited to envision themselves in a
peaceful, calm space like a place out in nature. They’re guided to notice the pleasing body sensations
the safe place evokes. That good feeling and sense of safety are strengthened with ABS. Clients are
invited to name a cue word that associates with the safe place like relax, beach, or mountain. That word
is paired with the sense of calm, and the pairing is strengthened with ABS. Later, calling up the cue
word helps clients regulate painful emotions by associating to their safe place. Clients who become
emotionally overwhelmed during trauma processing can pendulate between the trauma memory and
their safe place—keeping them inside their window of tolerance. While this may work well for some,
it may be insufficient to calm dysregulated, dissociative clients with lots of wounded child parts. This
chapter will describe a Special Safe Place where wounded parts can rest and play, surrounded by loving
Resources and fun things to do, until their time to heal.
For a worksheet that will guide you through these intervention steps while working with clients, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

What Is a DNMS Special Safe Place?
The DNMS Special Safe Place is not a safe place to calm the adult self. Instead it’s a positive, nurturing
place for a client’s wounded child parts—an imagined home base where they can hang out and have
fun. It’s a place like a daycare center, scout camp, mountain resort, amusement park, or beach resort.
It’s populated with Resources who provide the wounded parts loving support, supervision, and
containment. It’s a place for child parts to experience anxiety-free play and exploration. The Special
Safe Place serves two purposes: (1) It gives all wounded parts a fun, safe place to wait until their time to
heal, and (2) it’s a place we invite wounded parts to settle into after we’ve finished some healing work.

Establishing a Special Safe Place
There are five steps to establishing a Special Safe Place.

Step 1: Explain the Special Safe Place
We start by explaining the Special Safe Place by saying something like this:
I’d like to help you establish a wonderful fun, safe place for wounded child parts to hang out—a
place for them to rest and play. This is not a permanent place to put them to get them out of the
way. Rather, it’s a fun place for them to wait until the perfect time for them to heal. For example,

130

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

this place could be a beach resort, a mountain resort, an amusement park, a scout camp, a water
park, or even Disney World. If it’s a beach resort, it would have a beach, but it could also have a
swimming pool, a playground, a tennis court, a volleyball court, a bowling alley, a movie theater,
an art room, a reading room, a petting zoo, horseback riding, a hotel with sleeping rooms, and a
kitchen with lots of great food. This place is for your wounded parts only. Clones of your Resources
will be everywhere—at the beach, at the pool, with the horses, in the kitchen, wherever child parts
might want to hang out. They’ll be available to provide any comfort, attention, protection, or
supervision that’s needed.

Step 2: Invite Client to Envision a Special Safe Place
Next, we invite the client to envision the Special Safe Place that’s perfect for her wounded child parts.
It could sound something like this.
Start thinking about the perfect place for your wounded parts to hang out to rest and play. It doesn’t
have to be the beach resort I’ve just described, but it should be a place that’s fun with lots of
interesting things for kids of all ages to do. What kind of place would you want that to be?
As we discuss it with the client, we guide the process to ensure she establishes a wonderful place for
child parts of all ages to hang out. If the client describes a boring place, we suggest additions to make
it more fun like animals, a swing set, or a jungle gym. It’s okay if wounded parts prefer a boring place
at first. Later, as they begin to heal, they’ll want to make it more fun.
The place could be entirely fictional like a tropical island on the planet Zorniff. It could be a real place
the client enjoyed visiting in the past, like Grandpa’s lake house, but only if it’s completely free of bad
memories. Most importantly, it should feel safe, restful, and fun for children of all ages. Once the client
can describe her Special Safe Place, we say something like this:
Picture this place with no one there—no child parts or Resources. Just picture the beach, swimming
pool, playground, tennis court, bowling alley, movie theater, art room, etc.

Step 3: Bring in the Resources
Once the client is picturing it, we invite her to bring in the Resources.
Now bring in the Resources. Make clones of them. Put a team of Resources everywhere child parts
will want to hang out—on the beach, by the swimming pool, at the playground, by the tennis
court, in the bowling alley, movie theater, art room, and so on. When all the Resources are in place,
tell me how that feels in the body.
It will probably feel good, but if it doesn’t, we ask something like “What about this feels uncomfortable?
What would make it better?” We address whatever concerns are revealed. If wounded parts are worried
it’s not safe enough, we can add guard dogs or razor wire. We can put it on a remote island or surround
it with a castle wall. We can partition the place to separate feuding parts. We can create a teen hangout
space that’s off limits to young parts. We listen to and accommodate all preferences to establish a
place where all parts feel safe enough to rest and play and where they know the Resources will honor
them and meet their needs.

Step 4: Bring in All the Wounded Parts
When the Special Safe Place feels good with the Resources in place, we invite the wounded parts in.
We don’t need to identify each one. There will be time in the future to get to know individual parts.
We say something like:

Chapter 8: Establishing a Special Safe Place

131

I’d like to invite all your wounded parts to come hang out in this special place made just for them.
No need to count them or name them. When they have settled in here, tell me if they like it—if it
feels comfortable and safe.
Wounded parts are usually very happy to settle in. If it feels good, we strengthen the good feeling with
ABS. If it doesn’t feel comfortable or safe, we must find out why. If it’s because wounded parts are
reluctant to trust the Resources or be cared for by the Resources, we say something like:
You’re welcome here no matter what concerns you have. The Resources will love and accept you
exactly as you are right now, unconditionally. The Resources will work to earn your trust over time.
If one or more wounded parts are holding deeper fears or concerns, we invite them to approach the
Resources and encourage them to share their worries with us and the Resources. (See Chapter 10.)
Most concerns will be rooted in misunderstandings and faulty assumptions which can be remedied
with the appropriate psychoeducational interweave. (See Chapters 11 and 12.) Parts may perceive the
dangers of the past are still present now. This can be remedied by orienting them to present time. (See
Chapter 13.)

Step 5: Give It a Name
Once a Special Safe Place is established, we invite the client to name it. It could be called Beach Resort,
Mountain Retreat, Lake House Getaway, or whatever the client prefers. We’ll refer to it by that name in
the future.

Other People in the Special Safe Place
The Special Safe Place is just for a client’s wounded parts and Resources. A client may select a very
public place, like an amusement park, where a child would encounter strangers—like other customers
and park employees. Since this is for parts of self only, we explain no strangers are allowed, but their
Resources can serve refreshments and operate rides. If the client protests, and insists park employees
must stay, we can leave them in place. We consider them little r Resources since they’re present in
service of wounded parts.
Sometimes a client will want chosen friends or family to hang out with them in their Special Safe Place.
I generally discourage this, but if I get pushback, I’m open to including any friends or family members
who can join wounded parts in a supportive role—as a big R or little r Resource.

Warning: Special Safe Place Is Not Exile
Wounded parts will be happy to hang out in their Special Safe Place so long as they feel the adult self
will listen to them, understand their concerns, and take their emotional needs seriously. If they feel
ignored, abandoned, or dumped there, they’ll push back. If they feel rejected by an angry client who’s
sending them there for a timeout for being “bad,” they’ll rebel. So long as wounded parts believe their
needs are considered a high priority, they’ll be happy to hang out in their Special Safe Place while the
client does adult stuff, knowing she’ll be available when they need her.

How to Use the Special Safe Place to Manage Stress
The Special Safe Place can also be used to help clients manage stress around future events. For
example, if a client is planning a visit to a doctor—a visit that is likely to trigger wounded parts—she
can talk to the parts about the visit in advance. She can tell them what’s going to happen, how long it
will take, and when it will be over. She can invite them to hang out at their Special Safe Place with the

132

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Resources who will keep them busy with a movie, a story, or some sort of play—with a promise to
check in on them as soon as the doctor visit is over. That increases the probability that the client will
be able to stay in her most adult self throughout the doctor visit.

Sample Client-Therapist Dialogue: Establishing a Special Safe Place
The following is a typical client-therapist dialogue for establishing a Special Safe Place. This is a
continuation of ego state therapy with Carla.
Explaining the Concept
Therapist:

Therapist:

Therapist:

Yes, I need lots of kittens and cats.

Chapter 8 Summary
This chapter outlines the steps for guiding clients to establish a wonderful, fun place for their
wounded child parts to hang out—an imagined home base where they can experience anxietyfree play and exploration. It’s a place like a daycare center, scout camp, mountain resort,
amusement park, or beach resort populated with Resources who provide loving support,
supervision, and containment. This Special Safe Place gives all wounded parts a fun, safe place
to wait until their time to heal. It also serves as a place for them to settle into after we’ve finished
some healing work.

Notes
1.

Shapiro, F. (2017).

Chapter 9
Setting an Effective Therapy Goal

Clients seek therapy to stop problematic behaviors, silence negative beliefs, and calm intense painful
emotions—conditions that make it hard to thrive. So, they start therapy with a goal. A goal a client’s
adult self is ready to pursue may be feared by wounded parts. The more wounded parts object to a
goal, the more likely they will block all attempts to meet it. This chapter explains how to identify
problematic therapy goals and replace them with goals all wounded parts will support.
For a worksheet that will guide you through these intervention steps while working with clients, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

Discovering the Importance of an Effective Therapy Goal
I discovered the perils of an ineffective therapy goal with a client I’ll call Joan. Joan and I worked
together for about a year. During that time, she mobilized a Resource team that helped many wounded
parts heal. She took a therapy break for about two years. When she returned, she said was getting
easily triggered by her boyfriend’s insensitive behaviors. When this happened, she’d verbally attack
him with outbursts of hostile anger. This was happening with more frequency, and she wanted help
getting it under control. Our first task was to remobilize her Resources. I expected it would take a few
minutes, but for some reason, it was taking much longer. After about three sessions of wrestling with
blocks and distractions, I stepped back to look at the big picture. I asked, “Joan, do you have any
wounded parts who object to your therapy goal?” She immediately said, “Yes.” I invited those parts to
talk about their objections. They explained that her boyfriend’s insensitive comments and behavior
evoked a sense of shame with feelings of powerlessness. This was immediately followed by anger,
which felt powerful. Wounded parts were worried that stopping the anger would mean losing a sense
of power. As I listened to their concerns, I came to understand the anger helped them neutralize the
sense of shame that made them feel powerless. So, I asked the wounded parts if it would help to
change the therapy goal to “help all wounded parts overcome lifelong chronic shame.” They said “yes.”
Once we changed the goal, Joan was able to easily reconnect to her Resources within a few minutes.
This experience with was an eye-opener for me. I began to wonder how many times a therapy goal
was directly connected to processing complications. When I began to routinely check in with wounded
parts, to see if they had objections to the adult self’s goal, I discovered it was very common.
Wounded parts can be very attached to coping behaviors, attitudes, and strategies that the adult self
doesn’t like or want. For example, wounded parts who are depressed might lift their mood with

136

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

excessive consumption of junk food, alcohol, or pornography. Wounded parts who are fearful might
feel safer in isolation, by being aggressive, or by mistrusting everyone. Wounded parts who are lonely
might feel more connected to others by people-pleasing or overachieving. These “solutions” often
originated in childhood to cope with stress that could not be managed another way.
When wounded parts object to a therapy goal, we find out why. By listening to their concerns, we can
figure out the unmet developmental needs driving their “solution.” We can help them select a new
goal that’s aimed at remediating those unmet needs. For example, wounded parts who object to the
adult self’s goal of “ending emotional eating” may prefer a goal like “to help all wounded parts feel
seen, heard, and valued.” Wounded parts who object to the goal of “becoming more assertive” may
prefer a goal like “to help all wounded parts feel safe expressing thoughts and feelings.” Meeting the
wounded parts’ new goal will also address the adult self’s original goal. They’re really the same goal,
just worded different ways.

Two Types of Therapy Goals
Global goals: When clients tell us about their global goals in their first therapy session, they’re usually
broadly stated—like a desire to reduce anxiety, set good boundaries, or end emotional eating. Within a
broadly stated problem, a client may have a variety of struggles. For example, anxiety may get triggered
when traveling, giving a presentation, feeling criticized, or doing laundry. Trouble setting boundaries
might come up in relationship with children, in-laws, or coworkers.
Session goals: Clients often come to session troubled by a current or recent triggering event, giving us
an opportunity to help the triggered parts heal. We set a narrowly worded session goal tailored to the
needs of the moment. For example, if a coworker’s critical comment triggers a wounded part holding
the negative belief “I’m worthless,” the session goal might be “to find and stabilize the wounded part
that got triggered by the coworker’s comment.”

Checking for Objections to the Therapy Goal
We always check for objections to global goals early in therapy. We only check for objections to session
goals if a client becoming sleepy, achy, distracted, or oppositional at the start of some healing work.
Because the body reverberates with the concerns and fears of wounded parts, we inquire about objections
by checking with the body. We say to the client, something like:
I want to be sure we don’t pursue a therapy goal that your wounded parts object to. To find out
if any parts object, I’m going to ask you to close your eyes, look inside, and answer a question
about each goal. Just let your body (not your head) answer with a YES, NO, or MAYBE. Ready?
When ready, we ask:
Are there any wounded parts who object to the goal of __________?
If the body answers with a “yes” or “maybe,” we ask to speak to the parts who object.
May I talk to the part or parts who object to this goal?

Inviting Parts to Talk about Their Objections
When parts are willing to talk, we start with a warm welcome and invite them to tell us what they don’t
like about the goal.
Welcome. I’m really glad you’re here. You must have a very good reason for objecting to this
goal. Tell me about that.

Chapter 9: Setting an Effective Therapy Goal

137

If their explanation doesn’t make sense, we ask lots of questions to get clarity. It helps to start by getting
the age of parts who object. That helps us identify the part of the client’s history that’s relevant to the
objection. For example, a two-year-old part may be more interested in securing close connections,
while a fifteen-year-old part may be more interested in securing independence. Here are some sample
questions that may help us get clarity:
How old are you?. What happened to you at age _______?
How does

emotional eating / gambling / drinking

When did you start

help? What problem does it solve?

emotional eating / gambling / drinking

?

If the adult self stopped
emotional eating / gambling / drinking
what bad thing would happen? Would there be a downside to that?
If the adult self started
setting boundaries / being assertive
bad thing would happen? Would there be a downside to that?

, what would you lose, or what

If the adult self felt
less angry / more confident
, what would you lose, or what bad thing
would happen? Would there be a downside to that?

Validating Wounded Parts’ Objections to the Goal
Wounded parts really want to be seen, heard, and understood. It helps when we reflect back what we
understand with validating words. We need to show we’re on their side. This is essential for building
rapport and trust. Here are some sample reflections.
It makes perfect sense you’d object to ending emotional eating if that meant taking away the
best way you know to manage shame and despair. I don’t want you to lose your ability to manage
shame and despair either.
It makes perfect sense you’d object to giving up gambling if that meant taking away the best
way you know to shut out painful memories. I don’t want you to be overwhelmed by painful
memories either.
It makes perfect sense you’d object to setting boundaries with people if you expect they’d get
mad and reject you. Connection is important. I want you to have valuable connections to people
who matter to you.
It makes perfect sense you’d want to hold on to hostile anger if it helps you feel powerful and in
control. I want you to feel in control and empowered to make your life better.
Wounded parts may not know there are other ways their problems can get solved. We can introduce
that as a possibility. Here are some sample scripts for doing that.
If there was a way to manage feelings of shame and despair without emotional eating, would
that interest you?
If there was a way to process through painful memories so they no longer bothered you, would
that interest you?
If there was a way to maintain healthy connections to people who matter while setting good
boundaries for yourself, would that interest you?
If you could feel in control and empowered without hostile anger, would that interest you?

Suggesting an Alternative Goal
When we understand the wounded parts’ attachment to a problem well enough, we can offer an
alternative goal that’s a match for the unmet needs driving the unwanted behaviors, beliefs, or
emotions. The best goals are not about stopping the symptom of a problem—they’re about addressing
the trauma or unmet needs at the root of the problem. They’re goals about empowerment, healing,

138

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

boosting self-esteem, and ensuring safety. Here are some sample goals that we might invite wounded
parts to consider.
To help all wounded parts feel seen, heard, and valued.
To help all wounded parts understand they’re safe now.
To help all wounded parts understand they deserve love.
To help all wounded parts get the protection they deserve.
To help all wounded parts understand they have power now.
To help all wounded parts heal from lifelong chronic shame.
To help all wounded parts get all their unmet childhood needs met.
To help all wounded parts begin to grow to meet their full potential.
To help all wounded parts feel safe expressing their thoughts and feelings.
Here are some sample questions for offering an alternative goal.
What if we change the therapy goal to this: “To help all wounded parts heal from lifelong chronic
shame and despair?” Would that interest you?
What if we change the therapy goal to this: ”To help all wounded parts heal from your painful
past so your old wounds cannot get triggered again?” Would that interest you?
What if we change the therapy goal to this: “To help all wounded parts feel empowered to express
their thoughts and feelings?” Would that interest you?
Wounded parts might or might not be interested in a suggested alternative. If it’s not embraced, we
invite them to talk about why. Here are some possible follow-up questions.
Help me understand what’s wrong with this goal?
Does this goal miss the mark? What is it missing?
Do we need to add another goal?
What goal would be more on target?
What goal would be more appealing?
If parts offer a goal that’s unrealistic like “for my mother to be nice to me,” we can suggest a realistic
alternative like “to help all wounded parts understand they have value even if Mother is not nice.” It
might take a few iterations to word a goal just right.
Sometimes parts need several goals working in concert. Here are some sample goals that might pair
well together.
To help all wounded parts feel safe asking for what they need.
To help all wounded parts feel supported in expressing painful emotions.
To help all wounded parts understand they deserve to be heard and understood.
If wounded parts seem unable to discuss this or come to some agreement on a new goal, they may
need to do some healing work first. We can apply all the stabilizing interventions described in Chapters
10-14. Once that healing work is complete, the wounded parts should be ready to reveal what they
need and agree on a new goal.

Checking for Objections to the New Goal
When all parts seem to agree on a new goal, we ask the client’s adult self:
Close your eyes, and just let your body (not your head) answer. Answer with a YES, NO, or MAYBE.
The new therapy goal is ______. Are there any wounded parts who object to this goal?

Chapter 9: Setting an Effective Therapy Goal

139

If the body gives us a NO, the client’s new therapy goal is set and ready to go. If the body gives us a
YES or MAYBE, we can follow up to find out why. We edit the goal or add additional goals as necessary
until the body tells us all parts are in agreement.

Inviting Wounded Parts to Settle In
After setting a new global therapy goal, we always invite wounded parts to settle into their Special Safe
Place with a script like this:
So, to all the wounded parts who came out to have this conversation with me, thank you so much
for helping with this. In a moment, I’ll be inviting you to settle into your Beach Resort. Is there
anything you’d like to say or ask before you settle in? (Wait for a response) Now I'd like to invite
you all to find a nice, cozy, safe place to settle into your Beach Resort, settle in with the
Resources—just find a good place to rest and play until the next time you’re needed.
There’s one exception to this. If the wounded part that’s objected to the goal is a part we’re about to
work with, we just flow right into processing. We’ll invite the part to settle in later, after we’re finished
with the processing or when the session is over. See an example of how setting a session goal flows
into processing, in the sample client-therapist dialogue on page141.

Good point. How about this goal, “to help all wounded parts understand that
anticipating the benefits of accomplishment can motivate hard work better than
shame and fear?” Would that help?
I don’t know. That’s pretty scary. What if that kind of motivation doesn’t work?
Okay. I hear you’re really scared. How about this goal, “to help all wounded parts
learn that anticipating the benefits of accomplishment can motivate hard work?”
Right now, your motivation for hard work comes from shame and fear. If wounded
parts learn this, they’ll have the option to choose motivation by the carrot or the
stick. Would that help?
That sounds better.
Sixteen-year-old, keep in mind that the Resources will be here to provide loving
encouragement and support you and all other parts, as you work hard to complete
your schoolwork. They’ll be here to offer you motivating carrots.
