Chapter 9 Summary
This chapter explains why therapy goals focused on changing unwanted behaviors can upset
wounded parts who are fond of those behaviors. It outlines steps to determine if any wounded
parts object to the client’s therapy goals. When they do object, we encourage wounded parts
to tell us what they need. We enlist their help in replacing problematic goals with new goals
that all parts like. New goals are focused on meeting their emotional needs, not changing their
behaviors, beliefs, or emotions. This sends a message to all wounded parts, that their needs
matter and will be addressed. This is an important step, because it reduces the possibility of
processing blocks coming up later.

Chapter 10
Nurturing Interventions

In this chapter we focus on how to seek out and find wounded child parts who are stuck in the past,
feeling powerless and lost, so we can bring them out of the shadows. I’ll show how we get them connected
to loving Resources so they can finally get the nurturing they need. This is the first in a series of stabilizing
interventions. The rest are described in Chapters 11-14.
For a worksheet that will guide you through these intervention steps while working with clients, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

Status Categories of Wounded Parts
Wounded parts will fall into one of three status categories: currently triggered, recently triggered, or
dormant. We can stabilize all wounded parts with the same basic steps and principles, but how and
when to work with them will depend on their initial status. Let’s explore each category.
 Currently Triggered: Currently triggered wounded parts are front and center now, feeling intense
emotions. They could be upset about something that happened before a session or during a
session. This includes parts who show up during a session to block processing. Currently triggered
parts get the highest processing priority. We aim to work with them right away.
 Recently Triggered: Recently triggered wounded parts were upset within the last week or so. They’re
not front and center now but they’re very close to the surface. They could easily get triggered again.
Recently triggered parts are a high priority for processing whenever they consume a client’s attention.
 Dormant: Dormant wounded parts have not been triggered recently but have the potential to be
triggered in the future. Most wounded parts fall into this status category. Dormant parts are
connected to important longstanding issues, like fear of rejection, urge to self-harm, or people
pleasing. Big issues like this are usually connected to lots of painful experiences and lots of
wounded parts. While work with dormant parts is usually a low priority, it can become a higher
priority when a client is no longer being triggered on a regular basis by routine life events.

Selecting a Target Issue for Stabilization
Target issues are connected to wounded parts who are stuck in the past. They hold negative beliefs
about self, like “I’m worthless,” “I’ll never be good enough,” and “I’m not safe.” A target issue could be
a current trigger, a recent trigger, or a longstanding issue. It’s something disturbing that preoccupies
the client.

146

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Targeting a Current or Recent Trigger
Sessions often begin with questions like “How are you doing? How have you been since our last session?”
Dysregulated clients will often describe recently stressful events that have triggered an emotional
overreaction, intense anxiety, or a profound sense of powerlessness. Some events, like a fender bender,
can legitimately upset the adult self while also triggering a wounded part. Any experience that’s
triggered a wounded part can become a target issue. But before we launch into ego state stabilization,
it’s best to verify that the distress is actually coming from a wounded part. We ask questions like, “Did
this experience trigger a wounded part?” “Did a wounded part overreact to this event?” and “Is the
negative belief that got triggered held by your most adult self or a wounded part?” If a wounded part
is connected, ego state therapy will help.

Targeting a Longstanding Issue
When we target a longstanding issue, we’ll be contacting dormant parts. Since they have not been
recently triggered, it helps to have a specific incident in mind to bring the parts into focus. This could
be an incident from the client’s childhood, an incident from adulthood, or a hypothetical incident. Let’s
say my client Noah wants to work on fear of rejection. Any of the following target incidents could help:
 Childhood event: Recalling at age four, his mom saying “I hate you” before locking him out of the house.
 Adulthood event: Recalling his girlfriend of two years, breaking up with him right after he proposed.
 Hypothetical event: Imagining applying for a job and being rejected.
He may hold negative beliefs connected to fear of rejection, like “I don’t matter,” or “I’m worthless.”

Getting Pre- and Post-Ratings
Before stabilization we could ask, “How disturbing does the target incident feel, 0-10?” or “How true does
the negative belief feel, 0-10?” Then we can ask these again when stabilization is complete. Getting
ratings can be handy, but it’s not mandatory. It’s more important to gauge stabilization success by
tuning in to a client’s body language, facial expressions, and verbal expressions. While 0-10 ratings can
be useful, there only one measure of how effective the stabilization was.

Planning the Invitation
Once we selected a target issue we ask the client, “Would you like to work on this today?” If “yes,” we
make a plan to invite forward the part or parts we want. We’ll usually use this all-purpose format:
I’d like to invite to approach the Resources the part or parts who
tell me what you notice.

. When you’re ready,

We fill in the blank with the client’s current, recent, or future upset, or a longstanding issue. Here’s an
example of each:
I’d like to invite to approach the Resources the part or parts who are angry about your mother’s
phone call just now . When you’re ready, tell me what you notice.
I’d like to invite to approach the Resources the part or parts who got triggered yesterday at the
carwash . When you’re ready, tell me what you notice.
I’d like to invite to approach the Resources the part or parts who are afraid to get on an airplane
tomorrow . When you’re ready, tell me what you notice.
I’d like to invite to approach the Resources the part or parts who are terrified of rejection . When
you’re ready, tell me what you notice.

Chapter 10: Nurturing Interventions

147

Usually the words we need to fill in the blank are obvious. But sometimes a triggering event is so
complicated the best invitation is unclear. When this happens, we can ask the client “What invitation
do you believe will help us get to the wounded part who most needs help today?” If the client isn’t
sure, we can offer some ideas. This is what it could sound like:
Therapist:
Client:
Therapist:

Client:
Therapist:

I think I finally understand what happened to you at work yesterday. What invitation do
you believe will help us get to the wounded part who got triggered?
I’m not sure.
Well, I could invite forward the part or parts who got triggered when your boss shamed
you yesterday. Or, the part or parts who felt humiliated by your boss yesterday. Or, the
part or parts who felt humiliated when your coworkers laughed at you yesterday. Or, the
part or parts who wanted to run away after your boss humiliated you yesterday. Do any
of these seem like the right invitation, or is there a better one?
How about inviting the part that got triggered when my boss shamed me in front of my
coworkers yesterday?
Perfect. I can do that.

Sometimes a client has had so many recent triggers she cannot easily determine which one we most
need to work on. When this happens, we can use a catch-all invitation like:
“I’d like to invite to approach the Resources the part or parts who most need my help today. When
you’re ready, tell me what you notice.”
Once we know what invitation we’ll be using, we invite the client to connect to the Resources.

Connecting to Resources
We say to the client, “Connect to your Resources. Tell me when you’re connected.” Once connected,
we start ABS and say, “Take as long as you need to let that strengthen. Tell me when it’s strengthened
all the way.” Once it’s strengthened, we ask, “How’s the connection?” If the connection is at least
moderately good, we proceed to inviting parts forward.

Complications Connecting to Resources
Two common complications can arise when we invite clients to connect to their Resources.
 Something is Blocking the Resources: For directions on how we find a clear processing blocks, go to
Chapter 18. The whole chapter can be helpful, but focus especially on the Wounded Parts Block
Access to Robust Resources section, page 249.
 Client is Too Upset to Connect to Resources: If a wounded part is currently triggered, the client may
be unable to connect to the Resources. When that happens, we must help change her state of mind
just enough so she can make the connection. Here are several things we can do:
▪ We can invite the client to access the scent, object, or sound they paired with the Resources.
▪ We can briefly discuss something completely off topic. For example, we can tell a joke or story,
discuss something from the news, or ask about a recent personal success.
▪ We can ask questions only the client’s adult self can answer like “What’s COVID-19?” “What’s a
GPS device?” “What’s a thumb drive?” or “Who is the vice president?”
▪ We can orient the client to the present by looking around the room, looking in a mirror to see
an adult face and body, looking at a current calendar, or looking at their driver’s license.
▪ Go to Working with Very Triggered Parts section, page 151, if a wounded part seems inconsolable.
Once a client is calm enough to connect to her Resources, we apply ABS to strengthen the connection.

148

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Inviting Wounded Parts to Approach the Resources
We invite wounded parts forward with the invitation we planned (page 146). Clients tell us what they
notice. They will usually see or sense one or more wounded parts coming forward. They may disclose
the part or part’s age, mood, or both. Here are some typical reports:
I see her.

I see a group six of parts.

There’s a part here.

There are three fearful parts here.

She’s four years old.

Looks like a lot of parts. Not sure how many.

It’s an angry part.

I see a sad two-year-old and an angry twelve-year-old.

I see an angry four-year-old.

There’s a group of surly teens.

Occasionally what clients notice is not obviously a wounded part. For example, they may describe an
emotion, a body sensation, a metaphor, or a scene. Sometimes clients notice nothing at all. Here are
some atypical reports:
I feel anger.

I see a dark blob.

I want to cry.

There’s a large black spider.

I feel sleepy.

I see an empty rowboat on a lake.

I’m getting a headache.

Lots of people are screaming and running away.

I feel sick to my stomach.

I don’t notice anything.

Welcoming Parts and Starting a Dialogue
We start a welcoming dialogue with the part or parts who have come forward. How we start a dialogue
depends on what the client is noticing. We aim to get the parts’ age and mood, before directing her
to connect with the Resources. Let’s talk about the various possibilities.
 One Part Shows Up: Most of the time one part shows up. We ask, “May I speak to this part?” If “yes,”
we offer a warm greeting. “Welcome. Thanks for coming. I’m really glad you’re here.” If the client
has not already told us the part’s age or mood, we ask, “Can you tell me how old you are?” and/or
“What kind of mood are you in?” (See Getting the Wounded Part’s Age sidebar, page 149.)
 Two or More Parts Show Up: We begin by asking, “How many parts are there?” If the client cannot be
precise, we get a rough estimate. Then we say, “May I speak to these parts?” If “yes,” we offer a warm
greeting. “Welcome. Thanks for coming. I’m really glad you’re all here.” If the client has not already
told us their ages or moods, we ask. “So, girls / boys can you tell me how old each of you are?
There could be lots of different ages. Then “So, girls / boys what kind of mood are you in?” There
could be one shared mood or several different moods.
 Emotion Arises: When a client notices an emotion, like anger, fear, or despair, we begin by asking “Is
a wounded part feeling sad / angry / afraid ?” If “yes,” we ask, “May I speak to the part?” If “yes,”
we offer a warm greeting. “Welcome. Thanks for coming. I’m really glad you’re here. Can you tell
me how old you are?”
 Body Sensation Arises: When a client notices a body sensation, like a headache, sleepiness, pain, or
discomfort we begin by asking “Is a wounded part connected to the sleepiness / headache / tension ?”
If “yes,” we ask, “May I speak to the part?” If “yes,” we offer a warm greeting. “Welcome. Thanks for
coming. I’m really glad you’re here. Can you tell me how old you are?” Then, “What kind of mood
are you in?”
 Metaphor Shows Up: A wounded part can initially appear as a metaphor like a dark blob or insect.
We begin by asking the client “Does it feel as if this (metaphor) is a wounded part?” If “yes,” we
ask “May I speak to the part appearing as. (metaphor) ?” If “yes,” we offer a warm greeting.

Chapter 10: Nurturing Interventions

149

“Welcome. Thanks for coming. I’m really glad you’re here. Feel free to appear this way as long as
you need to. Can you tell me how old you are?” Then, “What kind of mood are you in?”
 A Scene Appears: A scene is usually a float back to a painful experience. We begin by asking “Is there
a wounded part connected to this scene?” If “yes,” we ask “May I speak to the part? If “yes,” we offer
a warm greeting. “Welcome. Thanks for coming. I’m really glad you’re here. Can you tell me how
old you are?” Then, “What kind of mood are you in?”
 Client Notices Nothing: Sometimes a client tells us nothing happened—no wounded parts, surging
emotions, sensations, metaphors, or scenes arise. Many different things can account for this. Let’s
explore each one.
▪ An invisible part showed up: Sometimes the invited part shows up invisible. We ask the client,
“Does it feel like an invisible part has shown up?” If “yes,” we can say, “May I speak to this part?”
If “yes,” we offer a warm greeting. “Welcome. Thanks for coming. I’m really glad you’re here.
Feel free to stay invisible as long as you need to. Can you tell me how old you are?” Then, “What
kind of mood are you in?” (As invisible parts heal, they eventually choose to become visible.)
▪ Invitation was poorly worded: A poorly worded invitation may fail to draw out the parts we want.
We can ask the client, “Is there a better way to invite forward the part or parts we need to work
with?” A better invitation may connect us to the part(s) we need to work with.
▪ Issue is not connected to a wounded part: If a client’s upset is normal and appropriate, and not
connected to a wounded part, no wounded part will appear. Some other intervention is needed.
▪ Part(s) are afraid to come forward: We can ask the client, “Is there a part or parts afraid to come
forward?” If “yes,” we ask, “May I speak to them?” If “yes,” we say, “Welcome. I’m glad you’re here.
You don’t have to approach the Resources. But can you tell me why you’re too afraid to come
forward?” Once we understand the fearful part(s)’ concern, we validate it. We attune with radical
acceptance. We clear misunderstandings and fill in missing information. We provide whatever
support is needed until they can come forward.
▪ One part is blocking other part(s) from coming forward: We can ask the client, “Does it feel
like a part or parts are being blocked from coming forward?” If “yes,” we ask, “May I speak to the
part doing the blocking? If “yes,” we say, “Welcome. I’m glad you’re here. You must have a very
good reason for blocking part(s) from coming forward. Tell me about that.” Once we understand
the intrusive part’s concern, we validate it. We attune with radical acceptance. We clear
misunderstandings and fill in missing information. (See Working with Intrusive Parts section,
page 152.) When the part no longer needs to block other parts, we read the invitation again.
When the invited part or parts show up, we welcome them, get their age and mood.

Getting the Wounded Part’s Age
We ask about a part’s age for two reasons: (1) It helps us
place the part within the client’s history, and (2) it gives us
something to call the part, e.g., “Hi there, four-year-old.”
When we ask for an age, parts might give us a single age,
a range of ages, or no age. If we’re given no age it could
be because (a) the part doesn’t know or (b) the part knows
but chooses not to tell us.
Because wounding experiences can be repeated over
many years, it’s not unusual for a part to be a range of ages
like two to ten, or four to nine.

If a part does not know her age, we can ask questions
to narrow it down. “Are you more than ten, less than
ten, or about ten?” With that answer, we can follow
with, “Are you more than five, less than five, or about
five?” or “Are you more than fifteen, less than fifteen,
or about fifteen?” It may be sufficient to narrow the
age down to a five-year window.
If a part chooses not to disclose her age, we accept
that boundary gracefully. We can revisit the question
later after rapport has been built and some healing has
occurred. Eventually the part will gladly reveal her age.

150

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Connecting Wounded Parts to the Resources
Once we get the part or parts’ age and mood, we ask “Can you see the Resources?” If “yes,” “Do they
look good to you?” If “yes,” “Do the Resources understand why you’re feeling sad / angry / afraid ?”
If “yes,” “What’s it like to notice that?” This usually feels very good. We strengthen this connection by
adding something like “Feel free to be
sad / angry / afraid
as long as you need to. While you're
doing that, notice the Resources will be here for you, ready to understand. They don’t need you to feel
any differently than you do right now. They’re just here to love and support you while you feel whatever
you need to feel. How does that sound?” Parts usually feel a sense of relief.
Once a wounded part is feeling seen and heard by the Resources, we ask “Would you like to get up
close to the Resources?” If “yes,” we say “Get up close, right in between them. Does that feel safe and
comfortable?” It probably will. If it doesn’t, we give the part permission to mistrust. We might say
“You’re welcome to mistrust the Resources as long as you need to. It’s their job to earn your trust.”
Sometimes wounded parts who feel safe and comfortable with the Resources, say the Resources do
not understand their painful emotions. When this happens, we invite the parts to tell us a little of their
story. Once the story reveals the reason for the painful emotions, we ask again if the Resources
understand. Robust Resources will say they do.
Sometimes wounded parts will say the Resources do not look good. There are many possible reasons
for this. A list of reasons and potential remedies are provided in the Resource-Related Concerns section
of Chapter 18 on page 247.

Getting the Story
We invite wounded parts to tell us their story. This helps us better understand how they got stuck and
gives us a chance to support them with lots of attuned validation, empathy, and radical acceptance.
We say, “Tell me your story. What happened to you at age ___ that’s kept you stuck in the past all this
time?” When the wounded part answers, we convey complete warmth and acceptance not just with
our words, but with our tone of voice and body language as well. We use a warm and expressive tone.
We say things like, “Oh my! I’m so sorry that happened to you!” We validate. “They put you in a huge
double bind. Your anger was justified.” We empathize. “It must have been very painful to have to keep
your secret. I’m so sorry you had no one to tell.” If we’re not sure how to validate or empathize with a
part of the story, it helps if we can imagine what it would have been like to be the client, at that age,
having that wounding experience. That empathic connection may give us the needed words.
We seek clarity when we don’t understand. “That seems really important, but I don’t quite understand.
Please say more about that.” We ask follow-up questions to draw out new details, especially about
broken connections, like “Did you tell your parents about that? What did they say when you told them?”
All along the way, we invite the parts to notice that the Resources understand and empathize. We ask
things like “Do the Resources understand how painful that was?” “Do the Resources think it was okay
for your parents to shame you?” “Do the Resources believe you deserved to be protected?” and “Do
the Resources love you unconditionally even though you’ve made mistakes?”
The way we guide the conversation should convey to the wounded parts that we care about their story,
they matters to us, their experience makes sense, their reaction was normal and reasonable, the wounding
they endured was not justified, and the Resources love and accept them now, unconditionally. All this
attunement, validation, and empathy from us is very healing, but it means MUCH more coming from
the Resources!

Chapter 10: Nurturing Interventions

151

Wounded parts are stuck in the past, usually in childhood—stuck in painful experiences where emotional
needs or safety needs were not getting met. Since that’s where the wounding started, it’s usually best
to keep the conversation focused on childhood experiences. If parts want to talk about the current life
situation, we can ask them how it reminds them of painful experiences from their past. The fastest way
to stop overreactions or misperceptions about disturbances happening in adulthood now, is to get
wounded parts unhooked from the past.
The longer we work with a client, the more we know about their story. Sometimes when we encounter
a triggered part, we’ll already understand from their age and history, why they got triggered. We may
not need to spend a lot of time getting the story. It may be sufficient to get a part connected to the
Resources and feeling loved and safe, before moving on to the Chapter 11-14 stabilizing interventions.

Full Contextual Validation
Once the story is told, it helps if we can provide a full contextual validation. This includes a summary
of the wounding events, the unhelpful responses from people who should have helped, the client’s
emotional reaction to the wounding, an empathic validation, and support from the Resources. When
parts hear an insightful, attuned summary of their story from us, they know they’ve been seen, heard,
and understood. Here’s an example.
You got the one-two punch from your parents. The first punch is that they couldn’t meet your
emotional needs. POW! They didn’t teach you how to ask for what you need, and you couldn’t
trust they’d meet your needs if you did ask. When that made you angry, you’d show it by acting
out, but they didn’t wonder WHY you were angry or why you were acting out. They’d just shame
and blame you for acting out. That’s the second punch. POW! Your anger was the result of THEIR
behavior, but they punished YOU. That cycle went on and on until you were acting out in a loud,
hostile, and destructive way, and their shame, blame, and punishments got bigger and bigger. I’m
so sorry they missed the chance to really see you, understand you and actually help you! Fourteenyear-old, you were not acting out because you were bad. You were acting out because you were
misunderstood, but don’t take my word for it. What do the Resources say?

When Parts Refuse to Talk to Us
Sometimes a part we ask to speak with refuses to talk with us. This usually happens when a part expects
that we’ll be judgmental, blaming, or punishing, just like childhood authority figures were. If we can
convince the part that we’ll be open, loving, accepting, and kind, she’ll be more likely to come forward
and talk. Here’s an example of something we can say to start a dialogue with a part reluctant to connect:
If I could talk to this part, I’d say “Welcome. I’m so glad you’re here. What you think, feel, and believe
matters to me. I expect you’ve been hurting for a very long time. I’m so sorry about that. I want
you to know that I don’t want to hurt you, judge you, punish you, or get rid of you. I just want to
get to know you—to understand you. I want to help you get connected to a team of loving
Resources who can look out for you and nurture you day and night. You’re not required to talk to
me or to connect with the Resources.” I just wanted you to know what I’d say to you if you were
willing to talk. I’ll be here for you if or when you ever want to connect.

Working with Very Triggered Parts
Sometimes we encounter a wounded part who is too emotionally distraught to talk. We lean in with
calm compassion and say “I’d like to speak to the part or parts who are triggered right now. Welcome.
I’m really glad you’re here. I can see you’re really hurting right now.” We offer comforting, validating,

152

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

empathic words like, “I’m right here. I’m on your side. I’ll support you in any way I can to help you get
through this. I’m ready to listen whenever you’re ready to talk about it. I’m here for you. Take as long
as you need.”
If the Resources are already online, we ask the part “Can you see the Resources? Do they understand
why you’re upset right now?” If the Resources are not yet online, we say “Look to the right and you’ll
see the Resources approaching you. Do you see them.” When they’re seen, we ask “Do they understand
why you’re upset right now?” It helps to guide the conversation with simple YES/NO questions, so the
part can answer by nodding or shaking her head.
Once the part is in touch with the Resources, we can strengthen the connection by saying things like,
“Notice they’re here to love and support you. They understand how hard this is for you right now. Feel
their support.”
Very triggered parts are often lost in time, feeling like a hurtful event is happening now. It very important
for us to orient them to present time (see Chapter 13) and explain to them they’re reacting to a harmless
recording (see Chapter 14). This can have a profound calming effect.

Working with Intrusive Parts
Sometimes a wounded part will intrude while we’re in the middle of a healing intervention with another
part. The intrusion may be to ask for urgently needed reassurance, to ask a question, to report a flashback,
or to block processing. These intrusions always happen for a good reason. And because they bring a
sense of urgency, they get top priority. Intrusive parts can show up overtly or covertly.
 Overt Intrusion: When a part intrudes overtly, a client will say something like “A five-year-old has
just appeared. She looks worried and afraid,” or “A ten-year-old is here, upset that you’re meddling.”
When this happens, we ask “May I speak to the part?” If “yes,” we say “Welcome! I’m really glad
you’re here. You must have a very good reason for showing up right now. Tell me about that.”
 Covert Intrusion: When a part intrudes covertly it’s less obvious. The part sends a subtle signal with
symptoms like sleepiness, a headache, body tension, or distracting thoughts. To verify the symptom is
a signal from an intruding part, we ask the client, “If the sleepiness / headache / tension
could
talk, what would it say?“ or “Is a wounded part connected to your sleepiness / headache / tension ?”
If the source of the symptom does not become clear, we ask follow-up questions like these:
When exactly did you start feeling sleepy?
What were we talking about just before that started?
What were you thinking about just before that started?
Did you get enough sleep last night?
Did you have a headache when you came in?
Do you have a good reason for being distracted?
If we determine that a wounded part is covertly intruding, we say “I’d like to speak to the part
connected to the
sleepiness / headache / tension . Welcome! I’m really glad you’re here. You
must have a very good reason for showing up right now. Tell me about that.”
Whether a part shows up overtly or covertly, we listen to the very good reason for the intrusion. Once
we understand the concern, we validate it. We attune with radical acceptance. If there’s something we
don’t understand, we ask for clarification. We clear misunderstandings and fill in missing information.
Once we establish some rapport, we ask for the part’s age. The age of the part helps us put the concern
in the context of earlier life experience. When a concern appears to be handled, we ask “Is this all you
need for now?” If “yes,” we invite the part to settle into her Special Safe Place.

Chapter 10: Nurturing Interventions

153

Some concerns are simple and may be completely remedied within a few minutes. But some concerns
are complicated and take more time. Here are examples of both.
 Simple concerns: Here are two very different simple concerns and a remedy for each. (1) While I’m
in the middle of stabilizing a teen part, a toddler part intrudes. She says, “Please don’t forget me. I
want to heal too.” I offer reassurance that she won’t be forgotten. To prove that I mean it, I note in
my file that she’ll need my help soon. This provides the needed reassurance. Then I ask, “Is this all
you need for now?” When she says “yes,” I invite her to settle into her Beach Resort, then resume
work with the teen part. All this takes about two minutes. (2) When I start stabilizing a five-year-old
part, a protective fourteen-year-old makes the client very sleepy. When I talk to the fourteen-yearold, he reveals he’s worried that if younger parts heal, he won’t be important anymore and will
disappear. I assure him he’ll always be important and will not disappear. In fact, as more and more
younger parts heal, he’ll be free to heal and grow too. The Resources reassure him that they’ll be
lovingly connected to him, whatever his role is. This is a relief to hear. When I ask, “Is this all you
need for now?” he says “yes.” I invite him to settle into his Mountain Retreat, then resume work
with the five-year-old part. All this takes about 10 minutes.
 Complex concerns: Here’s an example of a complex concern. While I’m stabilizing a ten-year-old
part, the client suddenly gets a splitting headache. I find an eighteen-year-old part generating it. I
welcome him and listen to his concerns. He has a single worry about the help I’m giving the tenyear-old. This is easily remedied by clearing a misunderstanding, but he’s still agitated. He’s hurting
a lot and wants more attention. He’s worried the Resources cannot be trusted to understand him.
He doubts they will love him because his parents never could. It takes the rest of the session the
build rapport with him. The next session we resume our conversation. I clear six misunderstandings
and fill in lots of missing information. He starts to trust the Resources. I orient him to present time
and explain about the harmless recording. He feels a lot of relief. Then I ask, “Is this all you need for
now?” He says “no” and tells me he’s also worried about losing a familiar sense of identity. I explain
how healing will help him find a new sense of identity and he feels great relief. This calms him
enough that he’s ready to settle into his Mountain Retreat. All this has taken almost two whole
sessions. At the start of the next session I resume work with the ten-year-old.

Strengthening Positives with ABS
We use ABS to make big smiles bigger. There are no hard rules about how or when to apply ABS—we
must use our best judgment. I usually add ABS when a wounded part’s words, body language, and/or
facial expression suggest a profound, beneficial connection has been made with the Resources. When
we apply ABS, we say “Notice that good feeling. Take as long as you need to let it strengthen. Tell me
when it’s strengthened all the way.”

hard luck story of growing up poor. His dad was a bus driver. His mom was too ill
to work. They lived paycheck to paycheck. One day, his dad took him for a walk

him. I had a natural curiosity for science, and I liked the idea that he’d teach me
things. It would bring us closer together. I put away my video games and decided
to follow in his footsteps.

I dug into my homework and studies. Dad gave me additional books to read. He

He would say he was, but his idea of support was to shame me and withdraw if I

can do better.” He’d hiss with contempt and say, “Do I need to hire a tutor for you?”

It was.

No.

have made it my option. They would have wanted me to feel good about what I
could achieve, not shame me about what I couldn’t do.

Chapter 10 Summary
This chapter shows how we can select a target issue based on a current trigger, a recent trigger,
or a long-standing issue. Once a target is selected, we invite clients to connect to their Resource
team, then invite forward the wounded parts connected to the target issue. We encourage the
wounded parts to talk about their story while they receive lots of validation, empathy, and
support from us and the loving, attuned Resources. This helps wounded parts feel seen, heard,
and valued. It conveys to them that they matter. When wounded parts show up very upset or
scared, we modify the steps to attune to their sense of crisis, to calm them down as quickly as
possible. When wounded parts show up to intrude on work we’re doing with other parts, we
greet them, lean in, and learn what their concerns are. Addressing their concerns and meeting
their needs sends a message through the self-system that all wounded parts matter.

Chapter 11
Providing Needed Information

Wounded child parts often hold misunderstandings and faulty assumptions and, just like children, need
helpful facts and information. This chapter will explore a variety of ways we can convey information to
wounded parts. It offers helpful scripts and metaphors we can use to clear common misperceptions
and fill in missing information.

Ways to Provide Needed Information
We can help wounded parts learn needed information three ways: (1) referring them to the Resources,
(2) direct teaching with facts, and (3) encouraging self-discovery. Let’s explore each.

Referring Parts to the Resources
The Resources are especially good at conveying loving kindness to neutralize painful emotions like
shame and self-loathing. When wounded parts make comments or ask questions about self-worth, we
can refer them to the Resources. Here are some examples.
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:

Why do my parents hate me?
Ask the Resources. What do they say?
They say my parents did not get enough nurturing love when they were little, so
they don’t know how to give me what I need.

Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:

Will the Resources ever get tired of me?
Ask them. What do they say?
They say I delight them. They’ll never get tired of me. They’ll never leave.

Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:

When I made a mistake, Dad beat me. He said he had to because I was a bad child.
What do the Resources say about that?
They say just because I misbehave sometimes it doesn’t make me a bad child. They
say dad was wrong to beat me.
If the Resources had been your parents, and they’d seen you make a mistake, what
would they have done?
They would have told me I was a good girl who made a mistake. They would have
shown me how to do it right. I like that.

Wounded Part:

don’t know. For example, they often don’t know what appropriate parenting or competent caregiving
looks like. When we detect missing information and gaps in understanding, we address it directly with
a little help from the Resources. Here are some examples.

Encouraging Self-Discovery
Sometimes the most powerful insights come to wounded parts when they figure things out on their
own. We can plant seeds to make those insights blossom. There are several ways we can do this. We can
invite parts to imagine having had a skillful caregiver. We can refer parts to the adult self’s competent
caregiving experiences. We can invite parts to see a situation from someone else’s point of view, and
we can educate with a story. Here are examples of each.
Imagine Having Had a Skillful Caregiver
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Wounded Part:

Therapist:
Wounded Part:

I couldn’t stop crying after my cat died. My mother told me crap happens in life
and I needed to toughen up. She told me I needed to get over it.
Imagine you’d had a mother who was loving, empathic, and kind. What would she
have done differently?
She would have understood why I was sad. She would have comforted me while I
cried.
My older brother and I would play checkers. If I won, he’d get mad and beat me
up. When I told Dad, he said I needed to figure out how to be nice to my brother
so he’d be nice to me.
Imagine you’d had a Dad who was fiercely protective of you. What would he have
done differently?
He would have been very upset and talked to my brother about it. He would have
made sure it never happened again.

Metaphors to Explain the Processing
The following metaphors can help us explain the healing process.
Helmet Metaphor: This helps clients understand how love and support from the Resources provide a
protective cushion to soften the blows of life’s disappointments, hardships, tragedies, and losses.
When a child’s emotional needs are met well, healthy, adaptive neural architecture forms. The
brain gets wired for managing painful emotions well. When something unfortunate happens, like
the loss of a friend, that healthy neural architecture will soften the impact—like a helmet—
making it easier to recover. But when emotional needs in childhood were not met well, the wiring
needed for emotional management may not fully form. Then, when something upsetting happens,
there may be little or nothing to soften the blow. That means the upset can hit very hard and be
difficult to get over. When wounded parts make a healing connection to loving Resources, it helps
the brain build the neural architecture needed to manage painful emotions. So when a
disappointment, hardship, tragedy, or loss happens, the Resources—like a helmet—provide a
protective cushion of love and support to soften the blow.
Car Accident Metaphor: This helps clients understand that when wounded parts are finally found, seen,
and heard, they experience a huge sense of relief, even if complete healing won’t happen until later.
Imagine your car slides off a remote, icy road in the dark of night, landing upside down in a ditch.
You’re injured. You cannot move, and you cannot reach your phone. You have no idea if anyone
saw you. All you can do is wait to be rescued. The longer you wait, the more fearful you become.
When you finally hear a siren, you begin to hope. When you hear footsteps and voices, your hope
grows. Finally, you hear a tap on the window and a reassuring voice. Eventually, you’re removed
from the car, loaded into an ambulance, and driven to the Emergency Room. As you’re jetted
away, an EMS tech provides desperately needed first aid and utters the message you most need
to hear—that you’re going to be okay. You can finally breathe a deep sigh of relief. You’re injured,
and you still need surgery, physical therapy, and lots of medical attention, but you’ve been found,
and you’re on the road to recovery. When we first contact parts who are very lost, dissociated, and
wounded, it’s like they’ve been in a wrecked car for a very long time, waiting to be found. We
connect them to loving Resources, orient them to the present time, and explain about the harmless
recording. Even though this does not heal them completely, they can finally breathe a deep sigh
of relief, knowing they’ve been found. We promise to continue to work with them to help finish
the healing.

168

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Archives Metaphor: This metaphor helps clients understand why memories of old wounding experiences
get easily retriggered, and how they can come to be archived without emotional charge.
An office worker sits at a desk with an in-box on top and a file drawer below. The in-box collects
new documents, memos, and papers for immediate attention and filing. The file drawer contains
documents that are accessed daily. Down the hall is a room with filing cabinets containing archives
of occasionally accessed documents. When we get very wounded by something that we cannot
make sense of, it goes in a mental in-box—maybe for years. If we have many wounding experiences,
the in-box could fill up with painful material that doesn’t get resolved or archived. That in-box
can become a daily source of overwhelming pain and stressful emotions. Healing means handling
the painful material completely and fully so it can be filed in the right place—ultimately in the
archive room. Once it’s archived, it’s still relevant history with all the beneficial life lessons attached
to it, but it’s no longer emotionally charged.
Hippie Commune: This metaphor helps clients understand dissociation and how just connecting wounded
parts to loving Resources can be very healing.
Dissociation is like an isolated hippie commune. In the Sixties, a group of hippies moved to West
Texas to start a commune. They lived off the grid with no radios, televisions, and telephones. There
were no roads in or out of the commune. They did okay for about twenty years, but when some
of them started getting sick, they sent a team to get help. They walked for miles until they found
a road and hitched a ride to the nearest town. When they told their story, medical assistance came
from the closest big cities. Texas built roads and put up phone lines, so the commune could easily
connect to resources from other parts of the state. When the doctors arrived, they found the
hippies dressed and talked a lot like they did in the Sixties. They knew nothing about the modern
world. The isolated hippie commune is like wounded parts of self that dissociated in the past. They
are disconnected from mature adult parts of the self that can help. Therapy can help get them
connected so they can enjoy the benefits and safety of living in the present.
Convenience Store Camera: This metaphor helps clients understand how the brain makes and saves
recordings of very wounding experiences.
A convenience store video camera records all the time. Insignificant moments are disregarded,
but when something important happens like a robbery or murder, the video recording is saved.
It’s played back over and over, until the police can make sense of what happened. In the same
way, your brain makes recordings of everything that happens to you. Recordings of important
events are saved—both good and bad. A single wounding event like a sexual assault can be saved,
or a series of events like chronic parental rejection can be saved. They’re saved until we can make
sense of what happened. Until then, if something reminds us of an old wounding experience, a
painful recording of it can play back, creating the illusion that it’s still relevant and still happening
now. But old recordings are not real life. They’re completely harmless.
Checking Account: This metaphor helps clients understand that once they mobilize internal Resources
they already had, they can use them to make life better.
Mobilizing a Resource team to meet the needs of wounded parts is like finding a checkbook to a
forgotten bank account. The money had been in the account for a long time, but until the
checkbook was found, the money could not be spent.

Chapter 11: Providing Needed Information

169

Scripts and Metaphors to Validate and Empathize
The following scripts and metaphors can help us validate and empathize with wounded parts’ stories.
Double Bind: This script gives the name “double bind” to the experience of being stuck with two or
more unfavorable options. It helps wounded parts who feel trapped in a double bind feel seen, heard,
validated, and understood.
So, little one, it sounds like you were caught in a double bind. That’s when you have two or more
choices, and they’re all bad. You had to choose between _______ and _______. That must have been
a very upsetting dilemma. It wasn’t fair. I’m so sorry it happened.
Mistreated Dog: This script helps wounded parts put responsibility for their anger and mistrust where
it belongs—on the people who hurt them.
So, little one, I hear you feel ashamed for all the times you lashed out in anger. I understand you
feel completely responsible for that. Imagine for a moment a dog who is tied up and beaten with
a stick. He’s not fed enough. He’s left alone for long periods. Every human he sees is abusive and
neglectful. One day, he gets loose and wanders into someone’s yard. He sees a man with a stick.
When he approaches the dog and raises the stick, the dog attacks. Would that reaction be the
dog’s fault? You needed your parents to treat you with respect and to take your needs seriously,
but they didn’t. The anger that evokes in you is perfectly normal. With help from the Resources,
we can help you heal the wounds that made you angry.
Tending a Garden: This script helps wounded parts understand that mistreated children often fail to
meet their full potential because of unskillful parenting. Their shortcomings do not reflect their intrinsic
worth or potential for future growth.
So, little one, imagine two gardeners, Chip and Diane. Chip tends his vegetable garden every day,
planting, watering, or weeding. He carefully maintains the fence that keeps out the rabbits and
raccoons. He invests a lot of time and money in his garden. His neighbor, Diane, plants vegetables
too, but she forgets to water and weed. She doesn’t have a fence, so wild animals dig up her
plants. She invests very little. When it comes time to pick the vegetables, would you expect Chip’s
and Diane’s harvest to be the same or different? Chip will have a better harvest, because he
invested more in his garden. You needed your parents to invest in you, like Chip invests in his
garden. You needed them to take your needs seriously, but they didn’t. Like Diane’s garden
struggles, you struggle. What if Diane hired Chip to tend her garden? Would you expect that in
time her garden would flourish too? The Resources are prepared to invest in you and help you
grow to meet your full potential and flourish.
One-Two Punch: This script gives wounded parts a name for the horror of being wounded by someone
and then being shamed, blamed, humiliated, or punished for a legitimate aversive reaction to it. This
helps wounded parts feel seen and heard.
So, little one, it sounds like you got the one-two punch. The first punch came from the wounding
experience
. The second punch came afterward when
person shamed, blamed, humiliated,
punished, etc.
you for
crying, acting out, protesting, etc. . That must have felt really bad. It
wasn’t fair. I’m so sorry it happened.
Positive Affect Intolerance: This script is for wounded parts who are afraid of positive experiences and
emotions. It helps them feel seen, heard, and understood.
So, little one, it makes perfect sense that you’d avoid feeling good if you expect something bad
will happen every time you do. Avoiding good feelings has been very protective! Feel free to keep
doing it as long as you need to, and while you’re doing that, notice the Resources will be here to
love and support you. They’re going to be here for you no matter what kind of mood you’re in.

170

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

They’re going to accept you exactly as you are right now—without conditions. How does that
sound? When you were younger, lots of bad things happened when you were in a good mood. If
being in a good mood was safe now—especially now that you have supportive Resources—would
you be interested in testing it out?
Therapy Sabotage: This script honors wounded parts who are driven to sabotage therapy.
So, little one, if you must sabotage therapy in order to feel safe, keep doing it. I want you to feel
safe! Notice the Resources are here to love and support you just as you are regardless of what
you believe or what you need to do to feel safe. While you’re standing your ground, tell me your
story. I’d really like to understand.

Scripts That Fill In Missing Information
Assumptions wounded child parts make about self and world are based on their limited understanding.
The scripts below offer a way to fill in missing information that might be fueling fears about the therapy
process and outcome.
Grief Can Process Through: Some wounded parts do not know that their grief can process through.
This script explains there’s a process for working through grief, so it’s not always a burden to bear.
So, little one, if you’ve never watched someone process through grief, you might believe that the
only way to manage the agonizing pain of loss is to block it out or avoid it. Does that sound right?
You might not know there’s a way to process through the pain of grief and loss so it can lessen
and fade out over time. Grieving is a process with a beginning, middle, and end. The beginning
and the middle are the most painful. The process works best when you have loving support—
someone to listen to your story while you feel your painful emotions. At the same moment it’s
distressing to feel the loss, it feels good to have their support. Your loving, empathic Resources
will gladly support you in working all the way through your grief. They don’t need you to rush
it—they’re willing to be present for you as long as it takes, and there is a giant treasure to be
found when the grief finally processes through. The treasure comes in the form of lessons learned
and wisdom gained. You’ll be better prepared to weather any future loss or grief. But don’t take
my word for it, ask the Resources. What do they say?
Relationship Breaks Can Be Repaired: Some wounded parts do not know relationship breaks can be
repaired. This script teaches them that there’s a process for repairing breaks so they can recover from
relationship friction.
A break happens when there’s friction between people—often because someone said or did something
hurtful. The hurt could be from something verbal or nonverbal, intentional or unintentional, real
or misperceived. Breaks can be repaired. It takes two willing people to repair a break. A break
cannot be repaired by ignoring it, discounting it, or trivializing it. “Making nice” will not repair a
break. Unrepaired breaks weaken attachment bonds. To clear a break, we get in our most adult
self and approach with an open mind and open heart. With a kind and curious voice, we ask
questions like “What were you thinking when you did/said _________? What did you want to
accomplish? What did you expect would happen?” We share our own reaction to the person’s
hurtful behavior, but we also aim to understand the other’s point of view. We clear any
misunderstandings and apologize when necessary. The repair should result in a win-win where
we all feel seen, heard, valued, understood, and, when necessary, forgiven. Some breaks can be
completely repaired in a single conversation. Sometimes complete repair occurs over time as
healing progresses with repeated reparative actions. Once a break is repaired, the emotional
connection is restored, and the attachment bond is strengthened. But don’t take my word for it,
ask the Resources. What do they say?

Chapter 11: Providing Needed Information

171

Expressing Anger Is Not Always Bad: Some wounded parts do not know that expressing anger can be
good. This script helps expand their understanding of anger so they can acknowledge and appreciate
the value of their own angry feelings.
Anger can be expressed skillfully or unskillfully. Any anger expressed with disrespect (for self or
other) is coming from a wounded part who’s triggered. At that moment, the angry person is lost
in unresolved wounds from the past. But anger can also be skillfully felt and expressed by a
person’s most adult self. It can even be conveyed respectfully with love and affection. Skillful
expression of anger can be essential for optimal survival. It doesn’t have to be loud or even verbal.
Sometimes it’s best expressed with quiet action. When we’re angry with someone we value, we
can try to repair the break. When break repair is not possible, skillful expression of anger means
respectfully setting firm boundaries. It may mean distancing a little, a lot, or when possible,
completely ending an anger-evoking relationship. Skillful expression of anger is an act of power—
the power to repair a break, seek safety, distance from someone toxic, or get justice. Sometimes
the skillful expression of anger fails to lead to an ideal outcome. But healing can happen when
we talk about our angry feelings with loving, compassionate, validating, and understanding
family, friends, or therapists. But don’t take my word for it, ask the Resources. What do they say?
It’s Okay to Make Mistakes: Some wounded parts do not know it’s okay to make mistakes. This script
gives them permission to be imperfect and to lean on the Resources for support and loving correction.
So, little one, childhood is an eighteen-year adventure of constantly making mistakes that require
loving correction. I hear that your parents shamed you for making mistakes and expected you to
be perfect. Children should never be expected to be perfect!! It’s unachievable. Instead, they should
be loved and cherished with all their imperfections—especially when they make mistakes. I’m so
sorry that didn’t happen for you. Now the Resources are here to love you exactly as you are with
all your strengths and weaknesses, no matter how many mistakes you’ve made in the past or will
make in the future. They’re here to lovingly help you learn from mistakes so you can grow to meet
your full potential. But don’t take my word for it, ask the Resources. What do they say?
Children Need Loving Correction: Some wounded parts do not know that loving correction is the
perfect response to misbehavior or mistakes. This script teaches the downside of punishment and the
importance of loving correction.
Caregivers who shame or punish a child for misbehavior believe it will motivate the child to stop
doing the unwanted behavior. For example, if shamed enough times for a messy bedroom, a child
will keep her bedroom clean but not because she wants to. She’ll do it because she’s afraid not to.
Motivating a child’s behavior with fear of shame and punishment comes at a cost. It creates an
emotional break with the caregiver. It erodes the child’s self-worth and self-esteem. It can
motivate other misbehaviors like inflicting harm on self or others. A child who misbehaves does
not need shame or punishment; she needs loving correction. She needs to know she’s loved and
valued no matter what she does. Children always misbehave for a reason—often because of
unmet needs. Loving correction meets the physical, emotional, or informational needs driving the
misbehavior. It feels validating and healing. Children raised with loving correction behave well
because they naturally and effortlessly want to. I’m so sorry you did not get enough loving
correction in childhood. Thankfully, the Resources can give it to you now. But don’t take my word
for it, ask them. What do they say?
Discerning If Someone Is Trustworthy: Some wounded parts do not know there’s a way to discern
people who are trustworthy from those who are not. This script teaches them how to tell the difference.
This is especially helpful information when wounded parts are inclined to mistrust everyone including
robust Resources.

172

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

People earn our trust or mistrust as we observe them over time. I’m going to tell you how to notice
the difference between people who can be trusted and people who can’t. I’ll tell you about
trustworthy people first. People can earn our trust when we see they’re consistently reliable—
when they do what they say they’re going to do most of the time. When they sincerely apologize
after a misstep, mistake, or broken promise. When they’re eager to acknowledge and learn from
mistakes and are ready to be vulnerable and accept personal responsibility. When they repair
breaks. When it’s distressing for them to see others harmed or exploited. When they’re
understanding and compassionate, inclined to help and support those in need. When they follow
the Golden Rule. In contrast, people should earn our mistrust when we see they’re comfortable
exploiting others. When they feel entitled to get whatever they want, even if it’s hurtful to others.
When they’re dishonest. When they get caught in a lie and then get angry, blame others, or tell
more lies. When they cannot apologize after a misstep, mistake, or broken promise. When they
make the same mistakes over and over, unable to learn and grow. When they get highly defensive
or blame others for their own mistakes. When they cannot tolerate unfavorable feedback, no
matter how respectfully delivered. When they cannot tolerate being seen as average, below
average, or vulnerable and must be perceived as superior. When they don’t care about others who
are hurting or in need. When they do not respect other’s boundaries. When they have a friendly
public persona and a hateful private persona. When they treat someone respectfully to their face
but disrespectfully behind their back. When they have a “me versus them” mentality—perceiving
others as competitors when they’re not. When they cannot or will not repair breaks or follow the
Golden Rule. But don’t take my word for it. What do the Resources say about this?
Misinterpretation of Events: Some wounded parts do not know that the way they were treated in
childhood subconsciously influences how they interpret current events. This script invites them to
consider how they might interpret current events differently if they’d gotten enough love and support
in childhood. (See Neural Networks, Ego States, and How We Make Interpretations section, page 176.)
So, little one, imagine you’d been raised in a big family with lots of grown-ups who were very nice
to you. Whenever you misbehaved, they’d talk to you about it with loving eyes and a gentle voice.
Whether you were in a good mood or a bad mood, they always want you to feel loved and wanted,
so you’d grow up feeling emotionally safe and secure. If that had happened, you’d have developed
a BIG neural network to hold all the family’s loving, supportive behaviors. Instead, your family
was unsupportive. As you grew up, your brain developed a BIG neural network to hold all your
family’s unkind, rejecting behaviors. So, when a stressful event happens like
, you
interpret it through your BIG neural network and conclude
. But imagine you’d grown
up in a loving, supportive family. Would you have drawn the same conclusion? The Resources
would like to meet your needs so well for you now that you can develop a BIG neural network to
hold all their loving support for you. Then, when something puzzling or upsetting happens, you’ll
be able to draw the right conclusions—conclusions that include the conviction that, no matter
what, you have value. But don’t take my word for it, ask the Resources. What do they say?

Scripts That Clear Up Misunderstandings
In childhood, clients develop beliefs and assumptions about who they are, how others will behave, and
what they must do to stay connected and safe. These beliefs are often misunderstandings that interfere
with the course of therapy. The scripts below offer remedies for some of the most common
misunderstandings that come up.
Fear of Losing a Familiar Sense of Identity: Some wounded parts fear that healing will take away their
familiar sense of identity. This script teaches that healing can help them replace an unhealthy sense of
identity with one that’s healthy.

Chapter 11: Providing Needed Information

173

So, little one, are you afraid of losing a familiar sense of identity? If YES: I completely understand.
Fortunately, you’ll never be without a sense of identity. Right now your sense of identity is
connected to anxiety / depression / self-loathing . While you maintain your old familiar identity,
the Resources will help create a new one, based on
inner peace / safety / self-love . Anything
of value from your old identity will be saved and anything you no longer need will fade away. But
don’t take my word for it, ask the Resources. What do they say?
Fear of Losing a Valued Coping Strategy: Some wounded parts believe that healing will rob them of
valued coping strategies like hiding, confronting, or numbing, leaving them defenseless. This script
offers reassurance that healing will not take away a valued coping strategy; it will give them more
options to choose from.
So, little one, this healing work will not take away any valued coping strategies, but it will help
you acquire many more options for coping. When you’re stressed, you could cope with your
familiar hiding / confronting / numbing / eating , or you could cope in a different way. Sometimes
coping with the old familiar way is the very best option. Sometimes it isn’t. Healing means the
Resources will be here for you when you’re stressed. They can help you choose the best coping
strategy on a case-by-case basis. But don’t take my word for it, ask them. What do they say?
Fear of Losing a Necessary Motivator: Some wounded parts believe that healing will take away fears
they need to motivate achievement like fear of shame, rejection, humiliation, or failure. This script
teaches them that healthy positives—like pride in accomplishment—can also be effective motivators.
So, little one, I’m hearing that fear has motivated you to accomplish things, and you’re worried
healing will take that important motivation away. I completely understand. Imagine you want to
motivate a donkey to walk forward. You can beat him with a stick, so he’ll walk forward to avoid
being hit, or you can stand in front of him holding out a carrot, so he’ll walk forward to get it and
eat it. The stick motivates with fear of pain. The carrot motivates with a desire for pleasure. Right
now, you’re motivated by fear. What if you could be motivated by pleasure instead? What if you
were motivated to accomplish things because achievement just feels good? What if the Resources
delighted in you and your accomplishments? Would that interest you?
Burden of Responsibility for Therapy Success: Some wounded parts believe they’re responsible for
making therapy go right. This can evoke a fear of failure that can block processing. This script teaches
that the burden of responsibility for therapy success is on the Resources and the therapist, not the
wounded parts.
So, little one, I hear you believe it’s your responsibility to make the therapy go right. I’m happy to
tell you—this is not your responsibility. The burden of responsibility for the therapy going right is
on me and the Resources. You’re not expected to do anything besides show up and be authentic.
It’s the Resources’ job to make it safe for you to tell your story and make sure you feel seen, heard,
and valued. But don’t take my word for it, ask them. What do they say?
Burden of Responsibility for Managing Life Challenges: Some wounded child parts believe it’s their
responsibility to manage the challenges of adult life, and they’re already doing it well. They may fear
the change therapy brings will undermine their important work. This fear can block processing. This
script teaches wounded parts that the burden of responsibility for managing the challenges of adult
life belongs to the most adult self.
So, little one, I hear you believe it’s your responsibility to manage the challenges of adult life. That’s
a really important job. I expect you take it very seriously and strive to do it right. I wonder if this
has become burdensome for you? If YES: I’m happy to tell you that you don’t have to shoulder
this responsibility. The burden of handling life’s challenges belongs to the most adult self. She has
a lot of experience and wisdom and is able to make good choices. If you will let the Resources

174

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

help you heal, you’ll be free to go play or evolve in whatever ways you need to, while the adult
self manages adult life. But don’t take my word for it, ask them. What do they say?
Burden of Responsibility for Childhood Wounding: Many wounded child parts believe they were
responsible for the wounding done to them or witnessed by them. Their sense of shame about this
can block processing. This script teaches that the responsibility for the behaviors of abusive, neglectful,
rejecting, enmeshing, or misattuned adults lies solely with those adults.
So, little one, I hear you believe you were responsible for the unkind way your mother / father _
behaved. You were not. Adults are responsible for their own unkind behavior. It’s never okay for
adults to be unkind to children. Adults should always respect, cherish, and protect children no
matter what the children do or say, no matter what mood they’re in. Adults are responsible for
managing their own emotions and impulses so children can feel safe. But don’t take my word for
it, ask the Resources. What do they say?
20/20 Intervention: This script is for wounded parts who believe that they’re responsible for the
childhood wounding they endured or witnessed. It invites them to self-discover how impartial
observers like a TV audience would react empathically to hearing their story. It helps them place the
responsibility where it belongs.
So, little one, I hear you believe you were responsible for unkind things done to you / others in
childhood. Imagine that while you were growing up with your family, a 20/20 TV show was filming
everything with hidden cameras all over the house. They were able to film exactly what happened.
When they broadcast your story, millions of people see it on TV. These viewers write to 20/20 to
express their reaction. What do their letters say? Do they feel empathy for you? Do they believe
you were responsible? Who do they believe was responsible?
Elephant Taming: Some wounded parts believe that they’re just as powerless now as they were in the
past. This script explains why they feel powerless now even though they’re not.
So, little one, it’s no surprise that you FEEL very powerless even though you actually have power
now. Let me tell you how elephants are tamed. A baby elephant is tethered to a stake in the
ground. He repeatedly pulls on the rope to try to get away, but he’s not strong enough to pull out
the stake and get away. Later, when he’s full grown, he is strong enough to easily get away, but
he doesn’t try. The memory of trying and failing to pull out the stake tells him it won’t work. His
erroneous BELIEF that he’s still powerless keeps him from getting away. What does this story teach
you about yourself?
Parka in Texas: Some wounded parts believe their safety depends on holding on to coping strategies
they needed in childhood, even if they are clearly detrimental now.
So, little one, I want to tell you about an Eskimo who lived by the North Pole. He wore a parka all
year round to stay warm and safe. Without it, he might have frozen to death. Then he moved to
South Texas where it’s warm most of the time. But he kept his parka on all the time, even in the
summer heat. He believed that even in hot weather, wearing a parka was essential for survival.
What did the Eskimo need to know? I would want the Eskimo to know that South Texas is not like
the North Pole. That he could stay warm enough without his parka most of the time. That if he
put his parka in a closet, he could take it out and wear it whenever the weather got cold. What
does this story teach you about yourself?
A Grain of Truth Legitimizes a Lie: Some wounded parts believe that the unkind messages they
received in the past are true. This script helps them see that a hurtful message can pair a truth with a
lie. For example, after a child does something that upsets her mother, her father says “You’ve upset
your mother—that’s proof you’re a bad girl.” His message has a truth (she upset her mother) paired
with a lie (that’s proof you’re a bad girl). When a truth and lie are conveyed together, it can create the

Chapter 11: Providing Needed Information

175

illusion that the lie is true. This script explains this to wounded parts. When they can clearly see the lie
they’ve been believing, they may stop believing it.
So, little one, (wounding person)
told you (hurtful message) . This message contained a
lie, but I understand why you’ve believed it. This message also contains a grain of truth. It’s true
that (name the truth) , but the lie was (name the lie) . You’re free now to believe what’s true
without believing the lie too. But don’t take my word for it, ask the Resources. What do they say?
Confusion Paired with Shame: Some wounded parts believe that if they feel shame, they must have
done something wrong. This script helps them understand that sometimes shame can come from a
conditioned response to confusion.
So, little one, sometimes in life, we experience shame and confusion together. For example, a child
finger paints on a wall, expecting Daddy will be pleased, but when he reacts in anger, she feels
confused and ashamed. If these kinds of experiences happen often in childhood, the brain can pair
confusion with shame. That means experiences later in life that evoke confusion will also evoke
shame, even if there’s no reason to feel ashamed. Did something like that happen to you? Ideally,
confusion will evoke curiosity and a healthy, appropriate search for understanding. Ask the
Resources if they’d be happy to reassure and comfort you whenever you’re confused, if they’d like
to help you make sense of what’s confused you so you don’t also feel ashamed. What do they say?
Healthy, Positive Self-Regard: Some wounded parts believe that a feeling of self-esteem is bad. They
don’t know the difference between healthy, positive self-regard and selfish arrogance. This script teaches
them the difference.
So, little one, I hear you believe if you think highly of yourself, you’re not humble, which is bad.
Let’s talk about the difference between people with a healthy, positive self-regard and people with
selfish arrogance. People with healthy, positive self-regard see dignity and value in self and others.
They believe everyone deserves to be treated with respect—everyone deserves to thrive. They aim
to see their personal strengths and weaknesses accurately and to improve on their weaknesses
whenever possible. People with selfish arrogance have an inflated sense of self-importance.
They’re focused solely on their own needs and desires. They don’t mind hurting others to get what
they want. They do not see dignity and value in others and lack empathy for others’ pain, weakness,
or suffering. They’re unable to see their own personal weaknesses accurately, so it’s hard for them
to learn and grow. But don’t take my word for it, ask the Resources. What do they say?
Fear of Setting Boundaries: Some wounded parts believe that setting boundaries is not okay and will
inevitably result in shame, rejection, or punishment. This script teaches them the value of setting good
boundaries, what it looks like, and how to deal with people who violate boundaries.
Setting good boundaries means establishing healthy limits on the way people interact with us. If
those limits are violated, there’s a consequence. For example, in the middle of an altercation with
someone, I might calmly say, “If you cannot discuss this respectfully with me, I’m going to end
this conversation now.” Good boundaries can be rigid or flexible. There’s a time and a place for
each type. Here’s a flexible boundary: “I know you’re capable of discussing this respectfully. Let’s
try it again later once you’ve calmed down.” Here’s a rigid boundary: “You’ve proven time and
time again you cannot discuss your upsets respectfully. This relationship is not going to work. It
ends here.” Setting boundaries is our way of asserting that we deserve to be treated with respect
and dignity and that our needs matter. We have a right to say “no” to requests that feel
unreasonable or hurtful to us. Setting and enforcing good boundaries helps us maintain the
healthiest possible relationships with the people around us. We should aim to surround ourselves
with trustworthy people who respect our appropriate boundaries, and distance ourselves from
people who willfully violate them. Setting good boundaries is not only okay, it’s essential for our
good mental health. But don’t take my word for it, ask the Resources. What do they say?

176

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Basket of Kittens or Puppies: Some wounded parts cannot imagine having a positive relationship with
the Resources simply because they don’t know what that would be like. This script helps wounded
parts self-discover what it might be like to have a loving connection with their Resources.
So little one, close your eyes. Now look down. You’re going to see a basket of kittens (or puppies).
Do you see them? If YES: Do they look cute? If YES: Do you feel a tug of affection for them? If YES:
Lean over and pick one up. Do you have an impulse to nurture and protect it? If YES: If it was
hungry would you want to feed it? If YES: If it would hurt would you want to help it heal? If YES:
Do you want to share some delightful playtime with it? If YES: This kind of affection you feel for
this kitten (or puppy) is the same affection the Resources have for you. They want to be here for
you, to delight in you, nourish you, and protect you. But don’t take my word for it, ask the them.
What do they say?

Neural Networks, Ego States, and How We Make Interpretations
The brain is made up of lots of cells called neurons. Lots of interconnected neurons form neural networks.
As we grow up, things we learn get stored in neural networks. That includes what we learn about ourselves
and our world through our relationships with childhood caregivers. Caregivers can be affirming or hurtful.
The frequency and intensity of their affirming or hurtful messages determines the size these neural
networks will be by the time we reach adulthood.
A child who grows up being frequently praised, validated, and encouraged by loving caregivers will
develop a large neural network holding this self-affirming information. This neural network holds healthy
parts of self. Likewise, a child who grows up being frequently invalidated, shamed, and discouraged by
unkind caregivers will develop a large neural network holding this self-debasing information. This neural
network holds many wounded parts of self.
All this matters because when things happen to us and around us, our first interpretations get made
through our biggest neural networks. Consider the following scenario:
Juan, Javier, and Ricardo were team members on an important project for work. They’d invested a
lot of time and effort into making it successful. They sat in a conference room with Maria, their
boss. This was an important moment for them. They had one hour to give her their report. She
would appraise their progress and decide about funding an extension. They were hoping for a good
outcome. Ten minutes into their meeting she took a phone call. She said into the phone, “I’m so
glad you called. No, I’m not too busy to talk. I’m in a meeting now but talking to you is more
important.” She left the room. The men waited for an hour, but she did not return.
By the end of the hour, each man drew a conclusion about what happened. Their largest neural networks,
forged from their childhood attachment experiences, colored their interpretations.
 Ricardo grew up with two loving, supportive parents. He was very confident about the quality of his
work and the value of the team. His healthy adult self was disappointed that Maria had left but did
not take it personally at all. He wondered if she may have had a good reason to take the call.
 Javier grew up with a loving father and a rejecting mother. He had some wounded child parts who
felt unloved and insecure. When Maria walked out, these wounded parts felt confused, rejected,
and hurt. At the same time, a healthy adult part resisted taking it personally.
 Juan grew up with two hostile, rejecting, neglectful parents who called him a loser. He had lots of
wounded child parts who felt like a failure. These parts felt devastated by Maria’s behavior, because
it reinforced their belief that Juan was not important and would never succeed.

Chapter 11: Providing Needed Information

177

Clearing Up Misinterpretations
When we have a stressful experience like this, it will be interpreted by our largest neural networks first.
If those networks hold lots of attachment wounds, the wounded parts connected to them will be
activated. This explains why some clients are so easily drawn to very negative interpretations about
things that happen—whether they want to be or not. We can explain this to clients in a way that can
validate, normalize, and neutralize their reactions, and lead them to important insights. Here’s an example
of how we can do that by telling a story:

He must have gotten an annoying text.

I made a mistake that upset him.

She grew up with nice people.

And Mavis developed a BIG neural network to hold all her family’s loving, supportive
behaviors. And it got bigger each year. When your boss walked out of the conference
