Chapter 11 Summary
Wounded child parts need to be accurately informed, just like children do. Most have been
attached to a set of false assumptions about self and world for a long time. This chapter outlines
many ways we can guide wounded child parts to shift these assumptions. For example, we can
get the Resources to teach them, we can teach them directly, or we can help them self-discover
information they’re missing. Most of the chapter is helpful scripts and metaphors. Some are for
explaining DNMS concepts, some are for filling in missing information, and some are for clearing
misunderstandings. It also provides a example of how we can lovingly challenge wounded parts’
misinterpretations. It’s a treasure trove of psycho-educational interweaves.

Chapter 12
Managing Enmeshment

What is Enmeshment?
Children need caregivers who support them in becoming empowered, wise, self-directed adults with
a unique sense of self. Children who are enmeshed with their parents fail to get that support. The notion of
enmeshment often evokes images of an overinvolved, intrusive parent who actively tries to live vicariously
through a child. The DNMS defines enmeshment more broadly. It’s defined as the preoccupation with
another person’s mood or well-being to the degree that it inhibits anxiety-free play and exploration. The
needs of self are perceived as less important than the needs of the other.
Within this broader definition, enmeshment can also occur when it’s not being actively cultivated by a
parent. For example, a child may be preoccupied by a parent’s depression, drinking binges, suicide
threats, homicide threats, anorexia, or debilitating medical condition, even if the parent is not beseeching
the child to meet his or her needs.
A wounded child part enmeshed with a parent may misperceive that she is the parent, that she cannot
exist without the parent, or that the parent cannot exist without her. The part may say something like
“I don’t know who I’d be without my mother,” “If I don’t stay close to my mother something bad will
happen to her,” or “I’m responsible for my mother’s well-being.” Child parts may believe that parentchild enmeshment is normal and adaptive. They may fear the alternative is complete emotional
disconnection. This is especially common when parents react to autonomy-seeking behaviors with
punishment, neglect, or rejection.
Wounded parts who believe enmeshment either solves a problem or protects someone else, may block
healing work they believe threatens the enmeshment. The DNMS has two interventions laser-focused
on addressing enmeshment. They include the Enmeshment Reversal and Octopus Pictures. They can
be used together or separately. I describe each one below.
For a worksheet that will guide you through these intervention steps while working with clients, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

Enmeshment Reversal Intervention
The Enmeshment Reversal intervention shows an enmeshed part she is actually separate from the parent
by inviting her to self-discover important, irrefutable differences. Whenever a wounded child part says
something that hints of enmeshment, like “It’s my job to keep my mother feeling happy,” we can apply
this simple intervention. It takes only a few minutes, so even when we’re not sure if enmeshment is an
issue, it’s worth trying. If a wounded part is not enmeshed, it will yield no benefit, but when enmeshment
is an issue—which is often—this intervention will pay off big!

180

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

We ask the enmeshed part a few questions that help contrast differences between the parent and the
child. If we don’t know the part’s age, we must get it first, so we know what kind of questions to ask.
Here are some sample questions we ask about the enmeshed caregiver.
So, little one, at age X , did your

mother / father

drink coffee? Did you drink coffee?

Did your

mother / father

cook dinner? Did you cook dinner?

Did your

mother / father

drive the car? Did you drive the car?

mother / father

mother / father

mother / father

Did you go to daycare? Did your

We tailor the questions to the age at which the part got stuck. For example, a question like “Could you
reach the high cabinets in the kitchen?” would work for young parts but not teen parts, while a question
like “Did you go to the mall with your friends?” would work for teen parts but not young parts. Once
we’ve made four or five positive contrasts, we ask:
Do you see you were a completely separate person from your
separate body?

mother / father , in a completely

The answer to this should be “yes.” If not, we ask more contrasting questions until we get a “yes” answer,
and then we ask:
Is that a new idea?
A part who’s been enmeshed will say with amazement, “Wow, yes, this is a new idea!” This new awareness
can be enlightening, empowering, and transformative. To verify that, we ask:
What’s it like to think about this?
Most enmeshed parts are relieved to understand this. Occasionally an enmeshed part will deny this is
a new idea, but we may see important shifts follow, nonetheless.

Octopus Pictures Intervention
Enmeshed parts often fear that losing an enmeshed connection means having no connection. When a
targeted issue involves an enmeshed caregiver, this fear can lead to blocked processing. To clear the
block, we help wounded parts self-discover that the opposite of an enmeshed connection is not no
connection; it’s a healthy connection. We say:
So, little one, picture two octopuses sitting side by side with all their tentacles knotted together. What
facial expression do they have—relaxed or anxious?
The most common answer is “anxious.” We then ask:
Now picture two octopuses sitting side by side but with all their tentacles free to move about. Are
they relaxed or anxious?
The most common answer is “relaxed.” We then say:
The opposite of an enmeshed connection is not no connection, it’s a healthy connection. But don’t
take my word for it, ask the Resources. What do they say?

Chapter 12: Managing Enmeshment

181

Robust Resources will agree. We then say:
Ask the Resources if they’re able to make a healthy connection to you now—the kind you wanted
with your mother / father but never got. What do they say?
Wounded parts are usually relieved that the Resources are willing and able to make a healthy connection
to them now. This helps lessen the enmeshment grip with the parent.

Other Helpful Interventions
These two interventions can help stabilize wounded parts by loosening the grip of enmeshment, but
additional interventions may be needed to reinforce this shift. Enmeshed parts will feel added relief
when we orient them to present time (see Chapter 13), and explain they’ve been reacting to harmless
recordings (see Chapter 14).
For example, my 40-year-old client Nora struggled with enmeshment. When she was ten years old her
father was suicidal. She was preoccupied with his well-being and felt personally responsible for keeping
him alive. He’d convey to her the nonverbal message “I need you to lift my mood and give me the will
to live. If you can’t, I’ll kill myself.” At the same time her mother conveyed the verbal message “You’re
responsible for stopping your father from killing himself.” Nora had a ten-year-old part who was deeply
enmeshed with her father.
I walked the ten-year-old part through the Enmeshment Reversal, and it helped a lot. But she was still
worried about her father. She did not know he’d died of cancer five years earlier. So, I oriented her to
present time, then got the Resources to tell her about her father’s passing and help her grieve. They
reassured her his death was not her fault. That was a huge relief. But the ten-year-old part was still
feeling twinges of guilt. I explained that her brain had made recordings of the two parental messages
that held her responsible for her father’s well-being. I explained those recordings were made 30 years
ago. They were not real life and could do no harm now. That helped the ten-year-old tune out the
messages, and she felt a huge burden life.

Present-Day Enmeshments
Some adult clients are in enmeshed relationships now. For example, a client could be enmeshed with
her own children, unaware of the negative consequences. A client might be enmeshed with a spouse
who engages in dangerous or self-destructive behavior. Or a young newlywed could be enmeshed
with her intrusive mother, fearing setting good boundaries would create family friction.
Often adulthood enmeshment has roots in unresolved childhood enmeshment. When the early
enmeshment bonds are broken and replaced with a loving, attuned connection to the Resources, the
dynamics of adulthood enmeshments can shift and change.

Chapter 12 Summary
This chapter opens with the DNMS definition of enmeshment: the preoccupation with another
person’s mood or well-being to the degree it inhibits anxiety-free play and exploration. The needs
of self are perceived as less important than the needs of the other. Wounded child parts who are
stuck in an enmeshing childhood relationship can fear healing. They may worry that healing
will have negative consequences. For example, it may break an important connection, it may
upset someone else, or it may disturb their sense of identity. This chapter offers two very loving,
gentle interventions that help wounded parts self-discover who they are, so they can begin to
comfortably think of themselves as separate and distinct from the enmeshing person. This can
lead to a profound sense of relief.

Chapter 13
Orienting to Present Time

We are taught to orient clients to the present time when they decompensate. For example, we may
invite the client to take deep breaths, feel her body in the chair, or look around the room to see where
she is. This can be very helpful for bringing a disoriented, distraught client into the present moment.
The DNMS Orient to Present Time is different. It’s for the majority of child parts who feel powerless,
very stuck in their painful past, believing the wounding is still happening now. It helps them self-discover
they’re no longer in the past and brings them into the safety of the present. It helps bring them out of
trance. It’s not merely a grounding exercise—it’s actually powerful healing! We orient parts to the
present with two self-discovery interventions: Invitation to See Evidence of the Present and Adult Body
Intervention. They can be used together or separately. I describe each one below.
For a worksheet that will guide you through these intervention steps while working with clients, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

Invitation to See Evidence of the Present
We ask wounded parts questions that get them to notice the wounding past is not happening now. I
have listed below some of the questions I often ask, with notes about how they help. If we know a
wounded part’s age, we call them out with it (for example, “So, four-year-old…”). Otherwise, we can
use a more general moniker like “little one.”
So, little one, where do you live?

The more dissociated a part is, the more certain
she’ll feel she’s still living in her childhood home.

So, little one, look out the window. Does that
look like childhood home ?

Seeing unexpected scenery out the window will pull
the part a little into the present.

So, little one, that painful childhood experience
had a beginning, middle, and end. How many
years has it been since it ended?

This may be the first time the part has considered
that it’s over and in the past. She may be surprised
to calculate how long it’s been.

So, little one, if that frightening experience
happened now, what could you do now to protect
yourself that you couldn’t do back then?

We’re looking for answers like “call the police,” “lock
the doors,” and “run away.” This helps parts
recognize they have power now that they didn’t
have when they were young.

So, little one, how long has it been since you last
saw wounding person(s) ?

It’s often been years. Some hurtful people are now
deceased. Wounded parts often don’t know this.

186

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

So, little one, how long has it been since you
lived with wounding person(s) ?

Most clients live independently of the parents who
hurt them. Parts are relieved to discover this.

When are you going to see them again? OR…
When are you going to live with them again?

The most common answer, “never,” brings a huge
sense of relief.

So, little one, if wounding person(s) . wanted
to move in with you now to do those wounding
things to you again, would you have to let them?

The most common answer is “no.” Parts are usually
delighted to see they now have power and choices
about their own living space.

So, little one, look around this room. Is this the
room where all that bad stuff happened?

Especially helpful when a wounded part is stuck in a
flashback, feeling lots of body sensations.

So, little one, look at the driver’s license in
your purse/wallet. Did you have a driver’s
license back then?

Very young parts will be surprised to discover they
have a driver’s license. This discovery will pull them a
little into the present.

How did you get a driver’s license?

When parts remember taking their driving test, it
helps them see they’re not young anymore.

So, little one, how did you get here today? Did
you drive the car? Could you drive at age ____?

If the client arrived by public transit, we can ask
“Could you take a bus by yourself back then?”

So, little one, look at your hands. Wiggle your
fingers. Do you see you can wiggle your adult
fingers? Is this what you expected to see?

Showing child parts they can animate their adult
hands helps pull them a little into the present.

So, little one, do you see that wedding ring?
What does that mean?

If client is not wearing a wedding ring, she may be
wearing other jewelry the child parts can notice.

Were you married back then when all that
bad stuff happened? How long have you
been married?

When parts remember getting married, they selfdiscover they’re not young anymore.

So, little one, touch the bridge of your nose. Do
you feel something on your nose? What is that?
Did you have glasses when you were age ____?

This is for clients who wear glasses.

So, little one, at age ____, could you touch the top
of a door? Stand next to that door now, and put
your hand up high. Can you touch the top now?

Helps parts notice how physically tall they are
compared to their childhood height.

So, little one, can you see we’re together in the
same room now? Was I with you back then when
all that bad stuff happened? If you can see me in
front of you now, are you still back there?

Gives parts another way to see they’re no longer in
the wounding experiencing.

So, little one, can you see the Resources are here
for you now? Were they with you back then when
all that bad stuff happened? If you can see them
around you now, are you still back there?

Gives parts another way to see they’re no longer in
the wounding experiencing.

We ask questions like these until the wounded parts get an “aha” moment. That may happen with one
or two questions, but very dissociated parts may need many more. This is just a sampling of questions.
We can make up many more questions, and tailor them for the maximum effect on the wounded part
we’re talking to.

part feels a profound transformation at the conclusion of this, we can strengthen the good feeling with

Because the client’s healthy adult self already knows she’s in an adult body, we direct all these questions
to wounded parts only. If a child part seems insulted by the intervention, we must check to see if the
client’s adult self is answering.

Orienting Wounded Part to the Present when Adult Self is Dependent
Some adult clients are currently dependent on someone else for their food, clothes, and shelter. Here
are three variations of this: (1) the permanently dependent client—like someone with a significant
disability, (2) the temporarily dependent client—like someone who moves in with her parents after
losing her job, and (3) a client who chooses to be dependent—like a professional woman, supported
by a husband, who has chosen to be a stay-at-home mother. Since these clients are not paying for
their own home, food, and clothes now, the words in the script will need to be modified.
For those clients who are permanently or temporarily dependent, we modify the script to highlight any
points of independence that are possible now but were not possible for the wounded part. For example,
perhaps a dependent client can bathe herself or make her own meals. A very young child couldn’t do
this. So, if the wounded part is age two, we can draw these contrasts. We work into the script any
activities clients can engage in now that prove they have power now that they didn’t have in childhood.
For clients who are dependent by choice, the modification is simple. Instead of asking questions like
“Who pays for your home now? …your clothes now? …your food now?” we ask “Can you earn money
to pay for your own home now? …your clothes now? …your food now?” The answer should be a “yes,”
helping the wounded part see she definitely has power now she didn’t have in childhood.

Orienting Wounded Adult Parts to the Present Time
When a wounded part is stuck in childhood, there are usually significant differences between being in
a child body then and an adult body now, so we can easily contrast these differences with this
intervention. But some wounded parts who got stuck in adulthood need to be oriented to the present.
How important this intervention is will depend on the client’s story. For example, consider the case of
a successful, sixty-three-year-old lawyer who has a twenty-year-old wounded part stuck in a college
campus sexual assault. When we talk to the twenty-year-old part, we can draw a contrast between
being a young, naive, poor, relatively powerless college student then, and being a wise, mature,
prosperous, successful professional now. We can modify the Adult Body Intervention script to highlight
those differences. But, if the client is age thirty-five now and the wounded part is stuck at age thirtyfour, there may be few contrasts to make. In this case, orienting to the present time may not be a key
intervention for stabilizing this part.

Filling in Lifespan Details
Some very dissociated parts will get disoriented when they learn they’re in an adult body now. They
may struggle to connect the dots between the past and present. We can help them link the past to the
present by filling in their lifespan details. There are two ways we can do this. We can invite wounded
parts to get their life story from the Resources, or we can summarize it for them. To get the Resources
to tell the story, we say something like “So, four-year-old, ask the Resources what’s happened since
you were age four. What do they say?” As the Resources tell the wounded part her life story, each

Chapter 13: Orienting to Present Time

189

milestone (school, marriage, children, etc.) described will start to sound familiar. This helps orient the
part to the present.
Alternatively, we can narrate a summary of the client’s life story focusing primarily on her developmental
milestones. Here’s an example of what a summary could sound like.
So, four-year-old, here’s what’s happened since you were age four. You finished kindergarten
and started first grade. You got all the way through grade school and went to junior high and
then high school. When you graduated from high school, you joined the Peace Corp. You worked
overseas for two years where you met Lewis. You came back to the United States at age twentyone and married Lewis. You attended college and got a certificate to teach grade school. Soon
after, you had a child—a beautiful daughter, Leslie. At age thirty, Lewis got very sick and passed
away. You taught school and raised your daughter as a single mom. Now she’s a mom, and you
have two wonderful grandsons. So, four-year-old, what’s it like to think about all that?
When we narrate a summary, we should leave out any unnecessary painful details from the client’s life
story. For example, in the summary above I mention that her husband Lewis had died, but I left out
that while she was in the Peace Corp she was kidnapped and sexually assaulted in Rwanda, in a monthlong ordeal. We only need to bring the wounded part up to date about how she got from childhood
to adulthood. We don’t need to tell her about all the client’s darkest moments.

Explaining Sharing Adult Body with the Resources
Some very dissociated parts believe the Resources are in a separate body. That means they could leave.
We can remedy this misperception by showing parts that they share the same body with the Nurturing
Adult Self, Protective Adult Self, and caregiver-self Provisional Resources. Here’s an example of how
this would sound if the client has a Nurturing and Protective Adult Self.
Therapist:

When Parts Protest Orientation to the Present Time
Occasionally we’ll encounter a wounded child part who takes offense at being oriented to the present.
In protest, a part might say something like:
You’re pissing me off. I already know what year it is. I know I’m in a grown-up body. I don’t want
to look in the mirror. Back off.
In response, we smile, nod, kindly say “Thanks so much for sharing that,” and go on to other interventions.
If the part is already fully in the present, this intervention won’t be necessary. If the oppositional part
really does need help orienting to the present time, continuing to build rapport with the part will
eventually bring her around. Eventually, she’ll be happy to orient to the present.
Sometimes the offense to the Adult Body Intervention comes from the adult self. Awareness of the
adult body is not new to the adult self, so the adult self may feel insulted if we ask her about it. For
that reason, it’s ideal to always preface the intervention with the age of the child part we’re talking to.
Here’s an example:
So, four-year-old, look in this mirror and touch your chin. Do you see that’s your adult hand on
your adult chin? Is that what you expected to see?
If we suspect the adult self is feeling offended, we should apologize for the imprecision, then ask again,
this time directing the question precisely to the wounded child part.

A Caution about Fictional Rescues
When a wounded child part feels very stuck in a threatening event from the past, a therapist could
craft a fictional rescue to transport her from a dangerous place to a safe place. This involves inviting
the child part to notice the adult self, or some sort of Resource, coming into the past to pull her out of
the bad situation and take her somewhere else. Here’s an example:
So, little one, notice your Viking Warrior coming into the room and punching your father just as
he’s about to molest you. The Viking picks you up and takes you to a safe place where your father
cannot hurt you.
While this can provide a measure of relief for a wounded part who believes she’s reliving a traumatic
event, it only succeeds in replacing her trauma trance with a fictional rescue trance. Because this is a
temporary change in state of mind, the part may need to be “rescued” again and again. Unfortunately,
fictional rescues serve to reinforce a wounded part’s misperception that an old wounding event is
actually happening right now—hence the need for a rescue.
I prefer to help wounded child parts get out of their trauma trance by helping them self-discover two
important things: (1) the threatening event from the past, that they seem to be reliving, ended long
ago, and (2) the present moment that they’re already now in, is safe. In my experience, when we orient
wounded child parts to present time, we can get more than just a temporary state change. We can also
get some trait change. It’s is an important part of healing.

Chapter 13: Orienting to Present Time

191

A Caution about Retrievals
When a wounded child part feels very stuck in a threatening or traumatic event, a therapist could use
a retrieval to bring her into the present. This involves inviting a child part to notice the adult self or a
Resource coming into the past event to retrieve her, to bring her into the present. Here’s an example:
So, little one, I understand the bullies on the playground are threatening you. Notice adult Jill is
coming to get you. She’s putting her arms around you for a nurturing hug. Can you feel that?
Now take her hand. She’s going to take you away from the playground and bring you into the
present, so you can finally feel safe.
Like a fictional rescue, a retrieval can provide a measure of relief for a wounded part who feels stuck in
a threatening event. My only concern is that the script may inadvertently convey to the part that she
was, in fact, still in the event. I personally believe it’s better to help child parts self-discover traumatic
childhood events ended many years ago, rather than leaving them to believe the retrieval just now
brought the event to an end.
While I prefer my Orient to Present Time interventions to retrievals, sometimes wounded parts are so
dissociated, they do not respond to them. When this happens, a retrieval may be necessary to bring
wounded parts largely in the present. We can then follow with the Adult Body Intervention to bring
them the rest of the way.

Chapter 13 Summary
Wounded parts are more or less in a trance. They feel powerless and stuck in their painful past,
because they believe the wounding is still happening now. This chapter describes two
interventions we can use to help wounded parts self-discover the safety of the present moment.
One intervention is a list of questions—like “How did you get that wedding ring?”—that challenge
wounded parts to account for why the world around them now is not like their childhood world.
The other intervention invites parts to notice their adult face and adult hands so they can begin
to appreciate the power they have now in an adult body. When we succeed in orienting
wounded parts to present time, they feel great relief.
.

193

Chapter 14
Explaining About the Harmless Recording

Consider that our brains make recordings of our significant life events1 and the messages we receive
during these events. These recorded messages can play back later—whether they are kind or unkind,
true or untrue. That’s just what brains do. When a hurtful message plays back, it can evoke very painful
emotions, leading wounded parts to believe the event is actually happening now. This keeps wounded
parts stuck in the past, stuck in a trance. In this chapter I’ll show you how we can explain to wounded
parts triggered by this, that the recordings are completely harmless and can be tuned out. This helps
bring wounded parts out of trance.
For a worksheet that will guide you through these intervention steps while working with clients, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

Significance of Important Life Events Getting Recorded
Some of our most significant life events happen in childhood in our relationships with caregivers. These
events can be affirming or wounding. Here are examples of how each type of childhood experience
leads to recordings that can play back in adulthood, influencing our capacity to manage stress.
 An Affirming Event: A dad teaches his ten-year-old daughter, Vicky, to ride a bike. He is patient
and kind. He promises to help her as long as she needs him. When she falls off the bike and scrapes
her arm, he holds her bleeding limb and comforts her. He lovingly reassures her the body will heal,
and she’ll be okay. Vicky grows up with many similar loving messages from him. At age thirty, she
gets injured in a car accident. Her dad’s reassuring words play back in her head, supporting and
encouraging her through the recovery process.
 A Wounding Event: A mother teaches her ten-year-old son, Victor, to ride a bike. She is impatient
and angry. When he falls off the bike and scrapes his arm, she insults him. As he sits on the ground
with a bloody arm, she walks away. She says he’s obviously too stupid to learn, so she cannot be
bothered to help him. Victor grows up with many similar hurtful messages from her. At age thirty,
he gets injured in a car accident. His mother’s shaming messages play back in his head, leading him
to feel worthless. This exacerbates his anxiety about his injuries, slowing the recovery process.
Notice that in both examples, the most important part of the story was not the trauma of falling off the
bike or the injury to the arm, but the messages conveyed by the parents. The messages we get from
our childhood caregivers constantly play back in our heads in adulthood. They influence the way we
interpret the life challenges we face. They can empower us to overcome adversity or succumb to it.

196

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Types of Wounding Messages
There are three types of wounding messages: verbal, nonverbal, and inferred. The harm done by each
type can range from mild to severe. The effects of wounding messages can be devastating whether or
not they’re spoke out loud.
 Verbal messages are conveyed out loud, like a parent saying to a child “I wish you’d never been
born,” or “You’re a bad child.” Of all three types, verbal messages are the easiest for clients to recall
and talk about, especially when they were spoken often.
 Nonverbal messages are conveyed by body language, facial expressions, gestures, actions, or
inactions. For example, a scowl can send a message like “You’re despicable.” Slamming a door on
a child can send a message like “I hate you.” Failing to help a child in a time of need can send a
message like “You don’t matter.” Some nonverbal signals are quite clear, leaving little doubt about
the message, but sometimes we’re left to make guesses about another’s thoughts or intentions.
 Inferred messages are incorrect guesses about what someone is thinking. For example, a young
boy’s mother has been hospitalized for several weeks in a coma. He tries to make sense of her
absence. He finally concludes she’s thinking “I’m not at home caring for you because you’re not
important to me.” This inferred wounding message could adversely affect the child long term.

Mental In-Box
Shapiro believed that dysfunctional traits, behaviors, beliefs, affects, and body sensations are rooted
in insufficiently processed memories of wounding experiences that are inappropriately stored in the
brain—encoded with emotions, beliefs, and physical sensations that occurred at the time of the event.2
I generally agree with this, especially when the wounding is from acute trauma like PTSD from a natural
disaster or combat, when the nervous system is overwhelmed by a shocking loss or threat. Survivors
of acute trauma usually understand the parameters of the experience intellectually even as they feel
devastated by it emotionally. The idea that physical-trauma memories like this are inappropriately
stored in the brain makes sense to me.
I believe childhood attachment wounding is quite different. I
believe these memories are initially stored exactly where they
should be—in a mental in-box, waiting for needed support.
A child will be confused by unkind messages conveyed by an
important caregiver. Ideally, she’d have someone to go to to
talk about it, someone who could help her make sense of the
hurtful messages and help her process the intense emotions
all the way through. With the right emotional support, her
sense of value would be restored. Such a healing interaction
would move the experience from the in-box to the archives.
But attachment-wounded adult clients did not have this
good fortune. Instead, they have an in-box full of confusing,
hurtful childhood messages still waiting to be processed.

What’s an In-Box?
In an office environment, an in-box
sits on top of a desk to hold incoming papers that need to be
processed. Each item in an in-box
gets evaluated and then handled.
That could mean paying a bill,
sending an invoice, writing a memo,
shredding a document, or throwing
a paper away. Once fully handled,
important items get archived.

Each wounding childhood message that is waiting in the in-box is connected to one or more wounded
child parts, along with the negative beliefs they hold, the painful emotions they feel, the uncomfortable
sensations they endure, and the maladaptive behaviors they engage in. All this can get triggered by a
similar event in adulthood that activates the hurtful childhood messages.

Chapter 14: Explaining About the Harmless Recording

197

Recordings versus Memories
We can all agree that the brain makes and stores memories of significant experiences. When it comes
to childhood attachment wounding, I prefer to think in terms of mental recordings rather than memories.
That’s because the recordings can get activated by stressful moments in adulthood and play back like
a home movie with some sort of internal audio and/or video track. A recording is not an exact
representation of an event like a real home movie would be, but it does represent the essence of the
wounding experience as it was perceived. When a recording plays back a wounding event with a hurtful
message, it can be quite vivid. It can activate one or more wounded child parts holding all the same
beliefs, emotions, and sensations that were present in the past. This full-body, emotional intensity leads
child parts to perceive that the old recorded experience that’s playing back, is actually happening now.

Explaining about the Harmless Recording
I’ve found that wounded parts feel great relief when they understand that their intense emotions have
been triggered because an old “home movie” of their wounding experience is playing back. I teach
them that the intensity of emotions creates the illusion that the movie is real life, but it’s not—it’s just
a harmless recording. The script goes something like this:
So, little one, when wounding person(s) hurt you by wounding behavior or message , your
brain made a recording of it, like filming a home movie, and that recording has been inside you
ever since. It sounds like recent triggering experience pressed “play” on that old recording. As it
played back, it evoked in you all the same painful emotions he/she/they evoked back then. Right?
The intensity of these emotions can create the illusion that the old recording is just as real and just
as important as
wounding person(s)
were back then. It creates the illusion that the old
experience is still happening and still relevant right now, but it isn’t. You’re not reliving that old
bad experience you’re just reliving your reaction to it. Ask the Resources if they believe the old
recording can hurt you now like wounding person(s) hurt you back then. What do they say?
When robust Resources say the recording is harmless, we ask the parts, “What’s it like to hear that?”
This is usually very reassuring for wounded parts to hear. This dialogue may not completely turn off a
recording, but it should feel much less threatening, enabling wounded parts to tune it out. This works
especially well at providing additional reassurance of safety to parts who are already oriented to
present time and feeling a loving connection to the Resources.

Metaphors to Explain about the Harmless Recording
Some wounded parts understand right away that these recordings are harmless, but others are not so
sure. The emotions a recording evokes can be quite intense, which can feel like a fresh wound—hence
the perception that a recording is not harmless. Here are several scripts we can use to further explain
why a recording is not a real threat.
Movie Metaphor—Short Version
So, little one, if you were to watch a scary movie, would you feel fear? Right, but you wouldn’t
actually be in danger, would you? That’s because feeling fear isn’t proof that you’re in danger. A
movie is not real life, and neither is this old recording. So just because it can evoke fear doesn’t
make it a real threat. How does that sound?

198

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Movie Metaphor—Long Version
So, little one, imagine you’re sitting at home watching a scary movie on TV. It gets to the part
where the man with the chainsaw is chasing the girl through the forest. Your heart is racing, your
palms are sweating. It feels like the danger the girl is in is your danger too, right? Just then the
doorbell rings. You pause the movie and go to the door. Your neighbor tells you all about the local
church charity auction and hands you an invitation. Five minutes later, you say goodbye to her
and close the door. Are you still feeling fear? No, because you were never really in danger. Here’s
the takeaway—feeling fear isn’t proof that you’re in danger. A scary TV movie is not real life, and
neither is this old recording. Just because it can evoke intensely painful emotions doesn’t make it
a real threat. But don’t take my word for it, ask the Resources. What do they say?
Waking Up from a Nightmare
So, little one, have you ever had a dream or nightmare that evoked intensely painful emotions? Was
there a moment just before you woke up and opened your eyes where the intense emotions made
the dream feel like real life? Then you see your alarm clock on your nightstand and realize, “Hey,
it’s just a dream!” The painful emotions fade out, replaced by a sense of relief. Right? When the
recording of this wounding experience plays back, it's just like having a bad dream that floods your
body with emotions. It feels like wounding person(s) are here right now, doing or saying those
unkind things again. It can feel like real life and a real threat, but it's not happening now. It’s not
real life. It’s just a harmless recording of something that happened long ago. Would the Resources
be willing to help you wake up so you can see it’s not still happening?
Scorpion in the Sink
So, little one, imagine walking into the bathroom one morning and seeing a big scorpion in your
sink. You’d probably have an intense emotional reaction, right? As you look closely, you discover
it’s just a plastic scorpion, left by someone as a prank. When you realize it’s harmless, does it
change your emotional reaction? When the recording of this wounding experience played back, it
evoked in you the same kind of emotions a scorpion in your sink might evoke. Is the recording
more like a real scorpion or more like a harmless plastic toy?
Child in the Lion’s Cage
So, little one, imagine a boy who’s been locked in a cage with a lion for many years. All he has to
protect himself is a chair. He hides behind it and hopes the lion won’t notice him. One day, a nice
man walks into the cage and says to the boy, “I’m going to take your chair away.” The boy reacts
by grabbing the chair and saying “NO!” The man asks, “Why do you need the chair?” The boy points
to the lion. The man says, “Oh, that old thing?” He grabs the lion and shakes it. A pile of feathers
falls out. “They took the real lion out years ago. Do you still need the chair?” The boy says, “No,
you can have it!” This recording is no more harmful than a pile of feathers. How does that sound?

Wounding Person is Still Conveying Hurtful Messages
Sometimes a person who was hurtful to the client in childhood is still hurtful in adulthood. When we
try to explain the recording cannot do harm now, a wounded child part may remind us of the harm
being inflicted now. We cannot minimize the reality of hurtful messages that are currently being
conveyed, but we can help wounded parts see that the context is different now. Here’s an example of
what that might sound like:
Nine-year-old, you’re right. Your father is still conveying ugly, belittling, shaming messages every
time you’re around him. And if he drinks too much, he might attempt to punch you. He may be

Chapter 14: Explaining About the Harmless Recording

199

exactly the same now as he was back then, but you’re very different now. You’re in a thirty-yearold body. You have power now that you didn’t have back then, including the power to stand your
ground. How often you talk to him and visit him is your choice now. It doesn’t really matter if he
likes you or not. You don’t live with him anymore and you’re not dependent on him. You’re old
enough to take care of yourself now. But don’t take my word for it, ask the Resources. What do
they say?
Furthermore, this intervention is aimed at helping the wounded part tune out messages that were
recorded long ago—messages that have been playing in her head ever since. So if the wounded part
says it doesn’t help because she’s still getting the message, we can say something like this:
Nine-year-old, this is a recording of a message your father conveyed to you in childhood. We
cannot stop him from saying these ugly things to you when you’re around him now, but we can
help you tune out the messages from him that play in your head when he’s not around. How does
that sound?

Five-year-old, I can tell you why it feels like she’s still here right now. When your
mother hit you and called you names, your brain made a recording of it—like
filming a home movie. That recording has been inside you ever since. It sounds like
the altercation you had with your neighbor this morning pressed “play” on that old
recording. As it played back, it evoked in you all the same painful emotions your

Right.

can do the same harm now you that your mother did back then. What do they say?
They say it can’t hurt me now.
What’s it like to hear that?
It’s a relief.

Chapter 14 Summary
This chapter begins with a review of how the brain can record a wounding message, like “You’re
bad,” from someone in childhood, then it play it back in adulthood. This playback can trigger
wounded parts stuck in that childhood experience. The intensely painful emotions this evokes
creates the illusion that the recording of the wounding person is just as real and just as
important now as the person was back then. In reality it’s not. This chapter offers a script that
effectively explains to wounded parts that the old recording is harmless—like a scary movie or
a bad dream is harmless. When the Resources confirm this, wounded parts feel great relief. This
makes it possible for parts to tune out the triggering, wounding message

1.
2.

201

McGaugh, J.L. (2013).
Shapiro, F. (2017).

Chapter 15
Wrapping Up a Session

Collectively, all the interventions described in Chapters 10–14 bring wounded parts out of trance and
into the safety of the present. Generally, we start by connecting parts to the Resources, orienting them
to the present time, and then explaining about the harmless recording. Interwoven into this
conversation is information needed to clear up misunderstandings, fill in missing information, or
manage enmeshment. This sequence of steps is a guideline, not a rigid protocol. To provide the most
helpful support we must stay attuned to the wounded parts and follow them wherever they tell us they
need to go.
That means from moment to moment, selecting from our list of interventions. The more attuned we
are, the more likely we are to sense which intervention is most needed next. For example, if wounded
parts show up terrified by a threatening recording, we might first need to explain that it’s harmless. If
they show up in a somatic flashback, we might first need to orient them to present time. If they show
up expressing fears about healing, we might first need to handle misunderstandings. As long as we
stay fully present with openness, kindness, and radical acceptance, we’re more likely to provide the
intervention needed most in the moment. (See Chapter 17 for more about attuning to clients.)
For a worksheet that will guide you through these intervention steps while working with clients, go
online to dnmsbook1.com (use the password DNMS).

Coming Out of Trance
We constantly watch for clues about how well the process is going from our clients’ facial expressions
and body language and the comments wounded parts make. We look for the point when a client’s
face lights up and the body relaxes. At the same time, we’re hearing wounded parts say things like
“That feels really good,” “It’s a relief,” or “I can see it’s over now.”
Indicators like this suggest a wounded part may be out of trance. We could assume that’s a signal that
it’s time to wrap up, but it’s best if we ask if there is something more that needs to be addressed. A
question could sound something like this:

204

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

So, little one, is there anything else you need to talk about or work on? Is there anything else on
your mind?
If we get a “yes,” we address the additional concern or new elaboration of the story with whatever
intervention is needed most—perhaps a dialogue with the Resource team, reorienting to present time,
reexplaining that the recording is harmless, and/or clearing misunderstandings. The secret to successful
stabilization is listening carefully and staying attuned so we can provide whatever is needed.

Checking for Changes in the Wounded Parts’ Reactivity
When a part says there’s nothing else to talk about, we check in on the targeted issue. The issue could
have been a recent triggering event, an anticipated triggering event, a long-standing issue, or a
processing block. Here are examples of what various check-ins might sound like.
For a recent triggering event:
So, ten-year-old, yesterday you got triggered when you found out your boss must cut 10 percent
of the staff in your department, and you were afraid you’d lose your job—afraid that would prove
you’re a failure. When you think about that now, does anything seem different?
For an anticipated triggering event:
So, seven-year-old, you’d gotten triggered thinking about attending the family reunion next week.
When you think about it now, does anything seem different?
For a longstanding issue:
So, four-year-old, we targeted your fear of rejection. When you think about applying for a job and
being rejected, does anything seem different now?
For a processing block:
So, twelve-year-old, you’d showed up to “protect” the five-year-old part from the Resources. Do
you still need to do that?
If a wounded part reveals aspects of the target that are still unresolved, we continue with stabilization
interventions, providing whatever information or reassurance is needed. But if the part has fully stopped
overreacting to the target issue, the trance has been broken, and stabilization has been achieved.

Inviting Wounded Parts to Settle In
Once a wounded part or parts are stabilized, we invite them to settle into their Special Safe Place so
the client’s adult self can be fully front and center again. Here’s what that might sound like.
I’d like to help you get completely unstuck at the perfect time. I’m not sure when that will be,
hopefully soon. In the meantime, I’m going to invite you to settle into your Beach Resort . Is
there anything you’d like to say or ask before you settle in?
Parts usually say something like “Thank you!” We say:
Find a nice, cozy, safe place to settle into your
Beach Resort
. Settle in with the Resources.
Find a good place to rest and play until the next time you’re needed.
After a few moments, we ask the adult self, “Are they all settled in?” Most of the time parts go play
so the adult self can be fully front and center again.

Chapter 15: Wrapping Up a Session

205

Therapist’s Aid for Sensorimotor Processing for Emotional Release

TASPER
When we pull on a TASPER, we activate muscles in the body. The
arms pull back while the legs push forward. When clients are
dissociated, this sensorimotor activity can help bring them back
into the body, back into their most adult self. Feeling the strength
of the body connects clients to a sense of empowerment. For
more information, go online to dnmsinstitute.com/tasper.

Getting the Adult Self Front and Center Again
Some very dissociative clients need help getting their most adult self front and center again after deep
ego state work. When we detect this might be a problem, we ask the client if she needs to be counted
up. If so, we count up with something like this:
One, coming up slowly. Two, a little bit more awake and alert. Three, notice your body in the chair,
notice your hands… your feet… your breath. Prepare to open your eyes. Four, let your eyes open.
Notice colors… notice sounds… notice your body in the chair… feel a little more awake, a little more
alert. (When client makes eye contact, we say…) And five, completely and totally awake and alert.
If a client is still a little disoriented after counting up, we can apply a sensorimotor activity like twisting
a towel or pulling on a Therapist’s Aid for Sensorimotor Processing for Emotional Release (TASPER). (See
TASPER sidebar above.) This can help bring a client fully back into her body. When the client is ready
to twist or pull, we look directly into her eyes, smile big, and say:
Bring yourself back into your most adult self, back into your body, back into the room. Feel the
strength and power in your adult body as you pull (or twist).

Checking In with the Adult Self
Next, we check in with the adult self to see if anything has changed. If we’ve worked with all the
wounded parts connected to the trigger, the client will likely feel substantially different about the target
issue. She may still need to manage painful emotions, solve a problem, or face a dilemma, but if all the
wounded parts who were triggered are now stable and calm, the client will be empowered to deal with
the challenge solely from her most adult self. That brings a sense of empowerment that can make a
big difference. Here are several sample check-ins:
A check-in for a current or recent triggering event might sound something like this:
Since you found out your boss must cut 10 percent of the staff in your department, you’ve been
worried about getting laid off. Is your reaction to that possibility the same or different now?
A typical reaction would be something like:
I’m still concerned about getting laid off. I hope it doesn’t happen, but something has shifted. I was
feeling hopeless and powerless, like it would be the end of the world and I’d never recover. I felt like it
would prove I’m a failure. That’s gone. Losing my job would be very stressful for sure, but I’m more
confident now that if I got laid off, I could manage the stress of it, find a new job, and recover.
A check-in for an anticipated triggering event might sound something like this:
The idea of attending your family reunion next week was very uncomfortable. How does it feel to
think about it now?

206

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

A typical reaction would be something like:
I come from a very dysfunctional family, and I expect to encounter the usual family dramas. I was
feeling like I’d have to pretend I’m someone else to please everyone. I’m not feeling that way now.
I’m prepared to be myself even if they don’t like it. If it gets too uncomfortable, I have the option
to leave early. I can do this.
If we’ve targeted a longstanding issue, a check-in might sound something like this:
When you think about your longstanding fear of rejection, does anything seem different now?
A typical reaction would be something like:
Yes. I’ve been fearful of dating since my last girlfriend dumped me. I’ve been assuming that any
woman who went out with me would treat me the same way. They’d act like they care, then dump
me. I know that could happen, but I don’t have to take it personally. If I start dating again, I might
find a woman who’s a good match for me. That’s different.
Once we’ve successfully stabilized the wounded parts connected to the target issue, we can expect the
client to report healthy reactions to the target issue, like those listed above. But sometimes the shift a
client reports seems incomplete. This can happen when there are additional wounded parts connected
to the issue—parts that did not come forward with our initial invitation. If that’s what happened, the
client may still be overreacting, albeit less. She may be close to a fully healthy reaction to the issue, but
not yet fully there. We can ask, “Are there any other parts connected to
target issue .” If so, we can
complete the work by inviting them forward. Once we’ve stabilized them all, the client will experience a
full shift, and a healthy relationship with the issue.

What to Expect Next
Bringing wounded parts out of trance can offer profound relief, but it doesn’t usually get them completely
unstuck. That means they could get triggered again. If that happens, we apply one or more of the
stabilizing interventions again. Repetition will strengthen their understanding of the safety of the present
and the unwavering support of the Resources, reinforcing the benefits of stabilization. Additional
treatments, like EMDR or comprehensive DNMS needs meeting, may be needed to complete the healing.
Foundationally unstable clients will get triggered often. They’ll have many wounded parts in need of
stabilization. When enough wounded parts get stabilized, the client will become more stable. Let’s talk
about what that looks like for a client like Carla. Carla grew up with trauma wounds and attachment
wounds inflicted by an enmeshing mother and a violent father. (Read her history on page 42.) Physical
traumas were inflicted periodically, while attachment wounds were inflicted every day. Consequently,
she has lots of wounded child parts who are easily triggered by ever-present reminders of her stressful
childhood.
When clients like Carla begin therapy, they come to each session reeling from a recent or current
trigger. Each session is an opportunity to get to know a triggered part, to connect her to the Resources,
and meet enough needs to bring her out of trance. Over a period of four to six months, we might find
and stabilize ten to thirty child parts. As more and more parts get stabilized, between-session triggering
episodes happen less often, they’re less intense when they do happen, and the client recovers more
quickly. Once a client like Carla can go for extended periods without wounded parts getting triggered,
she’ll be able to easily process her traumas with EMDR.

Chapter 15 Summary
This chapter describes how we wrap up a session. When it appears the stabilizing interventions
have brought the wounded parts out of trance, we check in with them to see if there’s anything
else they need our help with. When there’s nothing else they need from us, we check in to see if
the target issue that started out triggering feels any different to them now. If our stabilization is
complete, the triggering event will no longer transport the child parts back to a painful past.
We then invite the parts to settle into their Special Safe Place to rest and play until the next
time they’re needed. Often the triggering event is connected to a problem or concern the adult
self must handle. Stabilizing wounded parts helps the adult self feel more grounded and better
prepared to deal with the situation. This brings the client one step closer to safe and effective
EMDR.

Chapter 16
Controlling Reactive Parts

Working with any wounded parts can be challenging, but working with controlling reactive parts can
be especially so. That’s because they often aim to sabotage our efforts to help other wounded parts
heal, by being belligerent, withholding, intrusive, or manipulative. This chapter explains how we can
build rapport with controlling reactive parts and get them working with us instead of against us.

What are Controlling Reactive Parts?
Controlling reactive parts endeavor to protect themselves and other wounded parts from feelings of
shame, rejection, or humiliation. They do this by trying to control the behaviors of other wounded parts
by issuing warnings, threats, commands, admonitions, or criticisms.
Their influence campaign began in reaction to messages that were abusive, shaming, rejecting, neglectful,
or enmeshing—usually parental messages conveyed in childhood. They’d “motivate” the child to behave
in a way that evoked a more desirable parental reaction. For example, if parents were less abusive when
a child was quiet, a controlling reactive part would discourage making noise. Their message might be
kind, like gently saying, “Shhhh, you need to be quiet right now,” or hostile, like angrily screaming,
“Shut up you nitwit!” Either way her intentions were good, always aiming to solve a problem.
Now in adulthood, the wounding parents may or may not be around, but
recordings of their hurtful messages certainly are. When old recordings
get triggered and play back, it can evoke very intense emotions. This
motivates controlling reactive parts to convey their controlling message
to other wounded parts. Unfortunately, this does not succeed in turning
off the hurtful message, but it can create more internal conflict and
friction.
In their efforts to solve problems, controlling reactive parts often make things worse. Their actions may
sabotage relationships, school, work, therapy, and other good things. Because of this, our clients’ adult
self may see no value in these “bad” parts—often wishing they’d go away. That disdain for controlling
parts can inadvertently generate more internal friction and conflict.

210

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Encountering Controlling Reactive Parts
Controlling reactive parts are fundamentally mistrusting, particularly when it comes to therapy. They
can wield their influence in ways that are visible or invisible to us. For example, it’s visible to us when
they intrude in the middle of a session to loudly protest our “meddling.” But it’s invisible to us when
they try, in secret, to induce shame, fear, and mistrust in other wounded parts, to order to undermine
our efforts to help them.
When controlling reactive parts emerge in session, they act like they’re in charge. They’re often belligerent,
oppositional, or intrusive with us. They often aim to block processing or push us away. They’re frequently
dismissive of the Resources and suspicious of our intentions. If the adult self is hostile towards these
parts, controlling parts expect we will be too.
We need to help controlling parts move from ignoring the Resources to embracing them, from blocking
processing to supporting it, and from mistrusting us to trusting us. But these are mere secondary goals;
the primary goal is to help them feel valued exactly as they are right now. Radical acceptance is the
secret sauce for helping controlling reactive parts heal. The needed acceptance is radical because their
behavior can feel quite unacceptable.
Controlling reactive parts often evoke in therapists feelings of fear, confusion, and shame—making it
especially hard to embody radical acceptance! While we’re feeling these emotions, we must keep in
mind that these are young parts with a core of goodness. At the same moment they’re trying to
aggravate us, they really want our help. They want us to be strong for them. They have many unmet
needs and very much want our help in getting those needs met. Our first challenge is to gain their trust
by building rapport.

Building Rapport with Radical Acceptance
Controlling reactive parts predict that if they push us away, we’ll push them away, or if they’re angry
with us, we’ll be angry with them. They predict a negative reaction from us, but if we’re kind and
welcoming instead, they’ll be surprised. This surprise is called a positive prediction error.1 Controlling
parts will struggle to make sense of our unexpected kindness. In their moment of confusion, old
defenses are suspended, and new learning becomes possible. We build rapport one positive prediction
error at a time.
When talking to a controlling reactive part, we stay very warm, open, and friendly. We lean in, smile
big, and say, “Welcome! I’m very glad you’re here.” In a friendly voice, we ask, “How old are you?” and
“What kind of mood are you in?” We try to get to know the part by saying things like “Tell me about
yourself” or “Tell me your story.” We hope the part will tell us her story—whatever happened to her in
childhood that makes her so feisty today. If she’s reluctant to tell it, we get her talking about something
else. It could be any topic she’s comfortable talking about even if it seems completely unrelated to her
troubling behavior or childhood wounding. Sometimes it helps for us to disclose a personal story—
one that shows the part we’re capable of understanding her. In whatever way possible, we keep a
conversation going to show the part that she matters to us. All the while, we convey that we’re fully
here for her no matter her mood or point of view.
The more controlling reactive parts talk, the more likely they are to reveal misunderstandings or
missing information. It’s very important for us to address these gaps in their understanding. We must
be careful not to lecture them or insinuate verbally or nonverbally that they’re dumb or misinformed.
We can seed new perspectives by telling them stories that help them self-discover important
information. (See Chapter 11.) Everything we say, and the way we say it, should be calculated to honor
their sense of dignity and value, and build rapport.

Chapter 16: Controlling Reactive Parts

211

If a controlling reactive part becomes belligerent, we stay calm. It’s imperative that we not argue or
express disapproval even when we’re attacked. Instead, we welcome the friction and flow with it. We
use it as an opportunity to attune, validate, and empathize. Here are some examples of belligerent
statements a controlling reactive part might make, followed by an example of how to respond with
loving kindness.
Controlling RP:
Therapist:

No one can be trusted, especially not you.
I expect you’ve encountered lots of untrustworthy people in your life. You’re smart
to be suspicious of me.

Controlling RP:
Therapist:

You’re a lying bitch.
Okay. I hope you’ll tell me when you catch me lying to you.

Controlling RP:
Therapist:

You don’t really care. You’re just doing this for the money.
You’re not used to people caring for you, are you? I’m so sorry it feels unfamiliar. I
wish you’d felt cherished and cared for your entire life.

Controlling RP:
Therapist:

Admit it. You hate me.
You’ve known a lot of hateful people. I admit that you have lots of reasons to expect
I’d hate you.

Controlling RP:
Therapist:

You just want to get rid of me.
I want to get to know you.

Controlling RP:
Therapist:

I bet you can’t wait for this session to end.
I like getting to know you. This session will end soon, but I hope you’ll be back soon
so we can continue talking.

Controlling RP:
Therapist:

You’re so aggravating.
Okay. If being kind to you is aggravating, prepare to be even more aggravated. I’m
going to continue to be kind.

Controlling RP:
Therapist:

I’m just an annoying teen. You could never like me.
I expect a lot of people have been annoyed by your anger, but I’m okay with it.
You’re angry for a reason. Stay angry and annoying as long as you need to. I’d love
to hear about where it comes from and how it started if and when you ever want
to talk about it.

Controlling RP:
Therapist:

Go away and leave me alone.
I expect you’re pushing me way for a very good reason. Tell me about that. I’d really
like to understand.

Controlling RP:
Therapist:

I’m not going to talk to you. Butt out.
Okay. I won’t make you talk to me, but would you be willing to tell me why you
don’t want to talk to me? I just want to fully understand.

Here are some longer statements we can use to build rapport with controlling reactive parts. These
can serve as guidelines. We must tailor statements that match the needs of the parts we’re talking to.
So, little one, I want you to mistrust me as long as you need to. You’ve mistrusted everyone to try
to keep yourself safe. Keep doing it. It’s my job to earn your trust.
I completely understand why you believe you have to
push me away / keep your guard up /
mistrust me . If I'd had the same experiences you had, I'd believe that too.
I’m not here to change you. Stay exactly as you are as long as that works. I’m guessing you’ve waited
a long time for someone who just wanted to listen and understand. I’d like to do that for you now.

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

You’ve been hurt by people who started out charming and kind but later betrayed you. It makes
perfect sense that you’d be concerned I’d do that to you too. Remain cautious as long as you need to.
I want you to continue to believe you must
be confrontational / mistrust everyone / keep your
guard up as long as you need to. If it’s helped you in the past, it may help you in the future.
It sounds like you’ve been working hard for years to
keep people away / keep wounded parts
safe / master mistrust . I’m glad you’ve been trying to make your life better.
I don’t want to take away your power. Actually, I'd like to help you become more powerful—
powerful enough to grow to meet your full potential.
I fully support you in believing no one can be trusted / arrogance is protective
as long as you
need to, and the Resources do too. We’re NOT here to talk you out of believing that. We’re here to
fully support you WHILE you believe it. We’re here for you no matter what you believe or how long
you believe it.
I fully support you in feeling angry / confrontational / guarded as long as you need to, and the
Resources do too. We’re NOT here to talk you out of feeling that. We’re here to fully support you
WHILE you feel it. We’re here for you no matter what you feel or how long you feel it.
I believe you came into the world with a divine spark of love and light—a core of glowing goodness.
I expect you started out with a desire for all to go well. A desire to live in harmony with yourself
and others. A desire to make loving connections—to love and be loved. A desire to be seen, heard,
valued, and understood, but it didn’t go the way you wanted it to. Instead, your family was unkind
to you . I’m really sorry about that. It’s my hope that one day you’ll be able to connect with the
Resources in a way that gets you back in touch with your inner light. I hope one day you’ll be able
to receive their love so it can help you heal.
You deserve to be loved, understood, and cherished, no matter what’s been done to you and no
matter what you’ve done to others. I am hopeful that one day the light from your core of goodness
will once again shine.
WHAT we say is less important than HOW WE FEEL when we say it. Our face, voice, and body language
must convey a truly authentic, unwavering, loving acceptance. We may see in front of us an angry
adult, but we must ignore that and picture the controlling reactive part as a hurt, vulnerable teen or
child who wants to be cuddled and soothed. We must stay laser-focused on the part’s pure core of
goodness—intending for the words we choose and the way we convey them to feel like loving arms
holding the wounded part.
Radical acceptance and loving attunement will eventually win over controlling reactive parts. Each
rapport-building message opens a window for change a crack. The part will begin to say things like
“You confuse me,” “I wasn’t expecting that,” “You’re weird,” and “Why are you being so nice?” These
comments are signs that the part is trying to make sense of her prediction error. If we keep it up long
enough, the part will come to expect kindness from us. That’s when the trust and rapport really take
hold, and the window for change will open wide enough for meaningful shifts to happen.
This approach may transform a controlling reactive part within a single session, but sometimes many
sessions are needed. This is understandable considering that trust is built over time. Each time a
controlling reactive part feels seen, heard, and valued when she’s expecting to be rejected, some very
deep healing happens. Once rapport is established, controlling reactive parts will be open to all the
other stabilization interventions—bonding with the Resources, orienting to the present time, and
learning about the harmless recording. More importantly, stabilizing these feisty parts will calm a lot
of inner conflict. See a sample client-therapist dialogue on page 215.

Chapter 16: Controlling Reactive Parts

213

Mimicking Reactive Parts
A mimicking reactive part2 is a type of controlling reactive part who’s overidentified with a wounding
person. These parts have found that aggressively conveying a wounding person’s threatening, shaming,
wounding, or manipulative message can effectively motivate wounded parts to modify their behavior
in ways deemed helpful. For example, if an incestuous father threatened to kill his child if she told
anyone, a mimicking reactive part would form to threaten the child with that same message, any time
she was tempted to tell—with the aim of keeping her alive.
For added effect, mimicking reactive parts might even appear like a
wounding person, for example, looking like the incestuous father.
They may even claim to be the hurtful person. But their appearance as
a wounding person is just a façade—a mask or costume. Underneath
the façade is a wounded child part acting hurtful, in order to solve a
problem.
Now in adulthood, the hurtful person may or may not be around, but recordings of their threatening
messages certainly are. When those old recordings get triggered and play back, it can evoke intense
fear. This can motivate mimicking reactive parts to act like the wounding person. Unfortunately, this
does not succeed in turning off the message. It only succeeds in creating more internal distress.
When we dialogue with a mimicking reactive part, it helps to hold a mental picture of her as a wounded
child animating a mask or costume of the unkind person. We don’t dialogue with the façade but with
the child part wearing it. The part doing the mimicking will have different life experiences than the
person she’s mimicking. We have two aims when we work with these parts: (1) to convey we know who
she really is, and it’s not who she’s mimicking, and (2) to meet her emotional needs well enough to
bring her out of trance by applying all the stabilization interventions (Chapters 10–14). Until the part
comes out of trance, she’ll feel safer with the mask. We never ask a part to get rid of a mask, let it go,
take it off, or set it down. We just focus on meeting needs. When the part is sufficiently out of trance
and stabilized, she will naturally and effortlessly lose her attachment to it. See a sample client-therapist
dialogue on page 221.

Mimicking Reactive Parts versus Old Harmless Recordings
From our vantage point as therapists, a mimicking reactive part who appears in session as a hurtful
person could be confused with a harmless recording of the person. Here’s how we know the difference.
A mimicking reactive part will show up with an intent to solve a perceived problem. Often that solution
is to shut down a wounded part, block therapy, or stop us from “meddling.” Here’s an example.
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Mimicking RP:

My dad is here right now. He’s yelling at me to shut up. He says I’m not allowed to
tell you what happened.
Hi there! Welcome! I’m glad you’re here. You must be here for a very good reason.
Tell me about that.
You need to stop asking questions. Back off.

Notice that the wounded part tells the therapist that “Dad” is here right now with a message about the
therapy. Unless her Dad had commented on this therapy when he was hurting the client years ago,
this “Dad” is a mimicking reactive part. In contrast, a wounded part might hear a hurtful Dad recording
coming from the background. Because it’s a recording, there’s nothing in it about the therapist or the
therapy. It’s devoid of any intent to solve a problem. Here’s an example.
Wounded Part:

I’m hearing my dad say if I tell anyone what he did, he’ll kill my cat. I’m afraid.

214

Therapist:

Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Years ago, your brain made a recording of your dad threatening to kill your cat,
and that recording has been inside you ever since. Something just pressed play on
that old recording. That’s evoked in you all the same painful emotions he evoked
back then. Those emotions create the illusion that the recording of your dad is just
as real and just as important as he was back then. It creates the illusion that that
old experience is still happening and still relevant right now, but it isn’t. Ask the
Resources if they believe the Dad recording can do the same harm now that he did
back then. What do they say?
They say it can’t.
What’s it like to hear that?
Wow. That’s a big relief!

Caution About Persuading Controlling Parts to Change Jobs
Some ego state therapy models advocate managing conflict-generating parts by focusing on their
problematic behavior. They advocate persuading parts to give up their conflict-generating “job” and
replace it with a harmony-generating “job.” This approach might have a short-term benefit, but if it
doesn’t address the underlying reasons that motivate the need to generate conflict, it’s unlikely to have
a long-term benefit. It may even backfire, leading ultimately to more mistrust of therapy.
Controlling reactive parts will trust us more if we start by honoring their conflict-generating job and
invite them to keep it as long as they need it. That will cultivate rapport and trust. From there we can
guide controlling parts through all the stabilizing interventions, providing all the psychoeducational
interweaves they need. When enough of these needs are met, they will gladly stop generating conflict.

Clarifying Common Terminology
Other ego state therapy models refer to protector parts, critical parts, abuser introjects, and inner
perpetrators—terms that are often used interchangeably. If you’re familiar with these terms, you may
be tempted to equate controlling reactive parts with all of these. In my experience, these terms are
usually used without needed precision. I offer my thoughts about these categories, illuminating the
precision that I believe is essential for a good understanding and ultimately effective treatment.
 Protector Part: The term protector part is often assigned to belligerent or destructive parts, because
it’s commonly assumed that their aggression has a protective motivation. While I do believe that
all wounded parts act with good intentions, I prefer to invite these parts to tell me what their
motivations are rather than making a categorical assumption. That good intention might not be
protection. It could be something else like revenge (justice) or suicide (relief from pain). If we assume
the motivation is protection and we’re right, all is well. But if we’re wrong, the part will notice we’re
misattuned, and that’s a problem. We’ll be unable to help parts heal if we’re misattuned.
 Critical Part: Critical self-talk is often attributed to a critical part. But a critical internal dialogue can
come from many different sources. A powerless reactive part can be critical of herself (“I’m a
failure”). A controlling or mimicking reactive part can be critical of other parts (“If you’re not perfect,
you’re a failure”). Wounded parts can be haunted by a recording of someone’s critical message
playing back (“You’re a miserable failure”). These distinctions matter, because the source of a critical
message determines which intervention we need to use.
 Abuser Introject: Abusive self-talk is often attributed to an abuser introject, but abusive internal
dialogue can come from many different sources. A powerless reactive part can be abusive to herself
(“I hate myself”). A controlling or mimicking reactive part can be abusive toward other parts (“Be

Chapter 16: Controlling Reactive Parts

215

perfect or die”). Wounded parts can be haunted by a recording of someone’s abusive message
playing back (“I want to kill you”). These distinctions matter, because the source of an abusive
message determines which intervention we need to use.
 Inner Perpetrator: The term inner perpetrator usually refers to the internalization of a physically
abusive person. When a client senses an inner perpetrator, it could be a part of self, like a mimicking
reactive part acting like a perpetrator, or it could be a recording of a perpetrator playing back. In
my opinion, this is not a term we should ever use with clients. It might give the false impression
that the perpetrator who harmed them is literally inside them, continuing to do harm.

a continuation of ego state therapy with Carla.

Hi, Carla. Welcome back. How are you doing?
It’s been a stressful week. I’ve been watching the TV news about my neighbor, the one
who murdered his family. His wife had been to the police about him multiple times,
but she never pressed charges. She’d sent an email to her mother telling her that
if she died, her husband killed her. She knew he was dangerous. I don’t understand
how she could put her four-year-old daughter in the middle of this. He didn’t just
kill her, he killed their child too.
Given your history with domestic violence, I can see why this would be extra stressful
for you.
Yes. Me and Mama could have ended up in body bags too.
Does it feel like watching the TV news triggered a wounded part?
Definitely.
Would you like to work on this today?
I’m not sure. There’s something else going on. I got a call from my friend, Cindy
Lou, a nurse I used to work with at the hospital. She just took a job in the Emergency
Room. She called me about an opening on her shift for a part-time ER nurse. They
need someone for two shifts a week. The schedule would be perfect for me. I adore
Cindy Lou. We worked great together, and I’ve missed her. I’d love to work side by
side with her again.
This sounds like a big decision. Do you feel ready for a job like that?
The therapy has helped me a lot, but I’m not sure I’m ready for this yet. I’m isolating
less, but I’m not sure I’m ready to return to any job. Besides, the ER can be especially
brutal. A lot of ER patients are victims of violence. Lots of stabbings and gunshot
wounds. Working in an ER can be quite traumatizing.
Are there wounded parts triggered by the idea of working in the ER?
Yes. Triggered by the idea of taking any job but especially an ER job.
Would you like to work on this today, or does the other issue feel more urgent?
I want to work on one of these, but I don’t know which one. They’re both pretty vivid.
Okay. How about this? After you connect to the Resources, I’ll invite forward the
part or parts who most need help today.
That’s great.
