Explaining about the Harmless Recording
Therapist:

Controlling RP:
Therapist:

Controlling RP:
Therapist:
Controlling RP:
Therapist:
Controlling RP:

Twelve-year-old, I can explain why this terrible incident feels as horrible now as the
day it happened. When your father did this, your brain made a recording of it, like
filming a home movie, and that recording has been inside you ever since. It sounds
like recent reports in the news about the murders next door pressed “play” on that
old recording. When it plays back, it evoks in you all the same painful emotions
your father evoked back then. Right?
Right.
The intensity of these emotions can create the illusion that the old recording is just
as real and just as important as your father was back then. It can create the illusion
that the old experience is still happening and still relevant right now, but it isn’t.
You’re not reliving that old bad experience; you’re just reliving your reaction to it.
What’s it like to think about that?
Interesting.
So, twelve-year-old, ask the Resources if they believe the old recording can hurt
you now like your father hurt you back then. What do they say?
They say it can’t hurt me now.
What’s it like to hear that?
It’s a relief.

Sample Client-Therapist Dialogue: Working with a Mimicking Reactive Part
The following is a typical client-therapist dialogue illustrating work with a mimicking reactive part.
Background: This client was sent to an all-girls boarding school in New England at age twelve. She
endured routine sexual abuse from Dexter, a clergy member at the school. The sexual abuse was infused
with religious messages. She had a horse named Midnight that she rode often before she was sent to
boarding school. The dialogue starts with a wounded part, then turns into a dialogue with a mimicking
reactive part (Mimicking RP).
Therapist:
Wounded Part:

So, twelve-year-old, how did your relationship with Dexter start?
One Sunday, toward the start of the school year, he gave a sermon about being of
service to God. He said he’d mentor up to five girls who wanted help with that.
He’d select the five holiest girls who asked for his help. I wanted him to pick me. I

wanted a mentor and spiritual advisor, so I introduced myself to him after the
service. He asked me some questions and said he’d need a few days think about
whether to choose me. Three days later, he came to my room and said God had
chosen me for him to mentor. I felt really lucky.
What was your first mentoring session like?
It was nice. He was gentle and kind. He said God told him I’m extra special. We
prayed together. He read some scriptures to me, and we talked about how to build
trust in God.
What happened after that?
I’d go to his office two afternoons a week. At first it felt good, safe. He asked me
about my family. When I told him I’d felt unloved at home, he empathized. He
hugged me while I cried. I came to believe I could tell him anything, and he’d love
and support me.
That must have felt good.
It did. Then he started giving me gifts. He gave me a cross necklace and some
books about communing with God.
What did you make of that?
It seemed sweet. My parents never gave me gifts like that. I felt really special. He
was very handsome, so I kinda had a crush on him.
That makes sense. Then what happened?
After a while, the hugs got kinda creepy. He’d pat my butt or hold the hug too long
and moan. All the while he’d talk about trusting that God brought us together for
a reason. One day, he put his hand in my pants. He said that’s what God wanted,
that this was what it meant to be in service to God. I was pretty confused, but I

That must have felt very confusing. Your mind wanted you to believe this was okay,

Right. Afterward, I thought about it a lot. The next time I saw him, I told him I didn’t
want him to do that again. I thought he’d understand, but he got really mad. He
said we had an agreement that I would trust him on all things spiritual. He said the
necklace he gave me was proof that I could trust him. He said God would reject me
if I didn’t follow his guidance. He made me believe that if I didn’t agree, God would
be really mad and punish me, so I just figured out how to disconnect from my body
when he touched me.
I’m so sorry. That sounds terrible.
Meanwhile, I was being bullied by a classmate. I told Dexter about it. He was very
protective. He went to the head mistress and reported it. He helped get the bully
expelled. I was very grateful. I felt like I needed his protection, so I kept meeting
him. He was usually really nice.
Wow. That sounds like a big double bind.
It was, but he was touching other girls too, and some of them started to talk about
it. Rumors started to spread about the girls he was mentoring. Suddenly everyone
knew what was happening. I felt very humiliated and ashamed, like it was all my fault.
It wasn’t your fault. What he did was a crime. A twelve-year-old cannot give consent
for sexual contact with an adult.
My world came crashing down. Parents started showing up at the school demanding
answers. It was a big scandal. When the police came to interview me, I just wanted
to die.

Midnight was your horse, not Dexter’s. You have a loving heart of gold. There’s nothing
about Dexter’s abusive behavior that matches your good true nature.

Stop it! You’re messing everything up.

you’re needed.

Chapter 16 Summary
This chapter addresses the unique challenges of working with highly defended, mistrusting,
belligerent, or oppositional controlling reactive parts. Doing this well matters because these
parts often aim to sabotage our efforts to help other wounded parts heal. They often aim to
push us away by saying things like “Admit it, you hate me,” “You’re a lying bitch,” and “You
don’t really care, you’re just doing this for the money.” Parts predict this will evoke a negative
reaction from us. When we respond with radical acceptance instead, it creates a positive
prediction error and a moment of confusion. In that moment, old defenses are suspended, and
new learning becomes possible. This chapter illustrates how radical acceptance can get
controlling reactive parts working with us instead of against us.

In my 2009 DNMS book, I call mimicking reactive parts “reactive mimics.”

Chapter 17
Attunement

In order to be successful with the Chapter 10-14 stabilizations, we must be attuned to a client’s
wounded parts. Attunement—from us and the Resources—is the secret sauce in our healing recipe.
We need to use it liberally! DNMS defines attunement as an ability to sense another’s emotional state,
sense what is needed, and respond appropriately to meet those needs. Attuning involves…
 staying fully present for a wounded part’s story,
 validating and empathizing,
 asking insightful questions,
 summarizing what we understand,
 expressing heartfelt emotional resonance,
 detecting and applying the intervention wounded parts need most in that moment, and
 detecting and responding to subtle signals of distress, concern, or fear.
A lot of these I’ve described in earlier chapters. Let’s talk more about detecting and handling subtle
signals of distress.

Detecting Subtle Signals of Distress
Subtle signals of distress, concern, or fear can be communicated through facial expressions (e.g., frowning
or wincing), tone of voice (e.g., halting or straining), body language (e.g., fidgeting or jerking), and/or
body sensations (e.g., sleepiness or headache), and answering questions with vague words like “pretty
good” instead of “good,” or “I guess” instead of “yes.”
Signals can also be communicated via micro-expressions—fleeting facial expressions that convey various
emotions. These unedited expressions can reveal information that’s below conscious awareness, so
they’re important to notice, especially when they do not match a client’s words. Because they can flash
in an instant, we may only have an inkling or a gut feeling of a mismatch.

Following Up on Subtle Signals of Distress
Whenever we detect a signal of distress, anger, fear, or concern—from a macro- or micro-expression—
we need to follow up. We can ask a general question like “How are you doing?” or “What’s happening?”
or we can mention the signal we’ve detected. Here are three examples.
 A wounded part who’s just connected with a nurturing Resource for the first time says, “She looks
nice,” and suddenly starts wincing and fidgeting. We ask, “I see you just started wincing and
fidgeting. Is something making you uncomfortable?”

228

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

 After strengthening a connection to a Resource team, we ask the client how it feels. She says, “Pretty
good,” so we ask, “Why do you say ‘pretty good’ instead of ‘good’?”
 A wounded part who’s just been welcomed by the Resources suddenly looks very tired. She closes
her eyes and drops her head back. We say, “It looks like you’re suddenly feeling sleepy. What were
you thinking about just before that started?”
It’s tempting to make assumptions about what’s evoking distress. For example, if a child part feels
sleepy right after getting up close to the Resources, we could assume the Resources are making her
sleepy and ask, “Why are the Resources making you sleepy?” If we ask a more general question like
“What were you thinking about just before the sleepiness started?” we might find it has nothing to do
with the Resources. Latching on to an incorrect assumption is misattunement. It’s much better to be
open and curious and let wounded parts tell us what’s happening.
A symptom like a headache or sleepiness may or may not be related to processing. If it comes up
suddenly in session, it’s more likely connected to a fear or concern, but even that’s not a certainty. Here
are several things we could ask to check this out:
If the

sleepiness / headache / tension

could talk, what would it say?

Is the

sleepiness / headache / tension

connected to what we’ve been processing?

Is there a wounded part connected to the

sleepiness / headache / tension ?

If these do not elicit a definitive answer, there’s another great way to find out. Let’s say a wounded part
who is fearful of doing any processing does not want to come right out and say so. Here’s a possible
dialogue to find out for sure.
Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:

Sure, I guess.
Why do you say “I guess” instead of “yes?”
I’m getting a headache.

Ouch. I’ve been getting a lot of headaches lately. It’s nothing.

Just kidding. We are going to process with the nine-year-old today.

I want all wounded parts to know we’re not going to do any processing over an objection.
Is there a part who will come talk to me and tell me what this objection is all about?

Paranormal Signals
Some of us are able to detect signals paranormally. For example, I will suddenly start to yawn or get
sleepy for no apparent reason (often just before the client starts yawning). This will most likely happen
when transitioning from small talk into an ego state intervention or when a subconscious fear or
concern is arising during an ego state intervention. It’s usually a sign that there’s something the client

Chapter 17: Attunement

229

or a wounded part needs to express. The first time this happens with a client, I explain my yawning
with something like this:
I’m starting to feel very sleepy. Sometimes I get sleepy in session because it’s late or I’m tired, but
most of the time, it’s because of something going on with my client. Right now, my sleepiness
feels like it’s related to something happening within you. I want you to know I’m not yawning
because I’m tired, bored, or disinterested in you or your story. It’s actually the opposite. I’m
probably detecting a concern or need that’s important for you to express. Is there something you
need to tell me now?
Often the client does not immediately know what needs to be expressed, but with a few questions and
a bit of patience, it will surface. Once the concern gets expressed, my sleepiness instantly stops. Here’s
an example of a typical paranormal interaction. This script begins as the client and therapist are
transitioning from discussing the client’s previous week to resuming processing.
Therapist:
Adult Self:

to pick up where we left off with her?
Yes.

concern or need? Is there something you need to tell me?
Hmm. Not that I know of.
Has something important happened since our last session that you haven’t already
mentioned?
No, I don’t think so.

Definitely not.

(Long, thoughtful pause) Last week, right at the very end of our session, a tenyear-old part showed up.

important to say. Maybe we should start by inviting her back. How does that idea
feel in your body?
That feels right. Let’s do that.
Good. I feel awake now.

The first few times I get sleepy in session, clients/parts usually worry they’ve done something wrong
that’s upsetting me. I’ll reassure them that I’m not upset at all and I want to be totally tuned in. After
we’ve had a few successful encounters like this example above, clients come to appreciate the benefits
of my sleepiness. They value this high degree of attunement. In future sessions, when they see me
yawn, they’re glad I’m very tuned in. They’re aware that it’s an important part of the healing process
with a big payoff.

Misattunement
We cannot be perfectly attuned 100% of the time. Misattunement can happen when we’re projecting,
not paying close attention, failing to see reality clearly, attached to incorrect assumptions, or trying to
attune but missing the mark. Some misattunement is inevitable. When it happens, a client may say

230

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

something outright like “You’re not listening. You don’t understand,” or we may see subtle evidence
of misattunement in the client’s body language, tone of voice, or facial expressions.
A big therapist misattunement can elicit frustration, anger, and an urge to withdraw. Unfortunately, we
could misattribute this reaction to something else, like an internal conflict or an old childhood wound.
If a client or child part starts having a strong reaction, we must stop whatever we’re doing and find out
why. If it’s because we’ve been misattuned, we make a swift apology. “I’m so sorry—that was my bad.
I failed to understand. I’d like to get this right.” We discuss the misstep to fully repair the break and
get back on the same page. Repairing breaks can strengthen the client-therapist relationship. Failing
to repair can end it.
At a given moment, we’ll be more or less attuned to a child part we’re talking to. Sometimes being
attuned just means noticing that we’re misattuned—noticing that a wounded part is hard to read. We
ask the part follow-up questions like “What are you needing right now?” “How can I help you right now?”
“Is there something else we need to talk about?” or “Is there something I’m failing to understand?”
When the child part sees we’re working on becoming highly attuned, it sends the message “You matter
to me.” This can help get the attunement back on track.

Being Authentic, Animated, and Accepting
Imagine you’re a small child telling your grandparents how you feel about your favorite teddy bear
being destroyed by your dog. Your grandmother gets down on the floor with you. She looks right at
you with her sad, teary eyes. She puts her hand on her heart and in a sweet and gentle voice says “I’m
so sorry. I know how much that teddy bear meant to you. He was such a good bear. I know you’ll miss
him a lot. Good bears like that don’t come along every day. This is a very big loss for you. I’ll sit with,
and cry with you, for as long as you want to talk about it.” In contrast, your grandfather maintains a
detached demeanor. He says without any resonating emotion, “I hear that you’re sad. This must be
difficult for you. Perhaps the dog thought it was his teddy bear.” Which grandparent will you choose
to help you through your loss?
Many of us were taught to keep a detached demeanor with clients—to be neutral and not show strong
emotions—but that may feel unfriendly and unkind to wounded child parts. They want us to be fully
present, real, and authentic. If their story makes us sad or angry, they want to see that. Child parts want
us to animate our words, vocal tone, facial expressions, and body language—just like hurting children
do. Our voice and body language should convey warmth, unconditional acceptance, and the message
“I can sense your pain, but it does not overwhelm me. I’m here for you.”
Some therapy models encourage us to challenge irrational behaviors, beliefs, emotions, and urges in
a way that essentially tells clients their inner world is irrational and must be corrected. But to wounded
child parts, this can feel invalidating. They want to hear from us and their Resources that their chaotic
inner world makes sense to us, that we understand how it came to be. They want to know we empathize
and lovingly accept them exactly as they are, right now—no matter what they feel, think, or do. Some
might predict this radical acceptance would exacerbate the “illogic” of the client’s chaotic inner world.
In reality, this is where healing begins.

Getting Hidden Information by Asking the Body
Sometimes the client’s adult self can easily name the needs and concerns we must address, but when
the adult self is overintellectualizing or misattuned to the wounded parts’ needs, the direction she’s
suggesting we go may be wrong. We can get in touch with what wounded parts want by inviting the
body to tell us. All we need is carefully worded yes-no questions. We start by saying something like

Chapter 17: Attunement

231

“I’m going to ask a question. Close your eyes, and let your body answer with a YES, NO, or MAYBE,”
and then we ask a question. Here’s an example of how that might go.
Adult Self:
Therapist:

I got triggered at work today when a coworker told me she’d been sexually assaulted over
the weekend. I was assaulted exactly the same way ten years ago in my college dorm.
I’m so sorry. That must have been horrible for her and a disturbing reminder for you.

I’m doing better now. I’ve been able to keep myself distracted all day.

sexual assault trigger today?
The body says “yes.”
Hmmm. What do you make of that?
Sounds like we need to work on the assault trigger.

We will usually get a clear “yes” or “no” response to our question, but sometimes we’ll get a “maybe.”
A “maybe” needs clarification. We can ask the body additional yes-no questions until we get a solid
“yes” or “no” answer, or we can ask the wounded parts who answered “maybe” to tell us more about
what they need.

Confusion Is Our Friend
Sooner or later, every ego state therapist gets confused. We should expect it! Several confusing things
can happen. Here are some examples:
 A wounded part feels threatened by loving, robust Resources.
 A wounded part suddenly disappears for no apparent reason.
 A wounded part gives mixed messages about what she believes.
 Several parts are present, and they’re all talking, but it’s unclear which one is saying what.
Confusion is uncomfortable, so naturally we want to get clarity as soon as possible. It’s tempting to get
out of confusion quickly by making a snap assumption. After all, we’re the smart professionals who
should have all the answers, right? We think we should always know what’s going on. If our snap
assumption is correct, our follow-up will be helpful. But if our assumption is incorrect, our follow-up
will not be helpful, wounded parts will sense we’re misattuned, and we’ll get very lost, very fast. Making
assumptions is risky. It’s better to stop, be open and curious, develop hypotheses, and check them out.
With experience, we can develop a “drop-down menu” of good hypotheses about what’s going on.
For example, let’s say a seven-year-old wounded part says, “The Resources don’t understand my
anger.” That’s clearly confusing, because robust Resources should be able to do this. Four hypotheses
come to mind: (1) the Resources are not actually robust, (2) wounded parts who don’t understand the

232

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

anger are standing in for the Resources, (3) the seven-year-old part is projecting someone who doesn’t
understand the anger on to the Resources, or (4) the Resources don’t really understand her anger.
Each hypothesis can be tested. To test the first hypothesis (Resources are not actually robust), we
consider whether the Resources have been consistently effective in the past. If so, we can rule this one
out. To test the second hypothesis (wounded parts are standing in for the Resources), we ask the client,
“Are any wounded parts standing in for the Resources?” If the answer is “no,” we can rule this out. To
test the third hypothesis (wounded parts are projecting on to the Resources), we ask the seven-yearold part, “Do the Resources remind you of someone unkind?” If the answer is “no,” we can rule out
projection. To test the fourth hypothesis (Resources don’t understand her anger), we ask the Resources,
“Do you understand her anger?” Testing hypotheses this way keeps us from making erroneous snap
assumptions. If a single question does not give us our answer, we many need to ask many more. Once
we know why the wounded part believes the Resources don’t understand her anger, we can apply the
needed remedy. (For clarity on these hypotheses, go to Resource-Related Concerns section of Chapter
18 on page 247.)
If we have no starting hypotheses, or if after testing all our hypotheses we’re still confused, we must
ask follow-up questions. These general questions can serve as a starting point.
I’m confused. What exactly do you mean by _____________?
I’m confused. So, little one, say more about ______________.
I’m confused. Help me understand. You said ______ and ______. How do these dots connect?
A single answer to one question might be enough to get to clarity, but sometimes we need a long
discussion. I find it can be like walking down a long, winding steppingstone path in a garden, at night, with
only a flashlight. The flashlight (our query) is only bright enough to illuminate the next steppingstone
(one relevant clue). We ask questions until we find the first relevant clue. With that clue in hand, we ask
more questions until we find the next relevant clue. We keep asking questions until all the relevant
clues come into focus and confusion is replaced with understanding. Here’s an example of how that
can go:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:

Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:

So, seven-year-old, do the Resources remind you of someone unkind?
Yes. Maybe. I don’t know.
Say more about that.
I have a sense of something, but I don’t know who.
Okay. Let’s see if we can get some clarity. I’m going to name some people from
your past who might have failed to understand your anger. Just notice how your

Not what I was expecting.

One might expect that clients would think less of us if we admit that we’re confused, but if our query
conveys the underlying message “What you’re experiencing matters to me, and I will patiently work
hard to understand,” they’ll think, “Wow—my therapist really wants to get me! That’s fantastic. No one
has ever wanted to understand me this way.” Clients deeply appreciate the attunement, and that alone
can be very healing! If our confusion does trigger a wounded part’s fear of rejection, we can offer a
reassurance like this:
It makes perfect sense that you’d be worried my confusion would upset me and cause me to reject
you, but you need to know I’m very comfortable being confused. It doesn’t upset me at all. It just
makes me more curious about you. Let’s keep talking about this until I completely understand
your point of view.
Many of us are wounded healers. If our own childhood wounding has paired confusion with shame,
being confused by a client can trigger our wounded parts. When we’re triggered, we cannot honestly
say we’re comfortable being confused. That will be a big problem. I encourage any therapist who gets
triggered by confusion to seek therapy to resolve this.

234

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Holding a Mental Picture
As clients become aware of their wounded parts, they’re usually able to get a mental picture and/or
felt sense of them. This mental picture/felt sense will change as the processing unfolds. As the client
tells us about the wounded parts’ experience with each stabilization task, we want to hold a mental
picture of what’s transpiring within our client. When a client pictures or senses a new wounded part
arriving, we picture that. When she pictures or senses a part being reassured by the Resources, we
picture that. When she pictures or senses a part settling into her Special Safe Place, we picture that. As
long as our mental picture matches the client’s experience, we’re attuned. But because this is ego state
therapy, it can get complicated. One moment we’ll have a good mental picture and a clear understanding
of what’s happening, and the next moment an odd comment will mess with our picture. If we cannot
hold a clear mental picture, we’re confused and need to stop and get clarification. Here’s an example.
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:

Adult Self:

Therapist:

So, four-year-old, would it feel good to get up close to the Resources?
Yes. They look good. They’re reaching out to me. (Pause) Uh-oh! My parents are
angry. Resources aren’t strong enough. It’s all dark now. (Pause) They’re gone.
Brianna, I’m confused. I was picturing the four-year-old feeling good about the
Resources reaching out to her. Then I heard “My parents are angry,” “everything
went dark,” and “they’re gone.” Did the Resources go away, or did your angry
parents go away? Help me understand what happened.
The four-year-old was watching the Resources approach. She liked them but was
worried about getting too close. Then a dark angry cloud moved in. It felt like Mom
and Dad anger. It scared her away. I don’t know where the four-year-old is now.
The Resources are gone too. All I see is an angry cloud.
Thanks. That helps a lot. Now I understand.

The comment, “They’re gone,” is confusing because we don’t know which “they” are gone. It could
mean the Resources are gone, the angry parents are gone, the wounded part and Resources are gone,
the wounded part and angry parents are gone, or all the above. If we make an incorrect assumption
about which one of these happened, our follow-up intervention will be ineffective. By stopping and
getting clarification, we can learn what exactly transpired. This empowers us to provide the most
effective intervention. (In this case, we’d probably want to start a rapport-building dialogue with the
angry cloud—most likely a mimicking reactive part.)

Window of Nurturing Attunement
The Window of Tolerance is a term that refers to a zone of arousal. When we’re within this window,
we’re able to manage whatever stress we’re experiencing. If we’re outside this window, we’re either
hyper-aroused (freaked out) or hypo-aroused (numbed out). This is an especially important concept
for EMDR therapists, since effective trauma processing can happen only when a client is within her
window of tolerance.
When it comes to stabilizing wounded parts, there’s another important window—what the DNMS calls
the Window of Nurturing Attunement. Inside this window, wounded parts are comforted by the
Resources, so they feel safe and loved, held, and contained. When outside this window, they’re either
hyper-aroused or hypo-aroused, feeling stuck in their painful past and unable to manage their distress.
We can bring a wounded part into the Window of Nurturing Attunement by using a soft, reassuring
voice, speaking to the part as we’d speak to a child of that age. We validate, empathize, and show lots
of emotional resonance. We encourage a healing dialogue between the part and the Resource team.
That may be enough to bring the part into the window. If not, we can also orient her to the present
time and explain about the harmless recording. Even if we’ve done this in the past, we do it again. Each

Chapter 17: Attunement

235

repetition promotes the development of the neural architecture needed for the regulation of painful
emotions. Eventually the adult self will be able to do this for the wounded parts without our help. The
benefits of this stabilization will pay off well when it’s time for EMDR trauma processing.
Occasionally in a session, a wounded part will become very overwhelmed by something triggering. If
she gets too far outside the Window of Nurturing Attunement, the Resources (adult self) have gone
offline. At that moment, they are not available to reassure the part. If the client’s Resources have been
paired with something, we can invite the client to smell the scent, listen to the music, or look at and
touch the jewelry or object. This may be enough to bring the Resources back online and bring the part
back into the Window of Nurturing Attunement.
If that doesn’t work, we can softly touch the client’s knee, foot, or hand to help bring her back into her
body and into the present. We then start a conversation with her adult self about something we know
she’ll feel good about. For example, if a client loves her job as a veterinarian, we’d get her talking about
a recent success treating a sick animal. Here are some examples.
Tell me about something amazing you did this week.
Tell me about a recent success at

work / school / home

Tell me about your plans for your next

.

weekend / holiday / vacation

.

Tell me about something interesting that happened to you this week.
A few minutes of dialogue with the adult self should shift things. When the body appears calm, we check
in with the wounded part who was feeling overwhelmed. “So, little one, how are you doing now?” She’ll
likely be substantially calmer. This should enable the wounded part to connect to the Resource team,
bringing her into the Window of Nurturing Attunement. Here’s an example of how this might go.
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:
Adult Self:

Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:

Therapist:
Wounded Part:

(Uncontrollable crying) I can’t take it anymore. Dad won’t leave me alone!
So, nine-year-old, do the Resources understand why you’re in such despair?
(Uncontrollable crying) It’s never going to get better.
I hear you feel hopeless. Do the Resources understand why you feel so hopeless?
(Uncontrollable crying) No one understands. No one will ever understand!
So, Brianna, tell me about a success you had this week.
(Pause) Hmmm. Ahh. Well, umm, my book club met this week. We all had to take
a covered dish. I made a bean salad I’d never made before. It was delicious.

It’s called Texas Caviar. It’s made with black-eyed peas, onion, tomato, bell pepper,

Well done! (Pause) So how are you doing now? Feeling calmer?
Yes, I am.
So, nine-year-old, how are you doing now?
Better. Thanks!
Do the Resources understand how upset you were?
Yes. They’re giving me a big hug now. It feels good.

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

Attuned Grief Processing
Wounded parts often feel stuck in painful losses like a miserable childhood, failed relationships,
rejecting parents, etc. Many parts believe grief is too painful to explore with no idea that it can process
through to emotional relief. Grieving parts need lots of loving, validating, attuned support from us and
their Resource team. I describe how we provide that in this section, but first I talk about what’s not so
effective support: trying to cheer up parts with Yes-But Reassurance. Yes-But Reassurance sounds
something like this:
Yes, you had a miserable childhood, but you have a good life now.
Yes, you had lots of failed relationships in the past, but you have a loving wife now.
Yes, your parents were neglectful and rejecting in the past, but they’re nice to you now.
These comments could evoke a measure of temporary relief, or they could feel invalidating, dismissing,
or minimizing. Grieving parts want us to see their reality clearly and empathize fully. It’s better if we
say something validating.
Yes, you had a miserable childhood. You can’t put lipstick on that pig. It just sucks.
Yes, you had lots of failed relationships in the past, and those losses still hurt now. Ouch!
Yes, your parents were neglectful and rejecting in the past. That’s a big deal—a huge loss.
These statements are fully resonant with wounded parts’ emotions. When they feel completely seen,
heard, and understood, it brings a measure of healing and relief. It’s actually much easier to lean into
their agonizing grief with empathy, than to figure out ways to minimize it.

Handling Resistance to Grieving
Resistance to grieving is fairly common. It’s usually based on the false assumption that grieving feels
bad, and there’s no good reason to do it. When wounded parts express this concern, there are four
new ideas they need to hear from us: (1) working through grief has a beginning, a middle, and an end,
(2) with loving support, the painful emotions can process through to resolution and peace, (3) loving
support can actually feel good at the same time the sense of loss is agonizing, and (4) at the end of
grief processing, we receive a gift—wisdom and maturity—that helps us prepare for future losses.
Here’s an example of how this important information might be conveyed.
Wounded Part:
Therapist:

Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:

Wounded Part:

I’m so mad. My mom and dad dumped on me for years. It’s completely messed up
my life. I’ll never get that time back.
So, fifteen-year-old, you’re right to be mad. You have a lot to grieve here. Their
failure to support you has led to lots of missed opportunities. I’m really sorry that
happened. Notice the Resources are here to love and support you as you begin to
grieve this huge loss.
I don’t want to grieve. It’s just overwhelming pain.
Did anyone in your childhood ever talk to you about processing through painful
emotions?
I don’t think so. What does that mean?
I’m glad you asked. Working through grief has a beginning, a middle, and an end.
The beginning part is the most painful. It’s probably the part of grieving you know
best. If feeling that intense pain was the only part of grieving, you’d be right to
avoid it, but with loving support from those who care, you can process it through
to resolution and peace.
I’m not getting loving support. No one wants me to talk about it. Everyone around
me wants me to get over it. They’re right. I need to harden up and be strong.

here with you.

as long as you need.

With the right encouragement, wounded parts will feel safe enough to share their grief with the Resources.
When the good feeling from that loving connection feels stronger than the pain of the grief, adding
ABS may help process it more thoroughly.

Keeping Track of Who We’re Talking To
Our client’s adult self and all her wounded parts are in the same grown-up body. That presents a challenge
in detecting which part of self within the self-system we’re in dialogue with. Questions directed at the
adult self could be answered by a child part. Questions directed to a child part could be answered by
the adult self. Questions directed to one wounded part could be answered by another wounded part.
Changes in facial expression, body language, tone of voice, and gestures give us clues as to which part
is talking, but it’s not always obvious. We must stay vigilant to this reality. We must recognize when
we’re unsure who is answering, so we can stop and find out.
If we ask the adult self a question and the answer seems oddly childlike or immature, we can ask who
answered. Likewise, if we ask a child part a question and the answer seems oddly rational and mature,
we can ask who answered. Here are two examples.
Example 1
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:

So, Emily, are you ready to visit your family over the holidays?
I’m terrified to see my mother. I know she’s going to scream at me.
Is that a little one answering?
Yes.
Emma, is your most adult self terrified to visit your family?

238

Adult Self:
Example 2
Therapist:
Adult Self:

Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:

No. I expect it will be stressful, but I’m not terrified.
So, little one, how are you feeling about visiting your family over the holidays?
I’m not worried. I’ve got a paper due in January. I’ll take my laptop, so I
expect I’ll spend most of my time working on my paper. I know how to
stay out of my mother’s way.
Emily, is that your adult self answering?
Yes.
So, little one, how are you feeling about the visit?
I’m dreading it. I don’t want to go.

Not only can a client’s adult self have a different perspective than wounded child parts, but child parts
can have different perspectives from each other—perspectives driven by individual wants, needs,
beliefs, and concerns. That means various wounded parts can have competing agendas that lead to
internal conflicts and double binds. For example, what makes one part feel safe may make another part
feel afraid. As we work with one part, other parts are watching. Sometimes those other parts react to
what they’re observing and insert themselves into the conversation. It’s imperative that we notice when
this happens so we can clarify who is talking. Here are two examples of how that might sound.
Example 1

Uninvited parts show up for an important reason, so we want to follow that thread. It may be a very
short conversation, or it could be long and involved. Either way, we use whatever rapport-building
skills and stabilization interventions are needed to help the new part get the relief she needs. When
she no longer needs to intrude, we go back to working with the first part. (Read more about intrusive
parts on page 152.)

Challenges in Talking to Wounded Parts
Usually when we talk to wounded parts, we’ll be in a direct back-and-forth dialogue. We ask a question,
and the wounded part gives us her answer. Here’s a sample dialogue.
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:

So, little one, how old are you?
Four.
So, four-year-old, what mood are you in?
Sad.
I’m sorry to hear that. Tell me your story. What made you so sad?
My mom is really mean to me.

Sometimes the adult self conveys a wounded part’s answer to us. When this happens, we can ask the
part, “So, little one, can you speak to me directly?” If that’s not comfortable for some reason, we can
have the entire conversation with the adult self answering each question for the wounded part. Here’s
a sample dialogue.
Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:
Adult Self:
Therapist:
Adult Self:

So, little one, how old are you?
She’s four.
So, four-year-old, what mood are you in?
She’s sad.
I’m sorry to hear that. So, four-year-old, can you speak to me directly?
She’d rather not.
Okay. That’s fine. So, four-year-old, tell me your story. What made you so sad?
She’s sad because mom was really mean.

If the adult self is going to convey a part’s answer to us, we could direct our questions to the adult self
or to the wounded part. For example, I could ask, “So, Brianna, why is the four-year-old sad?” versus
“So, four-year-old, why are you sad?” I believe it’s better to direct all questions to the wounded part
even if we know the adult self is going to give us her answer. Asking the wounded part directly makes
it more likely any answers the adult self reports to us are genuinely from the part. If we keep asking
the wounded part questions directly, at some point she may feel comfortable answering us directly.
As we try to dialogue with a wounded part, a talkative adult self might monopolize the conversation
with irrelevant disclosures. If we’re not careful that can take us off track. We must notice when this
happens and bring the conversation back to the wounded part who needs to be stabilized. Here’s an
example of bringing the conversation back to a wounded part.
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:
Wounded Part:
Therapist:

Four.

Sad.

My mom is really mean to me. I ask her for a hug, and she sneers at me. She tells
me she never wanted me. She says she hates having to take care of me and wishes
I would die.

Yes.

acknowledge such disclosures and then return to tasks that stabilize the child part.

System-Wide Announcement
A system-wide announcement is a blanket message for reassuring many fearful parts, at the same time,
to build rapport and calm irrational fears, whether identified or not. Their fears are usually about
processing a particular issue or doing therapy in general. Here’s an example of what a system-wide
announcement could sound like.
I’d like all parts to listen now. Each one of you is valuable to me. I don’t want to harm any of you
or eliminate any of you. I want to help each of you heal so you can grow to meet your full
potential. You get to decide what that is. If you’ve been stuck in the past, I’d like to help you get
unstuck. I’m interested in any concerns or fears you have, and I’m willing to patiently answer all
your questions.
Afterward, we ask the client, “What do you notice in your body now?” If the body feels clear, the concern
is probably handled. If the body is not clear, we say to the system of parts, “I’d love to know more
about your concerns. Can a part come forward and talk to me?” We stay in dialogue with the concerned
parts until their issues are fully handled and the body is clear.

Talking to Parts Indirectly
Sometimes we want to deliver a message to a wounded part who’s triggered but cannot do it directly.
That could happen when the client’s adult self has not yet acknowledged having wounded parts or has
not given us permission to talk to wounded parts. A wounded part who’s triggered will hear everything
we say. Here’s an example of how we can send a soothing message to a triggered part by talking to
the adult self. My client Allison does not want me talking to her parts. She has not mobilized Resources
yet. One day a five-year-old part has a flashback to a horrific moment of physical abuse inflicted by
her mother. The part is overwhelmed with fear. I say something like:
Allison, I can see you’re very upset remembering what your mother did to you at age five. It may
help to know that while your mother was doing that, your brain made a recording of it, like filming
a home movie. It appears that talking to me about this today has pressed play on that old
recording. This has flooded you with all the same painful emotions you felt back then. Right? The
intensity of these emotions can create the illusion that the old recording of her is just as real and
just as important as her abuse was back then. It can create the illusion that those old experiences
are still happening and still relevant right now, but they aren't. You’re not reliving those old bad
experiences you’re just reliving your reaction to them. It might feel like your mother is here right
now, putting you in danger, but I can assure you, this old recording cannot hurt you now like your
mother hurt you back then. It’s completely harmless. What’s it like to hear that?
Since Allison’s mother does not currently play a significant role in her life, I can also ask questions like:
Where is your mother now? When was the last time you spoke to her? If she wanted to move in
with you now, and torment you like she did when you were five, would you have to let her? When
you were five you were completely dependent on your mother for a home to live in, clothes to

242

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

wear, and food to eat. Are you still dependent on her now? How does it feel to acknowledge the
power you have in an adult body now, that you did not have back then?

Chapter 17 Summary
In this chapter we explore a number of topics related to the art of attuning to wounded parts.
We look at how to detect and follow-up on subtle signals of distress. We explore how to correct
inevitable moments of misattunement. We ask the body to reveal unexpressed concerns and
beliefs when clients overintellectualize. We look at the complications of therapist confusion—
an inevitable part of ego state therapy. The Window of Tolerance is contrasted with the DNMS
Window of Nurturing Attunement—where wounded parts feel so loved and safe in the care of
the Resources that they can tolerate stress without getting hyper- or hypo-aroused. We explore
the difference between yes-but reassurance in response to grief, versus attuned support. The
challenges of talking to wounded parts are explored here, with remedies for common problems.

Chapter 18
Overcoming Processing Blocks

Processing blocks can arise as we endeavor to stabilize wounded parts. This chapter focuses on how
to detect and handle stabilization blocks. Guidance on how to clear blocks to mobilizing Resources can
be found in Chapters 6 and 7, the Resource mobilization chapters.
Therapists often think of processing blocks as a problem. I prefer to think of them as opportunities.
Investigating blocks helps us learn valuable information about a client’s internal world. Clearing blocks
does not just make way for the healing work; it is the healing work. It helps wounded child parts feel
seen, heard, and valued at moments they feel especially vulnerable. This can be very stabilizing. When
processing blocks arise, it’s best to lean in with a big welcome!

Importance of an Effective Therapy Goal
Clients start therapy with certain therapy goals in mind—perhaps to stop problematic behaviors,
silence negative beliefs, or calm intense, painful emotions. Efforts to pursue such goals may be blocked
by wounded parts who fear problems will arise if these goals are met. To prevent this problem, early
in the therapy we invite wounded parts to tell us their objections to the therapy goals. Together we set
new goals that all wounded parts are happy to support. If we do this up front, fewer blocks will come
up when we begin stabilizing wounded parts. Details about how to set effective therapy goals are
provided in Chapter 9.

Detecting a Concern
Sometimes blocking concerns are readily expressed, like when a wounded part says outright, “I’m afraid
the Resources will leave me.” But often we only get a clue. A clue can arise before, during, or after
processing. Here are some common clues:
 The sudden arrival of a headache, body tension, nausea, dissociation, or sleepiness.
 A sudden onset of yawning or fidgeting.
 A sudden surge of distracting thoughts.
 A sudden urge to end the session.

244

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

 A sudden change in the subject to something off-topic.
 A sudden increase in intensity of a preexisting body pain or fatigue.
 Fatigue or body disturbance right after stabilizing a part.
 Resources fail to show up, show up but then disappear, appear but then become vague, flicker in
and out, or seem very far away.
 A wounded part we’ve invited forward fails to show up.
 A wounded part we’re talking to suddenly disappears.
 Sessions are canceled for no apparent reason.

Eliciting a Concern
If we suspect a blocking concern, we must first find out what it is. There are many ways to inquire. We
can ask a question related to a symptom, the healing process, or the outcome. We can list possible
concerns, or we can imagine being the client. I elaborate on each of these below. There is no perfect
starting point, and we must follow our instincts. If our first question does not reveal a concern, we ask
another and keep asking questions until we understand what’s going on.
 Questions related to a symptom: Here are some sample questions that tag a symptom indicative
of a blocking concern. We can modify these to fit any situation.
If the

sleepiness

Why does this

could talk, what would it say?

headache

need to be here right now? How does it help?

What were you thinking just before the

fidgeting

started?

I see you’ve suddenly become sleepy . Is a wounded part connected to this sleepiness
? May I talk to that part? Welcome. I’m really glad you’re here. There must be a very good
reason for
feeling sleepy
. right now. Tell me about that.

 Questions about the healing process or outcome: When the process doesn’t unfold as we expect,
it helps to ask wounded parts questions about their core beliefs about healing.
So, little one, what does “healing” mean to you?
Do you believe it’s safe to heal?
What do you think the healing journey involves?
Who do you believe sets the pace of your healing journey?
Would anyone be upset if you healed?
How would “being healed” affect your life?
Do you have any concerns about one or more of the Resources?
If you were to
successfully heal / connect to the Resources / process your grief , what
would you lose, or what bad thing would happen? Would there be a downside to that?
If your wounding childhood parents were sitting here right now, commenting on your interest
in healing / bonding with the Resources / processing grief , what would they say to you?

 List possible concerns: We can also list possible concerns and invite wounded parts to tell us if any
of them are correct. For example, a client wounded by an enmeshing mother gets a headache when
I tell a wounded child part that the Resources want to nurture her. She can’t tell me why she has a
headache, so I ask the following.
So, little one, if you were to let the Resources nurture you, would that be the same as being
disloyal to your mother? …abandoning your mother? …discounting her positive qualities?

Chapter 18: Overcoming Processing Blocks

245

While I sometimes succeed in naming the concern, often all the possibilities I list are wrong. But
often it miraculously prompts the wounded part to say, “No, that’s not it, it’s _______________.”
 Imagine being the client: It may help to imagine what it was like to be the client at the age the
blocking part got wounded. We ask ourselves, “If this was my life, what would my biggest problems
be? What would I be most afraid of?” This may generate some helpful questions.

Validating a Concern
Once we understand the blocking fear or concern, we offer a heartfelt validation. This is important
because wounded parts are more likely to shift their point of view if they feel understood. A good
validation goes something like this:
I hear

(name fear/concern)

. That makes perfect sense considering

(validate fear/concern)

.

We fill in the first blank with a restatement of the fear or concern. We fill in the second blank with an
attuned validation—something we know about the part’s perspective that could account for the fear.
Here are two examples:
I hear you’re afraid you’ll lose your ability to mistrust. That makes perfect sense considering how
protective mistrust has been for you.
I hear you’re afraid of being nurtured. That makes perfect sense considering how your mother’s
nurturing was so painfully enmeshing.

Handling Blocking Concerns
There are five types of concerns that can block processing: (1) here-and-now issues, (2) missed priorities,
(3) missing information, (4) misunderstandings, and (5) an inability to envision the healing process. I’ll
describe how we handle each type.

Here-and-Now Issues
Here-and-now issues are about current concerns that take priority over deep processing of old wounds.
They’re concerns usually held by the adult self. This can happen, for example, when a client is too sick,
hungry, sleep deprived, distracted by a crisis, or upset with the therapist to focus on processing. The
remedy is usually obvious. The client may need to eat, sleep, or take time to recover from an illness.
She may need to talk about a current crisis or repair a break with the therapist. When a here-and-now
issue is fully resolved, the client should be ready to make processing old wounds a priority.
Sometimes wounded parts (holding unexpressed concerns) generate or exaggerate here-and-now
issues to distract the therapist with an aim to avoid deep processing. We should suspect this when a
client seems to have distracting here-and-now issues for many consecutive sessions. We can check on
this by asking questions like “Is there a wounded part fearful of returning to the processing?” or “If we
were to get this current problem resolved and return to healing wounded parts, is there something
you’d lose or something bad that would happen? Would there be a downside to that?” If we can get
the wounded part talking, we can find the blocking concern and clear it. Once it’s cleared, we should be
able to get back to the deeper work.

Missed Priorities
Distressing issues are ranked by the self-system in order of importance. Ideally, we’re always working
on the most important issue. When we start on a target that’s not really the highest priority, wounded
parts within the system will often try to stop us, often indirectly. For example, right after we invite a

246

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

wounded part to approach the Resources, a client suddenly gets very sleepy. We can say, “I’m going
to ask a question. Close your eyes, and just let your body answer with a YES, NO, or MAYBE. We have
selected to work on ___________ today. Is there something more important for us to work on right now?”
If the body says “yes,” we must invite forward the parts who can tell us what needs to be targeted.
Once we start working on the highest priority issue, the symptom (like sleepiness) will go away.

Missing Information
Wounded parts are usually child parts. And just like children, they have limited information. They’ll make
assumptions and draw conclusions about the therapy process or outcome based on their limited
understanding of self and world. When necessary, we must fill in that missing information. For example,
if they believe a relationship with the Resources will not go well because all relationships end in
irreparable breaks, we must explain how breaks can be repaired. If they expect the therapy will tap into
anger that’s shameful to express, we show them the value of expressing anger. If they expect the
therapy will unleash intense grief that they cannot process through, we explain that it can process
through with the Resources’ loving support. If they believe that no one can be trusted and decline to
trust us or the Resources, we teach them how to discern people who can be trusted from those who
cannot. If they expect therapy will go badly—just like childhood—we validate that expectation and
explain why it’s not true. Anytime wounded parts reveal blocking concerns based on missing
information, we teach them what they need to know so they can trust the process will be safe and the
outcome will be good. The section Scripts That Fill In Missing Information on page 170 provides
sample scripts on how to do this.

Misunderstandings
Wounded parts often hold beliefs and assumptions about who they are, how others will behave, and
what they must do to stay connected and safe. These beliefs can adversely influence their perception
of the therapy process and outcome. We must clear all misunderstandings that block therapy progress.
For example, if parts believe healing means losing a familiar sense of identity, we tell them they can
evolve a healthier sense of identity. If parts believe healing means losing a valued coping strategy, we
explain that they’ll gain more coping strategies. If parts believe healing means losing the motivating
power of fear, we explain that motivation can also come from desire. If parts feel a paralyzing burden
of past or future responsibility, we explain with whom the burden of responsibility truly lies. If parts
believe they’re powerless, we show them how much power they really have now. If parts are afraid of
being nurtured, we show them that it’s safe to be nurtured now. Anytime wounded parts reveal
blocking concerns based on misunderstandings, we explain what they need to know so they’ll trust the
process will be safe and the outcome will be good. The section Scripts That Clear Misunderstandings
on page 172 provides sample scripts on how to do this.

Trouble Envisioning the Healing Process
Some wounded parts are reluctant to engage in healing interventions because they can’t envision them
or don’t understand what’s involved. A part may say something like “I don’t trust the process because
I don’t know what it is.” This is easy to remedy. We describe the process as it would unfold with a
hypothetical client. We start with a hypothetical issue, describe connecting to the Resources, inviting a
triggered part forward, getting the part bonding with the Resources, orienting the part to the present
time, explaining about the harmless recording, and settling in. We invite the reluctant part to ask
questions about the interventions we’ve just described, to build a sense of trust in the process. When
the wounded part can see how safe, loving, and affirming the process is, the reluctance to engage
usually goes away.

Chapter 18: Overcoming Processing Blocks

247

Resource-Related Concerns
Nurturing Resources play a key role in the interventions that stabilize wounded parts. If wounded parts
have trouble making a loving connection to Resources, we must stop and find out why. There are four
main reasons for this: (1) wounded parts are projecting unkind caregivers onto robust Resources, (2)
wounded parts are standing in for robust Resources, (3) the “Resources” are not actually robust, or (4)
wounded parts are blocking access to robust Resources. Let’s take a closer look at each of these.

Wounded Parts Are Projecting onto Robust Resources
When clients first mobilize Resources, they’re experienced as loving, compassionate, nurturing, protective,
and attuned. Later, child parts we’re working with can project a negative attitude onto these loving
Resources—inaccurately perceiving them as incompetent, untrustworthy, indifferent, rejecting,
enmeshing, threatening, or unable to meet needs. This is most obvious when parts tell us outright that
they expect the Resources will behave like someone unkind from their past. They say things like “The
Resources will leave me just like my dad did” or “The Resources will smother me just like my mom did.”
Sometimes we just get clues, like when wounded parts saying things like “The Resources won’t like me”
or “They’re going punish me.” We follow up by asking, “Do one or more of the Resources remind you
of someone unkind?” or “Who in your past treated you that way?” If we discover parts are expecting the
Resources to act like someone wounding, we must break the projection so they can see the Resources
are really loving and kind. Four ways to break a projection are described below. If one doesn’t work,
we try another.
How to Break a Projection
 Refer to the Resource Anchor or Vetting: When wounded parts doubt the integrity of a Resource, we
can refer them back to their vetting experience for either the Healing Circle (page 75) or Provisional
Resources (page 113). For example, an elderly client named Bob has a Provisional caregiver-self
Resource named Grandpa Bob. We know from the vetting that Grandpa Bob competently cares for
his grandson, Tommy. In session, a wounded two-year-old part looks at the Grandpa Bob Resource
and senses his father’s rejection. It feels to this part as if Grandpa Bob is rejecting him too, but he’s
not. So, we ask the part, “Does Grandpa Bob love and cherish Tommy?” If “yes,” we ask, “Would he
ever reject Tommy?” If “no,” we say, “This Grandpa Bob Resource loves and cherishes you on exactly
the same way he loves Tommy. He would never reject you, just like he’d never reject Tommy. But
don’t take my word for it, ask Grandpa Bob. What does he say?”
 Compare Radiating Energy: We can help wounded parts make a contrast between wounding people
and loving Resources by comparing the energy they radiate. We say something like “Notice the
energy radiating from the Resources. Does that energy seem clean or dirty?” If it seems clean, we
ask, “Was the energy radiating from mother / father clean or dirty?” If “dirty,” we ask, “Can you
see the Resources are very different from mother / father ?” This contrast can help break a projection.
If the energy glow around the Resources is dirty, either wounded parts are standing in for the
Resources (see page 248) or the Resources are not actually robust (see page 249).
 Test the Resources: We can test the Resources with a hypothetical childhood scenario we tailor to
the part’s age and personal history. For example, we can say, “Let’s test the Resources. Let’s say
you’re eating a grape Popsicle on the floor while watching Saturday-morning cartoons. Your golden
retriever suddenly rushes in and startles you. You shriek, which wakes up your parents. They come
into the room and see you crying over a puddle of grape Popsicle melting into the carpet. What
would your childhood parents have done next?” Parts usually say parents would be angry and
punitive. We ask, “If the Resources had been your parents then, what would they have done?” Parts
usually say the Resources would be curious, loving, and kind. They would help clean up the mess.
This contrast can help break a projection. If the Resources fail the test (if they are not compassionate),

248

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

either wounded parts are standing in for the Resources (see below) or the Resources are not actually
robust (see page 249).
 Endorsement from a Trusted Resource: Sometimes wounded parts project onto one or two Resources
but not the whole Resource team. When this happens, a trusted Resource on the team may be able
to help break the projection by giving a positive endorsement. For example, my client Ariel mobilized
all three Healing Circle Resources. We selected an issue to work on and invited a triggered wounded
part to approach the Resource team. When she saw them, she said, “I don’t trust them. They’re going
to betray me just like my parents did.” She was clearly projecting qualities of her abusive parents
onto the NAS and PAS but maybe not the SCS. I asked the part, “Do you expect your Spiritual Core
Self will betray you?” When she said “no,” I asked, “Does your Spiritual Core Self trust the Nurturing
and Protective Adult Self? Does she think they’ll betray you like your abusive parents did?” The
wounded part said, “She’s says they’re good. They won’t betray me.” That positive endorsement
from the SCS helped break the projection.

Wounded Parts Are Standing In for Robust Resources
Sometimes wounded parts stand in for robust Resources. They’ll take over a nurturing or protective
Resource role, often because they fear losing their relevance within the self-system. We suspect this
when a wounded part we’re trying to stabilize says she does not trust a Resource or tells us a Resource
is unsupportive. We can ask something like “Is this Resource actually another wounded part?” or “Is
there a wounded part pretending to be this Resource right now?” If we confirm this, we must talk to
the part pretending to be the Resource.
We say, “May I speak to the wounded part standing in for the NAS / PAS ?” When we get a “yes,”
we say, “Hi there. Thanks for coming. I’m really glad you’re here. I’m sure you have a very good reason
for standing in for the NAS / PAS now. Tell me about that.” How we intervene will depend on their
answer. Reasons that wounded parts might be standing in, along with recommended remedies, are
described below. If one or more of these remedies are not enough to get a wounded part to feel good
about stepping back, stabilization interventions may help.
How to Handle Parts Standing In


If they’re afraid the Resources will behave in an untrustworthy way, we help them see the Resources
are loving and safe, like we do to break a projection. (See the How to Break a Projection section
on page 247.)



If they’re afraid of losing a position of relevance within the self-system because, for example, the
PAS has taken over as the main protector, we reassure them they will not become irrelevant. In
fact, they can become more relevant once they’ve healed.



If they’re afraid of losing a familiar sense of identity, we help them understand that healing will not
take away a sense of identity; it will help them develop a new one. (See the script on page 172.)



If they’re afraid of losing a valued coping strategy, we help them understand that they won’t lose
any old strategies, they’ll just get more of them. (See the script on page 173.)



If they think they’re supposed to be the Resources, we say, “The
NAS / PAS
must be healthy
adult parts of self. You’re forbidden to take that job. Your only job is to let the Resources meet
your needs—let them take care of, nurture, and protect you. How does that sound?”



If none of the above accounts for why wounded parts are standing in for the Resources, we must
verify that the Resources they’re covering for are actually robust. If they’re not, we must try again
to get a robust team. (See the ”Resources” Are Not Actually Robust section on page 249).

Chapter 18: Overcoming Processing Blocks

249

“Resources” Are Not Actually Robust
Sometimes we find weaknesses in a Resource team. We can usually detect weak Resources as soon as
wounded parts begin interacting with them. The first time we ask wounded parts if they feel safe and
comfortable with the Resources, we may get an answer like “I don’t know, maybe” or “Not really.” Or,
soon after we begin working with the Resources, wounded parts report they’re judgmental, indifferent,
unsympathetic, or tell her to “just get over it.” We start by asking, “Which Resources are being unkind
/ impatient / dismissive ?” When we know which ones are worrisome, we explore the possibility that
they are not actually robust. We start by checking for a weak anchoring experience in the Healing Circle
Resources or an insufficient vetting of Provisional Resources.
If there’s a problem with a Resource, we drop it, go back, and mobilize a robust Resource to complete
the team. This is what happened with my client Lillian. She had selected an anchoring experience for
her NAS and PAS meditations of caring for her pet tropical bird. When she first got the bird, they had
a wonderful bond. She was able to take very good care of him. With that in mind, she was able to easily
endorse each item on the list of skills (see page 77). Later, when we went to use the NAS and PAS, it
didn’t go well. She remembered that a year after getting the bird, she became too depressed to care
for him. The bird became ill and lost most of his feathers. She eventually gave the bird to a couple who
could nurture him back to health. We looked for a better caregiving experience to anchor the
meditations and were eventually able to mobilize a truly robust NAS and PAS.

Wounded Parts Block Access to Robust Resources
Wounded parts will block access to the Resources if they think doing so will handle a fear or concern.
There are several ways they can do this. They can stop Resources from showing up or, when Resources
are present, make them disappear, become vague, flicker in and out, or seem far away. They can also
make the client sleepy, achy, dissociated, or distracted. The parts blocking the Resources could be (1)
parts we’re just about to work with, (2) parts we’re in the middle of working with, or (3) parts watching
our work with other parts. We identify who is doing the blocking and inquire about the reason.
 Part we’re about to work with: If the block occurs just before we invite a wounded part forward,
we ask something like “Is the part I’m about to invite forward blocking the Resources right now?”
If “yes,” we start a dialogue. “Hi there. Thanks for coming. You must be doing this for a very good
reason. Tell me about that.” If “no,” the block is probably coming from a part that’s watching.
 Part we’re already working with: If the block occurs while we’re working with a wounded part, we
say something like “So, little one, are you blocking the Resources right now?” If “yes,” we say, “You
must be doing this for a very good reason. Tell me about that.” If “no,” the block is probably coming
from a part that’s watching.
 Part watching our work: When a targeted part tells us she’s not doing the blocking, we say, “I’d
like to speak to the part blocking the Resources right now. Welcome. I’m glad you’re here. You must
be doing this for a very good reason. Tell me about that.”
We listen for the concern to be revealed. If the blocking part cannot give a clear answer, we ask additional
questions like “If you were to allow the Resources to be fully present now, what would you lose, or what
bad thing would happen?” or “What were you thinking just before you decided to block the Resources?”
or “What was happening just before you decided to block the Resources?” We keep a dialogue going
until we fully understand. Once we know the blocking concern, we can clear it by providing missing
information, orienting to the present time, and explaining about the harmless recording. When all fears
have been fully addressed, the Resource team will return front and center.

250

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

When Blocks Won’t Clear
When wounded parts are too highly defended to let processing happen, our best attempts to clear
blocks will fall short, but there are still things we can do. First, we can lean in with a message like this:
Feel free to block the work as long as you need to. We’re not going to go there as long as you
object. Meanwhile, the Resources will be here for you to support you and love you unconditionally.
I look forward to the day when the idea of growing to meet your full potential feels good to you.
Next, we can plant seeds for future change. My favorite way to do that is with a question like:
If ___(healing could give you what you want)____, would that interest you?
For example, if a part fears that healing will make her more vulnerable, we could ask, “If you knew
that healing could make you less vulnerable, not more , would that interest you?” Or, if a part fears
that healing leads to others being hurt, we could ask, “If you could heal without anyone being hurt,
would that interest you?” Questions like this are designed to get wounded parts thinking about new
possibilities. It’s a way of planting seeds for future change.

Chapter 18 Summary
In this chapter we explore how clients and wounded parts may not say outright what their
blocking concerns are, but we can detect them from subtle clues. Questions and interventions
helpful in uncovering these concerns are provided here. Once we know what a blocking concern
is, we can provide an adequate remedy. Five types of blocking concerns are described in detail,
including here-and-now issues, missed priorities, missing information, misunderstandings, and
trouble envisioning the healing process. Four types of Resource-related concerns are outlined
as well. Processing can stall when wounded parts project unkind caregivers onto robust
Resources, when wounded parts stand in for robust Resources, when the “Resources” are not
actually robust, or when wounded parts are blocking access to robust Resources. Remedies for
all types of blocking concerns are discussed in this chapter. Some of the methods presented
here for detecting and clearing blocks are generally applicable and can be applied while doing
EMDR too.

Future Study

The best part of being a psychotherapist is helping our clients heal. It’s watching their unwanted
behaviors, beliefs, emotions, and urges diminish over time. We are challenged to understand our
clients’ wounding experiences so well that we can tailor their treatment to precisely what they need.
It’s my hope that this book has given you some new ideas about how to do that.
Many of our clients, like Carla, have both attachment wounds and trauma wounds. (See Carla’s history
on page 42.) While the stabilization interventions I’ve shared here provide significant healing for
wounded child parts, they do not usually complete the healing. We need to follow up with EMDR for
trauma processing and/or additional DNMS interventions for more substantial needs meeting.
The ego state interventions described in this book come from Stage 1 of three DNMS processing stages.
Stage 1 stabilizes wounded parts who are triggered. It pulls them out of trance and brings them into
the present moment where they feel safe and comforted by loving attuned Resources. Once stabilized
the parts are no longer triggered but could be triggered again. Stage 2 turns off the recordings of
hurtful messages (like “You’re bad”) that play back and trigger wounded parts. The messages may stay
off for a long time, or they may come back on. As long as the hurtful messages stay off, the associated
parts will not get triggered again. Stage 3 provides comprehensive needs meeting for wounded parts,
to get them completely unstuck so they cannot be triggered again. In the process, the recordings of
hurtful messages get archived and will never play back again. For more information about these
additional DNMS protocols and interventions, go to dnmsinstitute.com.

Appendix A
Maltreatment-Driven Neuroanatomical Abnormalities

Our brains develop in stages—from conception to early adulthood—in both structure and function.
Certain types of childhood wounding can do lasting harm to a brain’s developing neuroanatomy. These
abnormalities may make EMDR less effective for some clients. It may make it more difficult for them
to tolerate the emotional intensity of EMDR or more difficult to access adaptive information. Here are
a few examples of abnormalities detected in brain scans.
 Adults mistreated in childhood have a smaller corpus callosum,1 a smaller hippocampus,2 and a
neocortex with attenuated structure or function. 3
 Hearing parental verbal abuse is associated with less gray matter in the auditory cortex. 4
 Witnessing violence is associated with less gray matter in the visual cortex.5
 Maltreatment before puberty is associated with an increase in amygdala size. 6
 Maltreatment after puberty it’s associated with a decrease in amygdala size.7
 Adult survivors of childhood abuse have less integration between right and left hemispheres.8
Furthermore, a developing brain has sensitive periods—windows of time when specific regions make
important developmental leaps. Maltreatment during those sensitive periods appears to amplify the
harm.9 Here are two examples.
 The visual-limbic pathway, which connects the occipital cortex to the temporal cortex, supports
vision-specific emotional, memory, and learning processes. Important myelination of this pathway
occurs around ages seven to thirteen. Children who observe repeated acts of domestic violence
around this age will have less of this myelination. 10
 In a group of young women who were sexually abused as girls, researchers found periods of maximal
susceptibility for brain region development. For the hippocampus it was around ages three to five,
for the corpus callosum around ages nine to ten, and for the neocortex around ages fourteen to
sixteen.11
When a potential danger is detected (like hearing a loud, startling noise), multiple regions of the brain
work together to assess the risk and calculate an appropriate response. The more a brain is riddled
with neuroanatomical abnormalities because of childhood maltreatment, the more likely it is to make
an impaired assessment, leading to an emotional overreaction or underreaction.12
Our more foundationally unstable clients are likely to have neuroanatomical abnormalities. These
abnormalities may account for why our most wounded clients move through therapy with surprise
twists and turns. They may not respond to treatment with the same ease as our healthier clients. This
does not mean therapy cannot be helpful for them, but we should be mindful that the brain of a client
who was treated badly in childhood may not process efficiently.
