Appendix B
Mental Health and the Microbiome

The Human Genome Project (HGP), completed in 2003, gave us a complete human genetic blueprint.1
The logical next step was the Human Microbiome Project—launched to better understand the microbes
we host and the roles they play in our health and disease. 2 Since the project began in 2008, there’s
been a spike in microbiome research, providing a wealth of evidence that our microbial community is
able to influence our behavior, thoughts, and mood.3
Of the roughly seventy trillion cells that make up the human body, about forty trillion are microbes—
bacteria, viruses, and fungi—living on our skin, mucous membranes, and inside the gut. 4 Most live in
a complex ecosystem in the gut, which collectively functions as an extra organ.5 This microbiome hosts
both good and bad microbes.6
Good Microbes: Good gut microbes live within us symbiotically. They thrive on a diet of high-fiber fruit
vegetables, whole grains, legumes, and nuts.7 Fermented foods8 and vigorous exercise also help them
flourish.9 Good bacteria convert fiber into short-chain fatty acids, which nourish and strengthen the gut’s
mucosal lining to bolster immunity. They also make helpful metabolites, vitamins, and neurotransmitters
that serve to cool inflammation, fight off pathogens, protect against cancer, and support emotion
regulation.10 A healthy gut hosts a large variety of good microbes that can keep damage from bad
microbes to a minimum.
Bad Microbes: Bad gut microbes live within us parasitically. They thrive on a diet of low-fiber foods
like sugar, processed grains, meat, animal fat, dairy, and eggs. 11 They convert animal-protein foods
into trimethylamine, which the liver converts to the toxin trimethylamine N-oxide (TMAO). High levels
of TMAO accelerate the risk of atherosclerosis, kidney disease, heart attack, and stroke. 12
Dysbiosis: Dysbiosis is an unhealthy microbial imbalance. It happens when bad gut microbes flourish,
crowding out the good ones. It’s driven by exposure to antibiotics, 13 artificial sweeteners,14 food
additives,15 environmental pollutants,16 glyphosate,17 and/or prolonged consumption of a high-sugar,
high-fat, low-fiber diet.18 If such exposures are not stopped and remedied with foods that specifically
feed and restore good microbes, the dysbiosis will cause chronic inflammation that increases susceptibility
to depression, anxiety, cancers, diabetes, obesity, allergies, and many other modern diseases. 19
Leaky Gut: The large intestine has a delicate, single-cell-thick mucosal lining that’s tasked with keeping
fermenting food particles inside the colon while moving helpful substances—metabolites, vitamins,
neurotransmitters, and short-chain fatty acids—out to the bloodstream. This sensitive lining can be
damaged by dysbiosis, pathogens, parasites, drugs, alcohol, pollutants, and extreme stress—making it
leaky. A leaky gut allows pathogens and food molecules (which should be defecated) to ooze into the
bloodstream. This can create inflammatory conditions that make us more susceptible to a variety of
physical and emotional problems.20

256

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

The Gut-Brain Axis: There’s a constant bidirectional communication between the gut and brain through
multiple channels, including the nervous system, immune system, and endocrine system.
▪

The Nervous System: Gut bacteria play a role in emotion regulation by making neurotransmitters
such as serotonin, dopamine, and GABA, which send electrical signals to the brain via the vagus
nerve. A dysbiotic gut that’s unable to produce enough mood-stabilizing neurotransmitters can
contribute to depression and anxiety.21

▪

The Immune System: Cells in the gut lining react to ingested pathogens by producing and releasing
cytokines into the bloodstream—an inflammatory response that sounds an alarm. This alarm
evokes an immune reaction in both the body and brain intense enough to generate a temporarily
depressed mood. A leaky gut that continuously releases microbes and food molecules into the
bloodstream will generate chronic inflammation, which can contribute to persistent depression.22

▪

The Endocrine System: When the limbic system perceives an external threat (like a life-threatening
fire), the hypothalamus alerts the pituitary, which tells the adrenals to release anxiety-evoking
cortisol. Likewise, when there’s an internal threat to the gut, like a pathogen or inflammation, the
hypothalamus signals the pituitary to tell the adrenal to release cortisol. If chronic inflammation is
coming from a continuous leaky gut, cortisol will be constantly released. This can contribute to
persistent anxiety.23

This matters to us as psychotherapists because chronic inflammation from dysbiosis may contribute
significantly to our clients’ mental health symptoms. It can turn the volume way up on the depression
and anxiety driven by trauma and attachment wounds. For all the good psychotherapy does, it will not
resolve symptoms of anxiety and depression that are rooted in a dysbiotic or leaky gut.
For some clients, getting to a baseline of well-being may depend on adopting a healthier lifestyle—
independent of trauma processing. Functioning at our highest potential requires a nutritious diet,
regular exercise, restful sleep, and supportive emotional connections. Foundationally stable clients may
readily adopt healthy lifestyle changes once they understand the emotional benefits of change.
Foundationally unstable clients may be more resistant to lifestyle changes, especially when unhealthy
behaviors (like high sugar consumption) are considered essential for alleviating emotional pain.
Stabilizing wounded parts who are overly attached to health-sabotaging behaviors may give these
clients the best chance possible of making healthy lifestyle changes.

Appendix C
Popular Ego State Psychotherapies

Ego State Therapy
The term ego state therapy refers to a broad category of models and interventions for healing wounded
ego states. It’s also the title of a therapy approach developed by Jack and Helen Watkins while
researching multiple personality disorder in the 1970s. Their ego state therapy approach is based on
the belief that ego states constitute a "family of self" within an individual.1 Hypnosis and family/group
therapy techniques are used to resolve conflicts between ego states. Internal negotiations may use
directive, behavioral, abreactive, analytic, or humanistic techniques of treatment, usually under
hypnosis. Dependent ego states are helped to find the nurturing needed to heal, internally. According
to Watkins & Watkins, as internal needs are satisfied and conflicts are resolved, the internal family
becomes happy, and the whole person is well adjusted.

Internal Family Systems Therapy
Internal Family Systems (IFS), developed by Richard Schwartz in the 1980s, evolved out of the
observation that patients had an internal system of parts of self that interacted just like members of a
family.2 This approach is based on the idea that we all have a core Self that contains crucial leadership
qualities, like curiosity, competence, clarity, confidence, compassion, and acceptance. We also have
three types of wounded parts. Exiles are young traumatized parts who embody pain, terror, and fear,
which are isolated from the rest of the system. Managers are wounded parts who control situations and
relationships in an effort to protect exiles from feeling hurt or rejection. Firefighters are wounded parts
who engage when Exiles are activated in an effort to control and extinguish their feelings. Therapy is
designed to unburden parts so a client can be effectively led by the Self. For a detailed comparison of
the DNMS and IFS, go to www.dnmsinstitute.com/IFSvsDNMS.pdf.

Structural Dissociation
Structural Dissociation was developed by Onno van der Hart, Ellert Nijenhuis, and Kathy Steele in the
2000s.3 It posits the existence of an Apparently Normal Self (ANP) who is rational, present-oriented,
and committed to handling daily life, and Emotional Parts (EPs) who are holding the memories and
emotions of unresolved wounds. There are three categories of structural dissociation: Primary,
Secondary, and Tertiary. In Primary Structural Dissociation the client has one ANP and one EP, usually
from a single traumatic event. They are co-conscious. The EP holds the trauma memory, feels all the
related emotions, has flashbacks, and engages in defensive or offensive behaviors, like fight, flee, or
freeze. The usual diagnosis is PTSD. The ANP is in charge of daily living, which is occasionally disrupted
when the EP gets triggered. In Secondary Structural Dissociation the client has one ANP and multiple
EPs, from multiple wounding events. The wounding could be from traumas, unmet attachment needs,
or both. The usual diagnosis is Complex PTSD. The EPs are highly emotional, reactive, and dysfunctional,
and feel very stuck in the past. They are often triggered. They may try to take charge of daily living.
Tertiary Structural Dissociation is equivalent to Dissociative Identity Disorder (DID). The client has two
or more ANPs that handle different aspects of daily life. There are multiple EPs from many very

260

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

wounding events. Parts often lack co-consciousness. Amnesia is a defining characteristic. The EPs are
highly dysfunctional and often triggered. They frequently take charge of daily living. They perceive
adult life to be as dangerous as childhood. Treatment for structural dissociation is a phased approach.
Phase 1 is focused on symptom reduction and stabilization, in particular overcoming phobias of mental
contents and dissociative parts, and of attachment to and attachment loss of the therapist. Phase 2 is
focused on treating traumatic memories, in particular overcoming phobias related to traumatic
memories and to insecure attachment to the perpetrator(s), particularly in EPs. Phase 3 is focused on
integration and rehabilitation, in particular overcoming phobias of normal life, healthy risk-taking and
change, and intimacy.4

Transactional Analysis
Transactional Analysis (TA), developed by Eric Berne in the 1950s, is based on the idea that the human
personality is made up of three distinct ego states: a Parent, Adult, and Child.5 Each ego state is a set
of related thoughts, feelings, and behaviors from which we can understand interpersonal and
intrapsychic dynamics. Berne believed decisions made in childhood in the interest of survival could
become self-limiting and result in dysfunctional behavior. The Parent ego state has two parts (the
Nurturing and Controlling Parent), and the Child ego state has two parts (the Adapted and Free Child).
The therapy helps clients become free of inappropriate, inauthentic, and displaced emotions tied to
maladaptive childhood scripts.

Gestalt Therapy
Gestalt Therapy, developed by Fritz Perls in the 1950s, invites clients to integrate and accept parts of
their personality they have disowned or denied.6 Perls believed a top dog (controlling, blamer part of
self) and an underdog (victim, complainer part of self) are in constant internal conflict. His top dog is
considered an introject of societal authority figures and parents conveying “you should” and “you
shouldn’t” messages. The underdog resists the top dog’s messages. The empty chair technique is a
role-play used to reduce the internal conflict. The client engages these conflicted parts of self in
dialogue by playing the top dog in one chair talking to the underdog in another chair. The internal
conflict resolves as both parts come to understand and accept each other.

The SARI Model
The SARI Model, developed by Maggie Phillips and Claire Frederick in the 1980s, is a four-stage approach
for the treatment of clients with ego state conflicts and dissociative disorders.7 SARI is an acronym for
four stages of treatment. Stage 1 (S) is for establishing safety and stabilization; Stage 2 (A) is for accessing
the trauma and related resources; Stage 3 (R) is for resolving traumatic experiences and restabilization;
and Stage 4 (I) is for personality integration and the creation of a new identity. Phillips & Frederick
advocate the use of clinical and Ericksonian hypnotherapy at each stage of treatment.

Psychosynthesis
Psychosynthesis, developed by Roberto Assagioli in the 1950s, is an approach focused on the integration
of previously separate elements into a more unified whole.8 Those separate elements range from the
lower unconscious subpersonalities (preprogrammed, contradictory aspects of self) to the superconscious
(inspiration, guidance, comfort, strength, peace, hope, wisdom). Much of psychosynthesis is aimed at
recognizing and harmonizing subpersonalities. Methods commonly used include honoring parts of
self, acknowledging and meeting subpersonality’s needs, guided imagery, body awareness and
movement, symbolic art work, journal keeping, training of the will, goal setting, dreamwork, development
of the imagination and intuition, Gestalt, ideal models, and meditation.

Appendix C: Popular Ego State Psychotherapies

261

Voice Dialogue
Voice Dialogue, developed by Hal and Sidra Stone in the 1970s, aims to expand a client's ability to act
consciously.9 The therapy is designed to help a client develop an Aware Ego that is able to make choices
by taking the views of opposing selves into consideration. Those contrasting views come from Primary
Selves (responsible for executive functioning and decision making) and Disowned Selves (repressed
vulnerable parts of self) that are often in direct conflict. The goal of Voice Dialogue is not to change
parts of self but to bring sufficient awareness to internal dynamics that harmony can replace conflict.
One aspect of this work involves honoring the parts and acknowledging and meeting their needs.

Inner Child Psychotherapy
Inner Child Psychotherapy is a broad category. Many have written on this topic—each approaching it
with their own ideas of treatment. It’s based on the idea that adults who suffered neglect, abuse, or
unmet needs from childhood can heal past hurts by nurturing their child ego states. John Bradshaw
popularized this concept in the 1980s. In his book Homecoming,10 Bradshaw outlined methods for
using a wise and gentle old wizard self to adopt and nurture an infant self, a toddler self, a preschool
self, a school-age self, and an adolescent self. Nancy Napier’s books Recreating Yourself11 and Getting
Through the Day12 suggest ways to connect to an optimal future self and to identify and embrace
disowned child parts of self. Lucia Capacchione wrote several books proposing nondominant hand
drawing and writing to connect to and heal the inner child.13 Others who have promoted inner child
healing work include Bishop & Grunte,14 Chopich,15 Hay,16 Levin,17 Missildine,18 Paul,19 Taylor,20 and
Whitfield.21 The DNMS can be considered an Inner Child Psychotherapy.

Appendix D
Dissociative Disorders

Dissociative Disorders Defined
People with dissociative disorders experience a disconnection and lack of continuity between thoughts,
memories, surroundings, actions and identity to the degree it causes problems with everyday functioning.
The Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders1 (DSM-5) lists three main types of dissociative
disorders: Dissociative Identity Disorder, Depersonalization/Derealization Disorder, and Dissociative
Amnesia. (Two additional diagnostic categories, Other Specified Dissociative Disorder and Unspecified
Dissociative Disorder, catch those who do not fall squarely in one of the main three types.) Here’s a
brief description of each, per the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria.
 Dissociative Identity Disorder (DID): This diagnosis applies when an individual has two or more
distinctly different personality states (alter egos or alters) driving a discontinuity in sense of self and
sense of agency. This includes related alterations in affect, behavior, consciousness, memory,
perception, cognition, and/or sensory-motor functioning. Dissociative symptoms may be observed
by others or reported by the individual. The individual will have recurrent gaps in the recall of
everyday events, personal information, and/or traumatic events that are inconsistent with ordinary
forgetfulness. For example: Margaret has two distinctly different personality states, an alter named
Peg and an alter named Meg. Peg is a barhopping, man-chasing extrovert who wears makeup, short
skirts, and a red wig. Meg is a quiet, timid, introverted bookworm who doesn’t like men. If Peg goes
barhopping one night, or Meg stays in to read a book all night, the next day Margaret will be unable
to recall what happened.
 Depersonalization/Derealization Disorder (DPD): This diagnosis applies when an individual has
persistent or recurrent experiences of depersonalization, derealization or both. Depersonalization is
marked by experiences of unreality, detachment, or being an outside observer with respect to one's
thoughts, feelings, sensations, body, or actions (e.g., perceptual alterations, distorted sense of time,
unreal or absent self, emotional and/or physical numbing). Derealization is marked by experiences
of unreality or detachment with respect to surroundings (e.g., individuals or objects are experienced
as unreal, dreamlike, foggy, lifeless, or visually distorted. For example: Henry sits on his back deck
surveying his garden. He feels detached from his body, like he’s floating above the house. He feels
numb. In the garden, birds and bugs are flying around while squirrels are looking for acorns, but all
Henry sees is a lifeless fog. He is unsure of who or where he is.
 Dissociative Amnesia: This diagnosis applies when an individual has an inability to recall important
autobiographic information that cannot be accounted for by ordinary forgetfulness or an injury to
the brain. This includes amnesia for a particular stressful or traumatic event, or for identity and life
history. For example: One day Amber went the basement to do laundry. She found a suicide note
and an empty bottle of sleeping pills next to her sixteen-year-old daughter’s body. Later, when asked
to describe what she saw that day in the basement, Amber had no memory of finding the body.

Appendix D: Dissociative Disorders

263

Origins of Dissociative Disorders
Dissociation is a means of defending against and coping with intensely distressing experiences like
violent incest or soul-crushing emotional neglect. Dissociative disorders usually begin in early childhood
as an adaptive response to a physically or emotionally unsafe world, when a child is relatively needy and
powerless. The more caregivers fail at meeting a child’s emotional needs and safety needs the more
intense the childhood wounding will be. A dissociative disorder is more likely to develop if the wounding
was severe, started early in life, happened repeatedly and frequently, and took place over many
developmental stages. Foundational instability goes hand in glove with dissociation. The more
foundationally unstable clients are, the more dissociative symptoms they’ll have and the more those
dissociative symptoms will complicate their lives.

Diagnosing Dissociative Disorders
Four self-report screening tests have been developed to help identify those who may have a dissociative
disorder. They include the Multidimensional Inventory of Dissociation (MID),2 Dissociative Experiences
Scale (DES),3 Somatoform Dissociation Questionnaire (SDQ-20),4 and Multiscale Dissociation Inventory
(MDI).5 A screening test cannot diagnose a dissociative disorder, it can only signal the possibility of one.
These tests are relatively easy to administer, but they have serious limitations. Dissociative clients who
are reluctant to reveal their dissociative episodes may provide an inaccurate self-report. And alters who
wish to keep the dissociation secret, may give misleading answers, which can render a screening test
score invalid.
There are two diagnostic instruments that can diagnose dissociative disorders. They include Colin
Ross’s Dissociative Disorders Interview Schedule (DDIS),6 a highly structured yes/no test, and Marlene
Steinberg’s Structured Clinical Interview for Dissociative Disorders (SCID-D), an interactive, semistructured interview modeled on a clinical interview. 7 The SCID-D allows the interviewer to elicit
elaborate, rich descriptions of a client’s dissociative experiences, making it possible to assess the severity
of dissociative symptoms. Information gathered in the interview becomes relevant to treatment. For the
past twenty years, the SCID-D has been considered the gold standard in the diagnosis of dissociative
disorders, both clinically and in research. 8

The Importance of Diagnosing Dissociative Disorders
Diagnosing dissociative disorders in advance of EMDR is very important. Trauma processing can be
emotionally taxing for any client, but it can be especially destabilizing for dissociative clients. The better we
understand a client’s degree of dissociation, the better we can prepare them for EMDR. Administering the
SCID-D is an excellent way to assess severity of dissociative symptoms and obtain an accurate diagnosis.

Observational Clues to Dissociative Disorders
In my experience, clients will reveal the severity of their dissociation over time as we work though the
healing steps laid out in Chapters 4-15 of this book. Sometimes these revelations come early in the work.
Sometimes they come later, after dissociated parts trust us enough to risk being seen. The DNMS healing
interventions always convey unconditional positive regard and radical acceptance. As dissociated parts
notice this they feel reassured and eventually choose to emerge. As the stabilization process unfolds,
we can gather clues about the severity of dissociation from multiple observations. For example, clients
are more likely to be dissociative if they…
 grew up with caregivers who failed to meet their attachment needs.
 have high scores on my Quality of Attachment Interview Questionnaire. (See Chapter 4.)

264

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

 have child parts who are very easily triggered, with intense overreactions that are slow to recover.
 have child parts who are fearful of mobilizing Resources.
 have child parts who are highly defended—mistrusting of us and the Resources.
 have child parts fearful of the healing process or outcome.
 have child parts holding lots of faulty assumptions about self and world.
 have child parts who struggle to orient to present time.
 have child parts who are alarmed and disoriented upon discovering they’re in an adult body now.
 have child parts who experience the harmless recordings from the past as very vivid or dangerous.
 have child parts who struggle to understand these recordings are actually harmless.
 have controlling reactive parts (Chapter 16), intrusive parts, or parts who show up overwhelmed.
 have lots of processing blocks—especially blocks that arise covertly (page 152).
When I determine a client has a dissociative disorder, I’m less concerned about making a precise diagnosis
of the type (e.g. DID versus DPD) and more concerned with understanding the degree and severity.
The precise type of dissociation will not dictate my treatment approach. That’s because the therapy steps
are flexible enough to embrace and treat all five DSM-5 diagnostic categories. If a single diagnosis is
needed for insurance purposes, an Unspecified Dissociative Disorder diagnosis can serve as a placeholder
until more is known.

Treating Dissociative Disorders
In my experience, dissociative disorders are rooted more in attachment wounds than in trauma wounds.
When we apply the DNMS stabilizing interventions outlined in this book, with lots of dissociated parts,
we’re treating the attachment wounds and dissociation in ways that lead to real trait change. As more and
more wounded parts get stabilized, between-session triggering episodes happen less often, they’re less
intense, and clients recover more quickly. This is a sign of gains in foundational stability. Once a wellResourced, dissociative client is foundationally stable enough to manage emotions well for extended
periods, healing any remaining trauma wounds safely and effectively with EMDR becomes an option.
The stabilizing interventions described in this book can help heal wounded parts with all degrees of
dissociation from mild to extreme. Wounded parts who are mildly dissociative will generally be easier
to work with. They’ll be more open to the Resources’ support and will have fewer processing blocks.
Wounded parts who are extremely dissociative can be highly defended, making them extra challenging
to work with. The self-system can be very complex. It can take a long time for some parts to develop
enough trust in the therapist and the Resources to be open to change and growth. Because stabilizing
one wounded part can feel threatening to other parts, the therapy can take lots of twists and turns.
While the interventions in this book are designed to treat the full range of dissociative disorders,
treating extremely dissociative clients might require expert consultation.

Appendix E
Attachment Theory Overview

The Attachment System
According to John Bowlby,1 an inborn attachment system has emerged in humans over the course of
evolution. This system provides the instinctive motivation for a child to seek proximity to a caregiver
who will provide a safe, secure base from which to learn and grow. A child naturally anticipates that a
caregiver will provide a safe haven and a source of comfort in times of distress. This attachment system
increases the likelihood that young children, who cannot care for themselves, will survive and eventually
reproduce.
The attachment system motivates a child to bid for a caregiver’s attention, reassurance, comfort, and
protection with attachment behaviors. For example, a baby may cry, reach out, or cling to her mother,
a toddler may follow her mother or climb on her lap, or a five-year-old may make contact with her
mother by catching her eye or calling out for a vocal response. These behaviors are designed to elicit
a caregiver response that reassures the child she is loved and safe. An appropriate caregiver answer to
such bids might include holding, rocking, stroking the child, or making vocal or eye contact. The child
will return to sleep, play, or exploration once she feels sufficiently reassured.2 This helps the child
develop an enduring emotional bond with the caregiver.3

Attunement
According to Allan Schore,4 caregiver attunement conveys to an infant that she is loved, seen, heard,
and understood. It occurs during intense moments of nonverbal communication between an infant and
caregiver when their states of mind are aligned. They communicate their affect to each other via facial
expression, vocalizations, body gestures, eye contact and laughter. An attuned caregiver will sense what
the infant wants and needs from behavioral cues, whether obvious (crying, clinging, reaching) or subtle
(looking away, becoming numb), and will respond appropriately to those cues. This includes engaging
when the infant wants connection and disengaging when the infant wants distance. During affect
attunement, the infant and caregiver communicate their internal emotional states externally. For
example, they smile and laugh at each other. Alignment, a component of attunement, occurs when
one person alters her state of mind to approximate the state of mind of the other. This can be a oneway or two-way process. For example, a mother preparing an excited child for bed calms the child
down more easily if she first aligns with the child’s excited state and then brings the child down to a
calmer state.5

Mental Models
Mental models are summations or generalized representations of repeated experiences.6 They guide
a person to unconsciously interpret present experiences and anticipate future ones. The mental models
a child creates over the first two years of life are significant, because they form the basis for
assumptions about self, relationships, and the world that can last a lifetime. A baby who is skillfully

266

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

cared for and comforted by loving, attuned caregivers will learn to expect care and comfort in the
future. As the baby gets what she expects, over time the mental model of a predictable world with
safety and connection is strengthened.7
Within an intimate, attuned, interpersonal relationship, a young child’s immature brain uses the mature
functions of a caretaker’s brain to organize its own processes. 8 Loving, sensitive caregivers will amplify a
child’s positive states and modulate the negative states. When this is experienced repeatedly, the child
can construct a mental model for managing her own emotions. As a child’s needs for love, safety,
comfort, and connection are consistently met throughout childhood, she will grow into an adult who
can feel secure in herself and securely attach to others.
In contrast, a baby who is subjected to abuse, rejection, neglect, or enmeshment in the care of unskillful
caregivers will learn to anticipate more of the same. As this baby continues to get what she expects
(e.g., abuse or neglect), her mental model of an unsafe or insecure world is strengthened. Even if this
child later experiences a loving home, those mental models formed in the early years will continue to
contribute to her assumptions about self, relationships, and the world and to her interpretations of new
experiences. If a child is consistently led to expect mistreatment or rejection throughout childhood,
she will grow into an adult who does not feel secure within herself and who struggles to securely attach
to others.

Internal Representations of Caregivers and Self-Regulation
Children develop internal representations of their significant caregivers.9 This works to a child’s
advantage when skillful caregivers are able to manage and regulate her emotions well. By providing
an internalized sense of a secure base and a safe haven, these internalized caregivers can facilitate the
self-regulation of emotions. For example, each time a young girl suffers minor injuries playing in the
yard, her mother comforts her with a smile, a hug, and a Band-Aid. Her mother tells her, “It may hurt for
a while, but you’re going to be okay.” At age fifteen, she suffers her first romantic loss when her
boyfriend breaks up with her. As she grieves the loss, her internalized mother reassures her with “It’s
going to hurt for a while, but you’re going to be okay.” As children learn to manage their emotions,
they are able to individuate and mature in healthy ways.
In contrast, internalization of caregivers works to a child’s disadvantage when unskillful caregivers are
unable to manage and regulate her emotions well. Such internalized caregivers can perpetuate the
dysregulation of emotions by either generating or failing to soothe insecurity and anxiety. This
dysregulation will impair a child’s development of normal behaviors such as play, exploration, and social
interactions.

Attachment Styles
Individuals can form secure or insecure styles of attachment depending on their interactions with their
primary caregivers.

Secure Attachment
Children develop secure attachments to caregivers who rate high in maternal sensitivity,10 respond
promptly to distress,11 provide moderate stimulation, 12 are nonintrusive,13 interact with synchrony, 14
and are warm, involved, and responsive.15 Caregivers of secure infants interact positively, are rarely
over-arousing, and are able to stabilize a child’s disorganized emotional responses. These caregivers remain
calm and secure in stressful situations and regard their child’s negative emotions as meaningful and not
threatening.16

Appendix E: Attachment Theory Overview

267

Relationships between children and caregivers will inevitably experience emotional breaks. Loving, attuned
caregivers recognize and respond to these ruptures with soothing and comfort, which leads to the
necessary repair. This contributes to a secure attachment.
The benefits of a secure attachment last a lifetime. Securely attached infants trust they will be soothed
when upset, so they readily engage in play and exploration. Securely attached preschool children have
been observed to be more ego-resilient and independent, demonstrating higher self-esteem than
insecure children. They have friends, social skills, and empathy for peers in distress.17 Securely attached
adults are able to understand themselves and others, recognize their own internal conflicts, and realize
why their parents behaved as they did.18 Mary Main observed that securely attached adults, when asked
to discuss childhood experiences (including painful interactions with parents), were able to discuss their
experiences coherently, accept their parents’ failings, show compassion, value attachment, demonstrate
a strong personal identity, display ease with their personal imperfections, accept personal responsibility,
and see reality clearly.19
A secure attachment to loving, attuned caregivers is the most important of all childhood needs. Children
who grow up feeling securely attached to consistently loving, attuned caregivers become well-adjusted
adults with the ability to form secure attachments, regulate their own emotions, and hold a positive
outlook on self and the world.20

Insecure Attachment
A child will form an insecure or anxious attachment to caregivers who are not able to provide a secure
base or a safe haven. Ainsworth et al. observed two types of insecure attachments in children: avoidant
and ambivalent.21 Avoidantly attached children tend to enter adulthood with a dismissive state of
mind with respect to attachment, while ambivalently attached children tend to enter adulthood with a
preoccupied state of mind with respect to attachment.22
Avoidant/Dismissive Attachment
A child will develop an avoidant attachment to rejecting caregivers. Caregiver behaviors that predict
an avoidant attachment include withdrawing, hesitating, wincing, and arching away from the child.
These caregivers actively block awareness of a child’s attachment behaviors (e.g., crying, reaching).
They are averse to physical and emotional interactions with the child. Children will experience such
withdrawals as an assault on their safe haven. These caregivers are reluctant to organize the child’s
attention or behavior or modulate her emotions. They are unwilling to provide comfort, whether the
child’s distress is aroused by environmental upsets or by the caregiver’s behavior. 23 When there’s an
emotional break with the child, a rejecting caregiver will lack the willingness or ability to provide the
needed reassurance to affect a repair.
Avoidant toddlers exhibit little interest in adults attempting to attract their attention, and little
motivation to maintain contact. They appear undisturbed by a caregiver's departure and indifferent
about the caregiver’s return. While avoidant children feel angry that proximity to the caregiver is
restricted or blocked, they will not openly express their distress. Children may respond with rejecting
behaviors such as not looking at the caregiver or avoiding the caregiver entirely. They have learned to
expect that the caregiver will not provide comfort.
Main and Stadtman believe that avoidance is a mechanism to “modulate the painful and vacillating
emotion aroused by the historically rejecting mother.”24 Alan Sroufe observed three types of avoidant
preschoolers: (1) the lying bullies who blame others, (2) the shy, spacey loners who appear emotionally
flat, and (3) the obviously disturbed daydreamers with repetitive tics who show little interest in their
environment. The avoidant children were observed to be sullen and oppositional, often preying on

268

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

other children.25 They were disliked by their peers and described by preschool teachers as dishonest
and mean. While they normally exhibited negative, attention-seeking, dependent behaviors, they
would withdraw if injured or disappointed.
Adults who had an avoidant attachment style in childhood are said to have a dismissive state of mind
with respect to attachment in adulthood.26 Main observed that these adults, when asked to discuss
childhood experiences (including painful interactions with parents), tend to idealize one or both
parents, fail to support idealized characterizations with meaningful examples, show contempt for
attachment figures or attachment-related experiences, and describe themselves positively as strong,
independent, and normal. They minimize their negative childhood attachment experiences by
reporting they had little or no negative impact, insisting they were character-building experiences,
suggesting everyone has these experiences, and/or by summarizing a negative story with a positive
spin. They are not able to talk about or express feelings of vulnerability. Many are unable or unwilling
to answer questions about attachment experiences from childhood.
Generally speaking, children who grow up with an avoidant attachment to caregivers become adults
who are dismissive of emotional connections and attachments and who manage distress by ignoring
painful realities and repressing unpleasant emotions.
Ambivalent/Preoccupied Attachment
A child will develop an ambivalent attachment to intrusive/engulfing caregivers. Caregiver behaviors
that predict an ambivalent attachment (sometimes called anxious-resistant insecure attachment)
include routinely subjecting the child to high-intensity emotional stimulation while ignoring the child’s
bids for stimulation reduction (e.g., crying, looking away). These caregivers are emotionally insensitive,
haphazard, and unpredictable—emotionally available sometimes but not always. Even when they are
present, the child cannot be sure her signals and communications will be handled by the caregiver
appropriately. Because these caregivers are not attuned or sensitive to a child’s cues, they overwhelm
the child with stimulation. This interferes with the child’s ability to assimilate new experiences. These
caregivers are ineffective at setting boundaries and limits, reducing feelings of shame, and managing
the child’s aggressions.27 When there’s an emotional break with the child, an intrusive caregiver may
persist in making reparative contact but will do so without sufficient sensitivity or attunement. This
overwhelms the child and attempts to repair fail.
Ambivalent children alternate proximity-seeking behaviors with angry, rejecting behaviors—perhaps
punishing the caregiver for being unavailable. Because these caregivers do not function as a reliable,
secure base, the children can become preoccupied with the caregiver’s emotional states to the point
of not feeling free to play and explore independently. As this child displays heightened emotionality
and dependence, she succeeds in drawing the caregiver’s attention.28 The child becomes addicted to
the caregiver and to strategies to influence the caregiver to change. The ambivalent child learns that
fear will bring attention, so she looks for things to be afraid of. 29
Sroufe observed two types of ambivalent preschoolers: (1) those who were fidgety, impulsive, tense
with poor concentration, and easily upset by failures, and (2) those who were fearful, hypersensitive,
clingy, lacking in initiative, and prone to give up easily.30 These children were too preoccupied with
their own needs to have any feelings for the children around them. They were highly dependent,
exhibiting a weak sense of self. Their disruptive behavior often angered other children. Aggressive
encounters evoked a panic response, which made them easy targets for future bullying. The preschool
teachers described the ambivalent children as ineffective, emotionally immature, and incapable of
following rules. Ambivalent children worry about themselves and their intrusive caregiver, often
becoming the parentified child. The child may be afraid of going to school, not because something at

Appendix E: Attachment Theory Overview

269

the school is frightening but because she fears losing her caregiver or worries that her caregiver will
become unbearably lonely while she is away. 31
Adults who had an ambivalent attachment style in childhood are said to have a preoccupied state of
mind with respect to attachment in adulthood.32 Main observed that these adults, when asked to
discuss childhood experiences (including painful interactions with parents), would discuss disturbing
attachment experiences to excess. They would go on and on about parents’ failings, taking far more
than the usual conversational turn, demonstrating their preoccupation with anger. They exhibited a
lack of coherence by not answering the questions asked, wandering off topic, or oscillating between
positive and negative evaluations of a caregiver in rapid succession. They appeared incapable of
objectivity, insight, or genuine understanding about their attachment wounds and were consequently
unable to discuss them fruitfully or skillfully. They resorted to blame of self or others. Their sense of
self appeared closely linked to unresolvable disturbing experiences with their parents.
Children who grow up with an ambivalent attachment to caregivers become adults who are overly
dependent on others, lack the ability to manage their own emotions and impulses, and are preoccupied
with problematic emotional connections and attachments, both past and present.

Disorganized/Unresolved Attachment
In addition to the three childhood attachment styles listed above (secure, ambivalent, or avoidant), a
child may have a disorganized attachment to caregivers.33 The disorganized attachment status is a
secondary classification applied in addition to the child’s primary attachment status. For example, a child
may be secure-disorganized, ambivalent-disorganized, or avoidant-disorganized.
Infants identified as disorganized demonstrated unusual behaviors upon reunion with their mother
following a brief separation. Examples of these behaviors include sequential or simultaneous contradictory
behaviors (e.g., avoidance and contact-seeking gestures), incomplete approaches to the caregiver,
unusual body movements or posturing, fearful or dazed facial expressions, and rapid changes in affect.34
Main and Hesse attributed this disorganization to the child’s experiences with caregivers who are
dissociative due to unresolved attachment wounds, traumas, and losses. These caregivers slip into an
unresolved, anxious, dissociated state of mind and cannot provide the child with a secure base or safe
haven. When asked to discuss their experiences of childhood wounding, these adults become
disoriented, illogical, and incoherent.35
They hypothesize a child’s disorganized attachment is connected to frightening caregiver behaviors.
They reasoned that if a caregiver arouses a child’s fear, it places the child in an unresolvable paradox,
because the caregiver, who she’s instinctively drawn to go to for safety, is also the source of the fear.36
Karlen Lyons-Ruth et al. advanced two alternative hypotheses: (1) unresolved caregivers might display
competing or contradictory strategies for caring for the child, much as disorganized children display
competing or contradictory attachment strategies, and (2) any caregiver behaviors (e.g., rejection,
neglect, intrusion) that were unable to calm a child’s fear would also be potentially disorganizing,
whether or not those caregivers behaviors actually elicited the fear.37
Children who are not offered a safe haven from fearful arousal often become dissociated adults. As
they experience traumas and losses in adulthood, they are unable to resolve them, because their
disorganized mental models and internal representations lack the means to do so. 38

Earned/Autonomous Secure Attachment
Bowlby writes, “Fortunately the human psyche, like human bones, is strongly inclined towards selfhealing. The psychotherapist’s job, like that of the orthopedic surgeon’s, is to provide the conditions

270

Ego State Therapy Interventions to Prepare Attachment-Wounded Adults for EMDR

in which self-healing can best take place.”39 Individuals with any of the insecure attachment categories
listed above can achieve “earned” secure/autonomous attachment status. Individuals who have earned
secure/autonomous attachment status have finally made sense of their childhood wounds with the help
of one or more supportive relationships and/or psychotherapy.40 They are able to understand, organize,
and discuss painful childhood attachment relationships in a coherent way. (The term “earned” does
not refer to “deserving.”)

Caveat about Attachment Styles
Each attachment style described above reflects a relationship with a single caregiver. For example, an
infant could be securely attached to her nanny, avoidantly attached to her father, and ambivalently
attached to her mother. The attachment styles outlined above describe these classifications in their purest
form. While they are helpful categories for attachment researchers, in reality, many people do not fit
these classifications exactly as described. A given individual may exhibit behavior patterns from more than
one attachment classification.41 This is understandable considering that different ego states can have
different mental models, formed by the internalized representations of different significant caregivers.
